## 📚 Paper List

- **[InteractAgent: Agentic Human Motion Interaction with Memory-Augmented Reflection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25649)**  `arXiv:2610.25649`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Tao Zhang, Senhe Zhang, Zeyu Zhang, Yujia Zhang, Dong Gong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon human-scene interaction requires coordinated spatial reasoning, physical feasibility, and the ability to recover from compounding execution errors. Text-driven motion models are largely scene-agnostic and lack closed-loop correction, while geometry-conditioned approaches depend on paired scene-motion training or explicit 3D preprocessing that limits generalization to novel layouts and open-vocabulary goals. We present InteractAgent, an agentic multimodal LLM system for long-horizon human-scene interaction that plans, executes, observes, and reflects in a closed loop, using a single MLLM as both planner and evaluator and a frozen MotionLLM as the executor. InteractAgent integrates Online Observation for step-wise recovery decisions (continue/redo/replan) and Memory-Augmented Reflection for multi-round plan refinement with adaptive stopping. On UniHSI, InteractAgent improves task success and reduces penetration compared with training-free baselines. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Compile Once, Run Cheap: Frozen, Portable Skills for Embodied Agents, and What Recursive Self-Improvement Adds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25114)**  `arXiv:2610.25114`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jingwen Bai, Guochao Bai, Canbo Zhang, Shuangqi Li, Minchao Hu, Weichun Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied AI lacks the data-rich pretraining that gave language models their agents and skills, so new manipulation capability costs demonstrations per task or a frontier model in every episode. We study a third route: a strong general model acts only as an offline author that writes, runs, diagnoses and repairs a manipulation skill in simulation until it passes an exam on layouts drawn after freezing; the frozen skill then runs as code alone or under a cheap caller that reads only its description. We call this skill-level recursive self-improvement Skill-RSI and specify a compile protocol (isolated, audited author; hidden validation layouts; no-feedback and inherit-only controls; tamper-evident ledger) and a reuse protocol that runs the identical files with executors from another model family. On 7 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks with cluttered tables, Claude Code compiled 4 tasks into 7 frozen skills, 5 from a first draft needing only qualification, at USD 4.29-8.22 of author cost each (USD 55 per skill counting the 13 failed campaigns). The other three tasks did not compile in 18 campaigns, including five with GPT-6 and a pre-registered 150-layout run: on the hardest task every campaign scored 78-95 of 100 but had 3-16 safety aborts, failing the gate of 95% success with zero aborts; a 100-episode exam bounds an abort rate only to 3.6%. On hidden layouts, repair that inherits the previous version beat from-scratch rewriting in 8 of 8 controlled campaigns (sign test p = 0.004); an inherit-only lineage attributes most of the gap to inheritance, with development feedback adding a little (3 of 3, p = 0.125). Write-once campaigns found no detectable first-draft effect of practice (minimum detectable effect 14 points). The frozen skills kept 148/150 successes on new layouts with no model and 147/150 with a DeepSeek-flash caller at USD 0.00028-0.00054 per episode; no direct-control baseline was run.
  </details>

- **[Automating the Design of Embodied Agent Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30111)**  `arXiv:2606.30111`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jian Zhou, Sihao Lin, Jin Li, Shuai Fu, Gengze Zhou, Qi Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents are typically built as hand-designed compositions of perception, memory, planning, and action modules. This modularity exposes a large architectural design space, but current systems still rely on researcher intuition to choose where information is stored, how observations are processed, and how model calls are connected. Agent Architecture Search (AAS) automates such design for text-domain agents, but has not been systematically evaluated on perceptual embodied agents. We study this transfer. To make embodied architectures searchable, we introduce AgentCanvas on the executor side, representing embodied agents as editable typed graphs with simulator execution and episode-level logs. On the optimizer side, we lift AAS from LLM-call loops to tool-using coding-agent sessions, adapting ADAS and AFlow and introducing Knowledge Distill Loop (KDLoop). Across four embodied executors (MapGPT, SmartWay, ExploreEQA, and VoxPoser), search discovers improvements that persist on episodes never queried during search. KDLoop and AFlow independently discover elevation-tolerant observation processing. KDLoop's implementation also improves success rates over the baseline when transferred to real robots. These improvements are not uniform: some gains remain uncertain or reverse on held-out episodes, and an apparent gain exploits evaluator-side information. The experiments further show how rollout noise, local edit basins, and incomplete use of episode-level evidence constrain search. Together, these results establish the potential of automated architecture search for embodied agents and characterize the challenges of applying it in simulated and real-world environments. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Where is Physical RSI capability from? An Observational Scaling Law of LLM-as-policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26848)**  `arXiv:2610.26848`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Zekai Li, Jiayi Yu, Jinlu Zhang, Luyang Niu, Zekun Qi, Jun Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we systematically investigate the mechanisms, scaling behavior, and generalization of physical Recursive self-improvement. We introduce PhysRSI-eval to quantify physical RSI through a five-axis metric: First-Attempt Performance, Efficiency, Success, Budget and Improvement from failure. Different VLMs are evaluated on 18 manipulation tasks under a fixed interaction budget. The analysis of experimental results motivates the Physical Barrel Hypothesis: different capabilities may constrain different parts of improvement. The five-axis metrics also distinguish systems with close public benchmark averages and show that high scores need not come with early or efficient gains. We study observational scaling from two perspectives: predicting the relative performance of VLM agents from their public capabilities, and forecasting later progress from early physical feedback. Together, these analyses ask how quickly and how far an agent can improve. Moreover, we conduct workspace interventions and then analyze what improvement leaves behind. Removing history reduces late-round success, frozen workspaces remain useful on unseen layouts, and three cross-task cases achieve success earlier. The results suggest that scene-specific adaptation and reusable experience can coexist.
  </details>

- **[SPAR: Structural Pruning and Action Recovery for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26487)**  `arXiv:2610.26487`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Shenli Zheng, Zhou Fang, Yuan Du, Yijiang Liu, Li Du_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The limited onboard computing resources of robots make the inference demands of vision-language-action (VLA) models costly to accommodate. Reducing these demands through aggressive compression, however, can degrade manipulation performance beyond what supervised fine-tuning alone recovers. We present SPAR (Structural Pruning and Action Recovery), which simultaneously prunes the hidden and feed-forward intermediate dimensions of the large language model (LLM) backbone through pruning-aware tuning, then applies reinforcement learning (RL) to recover policy performance. Applied to $\pi_{0.5}$, SPAR reduces the language backbone from 2.34B to 0.09B parameters and the full model from 3.37B to 1.12B, removing approximately 96% and 67% of their parameters, respectively. At this compression level, RL raises average success across the four LIBERO suites from 84.55% to 93.45%, an 8.90-percentage-point gain. The compressed policy achieves a $1.98\times$ full-policy inference speedup over the unpruned model. These results identify substantial intermediate-dimension redundancy and show that post-pruning RL can recover manipulation performance in compact policies.
  </details>

- **[GemNav: Discrete-Token Visual Robot Navigation using a Multimodal Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06882)**  `arXiv:2607.06882`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Peter Bohm, Saimunur Rahman, Abdelwahed Khamis, Sagun Man Singh Shrestha, Chris McCool, Peyman Moghadam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual navigation policies built on large pretrained models increasingly rely on large cross-embodiment datasets and expressive action-generation mechanisms. In this paper, we ask whether a pretrained Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) can be adapted efficiently for robot navigation by rethinking the representation and construction of navigation actions. We introduce GemNav, a visual navigation policy that represents each robot-local waypoint coordinate as a categorical distribution over discrete metric value bins and soft-decodes it into a continuous coordinate for execution. This probabilistic representation retains likelihood information over alternative coordinate values while providing a compact action space suitable for robot navigation. We further construct training trajectories using metric-distance goal sampling rather than fixed future frame offsets, exposing the policy to varying physical navigation horizons independent of dataset frame rate and robot speed. GemNav is trained on a single 8.7-hour public SCAND dataset, roughly three orders of magnitude smaller than competing cross-embodiment datasets. Extensive offline and closed-loop experiments show improved out-of-distribution generalization over frame-based sampling and substantially more reliable obstacle navigation than a matched continuous regression head. The same policy transfers without per-platform retraining across unseen environments and three physically distinct robot platforms.
  </details>

- **[BioProVLA-Agent: An Affordable, Protocol-Driven, Vision-Enhanced VLA-Enabled Embodied Multi-Agent System with Closed-Loop-Capable Reasoning for Biological Laboratory Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07306)**  `arXiv:2605.07306`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhaohui Du, Zhe Wang, Dongzhan Zhou, Minting Pan, Hongmei Fei, Xiwen Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biological laboratory automation can reduce repetitive manual work and improve reproducibility, but reliable embodied execution in wet-lab environments remains challenging. Protocols are often unstructured, labware is frequently transparent or reflective, and multi-step procedures require state-aware execution beyond one-shot instruction following. Existing robotic systems often rely on costly hardware, fixed workflows, dedicated instruments, or robotics-oriented interfaces. Here, we introduce BioProVLA-Agent, an affordable, protocol-driven, vision-enhanced embodied multi-agent system enabled by Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models for biological manipulation. The system uses protocols as the task interface and integrates protocol parsing, visual state verification, and embodied execution in a closed-loop workflow. A Tailored LLM Protocol Agent converts protocols into verifiable subtasks; a VLM-RAG Verification Agent assesses readiness and completion using observations, robot states, retrieved knowledge, and success/failure examples; and a VLA Embodied Agent executes verified subtasks through a lightweight policy. To improve robustness under wet-lab visual perturbations, we develop AugSmolVLA, an online augmentation strategy targeting transparent labware, reflections, illumination shifts, and overexposure. We evaluate the system on a hierarchical benchmark covering 15 atomic tasks, 6 composite workflows, and 3 bimanual tasks, including tube loading, sorting, waste disposal, cap twisting, and liquid pouring. Across normal and high-exposure settings, AugSmolVLA improves execution stability over ACT, X-VLA, and the original SmolVLA, especially for precise placement, transparent-object manipulation, composite workflows, and visually degraded scenes. These results suggest a practical route toward accessible, protocol-centered, and verification-capable embodied AI for biological manipulation.
  </details>

- **[GPT-6 Astra Is a Native Visuo-Tactile Policy for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26846)**  `arXiv:2610.26846`  `cs.RO`  
  _Chen Yang, Linzhe Shi, Hang Zhang, Mu Xu, Jiansheng Fan, Chen Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal models have enabled GPT-6 Astra to perform robotic manipulation through in-context learning. Yet contact-rich control and tactile perception are often regarded as strengths of specialist policies, leaving open whether Astra can directly understand and use tactile observations. We provide, to our knowledge, the first demonstration that Astra directly interprets vision-based tactile images and uses their contact cues for manipulation. Tactile RGB images enter through its existing visual interface, enabling few-shot closed-loop control on nineteen simulated UniVTAC and OpenViTac tasks with 210 sampled scene seeds per setting. With visuo-tactile input, Astra achieves mean success rates of 93.8% and 81.8%, exceeding the strongest reported specialist baselines in our comparison by 22.9 and 13.1 percentage points, respectively. Vision-only performance also leads these baselines, reaching 76.3% and 80.6%. Trajectory analysis reveals active probing, cautious grasping, contact-guided alignment, and recovery, alongside difficulties with tangential loading and corrective execution. Our findings challenge the exclusivity of specialist policies in contact-rich manipulation and show that fine-grained vision can partially compensate for missing touch. They also identify vision-based tactile sensing as an interface for scaling contact understanding with foundation models and reveal signs of benchmark saturation. These results motivate joint visual-tactile pretraining, harder contact-rich evaluations, and physical-robot validation.
  </details>

- **[HandsOn: A Large-Scale Simulation Benchmark for Generalizable Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26765)**  `arXiv:2610.26765`  `cs.RO`  
  _Tianwei Luo, Yiming He, Hongjin Wu, Ting Pan, Debin Ren, Haorui Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  General-purpose manipulation requires reliable behavior across embodiments, objects, and task demands. We introduce HandsOn, a composable simulation platform and benchmark for cross-embodiment dexterous manipulation. It supports five robotic arms, six dexterous hands, and approximately 200 rigid, articulated, and deformable assets in randomized scenes. The benchmark pairs 18,000 curated human demonstrations with 66 human-verified tasks: Core-50 supports demonstration-based learning and execution tests, while Transfer-16 evaluates asset and relation transfer, skill composition, and deformable manipulation without target-task training demonstrations. Qualitative real-robot rollouts illustrate sim-to-real transfer. Across diffusion, vision-language-action, world-action, and prompted VLM-based methods, the best trained multi-task policy achieves 29.1% Core-50 success. Without benchmark finetuning, GPT-6 using move_eef for palm-and-finger control achieves 5.2% on Core-50 and 1.9% on Transfer-16 over 660 trials. Trained-policy stage diagnostics expose failures in contact establishment, action coordination, and maintaining achieved goals, with additional sequencing errors in composed tasks. HandsOn provides a shared basis for investigating manipulation generalization and the limits of reliable physical interaction.
  </details>

- **[Self-Crystallizing Behavior Trees: Amortizing Foundation-Model Reasoning into Deterministic Control for Autonomous Drones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25455)**  `arXiv:2610.25455`  `cs.RO`  
  _Behnam Moradi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Classical robot control, Finite State Machines and Behavior Trees (BTs), is deterministic and inspectable but fixed at design time: it fails on situations its designers did not anticipate. Foundation models can synthesize new control logic at runtime, at a cost in latency and non-determinism hard to accept in safety-critical aerial systems. Existing LLM-driven approaches keep the model in the loop permanently and discard what it produces. We propose Self-Crystallizing Behavior Trees (SCBT), in which the foundation model is a compiler rather than a runtime. When a BT node fails, an LLM proposes a minimal, mission-agnostic subtree patch; CMA-ES tunes its parameters in simulation; a contextual bandit decides whether a stored patch or a new LLM call is worth its cost; and a three-stage admission pipeline (static check, invariant-monitored replays, an empirical margin over the unpatched tree) admits only patches that demonstrably recover. Admitted patches are stored in a fleet library and, once the LLM call rate decays, crystallized into a deterministic BT that no human wrote. We implement SCBT on Microsoft Project AirSim with a schema-constrained LLM pipeline (Gemini 3.1 Pro) and a lockstep simulator. Over three seeds of a 20-episode learning phase with wind-gust and battery failures, SCBT used $11\pm1.4$ LLM calls against 26 for whole-tree regeneration and 22 without a library, with 0.33 calls per episode in the second half. The crystallized trees, 28 and 33 nodes with two patches each, reached $97\pm2\%$ mission success with no learning running, against 12\% for the unpatched tree and 98\% for a hand-coded expert tree, and reproduce decision traces exactly when re-ticked offline. Two negative results: replay admission rejected useful wind recoveries by attributing wind-induced geofence excursions to the patch, and a static-only ablation generalized better to held-out failures.
  </details>

- **[SurGPT: A Multi-Task Foundation Model for Robotic Surgical Skills Assessment and Gesture Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25426)**  `arXiv:2610.25426`  `cs.RO`  
  _Parham M. Kebria, Saeid Nahavandi, Mehrdad Saif, Fernando Bello_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The assessment of surgical skills in robotic surgery training has traditionally relied on subjective evaluations by human experts, creating bottlenecks in surgical education and limiting scalability. We present a novel foundation model that automatically analyzes robotic surgical videos to provide comprehensive skill assessment and gesture understanding. Combining a visual encoder with a transformer architecture, the model performs three key tasks simultaneously: 1) surgical task classification (knot tying, needle passing, suturing), 2) skill level assessment on a standardized scale, and 3) natural language generation of surgical gesture descriptions. The model processes video sequences from the da Vinci surgical robot and generates human-readable text descriptions of surgical actions, similar to how language models generate text from prompts, using a multi-task learning framework with adaptive uncertainty weighting to optimize performance across all objectives. Our transformer-based architecture incorporates positional encoding for temporal understanding and attention mechanisms for spatiotemporal feature integration. Evaluated on the da Vinci Skills Simulator dataset, the proposed framework demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in surgical task recognition and provides interpretable skill assessments that correlate with expert evaluations, with the model's ability to generate natural language descriptions of surgical gestures opening new possibilities for automated surgical education, real-time feedback systems, and objective skill assessment in surgical training and certification programs.
  </details>

- **[Refuse the Harm, Complete the Task via Graph-of-Rules Planning for LLM-Enabled Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24603)**  `arXiv:2610.24603`  `cs.RO`  
  _Ali Taheri, Alireza Taban, Qizhou Wang, Wanhua Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in embodied AI for robotic manipulation and planning, but their deployment in physical systems exposes vulnerabilities such as jailbreak attacks that induce unsafe behavior. Existing inference-time defenses are mainly evaluated on short instructions containing a single hazard, whereas realistic tasks may interleave safe and unsafe sub-tasks, conceal hazardous steps, or combine individually safe actions into unsafe outcomes. Such compound instructions make it harder for a single safety decision to distinguish hazardous sub-tasks from legitimate ones, risking either overlooking the hazard or rejecting the whole instruction. We introduce safe task completion, a task that requires an LLM-enabled robot to refuse the harmful part of an instruction while completing its legitimate part. To address this problem, we propose RuleGraph, a multi-agent Graph-of-Rules planning framework that distributes safety enforcement across multiple reasoning and verification stages. Five cooperating agents are used to ground general safety principles into world-specific rules, assess the instruction's sub-tasks in context, and derive a safe goal from the legitimate ones. Planning toward this goal is then organized as a graph of rule-specific nodes, where higher-level nodes inherit the union of their parents' rules, and candidate plans are progressively verified and aggregated until a single plan satisfies every relevant rule. In this way, RuleGraph reduces reliance on a single centralized safety decision. We evaluate RuleGraph on pure-attack and compound instructions across two benchmarks in two environments using Attack Success Rate (ASR) and Task Success Rate (TSR). RuleGraph reduces ASR to 0% on both benchmarks while achieving the highest TSR among all methods, reaching 98.57% on the RoboGuard benchmark and 84.54% on our long-horizon compound instructions.
  </details>

- **[AutoPruner: Agentic Depth Pruning for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23880)**  `arXiv:2610.23880`  `cs.RO`  
  _Tao Lin, Yikeng Chen, Qiwei Liang, Zinan Xie, Zhengtu Liang, Renjing Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Layer pruning reduces the size and inference cost of vision-language-action (VLA) models, but selecting a deployable configuration requires deciding both which layers to remove and how to recover task performance. These decisions depend on the model and task, and a failed trial can reflect either the deletion set or insufficient recovery. We present AutoPruner, an agent-driven framework that jointly searches pruning structures and recovery recipes through closed-loop experimentation. Offline layer interventions measure changes in action output to provide functional evidence. An LLM agent combines this evidence with evaluation history and retained diagnoses to propose deletion sets and recovery recipes within a search budget. Each proposal is executed and evaluated on the target tasks, and the resulting feedback guides the next experiment. Across OpenVLA-OFT, StarVLA, and FastWAM on four LIBERO suites, AutoPruner removes 16.7--87.5% of backbone layers while maintaining task success above 90%, with measured inference speedups of up to 2.50$\times$. Further analysis shows that functional probes identify action-sensitive layers despite high representation similarity, and that recovery can make previously failing structures viable.
  </details>

- **[LLM-Flock: LLM-Driven Decentralized Multi-Robot Flocking via Influence-Based Consensus with Collision Avoidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.06513)**  `arXiv:2505.06513`  `cs.RO`  
  _Peihan Li, Lifeng Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities in reasoning and planning, prompting researchers to explore their use as decentralized decision-makers for multi-robot formation control. However, directly applying LLMs to such tasks often leads to unstable behavior, in which robots may collapse to the centroid of their positions or diverge entirely, due to hallucinated reasoning and limited coordination awareness. We propose LLM-Flock, a decentralized multi-robot flocking framework that addresses these challenges through two key mechanisms: (1) an influence-based consensus protocol, where robots iteratively refine individually generated formation plans by adopting those of more connected neighbors, driving the team toward a unified plan without centralized control, and (2) a two-layer collision avoidance strategy that pairs LLM-based urgency reasoning with a programmatic collision checker and re-prompting mechanism. We evaluate LLM-Flock across five state-of-the-art LLMs, including both closed-source (GPT-5, Claude 4.5) and open-source (Llama3.1-405b, Qwen-Max, DeepSeek-R1) models, demonstrating notable improvements in formation stability and convergence over prior LLM-based methods. Real-world experiments with Crazyflie drones further validate the framework's applicability to physical multi-robot systems.
  </details>

- **[Reward and Behavioral Measures of LLM Coordination in Dining Philosophers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07845)**  `arXiv:2606.07845`  `cs.MA` `cs.LG`  
  _Najmul Hasan, Prashanth BusiReddyGari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does high reward indicate productive coordination among language-model agents sharing resources? We examine this question in dining philosophers, where instances of the same model must acquire shared forks to eat. We evaluate seven model conditions across 630 episodes at five, ten, and fifteen philosophers, measuring scalar reward, deadlock, and meal throughput. At five philosophers, Mistral-Small 24B receives mean reward 0.827 while producing no meals. The reward decomposition explains this mismatch: avoiding deadlock earns a bonus without requiring meal production. To assess a policy trained with this reward, we compare the final checkpoint from a single Qwen3-14B group relative policy optimization (GRPO) training run with its base model. At five philosophers, the trained model has lower throughput (0.008 versus 0.020) and a higher deadlock rate in point estimates. Differences in mean episode reward are not statistically significant at any evaluated philosopher count. These findings show that the evaluated reward can assign high scores without task progress and support reporting behavioral measures alongside reward when comparing models and evaluating fine-tuned policies.
  </details>

- **[The Weight of Teamwork: Does Communication Structure Help LLM Scientists Search for Scientific Laws?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24131)**  `arXiv:2610.24131`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jianing Shi, Zaibin Zhang, Shijun Lei, Zhenfei Yin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Team performance is often reported as one delivered score, even though that score is produced in two stages: a system generates candidate answers and a selection rule returns one of them. We use symbolic regression as an observable scientific-search setting and compare fixed-budget LLM teams that differ in peer visibility and update timing, evaluating both their frozen candidate archives and the expressions selected from those archives. Distributing the call budget across more independent trajectories produced the largest measured gain, improving mean held-out fitting score Q by 0.70 at the widest tested allocation. Under a common archive-wide selector, the implemented Ring policy reduced mean Q by 0.33 relative to Independent, while Star and Complete remained unresolved. Holding the generated archives fixed, changing the delivery rule changed the measured architecture gaps, including the sign of the Complete point estimate. These results support reporting candidate generation and final selection as separate endpoints. They do not identify a communication mechanism or a universally preferred topology; rather, they show that an architecture ranking is not identified by communication structure alone when the rule that returns the final answer is left unspecified.
  </details>

- **[Behavioral Probes for Information Flow in LLM Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25966)**  `arXiv:2610.25966`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Junhui Chen, Yu Zhang, Lantian Li, Dong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most existing approaches to LLM swarm optimization are utility-driven, focusing on final task utility while leaving the processing, integration, and reliance of information within individual interactions largely implicit. To address this, we study LLM swarm interactions from an information-flow-based view and introduce behavioral probes to quantify information flow patterns. Specifically, we repurpose External Context Score (ECS) and Parametric Knowledge Score (PKS)---originally designed for hallucination analysis---to measure a node's external-context dependence and parametric-knowledge dependence, respectively. Across controlled and realistic swarm settings, we observe consistent patterns: ECS increases with input relevance and distinguishes useful multi-source contributions, while PKS decreases as informative context accumulates. Building on this heuristic interpretation, we demonstrate how these probes can complement utility-driven structure optimization in two controlled applications. Probe-guided node pruning reduces redundant communication and token consumption, and probe-guided reasoning-path search accelerates convergence compared to utility-only baselines. Our empirical results suggest that behavioral probes can make the internal information flow of LLM swarms observable and serve as effective auxiliary signals for structure optimization.
  </details>

- **[Discovery-Probability Graph Planning for Embodied Agents in Tasks with Hidden Cooperation Requirements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26359)**  `arXiv:2610.26359`  `cs.MA`  
  _Zihuang Cai, Changjian Chen, Zhuo Tang, Chang Tang, Kenli Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied agents are increasingly capable of perceiving, planning, and acting autonomously in dynamic environments, demonstrating strong performance on complex cooperative tasks. Recent advances typically assume that cooperative tasks are known in advance. In real-world applications, however, hidden-cooperation tasks are more common: the need for cooperation only becomes apparent after an agent observes and interacts with task-related targets. In this paper, we first present HiddenCoop, a benchmark for effectively evaluating agent cooperation in hidden-cooperation tasks. We further propose Discovery-Probability Graph Planning (DPGP), a method for enabling effective agent cooperation in such tasks. At the core of DPGP is a discovery-probability graph that encodes the probability distribution over the number and locations of task-related targets, facilitating effective and efficient information exchange among agents. A search algorithm is then used to derive action plans that minimize expected costs. Experiments show that DPGP achieves cooperative-task success rates of 99.3% on C-WAH and 58.3% on TDW-MAT, surpassing all compared baselines, while also reducing overall embodied execution time and substantially lowering LLM token usage. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning Who Talks to Whom: Role-Aware Communication Topology Scheduling for Efficient LLM Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25851)**  `arXiv:2610.25851`  `cs.MA`  
  _Haibin Li, Wenya Yu, Xuefeng Jiang, Jia Li, Xiao Han, Shuai Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent systems driven by large language models (LLMs) rely on structured communication topologies to facilitate information exchange and collaborative reasoning. However, effectively scheduling these topologies to dynamically determine who talks to whom and when to communicate remains a fundamental challenge in balancing collective reasoning performance against exponential token overhead. Existing topology scheduling approaches either enforce static communication graphs that cannot adapt to dynamic problem-solving states, or overlook agent role complementarity while failing to explicitly incorporate the synthesized graph structure into team-level decision evaluation. To tackle these limitations, we propose ACTS, a role-aware adaptive communication topology scheduling framework for efficient LLM multi-agent collaboration. Orchestrated within a Centralized Training with Decentralized Execution (CTDE) paradigm, ACTS introduces a novel dual-attention mechanism. Specifically, a Role-conditioned Relational Attention module dynamically selects variable-cardinality interaction partners based on agent roles and evolving execution states, while a Topological Attention Mixer explicitly embeds the directed communication topology into team value decomposition for precise credit assignment. Comprehensive evaluations across 9 challenging benchmarks covering code generation, multi-step reasoning, general knowledge, and competitive mathematics demonstrate that ACTS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, boosting average accuracy by up to 6.19 percentage points while reducing token consumption by over 15%. The project website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Federation over Harness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24007)**  `arXiv:2610.24007`  `cs.MA`  
  _Dixi Yao, Tahseen Rabbani, Tian Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern LLM-powered agents increasingly improve through experience sharing, where distributed agents distill local reasoning experience into reusable knowledge, such as a shared insight library. Existing approaches, however, still heavily rely on manually designed prompts and workflows that determine both how local agents extract useful reasoning traces and how a central/global agent aggregates them to obtain common patterns and insights. We propose Federation over Harness (FoH), a framework that automatically evolves the harness for both local and global components in this distributed multi-agent reasoning pipeline. A server agent evolves the client harness for task solving and self-reflection while, at the same time, evolving its own harness for aggregating distributed reflections, maintaining shared knowledge, and updating subsequent client harnesses. Importantly, FoH requires no extra validation signals, such as ground-truth labels or validation data, during evolution; it learns solely from self-generated reflections produced by distributed agents. Experiments on multiple benchmarks show that FoH consistently improves over single-agent baselines with a fixed harness and with harness self-evolution by 53.57% and 29.76%.
  </details>

- **[RE-SAC: Disentangling aleatoric and epistemic risks in bus fleet control: A stable and robust ensemble DRL approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18396)**  `arXiv:2603.18396`  `cs.LG` `cs.RO`  
  _Yifan Zhang, Liang Zheng, Qifan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bus holding control is challenging due to stochastic traffic and passenger demand. While deep reinforcement learning (DRL) shows promise, standard actor-critic algorithms suffer from Q-value instability in volatile environments. A key source of this instability is the conflation of two distinct uncertainties: aleatoric uncertainty (irreducible noise) and epistemic uncertainty (data insufficiency). Treating these as a single risk leads to value underestimation in noisy states, causing catastrophic policy collapse. We propose a robust ensemble soft actor-critic (RE-SAC) framework to explicitly disentangle these uncertainties. RE-SAC applies Integral Probability Metric (IPM)-based weight regularization to the critic network to hedge against aleatoric risk, providing a smooth analytical lower bound for the robust Bellman operator without expensive inner-loop perturbations. To address epistemic risk, a diversified Q-ensemble penalizes overconfident value estimates in sparsely covered regions. This dual mechanism prevents the ensemble variance from misidentifying noise as a data gap, a failure mode identified in our ablation study. Experiments in a realistic bidirectional bus corridor simulation demonstrate that RE-SAC achieves the highest cumulative reward (approx. -0.4e6) compared to vanilla SAC (-0.55e6). Mahalanobis rareness analysis confirms that RE-SAC reduces Oracle Q-value estimation error by up to 62% in rare out-of-distribution states (MAE of 1647 vs. 4343), demonstrating superior robustness under high traffic variability.
  </details>

- **[HarnessPro: Recursive Harness Self-Improvement with Pareto Optimization for Performance-Cost Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26770)**  `arXiv:2610.26770`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Zhuoshi Pan, ZhiYuan Feng, Yukuo Cen, Honglin Lin, Junru Lu, Zhongxia Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness development plays a crucial role in building capable AI agents. Recent approaches improve harnesses through recursive self-improvement (RSI), but their performance gains often come with higher execution costs. We introduce HarnessPro, a label-free method for evolving harnesses to improve task performance while reducing execution cost. In this setting, candidate harnesses are evaluated using LLM-generated tests without access to ground-truth solutions or official tests. Specialized agents diagnose execution trajectories, plan updates with different performance-cost priorities, and implement them as candidate harnesses. Rather than returning a single harness, HarnessPro uses normalized weighted squared-distance selection to provide cost-focused (lite), balanced (medium), and performance-focused (heavy) harnesses for different deployment budgets. Selected harnesses enter the next iteration, where execution feedback guides further updates. Our experiments cover Terminal-Bench 2, SWE-Bench Pro, and ProgramBench, using GPT-5.5 with Codex and GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Across these benchmarks using GPT-5.5 with Codex, the heavy mode of HarnessPro achieves an average pass-rate gain of 2.0 percentage points and an average per-task cost reduction of 26.1% relative to RHO. This advantage also holds when using GLM-5.2 with Claude Code. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Copying Under Repetition: From Prefix Matching to Position Hashing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26722)**  `arXiv:2610.26722`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Anxin Guo, Tianlong Huang, Chenxiao Yang, Zhiyuan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier LLMs can copy random text almost flawlessly, yet they often overrun or truncate long blocks of repeated characters. In repeated strings, matching local content no longer reliably identifies where copying should continue or when it should stop. Nevertheless, we show that a two-layer transformer with $O(\log\log N)$-bit precision and $O(\log N/\log\log N)$ width can copy every string up to a chosen design horizon $N$ and terminate correctly under arbitrary repetition. We use RoPE-style rotations of attention values and outputs (VO-rotations) with small prime periods to encode distances from delimiter anchors modulo those primes. By the Chinese Remainder Theorem, these periodic encodings jointly distinguish all required distances, allowing one retrieval rule to select either the next source token or the end marker. In contrast, we prove a width-independent impossibility result for copying under sequential finite-precision softmax accumulation. It applies to transformers whose only positional input is an additive attention bias that eventually becomes constant, including NoPE and a recursive implementation of ALiBi. Once an identical-token run exceeds the resulting saturation threshold, further repetitions leave the logits unchanged. If this threshold grows sublinearly with $N$, these models cannot both complete a long all-identical copy and stop correctly, a pattern resembling the termination errors of frontier LLMs. In synthetic copying experiments, baseline transformer models fail on long repeated strings even within the training range. Trained VO models, by contrast, generalize beyond the training lengths with near-perfect accuracy up to length $\approx 5005$, the selected primes' period product.
  </details>

- **[An Empirical Study of Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning for Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26370)**  `arXiv:2610.26370`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Minglei Li, Peng Ye, Haonan He, Jingqi Ye, Qingyu Yang, Tao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods are critical for adapting large models under resource-constrained settings. However, as mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures, which offer efficient inference at massive parameter scales, gain increasing prevalence in the community, a critical gap arising from the lack of systematic recipes for applying PEFT to MoE models. To narrow this gap, we conduct a large-scale systematic study that investigates a set of essential design recipes, including which modules should be adapted, which granularity should be used to adapt MoE modules, which PEFT method to use, and to what extent should the load-balancing objective be used, across three MoE backbones on downstream tasks and general knowledge tasks. Our experiments yield several key insights. First, adapting MoE experts alone achieves the highest downstream performance with competitive general capability retention under a comparable parameter budget. Second, native expert-weight updates offer a better downstream--retention trade-off than adapters that bypass experts or entire MoE modules. Third, LoRA and DoRA together define the strongest operating region for high downstream accuracy among the evaluated PEFT methods. Fourth, no nonzero load-balancing coefficient Pareto-dominates the zero baseline. We finally evaluate a weight-level redesign of MoE$^2$-LoRA on Qwen3. Moving its module-level bypass pool into native expert weights improves all four upstream endpoints by 2.4 to 17.9 points with 17% fewer trainable parameters and a 3.2-point average downstream cost.
  </details>

- **[Curriculum Learning Teaches LLMs Long-Horizon Implicit Reasoning, Up To A Limit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25612)**  `arXiv:2610.25612`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jimson Huang, Maxwell Nickell, Ruowang Zhang, Abulhair Saparov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs "reason" by externalizing intermediate steps as tokens through chain-of-thought (CoT), yet even frontier reasoning models hit abrupt performance cliffs on long-horizon tasks. Implicit reasoning (reasoning within a single forward pass) is useful during CoT generation, as the model can "search ahead" to decide the correct next move without explicit search. Models with weak implicit reasoning are more likely to commit to wrong paths and must attempt to recover via backtracking, while models with strong implicit reasoning identify the correct proof strategy directly, shortening the CoT. However, prior work shows LLMs struggle to reason implicitly beyond shallow depths, with no relief from scale. We investigate whether curriculum learning can extend this depth by fine-tuning on examples with progressively increasing vocabulary size and lookahead (the number of steps a model must search ahead to identify the correct next move). At matched compute, curriculum-trained models achieve lookaheads where no-curriculum training fails. Qwen3-0.6B reaches 98% accuracy on problems with lookahead up to 256 with curriculum, compared to 45% without. However, progress slows as fine-tuning continues, following a saturating exponential that suggests a maximum intrinsic lookahead to which models can learn to reason implicitly. Larger models reach higher fitted ceilings, fine-tuning pretrained models far outpaces training from scratch, and curriculum step sizes that are too large stall progression. Finally, the capability acquired on synthetic data transfers to natural language reasoning, with curriculum-trained models outperforming models trained without curriculum at matched compute on 11 natural language reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Text Dominance in Paralinguistic Conflict: Tracing Suppression Through Audio LLM Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25599)**  `arXiv:2610.25599`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ankur Banerjee, Vishnu Guntakala, Danity Pike, Advay Roongta, Kevin Yang, Ruizhe Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Audio LLMs deployed in settings such as crisis hotlines and customer support are only useful beyond speech-to-text pipelines if they use acoustic cues rather than the transcript alone. We test whether they prioritise transcript content over paralinguistic cues (emotion, speaker identity, sarcasm) and trace the mechanism when they do. Across six audio LLMs under LISTEN-style conflict conditions, the mean text-dominance rate on mismatched text+audio items is 0.78 (0.86 excluding sarcasm). In the most text-dominant screened model, probing shows speaker identity remains linearly decodable at ceiling at the language model's input, so the failure is not perceptual. Back-patching, reported to repair lexical text dominance, has no measurable effect here; but it also fails on its own source task in our replication (n=300), so we treat its null as uninformative rather than as evidence of non-transfer. Attention knockout partially restores audio-grounded output by blocking the claim from propagating through intermediate tokens during open-ended generation, whereas blocking the answer position's direct connection to the conflicting claim has no effect. The claim's influence therefore travels through an intermediate relay rather than a single severable edge. The effect appears only in free-form generation and not in closed-set multiple choice, suggesting that benchmarks relying on that format may be systematically blind to interventions of this kind. Code and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Indistinguishable Agents: Training Agent Privacy as Twin-World Indistinguishability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25161)**  `arXiv:2610.25161`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaming He, Guanyu Hou, Yi Yu, Xudong Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents search, shop and send messages for their users, and every action is seen by some party. Existing privacy training rewards agents for not mentioning protected items, yet an observer can infer a private fact from what the agent does. Since an observer learns a fact only if its view changes with the fact, we propose \methodfull (\method), which trains on twin worlds that differ only in the private fact. A frozen distinguisher scores each observer's view by its log-likelihood ratio for the true world, and paired GRPO trains the agent on task success minus this ratio. We prove that this objective controls the Bayes success of every observer on every channel. On shopping and messaging tasks \method removes the leakage that disclosure judges and query-level rewards miss, while the agent still completes its tasks.
  </details>

- **[Trackable Skill Library Lifecycle Management for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25126)**  `arXiv:2610.25126`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Xi Zhu, Yuanlin Duan, Changyi Yang, Kai Mei, Riyang Bao, Shichao Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill-augmented agentic RL has emerged as an effective approach to improving LLM agents on long-horizon tasks. Despite this progress, individual skills may follow distinct trajectories as the policy evolves, with some becoming redundant after the model internalizes their capabilities, others remaining valuable as external scaffolding, and still others turning harmful. However, existing skill library systems fail to identify each skill's true contribution due to free-riding among co-retrieved skills, difficulty confounding across tasks, and the absence of skill-specific counterfactual evidence, which prevents reliable skill lifecycle management. We propose TrackRL, an agentic RL framework where skill tracking drives skill library full lifecycle management. Specifically, within each GRPO group, we turn grouped sampling into online randomized controlled experiments. Independent random injection and within-group contrasts on the same task isolate each skill's true contribution, and reliability-weighted aggregation accumulates evidence across groups. As evidence accumulates, we continuously track skill utility, which measures the skill's causal contribution as external scaffolding, and model mastery, which reflects how far the corresponding capability has been internalized. Along this line, we formulate skill lifecycle management as an evidence-gated state-transition process that governs each skill from generation and admission through protected incubation to retention, retirement, or removal, while a soft capacity bound controls library growth. Experiments show that TrackRL outperforms recent skill-augmented RL baselines in both library-assisted and skill-free evaluation, with further analyses revealing how skill value shifts during policy learning and how evidence-driven state transitions shape skill library evolution. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Certified Sparse Attention via Perturbed Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24991)**  `arXiv:2610.24991`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Runguo Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse attention speeds up long-context decoding by reading few keys of the KV cache, but existing methods do not control the error of a query, and on real KV caches their errors have heavy tails. We show that perturbing the score of every key with an independent Exp(1) variable turns threshold retrieval into sampling keys with probability proportional to their attention weights. Lowering the threshold along a geometric grid gives nested samples, a Horvitz--Thompson estimate of the output at every level, and confidence bounds that hold simultaneously over all levels, so each head stops at the first level whose certificate meets the target error $\varepsilon$. With fresh perturbations at every decoding step, each certificate fails with probability at most $\delta$ during generation. At a suitable threshold, the estimator reads $O(\varepsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))$ values regardless of the context length, and no adaptive method reads fewer, up to constants. A 4-bit scan with deterministic bounds, shared across a grouped-query-attention group, generates the candidates exactly but reads every key. On three LLMs and ten settings at 16K--128K tokens, no certificate is violated, and at equal keys read our 90th-percentile error is 4.6--7.9$\times$ lower than that of the best of 15 baseline families. At equal bytes, the best baselines have 0.6--1.6 times our 90th-percentile error and at least 4.9 times our largest. End to end, our method stays within one point of dense attention on tasks and within 0.1\% in PG-19 perplexity, and the strongest baselines at equal bytes score as well. On CPU, decoding steps at 32K--128K tokens run 1.1--1.4$\times$ faster than dense attention on the same 16-bit cache.
  </details>

- **[ProbePO: Sparse Probes, Redistributed Credit for Critic-Free LLM Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24515)**  `arXiv:2610.24515`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Diyi Hu, Ruizhong Qiu, Lin Qiu, Hanghang Tong, Hanqing Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards assigns one terminal reward to an entire reasoning trajectory, obscuring how individual reasoning steps contributed to the outcome. GRPO broadcasts a group-normalized advantage to every response token, while finer-grained approaches often rely on dense auxiliary rollouts or branching schemes that can also alter the data used for policy updates. We cast the missing middle as budgeted measurement: with a limited rollout budget, which partial solutions should be evaluated, and how should their value estimates refine credit? ProbePO selects partial-solution states with a reward-free uncertainty score and estimates their expected verifier rewards under a capped continuation budget. ProbePO redistributes, rather than replaces, GRPO's trajectory-level advantage across tokens while preserving each trajectory's mean token-level advantage; probe continuations serve only as measurements. Across three DeepSeekMath and Qwen-family models spanning 1.5B-7B parameters, ProbePO consistently outperforms GRPO and achieves the highest accuracy among the four evaluated methods in 16 of 18 model-benchmark comparisons. Against dense-probing VinePPO on DeepSeekMath-7B, ProbePO achieves higher accuracy on all six benchmarks with 76% less end-to-end training time.
  </details>

- **[Continual Test-Time Learning with Evolution Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24137)**  `arXiv:2610.24137`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Guangya Hao, Yiting Duan, Longyan Tan, Yunbo Long, Hao Wu, Ge Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models increasingly operate in open-ended environments, where they are expected to continually adapt to evolving tasks and distributions, often without access to external supervision. Existing continual learning approaches rely on external supervision, such as labeled data, demonstrations, or reward signals, which can be costly or unavailable in practice. Test-time adaptation removes this dependence by learning from self-generated supervision, but existing methods derive that supervision from the single policy being updated, so once the policy drifts, its own learning signal drifts with it, leading to policy collapse, degraded reasoning, and catastrophic forgetting. In this paper, we propose \textbf{Evolution Strategy Reinforcement Learning (ESRL)}, a continual test-time learning framework that draws its self-generated supervision from a population of policies instead of the single policy being trained. ESRL perturbs an anchor policy in parameter space to form a population of nearby policies, samples one trajectory from each, and aggregates their answers into label-free group-relative learning signals for the central student. The anchor is held fixed within each block and refreshed from the student at block boundaries, so the population stays centered on a stable yet current policy and continues to supply diverse and reliable supervision as the student moves. In single-task test-time adaptation across mathematical reasoning, general knowledge, and medical domains, ESRL improves average accuracy by up to 31.7% relative to the base model and 16.6% relative to the strongest test-time adaptation baseline. Notably, ESRL substantially reduces catastrophic forgetting and achieves the strongest final performance in continual learning.
  </details>

- **[How Data Scales in Agentic Reinforcement Learning: Laws and Synthesis Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23946)**  `arXiv:2610.23946`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Bowei He, Yankai Chen, Xiaokun Zhang, Changjiang Han, Ye Yuan, Chong Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pretraining scaling laws treat training data as a one-dimensional quantity: a token count. Agentic reinforcement learning (RL) inherits this scalar abstraction, but modern synthesis pipelines now generate \textit{tasks}, \textit{environments}, and \textit{trajectories} independently and at very different costs, turning data into a structured design space. This raises a question pretraining never had to answer: given a synthesis budget, \textit{which axis} should be scaled? We address this question end-to-end with three tightly coupled contributions. First, we decompose agentic data into three cardinality axes (task, environment, trajectory), plus a reward-density control variable, and fit per-axis scaling relations on different large language models (LLMs) across math+code, SWE, web, and UI/tool application domains. Across all model sizes, model families, domains, and RL algorithms (PPO and GRPO) we measured, the scaling exponents order as environment $>$ task $>$ trajectory. When environment diversity is decomposed into world and environment-structure diversity, its advantage is mainly driven by the former, while the latter is just comparable to the task diversity. Second, we use the fitted laws as a common ruler to price synthesis methods, capturing each method's efficiency as a per-axis discount factor and identifying critical synthetic-to-real ratios at which collapse begins; these ratios differ by an order of magnitude across axes. Third, we cast budget allocation as a constrained optimization under our laws and discounts, deriving a Chinchilla-style compute-optimal recipe and validating it at held-out scale. The empirical results demonstrate that following the recipe yields up to $1.7\times$ compute efficiency over the dominant ``scale-the-trajectories'' practice.
  </details>

- **[User Model Extraction via Belief Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31603)**  `arXiv:2609.31603`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Ali Holmov, Yiran Huang, Kirill Bykov, Zeynep Akata_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) implicitly infer attributes of their users and adapt their behavior accordingly, yet these beliefs remain difficult to inspect and causally manipulate. We introduce Belief Self-Distillation (BSD), a unified read-write framework that bridges linear and causal probing by learning a compact user representation that can be both decoded and written back into the model. The frozen LLM acts as its own teacher, distilling beliefs from natural conversations without external annotations. Unlike conventional probing, BSD isolates not only information present in activations, but a state whose causal role can be directly tested. Across multiple model families, BSD faithfully recovers user beliefs and enables substantially stronger interventions than matched hidden-state steering. Crucially, we find that refusal depends not only on the request, but on the model's inferred user intent: changing this belief alters refusal while holding the request fixed. We further uncover a striking cross-model regularity: independently trained LLMs converge on a shared geometry for representing their users. Together, these results reveal implicit user models as readable and causally writable internal states with direct implications for AI safety, shaping how models condition safety decisions on whom they believe they are interacting with.
  </details>

- **[Local Conflict, Targeted Repair: A Perturbation Analysis of Multi-Domain RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02398)**  `arXiv:2606.02398`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Lei Yang, Siyu Ding, Deyi Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training improves large language models on individual domains but can degrade previously acquired capabilities. We study this interference in mathematical reasoning, code generation, question answering (QA), and creative writing (CW). Full-model domain gradients can be nearly orthogonal despite substantial selective degradation. Our structural analyses show sparse, weakly overlapping edits on computation routes that reasoning domains share, with directional alignment associated with transfer and conflict. A local perturbation model separates loss changes into a first-order term and a path-averaged curvature term, decomposes the latter along the actual rollback intervention, and provides a conditional explanation for recovery by short refresh. The experiments support this local sensitivity account. After Code $\rightarrow$ Math $\rightarrow$ QA $\rightarrow$ CW training, a short Re-Math refresh raises Math from 57.66 to 66.04 and achieves the highest average score among the evaluated baselines. A training-free rollback at a $9.8\%$ budget recovers $57.4\%$ of the QA-induced Math accuracy drop with a small QA change. Curvature probes at the Math checkpoint further show higher sensitivity along the selected direction than along matched random directions. Together, these results support sparse intervention and targeted refresh as effective ways to mitigate cross-domain degradation.
  </details>

- **[KernelClaw: A Multi-Agent Framework for GPU Kernel Optimization with Dual-Memory Mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10085)**  `arXiv:2603.10085`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Qitong Sun, Jun Han, Tianlin Li, Zhe Tang, Sheng Chen, Fei Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving GPU kernel efficiency is crucial for efficient AI systems. Recent work has explored leveraging large language models (LLMs) for GPU kernel generation and optimization. However, existing LLM-based kernel optimization pipelines typically rely on opaque, implicitly learned heuristics within the LLMs to determine optimization strategies. This leads to inefficient trial-and-error and weakly interpretable optimizations. Our key insight is to replace implicit heuristics with expert optimization knowledge that is aware of task trajectories. Specifically, we present KernelClaw, a multi-agent framework with a dual-level memory architecture. KernelClaw operates by coordinating agents with long-term memory of reusable expert knowledge and short-term memory to prevent repetitive backtracking. On KernelBench Levels 1-3, KernelClaw achieves a 100% success rate and average speedups of 6.98x, 7.89x, and 1.94x over Torch Eager on Levels 1, 2, and 3, respectively, outperforming prior baselines. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LLM as a Tool, Not an Agent: Code-Mined Tree Transformations for Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16555)**  `arXiv:2604.16555`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Masakazu Yoshimura, Zitang Sun, Yuiko Sakuma, Junji Otsuka, Atsushi Irie, Takeshi Ohashi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Neural Architecture Search (NAS) aims to automatically discover high-performing deep neural network (DNN) architectures. However, conventional algorithm-driven NAS relies on carefully hand-crafted search spaces to ensure executability, which restricts open-ended exploration. Recent coding-based agentic approaches using large language models (LLMs) reduce manual design, but current LLMs struggle to reliably generate complex, valid architectures, and their proposals are often biased toward a narrow set of patterns observed in their training data. To bridge reliable algorithmic search with powerful LLM assistance, we propose LLMasTool, a hierarchical tree-based NAS framework for stable and open-ended model evolution. Our method automatically extracts reusable modules from arbitrary source code and represents full architectures as hierarchical trees, enabling evolution through reliable tree transformations rather than code generation. At each evolution step, coarse-level planning is governed by a diversity-guided algorithm that leverages Bayesian modeling to improve exploration efficiency, while the LLM resolves the remaining degrees of freedom to ensure a meaningful evolutionary trajectory and an executable generated architecture. With this formulation, instead of fully agentic LLM approaches, our method explores diverse directions beyond the inherent biases in the LLM. Our method improves over existing NAS methods by 0.69, 1.83, and 2.68 points on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet16-120, demonstrating its effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[Pushing the Frontier of Black-Box LVLM Attacks via Fine-Grained Detail Targeting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17645)**  `arXiv:2602.17645`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaohan Zhao, Zhaoyi Li, Yaxin Luo, Jiacheng Cui, Zhiqiang Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Black-box adversarial attacks on Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) are challenging due to missing gradients and complex multimodal boundaries. Prior state-of-the-art transfer-based approaches such as M-Attack match local crops between source and target images, but this induces high-variance, nearly orthogonal gradients across iterations. We attribute this instability to (i) ViT translation sensitivity, which yields spike-like gradients, and (ii) a structural asymmetry between source and target crops. We reformulate local matching as an asymmetric expectation over source transformations and target semantics, and introduce M-Attack-V2 based on this formulation. Multi-Crop Alignment (MCA) averages gradients from multiple local views per iteration, while Auxiliary Target Alignment (ATA) replaces aggressive target augmentation with a small set of semantically correlated auxiliary images. Patch Momentum replays historical crop gradients, and a patch-diverse surrogate ensemble (PE+) strengthens transferable directions. M-Attack-V2 raises attack success rates over M-Attack from 40% to 61% on GPT-6.1 Sol, from 40% to 60% on GPT-6 Astra, and from 85% to 97% on Claude Sonnet 5.5. Code and data are publicly available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[recAMPLIFY: Large-Scale Pretraining of Recursive Protein Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26747)**  `arXiv:2610.26747`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Saurav Jha, Qianli Liao, Robert Vernon, Sarath Chandar, Quentin Fournier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Protein language models (pLMs) are powerful tools for protein annotation and mutation-effect prediction, but progress has largely depended on scaling model size and data, making them expensive and prone to memorization. Test-time scaling offers a complementary path, but methods developed for autoregressive LLMs do not transfer directly: masked encoders lack a generation loop, and amino acids do not verbalize in a reasoning language. Instead, we adapt recursive pretraining to masked pLMs, where a weight-tied Transformer core refines residue-aligned hidden states over an adjustable number of cycles. We instantiate this recipe as recAMPLIFY, a pLM with 155M unique parameters trained on the same 2T tokens as AMPLIFY-350M. Despite 56% fewer parameters, it improves CASP contact recovery and three out of four supervised tasks, notably raising fold accuracy by 22.5%. Because optimal depth for recAMPLIFY varies across tasks and assays, we then introduce Gated Mixture Recurrence (GMR), a framework that decodes each protein from a probability-weighted mixture of hidden states visited during recursion. GMR employs a learned residual gate at scalar, low-rank, or per-dimension resolution to control how much of each cycle's proposed update is accepted into the state. This unifies halting and gating into a single adaptive computation objective while recovering PonderNet-style halting as a special case without gates. Layered on top of recAMPLIFY, GMR further reduces expected layer depth by up to 37%, although the accuracy trade-off varies by task. Mechanistic analyses show that residual gating controls recurrence stability beyond the trained depth and that halt depth tracks biological properties.
  </details>

- **[Correctness-Dependent Asymmetric Target Distributions for Self-Distillation Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25973)**  `arXiv:2610.25973`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Suwan Yoon, Seongryong Jung, Changhee Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-distillation policy optimization provides dense token-level supervision for LLM post-training through feedback-conditioned self-teachers. However, directly matching the self-teacher can disrupt successful behaviors on correct rollouts while providing insufficient correction on incorrect ones. We propose Asymmetric Policy Optimization via Self-Distillation (APSD), which constructs correctness-dependent targets by mixing student and self-teacher distributions: interpolation preserves successful behaviors on correct rollouts, while extrapolation beyond the self-teacher strengthens correction on incorrect ones. We analyze APSD through divergence decomposition, reverse-KL barycentric geometry, and gradient analysis, highlighting the importance of gradient flow through student-dependent targets. Experiments under reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards and rich feedback show that APSD achieves strong overall performance against reinforcement-learning and self-distillation baselines and matches SDPO performance with 2.6x fewer training steps on average across Qwen3 model scales.
  </details>

- **[Computation, Not Memory: Chain-of-Thought and State Tracking in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25760)**  `arXiv:2610.25760`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Pedram Mouseli, Massieh Moayedi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language-model agents often reason before they act, and the value of monitoring that reasoning depends on how it relates to their actions. We ask how an agent's actions depend on its reasoning when the correct action depends on a changing state. In a small file-system task, background events announce which file's lock has been toggled but not its new value. The agent therefore has to reconstruct the current locks from its history before it can move files safely. With Qwen3-8B and Qwen3.5-9B, we separate two roles of reasoning: generating it before each action, and retaining earlier reasoning in the prompt. Most of the reduction in lock-violating commands comes from reasoning written at the moment of action. Retaining earlier reasoning adds little to rule-following, although it helps one model complete the task. The benefit depends on what the reasoning says. Filler text, reasoning written for another history and an accurate table of the state do not reproduce it. When we give the agent reasoning written for a history that differs in one lock value, the agent acts on that reasoning even against its own history. Without reasoning, linear probes recover the state from the model's activations about as well as a text classifier recovers it from the transcript. However, decoding accuracy does not predict which interventions change the agent's actions. In this setting, the benefit of reasoning for rule-following comes mainly from generating it at each step. The state that can be decoded without reasoning is a limited guide to how the agent acts.
  </details>

- **[CasePlay: Self-Play Reinforcement Learning from Case Reports for Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24952)**  `arXiv:2610.24952`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hao Wang, Zihan Wang, Kai Wu, Jian Yao, Yiwen Ye, Yongcan Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) is a promising way to improve the clinical reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs), but current pipelines often rely on expert-labeled questions or costly synthetic-data generation. Meanwhile, the medical literature offers a wealth of high-quality unlabeled documents, including clinical case reports that trace the full clinical course from initial presentation to final treatment decisions. We study whether document-grounded self-play can convert these reports into effective RL training signals. We identify two challenges that limit existing self-play methods in this setting: knowledge-point fixation}, where the proposer repeatedly asks about a narrow set of salient clues in a source report, and difficulty-quality mismatch, where appropriately difficult questions may still be poorly formed or shallow, limiting their value for reasoner training. To address these challenges, we propose CasePlay, a self-play framework with two key components: (a) a Knowledge-Conditioned Proposer, which anchors question generation to case-specific knowledge points so that questions draw on a broader range of evidence from the source report; and (b) a Rubric-Judge Reasoner, which augments answer-accuracy feedback with rubric-based quality feedback on clue integration, reasoning depth, and option quality, guiding the proposer toward reasoning-intensive questions that better support reasoner training. Extensive experiments across seven medically related benchmarks demonstrate that CasePlay outperforms existing self-play baselines and improves the SFT warm-start model by 3.1 points on average, highlighted by a 6.3-point gain on LiveClin. This approach offers an effective path toward continually improving medical reasoning ability through self-play RL.
  </details>

- **[Principled Under Pressure: Post-Training Decides Whether LLMs Act on Their Own Moral Judgment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08670)**  `arXiv:2610.08670`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Orion Reblitz-Richardson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent that can say an action is wrong and then take it anyway is a different failure from one that does not know better, and evaluations of stated values cannot see it. We ask how often open models act against their own moral judgment under pressure, and what decides and reduces it. We build a pre-registered panel of 248 scenarios across five kinds of pressure. Each scenario is posed to one model in two separate calls, as the agent choosing among fixed options and in the third person asking which is right; the model's own judgment is the reference, and the scenario's construction fixes which option counts as the violation. Every scenario has a twin with the pressure removed, and every model gets a positive control that bounds what the instrument can see. On OLMo-3-7B-Instruct, the model takes the action it judged wrong on one in five of the scenarios screened on its own actions, more often than with the pressure removed. Whether the gap appears depends on the post-training recipe: OLMo-3 and Meta's Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct carry it; Tulu 3, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and OpenAI's GPT-OSS-20B, read without reasoning, show none within stated detection bars. Meta's recipe and Ai2's Tulu 3 share the same Llama-3.1 weights; only Meta's carries the gap. On both models that carry it, reasoning about the stakes before acting moves the choice back toward the model's own judgment. What that deliberation tracks is the stated rule: where following a written rule harms an absent third party, it still moves OLMo-3 and GPT-OSS-20B toward the rule, not detectably diminished, while Llama-3.1's brake shrinks to about a seventh. Whether a model acts on its own moral judgment is therefore not settled by pretraining, and deliberation moves the action toward the stated rule, which protects people only when the rule does. The gap is a measurable target for post-training recipes, not a fixed property of the model.
  </details>

- **[Toollery: Scaling LLM Agents to Thousands of Skills and Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22218)**  `arXiv:2609.22218`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xiangxi Tian, Ran Guan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents are exposed to hundreds to tens of thousands of skills, tools, and API functions, full-library prompting becomes costly, slow, and less reliable: each added candidate increases prompt tokens and latency, while longer candidate lists introduce more distractors for LLM selection. We present Toollery, a training-free candidate-compression framework for scalable LLM skill/tool selection. Following established document-side query expansion, Toollery generates user-intent queries from each skill/tool specification and builds a retrieval index that maps real user requests to compact candidate sets before final LLM decision-making. By treating high-level skills and atomic tools as selectable capabilities, Toollery can be applied to both skill libraries and tool registries. We evaluate Toollery on the roughly 79K-capability SkillRouter benchmark, BFCL-V4 with over 440 atomic tools, and 3,396 proprietary smart-cockpit requests over 220 tools. Across these settings, Toollery keeps online selection bounded to a compact top-K candidate set and improves recall over ordinary specification retrieval. At a fixed top-10 budget, Toollery improves end-to-end selection on the cockpit dataset, and maintains comparable AST Accuracy on BFCL-V4. These results support Toollery as a practical candidate-compression framework for large and evolving agent capability libraries, while showing that quality and cost gains depend on workload coverage and provider caching.
  </details>

- **[Rubric Dropout: A Simple Way to Mitigate Reward Hacking in Rubric-Based RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11669)**  `arXiv:2608.11669`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Minglai Yang, Xinyu Guo, Zhengliang Shi, Utkarsh Tyagi, Mian Zhang, Razvan Dumitru, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric-based reinforcement learning, in which an LLM judge scores responses against prompt-specific criteria, has become a standard way to post-train language models on tasks without verifiable answers. The rubric, however, is only a proxy for response quality, and a policy trained against it long enough learns to exploit the difference. We train models with GRPO on medical and science rubrics and grade their responses on out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks throughout training with two judges: the training judge and a stronger OOD judge. The two scores first rise together and then diverge. The policy is learning to satisfy the training judge without writing better responses, which is a form of reward hacking. To mitigate it, we propose Rubric Dropout, which applies dropout to rubric criteria: at each training step, a random subset of each rubric's criteria is removed from the reward, so the rewarded subset of criteria is resampled at each step. All responses sampled for the same prompt are scored on the same subset, so their rewards remain comparable, and evaluation always uses the full rubric. We also introduce Block Dropout, which drops semantically similar criteria together so that a near duplicate of a dropped criterion cannot keep rewarding the same behavior. Across both benchmarks and models from 1.7B to 8B, dropout improves the gold judge's score on OOD benchmarks by up to 3 percent on HealthBench-Hard and 7 percent on ResearchQA, and it reduces both the gap between the two judges and over-crediting. It keeps in-domain reward close to the baseline, and dropout rates of 30-50 percent work best. Block Dropout improves the gold judge's OOD score further at the same dropout rate. Changing which criteria are rewarded at each step is a simple, inexpensive way to make rubric rewards harder to exploit.
  </details>

- **[Non-Gaussianity Reveals Interpretable Directions in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11722)**  `arXiv:2606.11722`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Sida Liu, Feijiang Han, Sarah Wiegreffe, Furong Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Finding human-interpretable directions in language-model activations is important for understanding and controlling model behavior. These directions are usually discovered through indirect learning objectives such as sparse reconstruction, but it remains unclear what existing statistical structure distinguishes interpretable directions and whether that structure can be used directly for feature discovery.
