On the agent evaluation subset, LEKA achieves 43.5% Fast@1.0, compared with 13.0% for direct generation and 26.1% for Claude Code. LEKA and Claude Code use the same base model and optimization budget. Together, EdgeKernelBench and LEKA support unified evaluation and layered optimization of kernels generated by LLMs across edge backends.
  </details>

- **[Uncovering Semantic Hierarchies in Text-Attributed Graphs via Variational EM-based LLM-GNN Synergy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24624)**  `arXiv:2610.24624`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunhui Liu, Xudong Jin, Qizhuo Xie, Chunhui Zhao, Xiao Luo, Tao Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While hierarchical structures are intrinsic to organizing human knowledge, current representation learning on text-attributed graphs predominantly operates on flat semantic spaces, overlooking the rich coarse-to-fine granularity inherent in real-world data. To bridge this gap, we propose SHiFT, which synergizes the reasoning power of LLMs with the structural representation capability of GNNs within an Expectation-Maximization (EM) paradigm. In the E-step, we harness LLMs as expert taxonomists to induce an explicit semantic tree, introducing a lightweight Mapping & Diagnosis strategy that reduces inference costs by surgically updating the hierarchy only when anomalies are detected. In the M-step, we align the GNN encoder with this induced hierarchy from complementary perspectives, including clustering partition, topological skeleton, and semantic concepts. Theoretical analysis interprets SHiFT as a generalized Variational EM algorithm. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SHiFT not only achieves state-of-the-art performance but also uncovers high-quality, human-readable taxonomies. Our code can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Score to Diagnosis: Search-Independent Post-Diagnosis for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24535)**  `arXiv:2610.24535`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qian Li, Yuxiao Hu, Juncheng Liu, Yanhao Jia, Yuntian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression (SR) solvers employ diverse search mechanisms, yet most rely on the same low-information scalar fitness. Such scores provide little guidance on why a skeleton fails or how it should be revised. Recent methods develop new methods to address this bottleneck. Their enhanced feedback mechanisms are tightly coupled to their search processes and are difficult to reuse across diverse SR solvers. Instead, we ask whether a shared feedback mechanism can further improve diverse solvers facing the same bottleneck. To this end, we propose PostDiag, a search-independent feedback mechanism, which can be shared to different SR solvers. PostDiag introduces Patch-wise Feature Profiling (PFP) to capture local behavior through patch-wise features of level, trend, and roughness. Based on PFP, PostDiag quantifies Skeleton Expressiveness Risk by comparing the fitted candidate with the observations, and Skeleton Stability Risk by measuring variability of behavior after bootstrap refitting. The Diagnostic Risk Plane (DRP) combines two risks to determine whether a skeleton should be preserved, repaired, simplified, or rewritten, while the patch-wise discrepancies provide evidence for the intervention. An LLM editor then performs the diagnosis-guided intervention, and the validated revision is returned to the original solver through a lightweight adapter. Experiments across multiple LLM-based and GP-based SR solvers show that PostDiag consistently improves accuracy and accelerates convergence.
  </details>

- **[CoTs remain monitorable when being unfaithful requires computation: Replicating and Extending the Necessity Effect Across Eleven Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24495)**  `arXiv:2610.24495`  `cs.LG`  
  _Arav Dhoot, Yixiong Hao, Zephaniah Roe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While a LLM's chain of thought (CoT) is a promising approach to interpreting model outputs, prior work shows CoT's content does not always accurately reflect the reasoning process. Emmons et al. (2025) finds that models can follow a question hint without revealing so in its chain of thought, but when the hint is sufficiently complex the computation is forced into the CoT. This claim, however, has only been tested on Gemini models, and the datasets and code is closed source. We replicate their proxy task across eleven models from six families and find the necessity effect generalizes. Every model follows simple cues above a no-hint baseline, while concealed complex-cue following falls back near baseline for most models. We also extend the study from Emmons et al. (2025) to measure CoT-necessity and unfaithfulness across five cue families (four of them new) and three difficulty levels. The relationship between when CoT is necessary and unfaithfulness is task-specific and model-specific. For some hint types, models often verbalize their hint-following behavior even when the hint can be decoded in a single forward pass. These results support the case for CoT monitoring when unsafe behavior requires non-trivial computation, and that properties of CoT unfaithfulness are sensitive to model and setting.
  </details>

- **[An important Bottleneck in LLM Optimizers: Principle-Level Local Optima](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24291)**  `arXiv:2610.24291`  `cs.LG`  
  _Nian Ran, Tianhe Jia, Zhengyang Mu, Yue Wang, Sebastian Brulin, Markus Olhofer, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feedback-driven LLM optimizers are increasingly used for evolutionary search, Bayesian optimization, heuristic design, and scientific discovery. Yet rapid initial gains often give way to prolonged plateaus despite substantial remaining evaluation budgets. We investigate \emph{principle-level search collapse}, where an optimizer's search becomes confined to a narrow set of design principles, even though it may continue to generate distinct candidates and achieve small improvements. We assemble a diagnostic benchmark spanning molecular optimization, network sentinel-node selection, and GPU-kernel optimization, and introduce a decision-tree protocol combining principle narrowing, prolonged performance plateaus, and gaps to attainable reference solutions. Across 161 assessable runs on the three problem domains, 78.3\% receive support for collapse, including 55.3\% with strong support. Candidate-level variation can nevertheless conceal restricted search at the principle level. We then test \emph{diversity injection}, a common response to search stagnation, such as higher t, multiple LLMs, and web search, and show that it provides limited and inconsistent benefits: among 65 molecular runs, still 73.8\% and 53.8\% receive support and strong support respectively. We finally test reference-principle interventions and find that they improve some runs, but their benefits vary across models and tasks: access to a better principle does not guarantee its effective use. Our benchmark and diagnostic protocol provide a basis for evaluating future optimizers on principle-level collapse. Our findings highlight the need for online collapse detection and better mechanisms for generating and maintaining alternative principles during optimization.
  </details>

- **[Visual Causal-chain Bookmarking: Retrieval-Weighted Credit Assignment for Reinforcement Learning from Delayed Reward](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24032)**  `arXiv:2610.24032`  `cs.LG`  
  _Prathap Selvakumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RL agents trained on a single trajectory-level return face a credit-assignment problem: when a reward arrives many steps after the decision that caused it, every action gets the same signal regardless of causal responsibility. This paper studies Visual Causal-chain Bookmarking (VCBM), which concentrates credit on the decision points that determine an episode's return, via two instantiations. The first targets a fully observed, low-dimensional MDP (a delayed-reward watch-repair task), where VCBM statistically discovers which state components the agent causally controls and estimates per-decision-point action values directly, evaluated against ground truth and RUDDER, an LSTM-based baseline. VCBM converges to 95.07% correct decisions in a mean of 1,532.3 episodes across 20 seeds, versus RUDDER's slower, more variable convergence (mean 3,327.1 episodes; p<0.001); VCBM's discovered causal structure exactly matches ground truth in all seeds. The second targets a partially observed, language-based setting: an LLM agent trained with Leave-One-Out PPO (LOOP) in AppWorld, where causal discovery is intractable and a structural heuristic (state-mutating tool calls) substitutes for it, with credit distributed via retrieval over episodic memory and blended with LOOP's advantage. Over 200 iterations on identical Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct/LoRA/FSDP2 configurations, VCBM-blended LOOP improves task success over plain LOOP (0.354 vs. 0.458), reduces final-step max importance weight, and improves token efficiency, with a small KL exception favouring LOOP. The watch-repair result supports the claim that weighting causally relevant steps is more sample-efficient than an undifferentiated baseline or a learned return-redistribution network; the AppWorld result, limited to one seed per arm by a 2-GPU budget, is a compute-limited demonstration at a harder, natural-language scale, not a statistical confirmation.
  </details>

- **[Do Knowledge and Reasoning Oriented Benchmarks Induce Stable Representation Signatures in LLMs?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24029)**  `arXiv:2610.24029`  `cs.LG`  
  _Parsa Rahimi, Azim Dehghani Amirabad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Internal signatures could support monitoring, adaptive computation, and mechanistic analysis, but benchmark prompts can be separable without any shared computational distinction. We test whether a prespecified knowledge/reasoning label defines a reusable internal signature in five contemporary 7B to 31B models. We treat the \textbf{task} as the unit of inference probes hold out complete tasks, labels are permuted across tasks, and the best layer and stream are reselected for every permutation. Raw probes recover an eight-versus-eight-subject MMLU partition in all five models (macro-F1 $0.78$--$0.81$), but prompt text predicts it nearly as well: under an identical probe configuration, the activation advantage is only $0.017$--$0.070$ macro-F1 and is not significant. At the prespecified $0.01$ level, lexical adjustment removes significance while the adjusted states still identify individual subjects, and the selected site changes across task panels and selection rules. Additionally, two released base-training trajectories show no common direction. These pooled layerwise measures recover the task label but identify no transferable knowledge/reasoning signature at the tested sensitivity, which cannot exclude moderate effects. The resulting protocol gives future internal monitors and intervention studies a sequence of tests to pass. Project page: \url{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[Compaction-Aware Reinforcement Learning For Ascend Triton Kernel Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23950)**  `arXiv:2610.23950`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chuansai Zhou, Yanlin Liu, Bingning Huang, Shidi Xi, Chao Huang, Boxing Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automatically generating high-performance kernels with LLM-based agents promises substantial gains in development efficiency, but two obstacles remain. First, while increasing multi-turn interaction with execution environments is crucial for refining generation quality, continuous context accumulation rapidly exceeds the model's effective context window. Second, training long-horizon tasks via GRPO inherently incurs sparse rewards due to its reliance on terminal outcome evaluation, severely hindering step-level credit assignment; To address these obstacles, we propose ATRL (Ascend Triton Reinforcement Learning), a post-training method that refines GRPO into a compaction-aware, segment-level objective for long-horizon kernel generation. We also design a lightweight agent harness providing cascaded compilation, correctness, and profiling feedback, alongside an Ascend-specific knowledge base injected during agentic RL training and evaluation to compensate for the base model's missing platform expertise. Evaluations on NPUKernelBench and KernelBench, both executed on Ascend hardware, show that ATRL consistently surpasses all baselines, including GRPO, in both correctness and speedup.
  </details>

- **[Progress-Aware Distillation for Mitigating Stagnation in Small Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19439)**  `arXiv:2610.19439`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuanpu Cao, Saket Sathe, Hanyu Wang, Ziyi Yin, Fenglong Ma, Jinghui Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While LLM-based agent systems have demonstrated remarkable proficiency in reasoning and solving complex real-world tasks, they also come with substantial token consumption and inference latency, limiting their practical deployment. To enhance the efficiency of agent systems, recent efforts have focused on transferring agentic capabilities from large-scale models to small language models (SLMs) via supervised fine-tuning-based distillation. Nevertheless, as interaction trajectories grow longer, involving extended reasoning-action chains and extensive environmental feedback, distilled student agents tend to experience progress stagnation: becoming trapped in unproductive loops characterized by persistent failed strategies and difficulty making meaningful progress. Our systematic analysis shows that this phenomenon consistently occurs across diverse SLMs and task domains. To address this issue, we propose Progress-Aware Distillation (ProD), which explicitly detects and penalizes stagnation in student-generated trajectories. ProD encodes the stagnation gap between student and teacher into an adaptive margin for dynamically penalizing possible stagnation. By iteratively applying this procedure, ProD enables student agents to progressively approximate teacher behavior. Extensive experiments involving 8 SLMs, ranging from 0.6B to 8B parameters, on both in-domain and out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrate that ProD substantially mitigates progress stagnation while enhancing both the success rates and task-completion efficiency of distilled SLM agents.
  </details>

- **[SP$^2$ec: Adaptive Self-Speculative Decoding for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18735)**  `arXiv:2610.18735`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuqi Huang, Xingyao Li, Yunlong Hou, Fengzhuo Zhang, Jiachun Pan, Vincent Y. F. Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative Decoding (SD) has demonstrated its efficacy in accelerating large language model inference, but for Vision-Language Models (VLMs), the diverse input modalities make it difficult for a small trained drafter to achieve consistently high throughput. Self-speculative decoding reuses parts of the target model as a drafter which retains the multi-modal capability of the VLMs, yet its efficiency depends on choosing a suitable draft configuration for each prompt. We propose Single-Peak SPeculative decoding (SP$^2$ec), a training-free bandit algorithm that adapts the draft configuration during generation. SP$^2$ec exploits the single-peak throughput pattern observed in existing self-speculative methods, including SWIFT, KnapSpec, SpecVLM, and our simple cosSim. Our algorithmic design explicitly minimizes a novel notion of wall-time regret for each given query, corresponding to the empirical wall-time latency. Theoretical guarantees on the wall-time regret show that SP$^2$ec can exploit the optimal draft configuration, with negligible exploration cost. Extensive experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-7B-Instruct and LLaVA-NeXT-Mistral-7B across visual and textual tasks show consistent improvements of up to 11% over the original self-speculative methods, demonstrating the wide applicability of the proposed SP$^2$ec algorithm.
  </details>

- **[ID Balancing: Stable Training of Extremely Sparse MoE via PID-Based Load Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39137)**  `arXiv:2609.39137`  `cs.LG`  
  _Peng Jin, Zihan Qiu, Zekun Wang, Bo Zheng, Yang Xu, Tian Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) via Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) enables massive parameter growth with nearly constant per-token computation. However, further scaling the parameter count requires increasingly sparse routing, where expert load imbalance becomes more severe. This imbalance reduces parameter utilization and training efficiency, and can undermine training stability, becoming a bottleneck to reliable scaling. In this work, we unify two representative auxiliary-loss-free methods as incomplete Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID) controllers: DeepSeek's loss-free method acts as a fixed-step integral controller, while Kimi K3's Quantile Balancing functions as a generalized proportional controller. Building on this control perspective, we propose ID Balancing, an Integral-Derivative controller. It scales its integral term with load error and activates its derivative term only when imbalance worsens, enabling stronger corrections for large or worsening errors and smaller updates near balance. Evaluated across Top-$10$, Top-$5$, and Top-$3$ routing over $768$ experts, ID Balancing reduces worst-case backbone MaxVio and training-average backbone MinVio by over $50$% and $12$%, respectively, relative to the best baselines in the Top-$3$ setting. When the total parameter count increases from $18.9$B to $69.9$B (Top-$10$-of-$768$), ID Balancing's worst-case backbone MaxVio remains nearly unchanged and is approximately $89.6$% lower than that of the auxiliary-loss baseline. ID Balancing also maintains competitive language-modeling and downstream performance. The advantages of ID Balancing grow as sparsity increases, making it a promising solution for scaling larger, sparser MoE models.
  </details>

- **[REATS: LLM Reasoning-based Ensemble Learning for Adaptive Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10149)**  `arXiv:2608.10149`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xu Zhang, Chang Xu, Hui Sun, Nan Ma, Zijian Zhang, Peng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Due to the diversity of real-world time series, no single forecasting model consistently dominates across all samples. Ensemble learning addresses this by combining complementary model strengths, yet existing methods rely on fixed rules or black-box models based solely on numerical inputs, failing to leverage LLM reasoning for interpretable weighting decisions. We propose REATS, which leverages LLM reasoning capabilities as an intelligent ensemble router that jointly processes textual temporal pattern descriptions and numerical features to produce interpretable, sample-adaptive ensemble weights through chain-of-thought reasoning. To enable effective LLM-based ensembling, we study its key design choices and propose: (i) a structured input pipeline that transforms raw time series into hybrid textual--numerical representations with fixed token cost, enabling rule-based chain-of-thought construction without API dependency, augmented with retrieved similar-sample priors; (ii) a diverse multi-row weight supervision scheme coupled with a token-efficient percentage-table format that reduces numerical complexity and mitigates LLM hallucinations; and (iii) a two-stage fine-tuning framework combining SFT with GRPO, where a reciprocal reward mapping transforms the continuous unbounded MSE gap into bounded signals with amplified near-oracle sensitivity, addressing the uniform sensitivity and outlier-dominated advantage compression inherent in naive reward designs for regression-based GRPO. Experiments on eight benchmarks demonstrate that REATS outperforms competitive ensemble baselines while providing natural language explanations and demonstrating strong transfer learning and out-of-domain generalization to unseen candidate models.
  </details>

- **[BAPR: Bayesian amnesic piecewise-robust reinforcement learning for non-stationary continuous control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16170)**  `arXiv:2605.16170`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yifan Zhang, Liang Zheng, Qifan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world control systems frequently operate under \emph{piecewise stationary} conditions, where dynamics remain stable for extended periods before undergoing abrupt regime changes. Standard robust RL methods face a fundamental dilemma: a globally conservative policy wastes performance during stable periods, while a locally adaptive policy risks catastrophic failure when the regime changes undetected.We propose \textbf{BAPR} (Bayesian Amnesic Piecewise-Robust SAC), which unifies Bayesian Online Change Detection (BOCD) with robust ensemble RL. The BAPR operator -- a convex combination of mode-conditional Bellman operators weighted by a frozen belief distribution -- is a $\gamma$-contraction. A complementary counterexample, machine-verified in Lean~4, establishes a \emph{sharp boundary}: when beliefs depend on the Q-function, the contraction factor becomes $\gamma + \lambda\Delta$ (where $\Delta$ is the mode reward gap), and contraction fails exactly when $\gamma + \lambda\Delta \geq 1$. We derive a \emph{component-wise} formal error budget for the abstract operator -- every component machine-verified -- bounding post-switch recovery; the budget applies to the abstract mode-mixture operator and inherits to the implemented shared-critic algorithm only through the frozen-parameter design intuition. All results are formally verified with no \texttt{sorry} (1,145 lines across 3 Lean~4 files, 22 machine-verified theorems). BOCD drives an adaptive conservatism mechanism: the policy becomes maximally conservative after detected change-points and smoothly relaxes as confidence grows, with detection delay $O(\log(1/\delta))$. A context-conditioning module trained via RMDM loss provides mode-aware representations from simulator-provided mode IDs at training time and requires no mode labels at deployment.
  </details>

- **[Differentiable Mixture-of-Agents Incentivizes Swarm Intelligence of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15706)**  `arXiv:2605.15706`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xingjian Wu, Junkai Lu, Siyu Yan, Xiangfei Qiu, Jilin Hu, Chenjuan Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have catalyzed the development of multi-agent systems (MAS) for complex reasoning tasks. However, existing MAS typically rely on pre-defined or pre-compiled communication topologies, which limits their flexibility and adaptability to dynamic task requirements. In this work, we propose Differentiable Mixture-of-Agents (DMoA), a self-evolving multi-agent framework that enables elastic and adaptive agent collaboration during inference. Instead of statically constructing workflows, DMoA dynamically routes and activates agents at each reasoning step, allowing the system to implicitly simulate diverse communication topologies and adapt to evolving demands. To achieve this, we design a differentiable, context-aware routing mechanism that leverages recurrent structures to incorporate historical and contextual information, producing sparse agent activations in a step-wise manner. Furthermore, we introduce predictive entropy as self-supervised signals to optimize the routing process, enabling efficient test-time adaptation without external annotations. Extensive experiments across 9 benchmarks demonstrate that DMoA achieves state-of-the-art performance while exhibiting strong efficiency, robustness, and ensembling capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Requests of a Feather Must Flock Together: Batch Size vs. Prefix Homogeneity in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06046)**  `arXiv:2605.06046`  `cs.LG`  
  _Saksham Rathi, Preeti, Mythili Vutukuru_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auto-regressive token generation in large language models is memory-bound because it requires "attending to" key and value tensors (KV cache) of all previous tokens. Prior work aims to improve the efficiency of this decode process by batching multiple requests together, and maximizing batch size subject to GPU memory constraints. The key observation of our work is that with prefix-sharing workloads, smaller, prefix-homogeneous batches - where all requests share a common prefix - can achieve higher decode throughput than larger, heterogeneous batches, due to better spatial and temporal locality during KV cache accesses. However, current prefix-aware schedulers maximize prefix reuse within a batch only to reduce KV cache memory footprint, but do not stop batch formation at smaller homogeneous batches that could have performed better. Further, we show that shared prefix detection in existing schedulers relies on radix-tree traversals, incurring substantial CPU overhead that is often comparable to GPU execution time. This paper presents Feather, a prefix-aware scheduler that uses reinforcement learning (RL) to learn the optimal tradeoff between batch size and prefix homogeneity. We also introduce Chunked Hash Tree (CHT), a lightweight data structure that enables fast prefix detection and efficient request selection for the RL scheduler, avoiding expensive tree traversals. We integrate Feather into vLLM and SGLang, and our evaluation shows that Feather achieves 2-10X higher end-to-end throughput as compared to existing schedulers by reducing the total number of KV cache accesses, while doing no worse than the status quo when there insufficient prefix sharing.
  </details>

- **[Power Distribution Bridges Sampling, Self-Reward RL, and Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04542)**  `arXiv:2605.04542`  `cs.LG`  
  _Akiyoshi Tomihari, Issei Sato_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent analyses question whether reinforcement learning (RL) is responsible for strong reasoning in large language models (LLMs). At the same time, distillation and inference-time sampling, including power sampling, have emerged as effective ways to improve LLM performance. However, the relationship among RL, distillation, and sampling remains unclear. In this study, we focus on the power distribution, the target distribution of power sampling, and show that the power distribution bridges sampling, self-reward KL-regularized RL, and self-distillation. From the sampling perspective, we show that inexpensive local approximations cannot reproduce sequence-level power without information about possible suffixes. From the RL perspective, the power distribution is the closed-form optimizer of KL-regularized RL when the model's sequence-level log-probabilities are used as the reward. This identification leads to power self-distillation, an offline distillation surrogate that shares the same target distribution and amortizes the cost of power sampling into supervised training on teacher samples. We further show that power self-distillation can achieve self-reward sharpening, that downstream true-reward gains are governed by the covariance between true reward and self-reward under the power distribution, and that this ideal power-teacher gain transfers to a distilled student when the student is close to the power teacher. Experiments on reasoning tasks support our analysis: power sampling raises self-reward, true-reward gains depend on alignment with self-reward, and power self-distillation can match or exceed the performance of power sampling at much lower inference cost. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Diet Your LLM: Dimension-wise Global Pruning of LLMs via Merging Task-specific Importance Score](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23985)**  `arXiv:2603.23985`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jimyung Hong, Jaehyung Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities, but their massive scale poses significant challenges for practical deployment. Structured pruning offers a promising solution by removing entire dimensions or layers, yet existing methods face critical trade-offs: task-agnostic approaches cannot adapt to task-specific requirements, while task-aware methods require costly training to learn task adaptability. We propose DIET (Dimension-wise global pruning of LLMs via merging Task-wise importance scores), a training-free structured pruning method that combines dimension-level granularity with task-aware selection. DIET profiles activation magnitudes across tasks using only 100 samples per task, then applies majority voting to construct a single global mask. Experiments on seven zero-shot benchmarks using Gemma-2 2B and 9B models demonstrate the effectiveness of DIET. At 20% sparsity on Gemma-2 2B, DIET improves average accuracy by 2.1 percentage points over SliceGPT and by 9.2 percentage points over PuDDing. On Gemma-2 9B, DIET remains competitive with SliceGPT while consistently outperforming PuDDing at 10% and 20% sparsity.
  </details>

- **[Sparse Subspace-to-Expert Sharing for Task-Agnostic Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17616)**  `arXiv:2601.17616`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fatema Siddika, Md Anwar Hossen, Tanwi Mallick, Ali Jannesari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual learning in Large Language Models (LLMs) is hindered by the plasticity-stability dilemma, where acquiring new capabilities often leads to catastrophic forgetting of previous knowledge. Existing methods typically treat parameters uniformly, failing to distinguish between specific task knowledge and shared capabilities. We introduce Mixture of Sparse Experts for Task-Agnostic Continual Learning (SETA), a framework that resolves the plasticity-stability conflict through adaptive sparse subspace decomposition into task-specific expert modules. Unlike standard updates, where tasks compete for the same parameters, SETA separates knowledge into unique experts, designed to isolate task-specific patterns, and shared experts, responsible for capturing common features. SETA shares an overlapping parameter block only when the new task's update passes a subspace-agreement test and a sign test against its prior expert; shared knowledge is protected by adaptive elastic anchoring, and independently gated routers retrieve the relevant experts at inference without task identifiers. Extensive experiments across diverse domain-specific benchmarks demonstrate that SETA achieves competitive or superior overall performance relative to state-of-the-art continual learning baselines, improving overall performance over the strongest baseline by 1.1 to 1.7 points on TRACE and by up to 5.5 points on the O-LoRA benchmark, with backward transfer between -2.2 and -1.3 on TRACE, against 4.6 to 6.3 points of forgetting for MoE-CL and 8 to 15 points for the other LoRA baselines.
  </details>

- **[ELSA: Efficient LLM-Centric Split Aggregation for Privacy-Aware Hierarchical Federated Learning over the Network Edge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13824)**  `arXiv:2601.13824`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaohong Yang, Tong Xie, Minghui Liwang, Chikai Shang, Yang Lu, Zhenzhen Jiao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models (LLMs) in resource-constrained edge networks poses fundamental challenges in computation, communication, data heterogeneity, and privacy. To address these challenges, we propose ELSA (Efficient LLM-Centric Split Aggregation), a distributed fine-tuning framework that integrates split learning (SL) with hierarchical federated learning (HFL) to coordinate computation and model aggregation across heterogeneous clients, edge servers, and the cloud. ELSA introduces three key innovations. First, it employs a task-agnostic, behavior-aware client clustering mechanism that constructs semantic fingerprints from public probe inputs using symmetric Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, complemented by prediction-consistency-based trust scoring and latency-aware edge assignment to account for data heterogeneity, client reliability, and communication constraints. Second, it adopts a resource-aware dynamic model partitioning strategy that distributes the LLM across clients and edge servers in three segments, while reserving the cloud for adapter aggregation. This design coordinates computation across heterogeneous nodes while balancing on-device computational demands and global convergence behavior. Third, it integrates computational sketches with semantic subspace orthogonal perturbation (SS-OP) to reduce communication overhead and mitigate privacy leakage during model exchanges. Extensive experiments across diverse NLP tasks demonstrate that ELSA outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in model performance, convergence behavior, and robustness. Further, on-device profiling on NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPUs and Jetson AGX Orin edge devices characterizes the memory footprint, energy consumption, and execution latency of key training stages, providing empirical evidence of ELSA's practical feasibility.
  </details>

- **[TimeART: Towards Agentic Time Series Reasoning via Tool-Augmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13653)**  `arXiv:2601.13653`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xingjian Wu, Junkai Lu, Zhengyu Li, Xiangfei Qiu, Jilin Hu, Chenjuan Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series data widely exist in real-world cyber-physical systems. Though analyzing and interpreting them contributes to significant values, e.g, disaster prediction and financial risk control, current workflows mainly rely on human data scientists, which requires significant labor costs and lacks automation. To tackle this, we introduce TimeART, a framework fusing the analytical capability of strong out-of-the-box tools and the reasoning capability of Large Language Models (LLMs), which serves as a fully agentic data scientist for Time Series Question Answering (TSQA). To teach the LLM-based Time Series Reasoning Models (TSRMs) strategic tool-use, we also collect a 100k expert trajectory corpus called TimeToolBench. To enhance TSRMs' generalization capability, we then devise a four-stage training strategy, which boosts TSRMs through learning from their own early experiences and self-reflections. Experimentally, we train an 8B TSRM on TimeToolBench and equip it with the TimeART framework, and it achieves consistent state-of-the-art performance on multiple TSQA tasks, which pioneers a novel approach towards agentic time series reasoning.
  </details>

- **[Causal inference and model explainability analysis of a real world sales dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.12605)**  `arXiv:2512.12605`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pranav Gupta, Nithin Surendran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most major retailers today have multiple divisions focused on various aspects, such as marketing, supply chain, online customer experience, store customer experience, employee productivity, and vendor fulfillment. They also regularly collect data corresponding to all these aspects as dashboards and weekly/monthly/quarterly reports. Although several machine learning and statistical techniques have been in place to analyze and predict key metrics, such models typically lack interpretability. Moreover, such techniques also do not allow the validation or discovery of causal links. In this paper, we aim to provide a recipe for applying model interpretability and causal inference for deriving sales insights. In this paper, we review the existing literature on causal inference and interpretability in the context of problems in e-commerce and retail, and apply them to a real-world dataset. We find that an inherently explainable model has a lower variance of SHAP values, and show that including multiple confounders through a double machine learning approach allows us to get the correct sign of causal effect.
  </details>

- **[Optimizing Prompt Sequences using Monte Carlo Tree Search for LLM-Based Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.05995)**  `arXiv:2508.05995`  `cs.LG`  
  _Fei Xu Yu, Gina Adam, Nathaniel D. Bastian, Tian Lan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mathematical optimization is a core tool in engineering, operations research, and data science, but modeling real problems remains difficult and time-consuming. In practice, an expert must work with clients to translate informal domain descriptions into precise objectives, constraints, and solver-ready code; small mistakes in this process can lead to infeasible, nonconvex, or suboptimal formulations, and the iterative process is often slow. Large language models (LLMs) could automate this translation from natural language to optimization code, but standard prompting often fails on realistic tasks: it struggles with long, structured task descriptions, mishandles constraints, and produces infeasible, non-convex, or non-executable programs. We introduce Monte Carlo Tree Search for Reasoning Decomposition Plans (MCTS-RDP), which performs pre-hoc test-time scaling by searching over decomposition plans and their resulting programs before committing to the final output. MCTS-RDP treats plan construction as a sequential decision problem: given a problem description, it decomposes the task descriptions into logical components and generates candidate plan steps for each component. Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) then explores and refines Reasoning Decomposition Plans (RDPs) prior to code synthesis, balancing exploration and exploitation across alternative plan sequences. Each candidate plan is rolled out into executable code, executed, and scored with an execution-grounded evaluator measuring executability, constraint satisfaction, and solution quality; this feedback is backpropagated through the search tree to guide subsequent search. Across wireless optimization tasks and the NL4OPT and NLP4LP benchmarks, MCTS-RDP consistently outperforms strong baselines, improving execution success, reward, stability, and optimality attainment.
  </details>

- **[LVMH: A Meta-Optimization Harness for Long-Video Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23904)**  `arXiv:2610.23904`  `cs.CV` `cs.MA`  
  _Liwei Che, Benlin Liu, Dai Li, Xiangyan Li, Zijian Wu, Changjiang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-video agents must map hours or days of multimodal evidence into a bounded context while respecting token and latency budgets, making memory design a context-management challenge. Existing memory systems largely rely on hand-designed policies, leaving open how to jointly optimize memory design for performance and efficiency. We propose Long Video Meta-memory Harness (LVMH), an automated multi-agent framework that formulates memory-policy development as multi-objective code search around a fixed answer model. To guard against self-certification and development-set overfitting, LVMH separates implementation from independent review and enforces protected observation boundaries. Persistent meta-memory records past proposals, traces, costs, and failures to guide subsequent policy revisions. Through this harness, we discover Self-Evolving Evidence Memory (SEEM), a "text-first, vision-second" policy that answers from compact structured event memory and evidence specialists, consulting localized frames through a selective visual gate. The policy evolved on EgoLifeQA generalizes to unseen benchmarks, outperforming Meta-Harness on both Ego-R1 Bench and Video-MME (L) with GPT-5 mini. Across the three benchmarks, SEEM with GPT-5 achieves 65.7% average accuracy, surpassing the previous state of the art by 7.7 percentage points with approximately 32x lower average evaluation token use and 91x shorter average response time.
  </details>

- **[Write Once, Query Many: Evolving Compact Reusable Memory for Long Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24537)**  `arXiv:2610.24537`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Kerui Chen, Jinglu Wang, Yun Wang, Xinjiang Wang, Yan Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long videos often exceed the context capacity of multimodal large language models (MLLMs), while agentic search repeats costly video processing and grounding for each query. Reusable memory can amortize this cost, but must preserve information for questions unknown during construction. We propose Video-EMem, an evolvable video memory harness built on time-anchored perceptual observations. This representation combines compact free-form text with sparse images to preserve semantic content and complementary visual details across temporal scales, supporting temporal navigation without a fixed semantic schema. A Writer encodes each video once by consolidating overlapping multiscale observations, and a Reader retrieves and organizes evidence solely from the resulting memory. With the underlying models frozen, we evolve their workflows and operators using distinct feedback: query-independent source fidelity and coverage for the Writer, and QA failures in evidence localization and utilization for the Reader. Experiments support this separation for generalization beyond development queries, with our evolution strategy improving average accuracy by 7.2% over the unevolved harness. While reducing storage by about 99.86% on average relative to raw videos, Video-EMem improves Qwen3-VL-30B by an average of 2.6% across three long-video QA benchmarks over direct input of 2,048 frames. Using the same o3 answer model as the state-of-the-art agentic method, it improves average accuracy by 2.5% with lower per-query latency.
  </details>

- **[FrameScout: Scouting Query-Relevant Frames for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23975)**  `arXiv:2610.23975`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Haonan Hu, Shuhao Chen, Weisen Jiang, Lizhao Gao, James Kwok, Yu Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long video understanding requires VideoLLMs to answer queries about videos spanning tens of minutes to hours, but such long videos produce far more visual tokens than current VideoLLMs can process. To address this issue, keyframe selectors are proposed to select the most query-relevant frames by computing the similarity between frame and query embeddings. Existing selectors either encode frames independently without temporal context or process chunks in isolation with ranking objectives that are difficult to optimize. To alleviate those limitations, we propose FrameScout with two core designs: (i) a streaming selector architecture that leverages a sliding-window KV cache and successor aggregation to produce temporally-contextualized frame embeddings; (ii) a frame-query contrastive objective that aligns frame and query embeddings in a shared space, directly training the selector to distinguish relevant frames from irrelevant ones. Extensive experiments on three long-video benchmarks and six base VideoLLMs demonstrate that FrameScout consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Seeing to Reporting: A Theory of Reliable Visual Set Generation in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25503)**  `arXiv:2610.25503`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Junzhe Shi, Yuan Gan, Shida Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are asked to report what they see: to count objects, box them, parse a screen or pick a click target (GUI grounding). We argue that reporting is part of the visual task, not merely its formatting. In a dense scene with 173 shapes, GPT-6.1 lists 173 records that match every shape, yet the counts it states in the same response sum to 188. From Seeing to Reporting (FSR) is a theory of visual reporting and a set of inference-time procedures that improve the reports of frozen VLMs. It models reporting as a chain from responses to decoded records, constructed sets and task answers, linked by task-compatible matching. The chain gives exact relations for deleting records, averaging their coordinates and switching readouts, and it splits the error of one answer chosen among several calls into missing candidates, lost representatives and wrong choices. These relations guide procedures for counting, detection, document layout, screen parsing and GUI grounding that use the task specification and a shipped configuration, without requiring labels from the target task. On 50 dense synthetic scenes, a one-call reporting-and-decoding protocol cuts per-type count error by 85.2% for GPT-6.1 and 98.8% for Claude Sonnet 5.5. On 600 ScreenSpot-Pro items, a GUI-grounding tier that combines three models lowers the miss rate from 13.67% for GPT-6.1's two-stage zoom to 9.33%, against 12.67% for three such zooms combined by a cluster vote at about the same cost. A plain vote over the same three models misses 12.33%: the other models supply the candidates, and selecting among them turns those candidates into fewer misses. We release the protocols, the stored responses and the analysis code.
  </details>

- **[EgoVine: Post-Training with Branching Experience Compiled from Real-World Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23907)**  `arXiv:2610.23907`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Qinchuan Cheng, Bowen Lyu, Zhantao Gong, Angela Yao, Cheston Tan, Shijie Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world egocentric videos capture multi-step household procedures, but their recorded trajectories leave alternative actions and recovery from the learner's own mistakes largely unexplored. We introduce EgoVine, a post-training framework that turns a fixed demonstration set into interactive experience for long-horizon planning. EgoVine operates in executable symbolic environments compiled from real-world video annotations, where an agent must coordinate object manipulations under partial observation and account for delayed consequences. Restoring a decision state allows the learner to explore alternative actions and compare their eventual outcomes. Union jointly trains action generation and consequence prediction in one model, using the same executions to supervise candidate comparison and recovery. Verified repairs add recovery targets from failed attempts, while comparisons among sampled continuations guide policy updates. The updated planner then returns to the environments, with its own decisions and mistakes shaping the next round of experience. On a shared benchmark, EgoVine-1.5B achieves 81.8% success on Main planning tasks and 66.7% on longer task compositions, outperforming all evaluated Flash LLM baselines on both. EgoVine connects real demonstrations to an iterative learning process that trains compact planners on choices and futures beyond the recorded trajectories.
  </details>

- **[GraFT: A Training-Free Framework for Spatial Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models via 3D Scene Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03892)**  `arXiv:2609.03892`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Junqing Du, Fernando Ropero, Erkin Turkoz, Yanfeng Zhang, Lu Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D spatial reasoning underpins understanding and acting in the physical world, yet it remains unreliable in current multimodal large language models (MLLMs). These models falter at precise geometric measurement, at transforming between egocentric and allocentric viewpoints, and at grounding fine-grained appearance. The most common remedies fine-tune the model on large-scale curated spatial-reasoning datasets or attach dedicated encoders for 3D geometry, which typically couples the solution to costly supervision and a specific backbone. We instead introduce GraFT, a training-free framework that supplies the missing 3D structure through a compact, easily maintained 3D scene graph (3DSG). GraFT supplies the graph's 3D structure to the frozen model in three forms: as values computed by symbolic tools, as a bird's-eye-view (BEV) the model reads as an image, and as egocentric frames the geometry selects. On ScanQA, GraFT improves every metric over the same-backbone baseline, raising CIDEr by 27%. On VSI-Bench, GraFT improves frozen MLLMs by up to 65%, surpassing every proprietary and general-purpose open-source baseline, and several prominent fine-tuned spatial models.
  </details>

- **[PLANE: Plug-and-Play Fine-Grained Visual Experts for Hallucination Mitigation in Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25146)**  `arXiv:2610.25146`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jianghao Yin, Lang Yu, Qianxi Yu, Qin Chen, Jie Zhou, Zhikai Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) commonly suffer from hallucinations, which appear as fabricating non-existent objects or outputting incorrect attributes and spatial relationships in images. Previous works empirically indicate that CLIP-style vision encoders tend to focus on primary objects while ignoring image details, which is one of the causes of hallucinations. To address this issue, we propose plug-and-play fine-grained visual experts (PLANE) comprising two components: LoRA visual experts operating on CLIP for multi-aspect image encoding, and an instruction-aware gate network for adaptive image feature fusion. During training, we construct a fine-grained image-caption pairs dataset, where images are described from three aspects: objects, attributes, and relations between objects. Image-caption pairs from different aspects are used to train the three fine-grained LoRA experts respectively. Aiming at comprehensively evaluate our method's capabilities, we manually annotate a fine-grained hallucination evaluation benchmark for LVLMs which is GPT4-independent during evaluation phase (GIFT). We conduct experiments both on popular hallucination evaluation benchmarks and GIFT. The results show that our method effectively reduces hallucinations across models of different types and sizes while introducing minimal inference latency. Compared to advanced baselines, our model also demonstrates superior performance.
  </details>

- **[From Scientific Crop Imagery to Agronomic Reasoning: A Multimodal LLM Benchmark for Plant Phenotyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09907)**  `arXiv:2604.09907`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yu Wu, Guangzeng Han, Ibra Niang Niang, Francia Ravelombola, Maiara Oliveira, Jason Davis, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-throughput, effective and comprehensive phenotyping is a critical prerequisite for crop improvement. While phenotypic analysis was traditionally performed manually, recent advances in multimodal foundation models, especially in vision-language models, have offered new opportunities for automated plant phenotyping. However, plant science remains particularly challenging for general-purpose VLMs because it requires domain-specific knowledge, fine-grained visual interpretation, and complex agronomic reasoning. We introduce PlantXpert, an evidence-grounded multimodal reasoning benchmark for plant phenotyping across four major crops: soybean, cotton, rice, and corn. Our benchmark provides a structured and reproducible framework for agronomic adaptation of VLMs, and enables controlled comparison between base models and their domain-adapted counterparts. We constructed a dataset comprising 545 digital images and 4,351 benchmark samples spanning key agronomic domains including disease, pest control, weed management and yield. The benchmark can assess diverse capabilities including visual expertise, quantitative reasoning, and multi-step agronomic reasoning. We trained and evaluated a total of 11 state-of-the-art VLMs. Fine-tuning on our dataset improves overall accuracy for all models, with many achieving around 70% accuracy, and improvements on 9 of 11 remaining statistically significant after Holm correction. We also identify diminishing returns from model scaling, uneven performance across crop subsets, and persistent weaknesses in quantitative reasoning and specialized image analysis. These limitations show that general multimodal competence alone is insufficient for reliable agronomic reasoning and demonstrate the value of domain-specific adaptation. We hope PlantXpert can serve as a foundation for advancing evidence-grounded multimodal reasoning in plant science.
  </details>

- **[FrameGRPO: Incentivizing Video Reasoning via Multi-Frame Rollouts and Temporal Credit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26022)**  `arXiv:2610.26022`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaxuan Sun, Biao Wang, Shuai Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, particularly Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has improved video reasoning in multimodal large language models. However, standard video GRPO varies only linguistic trajectories while conditioning every rollout in a group on the same sampled frames, which can produce homogeneous rewards and weaken group-relative learning when the shared visual evidence is insufficient or distracting. We propose \textbf{FrameGRPO}, a simple and effective reinforcement-learning framework that requires neither auxiliary networks nor changes to the model architecture. Specifically, FrameGRPO combines two lightweight components: (1) \textbf{Multi-Frame Rollouts}, which use only uniform temporal subsampling of a single maximal-frame decode to construct aligned views that diversify visual evidence and reasoning trajectories; and (2) \textbf{Gated Temporal Efficiency Credit}, which selectively reinforces a correct lower-ratio trajectory when all rollouts in a fixed full-ratio reference subset fail, without imposing an unconditional preference for fewer frames. Extensive experiments across diverse video understanding and reasoning benchmarks demonstrate robust overall improvements over standard GRPO, highlighting temporal-view diversification as an effective mechanism for exploration and credit assignment in video MLLMs.
  </details>

- **[ReTrace: Visual Backtracking for Efficient Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25005)**  `arXiv:2610.25005`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Guangzong Si, Dong Wang, Yifan Yu, Miao Liu, Panwang Pan, Wentao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) encode images into dense visual token sequences, creating substantial attention and KV-cache overhead. Existing visual token compression methods reduce this cost by pruning low-saliency tokens in shallow layers, but this practice couples temporary computational deactivation with irreversible information deletion. We show that this coupling breaks an intrinsic inference pattern in MLLMs. Through layer-wise attention analysis, we identify \textbf{Visual Backtracking}: visual pivots remain spatially stable, yet in middle layers they actively retrieve context from tokens that appeared unimportant earlier. Thus, early low-saliency tokens can be inactive for computation while still being useful for later reasoning. Motivated by this finding, we propose \textbf{ReTrace}, a backtracking-aware compression framework that introduces a dormant token state. ReTrace caches non-pivot tokens outside expensive layer-wise computation, restores them during the backtracking window, and prunes only after their information has been absorbed. Experiments on Qwen2.5-VL and InternVL3 demonstrate nearly 90\% reductions in FLOPs and KV cache, a 2.83$\times$ prefilling speedup, and strong performance retention across complex vision-language benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[HumanSkill-4D: Training-Free Language Querying of Animatable Gaussian Avatars in Space and Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24452)**  `arXiv:2610.24452`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _unsu Kim, Naeun Kim, Dongyoon Han, Seungryul Baek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce avatar referring segmentation, which selects regions of animatable Gaussian avatars from language queries. A query can also specify a pose or motion to select matching intervals. This lets users interact with avatars for local editing and motion inspection. However, making these selections with existing language Gaussian splatting methods poses two challenges: adjacent parts and garments are hard to separate and may be referred to indirectly (region ambiguity), while fine-grained motions can look alike in RGB (motion ambiguity). We address these challenges with HumanSkill-4D, a training-free framework that shares a multimodal large language model (MLLM) and geometric operators across avatars. For each avatar, a semantic memory links body-part and garment categories to persistent Gaussian regions and appearance descriptions. The MLLM uses category names and descriptions to select categories by appearance, relation, or function. To find matching intervals, it composes operators into programs that check query conditions on driving joint trajectories stored in a motion memory. These category choices and operator outputs make selections traceable, while composing or adding operators supports new motion conditions. To evaluate this task, we introduce three human-annotated benchmarks and show that HumanSkill-4D outperforms the baselines in part segmentation and 3D and 4D referring segmentation, with compact memories and only minutes of setup per avatar.
  </details>

- **[Do 3D Large Language Models Really Use 3D Evidence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24447)**  `arXiv:2610.24447`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yifei Zhao, Fanyu Zhao, Han Zhang, Yixuan Lin, Yuyang Hou, Bin Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent 3D Large Language Models (3D-LLMs) have shown strong performance on scene question answering with extensive post-training. Yet, existing benchmarks rarely evaluate how 3D-LLMs respond to referential ambiguity, insufficient scene evidence, or false presuppositions, obscuring whether their responses are grounded in 3D evidence. In this work, we introduce UNICORNS, a benchmark for evaluating the reliability of 3D-LLM responses across four evidence conditions. UNICORNS contains 11,387 questions across 557 ScanNet scans, covering eight challenge categories alongside answerable controls. A response passes only if it satisfies the required response behavior and contains no scene claim unsupported by the available evidence. Evaluation of six open-sourced 3D-LLMs reveals a pronounced performance gap: even the strongest zero-shot model achieves 61.22% accuracy on answerable controls, but only 40.94% across the eight challenge categories. To address this gap, we further propose HORN, an obligation-aware post-training method combining group-relative task rewards, leaf-conditioned obligation rewards, and supervised control replay. Extensive experiments demonstrate that HORN reaches $75.1 \pm 1.0\%$ across the challenge categories and $77.2 \pm 0.8\%$ overall, outperforming all baselines without degrading answerable-control accuracy. Our findings enable a systematic evaluation and improvement of evidence-grounded 3D reasoning for 3D-LLMs (code:this https URL).
  </details>

- **[ZeroDSS: Semantic Segmentation from Class Names Alone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24405)**  `arXiv:2610.24405`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Tian Ming, Marzi Heidari, Yuhong Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic segmentation depends heavily on real images with pixel-level annotations, making dense supervision a major obstacle to deployment in new domains. We study task-specific data-free semantic segmentation, where training uses generic pretrained generative priors but has access to no target-domain images, masks, or captions--only a predefined category set. We introduce Zero-Data Semantic Segmentation (ZeroDSS), a feedback-driven approach that treats synthetic supervision as an adaptively controllable training distribution rather than a direct surrogate for real data. ZeroDSS constructs a semantically structured prompt distribution through LLM-guided compositional generation and adaptive class rebalancing, then converts the prompts into image-mask pairs using diffusion attention. Its feedback-driven distribution refinement consists of a two-phase closed-loop training procedure with a single loop-back step. In Phase I, a segmenter is trained using confidence-weighted supervision and dual-scale consistency. In Phase II, model feedback is used to adaptively target underperforming categories and refine diffusion-derived masks using model predictions. After this refined initial training, a teacher-student framework is utilized in the final phase to iteratively improve the fused supervision and segmenter through exponential moving average (EMA) updates. Using synthetic training data only, ZeroDSS achieves 35.0% mIoU on MS COCO with DeepLabV3, and 66.3% and 63.5% mIoU on PASCAL VOC with DeepLabV3 and Mask2Former, respectively. These results show that explicitly constructing and iteratively refining the synthetic supervision distribution can substantially reduce the synthetic-to-real gap in dense prediction.
  </details>

- **[PACE: Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24005)**  `arXiv:2610.24005`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Lei Tian, Chunzheng Zhu, Kai Wu, Yifan Zhang, Haihua Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Native multimodal large language models (native MLLMs) jointly encode visual and linguistic sequences within a unified autoregressive Transformer, emerging as a promising paradigm for multimodal understanding. Unlike conventional ViT-based MLLMs relying on external vision encoders, ViT-free native MLLMs tokenize pixel patches directly, potentially suffering severe visual token redundancy. A pilot study confirms this: unlike ViT-based models that degrade under compression, ViT-free MLLMs tolerate aggressive compression with near-lossless or even improved performance. Yet existing compression methods target ViT-based settings, leaving native MLLMs, which stand to gain the most, unexplored. To close this gap, we introduce an analysis pipeline centered on token discriminability and cross-dimensional heterogeneous redundancy, revealing that (i) redundancy is distributed independently and heterogeneously along height and width axes, and (ii) early-layer visual tokens are indiscriminable and uniformly redundant. Guided by these findings, we propose PACE (Pareto-Adaptive Compression for Efficient Native MLLMs), the first visual token compression framework tailored for native MLLMs with Multimodal-RoPE. Leveraging dimensional decoupling of positional encoding, PACE decomposes attention into two orthogonal spatial saliency estimates along height and width, gated by a positional attention signal, yielding direction-aware importance with negligible overhead. It further casts token budget allocation as Pareto optimization over performance preservation, inference efficiency, and spatial coverage, jointly identifying optimal compression depths and retention ratios. Experiments show PACE maintains near-lossless performance at only 25%-50% visual token retention, even improving several benchmarks, while delivering over 2x FLOPs reduction.
  </details>

- **[RL Teaches VLMs Where to Attend but Not How to Perceive](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23983)**  `arXiv:2610.23983`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziqian Zhang, Junjie Liao, Zhining Zhang, Wentao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although reinforcement learning (RL) effectively trains Vision-Language Model (VLM) agents, the mechanisms behind these capability gains are poorly understood. Across four diverse agent environments, we investigate whether the task-success gains from multi-turn RL are accompanied by improved grounded visual question answering about the environment. To assess this, we design environment-specific grounded VQA tasks that reveal improvements in grounded visual understanding. Representational probing shows that vision-encoder probe accuracy remains relatively stable, while LLM-layer probe accuracy improves. Attention analysis further reveals that LLM layers pay more attention to visual tokens during RL training. To test whether increased attention to visual tokens contributes to VQA gains, we introduce a lightweight plug-and-play intervention using a fixed attention bias. Without updating model parameters or computing gradients, this intervention partially restores VQA gains on the untrained model. These results reveal better utilization of visual information as a key mechanism behind the gains from RL.
  </details>

- **[When the Editing Intent Is Split: A Cross-Modal Jailbreak Attack for Large Image Editing Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23909)**  `arXiv:2610.23909`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xianmin Ye, Zhiyuan Fan, Tianju Liu, Yuhang Wang, Zhenxing Niu, Haichang Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large image editing models increasingly infer editing intent from both textual and visual inputs, creating an underexplored safety risk when harmful intent is distributed across modalities. In this work, we formalize this split-intent vulnerability and propose the Split-Intent Jailbreak Attack (SIJA), which asymmetrically distributes an unsafe editing intent by specifying the editing referent in text while concealing the safety-critical action and specification through graphical visual semantics in an auxiliary cue. To systematically study this threat, we introduce the Split-Intent Image Editing Safety Benchmark (SI2ESBench) for evaluating image editing models under split-intent inputs. Extensive experiments on representative commercial and open-source models demonstrate that SIJA effectively compromises state-of-the-art image editing systems, achieving ASRs of 68.28% on GPT Image 2 and 91.38% on Qwen-Image-3.0-Pro. Our results expose a mismatch between multimodal editing and safety assessment: the distributed intent can be recovered to execute the requested transformation, yet its harmfulness often remains undetected by existing safeguards. To mitigate this vulnerability, we propose editing-intent reconstruction, a defense principle that explicitly evaluates the complete transformation implied by the joint multimodal context and substantially improves rejection of split-intent attacks. Our findings expose a cross-modal safety gap in modern image editing systems and highlight the need to assess the jointly inferred transformation rather than individual inputs in isolation.this https URL
  </details>

- **[EMMA: Event-Driven Multimodal Memory System with Multi-Agent Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23892)**  `arXiv:2610.23892`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Ruochen Zhao, Jiazheng Kang, Gezi Li, Dongying Lin, Ting Bai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long video understanding requires reasoning over hours of content that far exceed the context window of multimodal large language models (MLLMs). Existing approaches compress video into fixed-length clips, yet events are fragmented and unrelated content is merged, yielding noisy retrieval. To overcome this challenge, we propose EMMA, an Event-driven Multimodal Memory system with Multi-Agent retrieval, which organizes memory around semantic events in three stages: (1) Event segmentation: a dual-stream detector adaptively locates event boundaries by fusing visual semantic drift with spoken transitions, rather than fixed windows. (2) Memory construction: built on the detected events, a three-level hierarchy of raw video, clip-level memory, and event-level memory stores content at progressively higher semantic abstraction. (3) Memory retrieval: a multi-agent framework collaboratively queries this hierarchy through query decomposition, tiered search, evidence validation, and answer generation, narrowing evidence from coarse events to precise clips. Extensive experiments on three challenging benchmarks demonstrate substantial improvements, showing the effectiveness of our event-aligned memory system. Code, prompt templates, and reproduction scripts are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Visual Text Compression as Measure Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06708)**  `arXiv:2605.06708`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Lv Tang, Tianyi Zheng, Yang Liu, Bo Li, Xingyu Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual text compression (VTC) promises efficient long-context processing by rendering text into an image and re-encoding it with a vision-language model, often producing $3$--$20\times$ fewer decoder tokens than subword tokenization. Yet token savings do not translate predictably into downstream utility: on some tasks the visual path matches or exceeds the text path, on others it collapses, and the compression ratio itself does not predict which regime will occur. The missing quantity is therefore not another summary of efficiency, but a principled measure of task-relevant information loss induced by visual encoding. We address this problem by formulating VTC in the language of measure transport. Treating text and visual tokens as empirical probability measures, we show that the ViT patch encoder induces a push-forward map whose transport cost decomposes into a precision cost from within-patch aggregation and a coverage cost from cross-patch fragmentation. Both terms are estimable from downstream-label-free probes. This formulation yields two operational consequences: a downstream-label-free routing criterion that selects whether to use the visual path for a given input or benchmark instance, and a transport-informed foveation mechanism that re-encodes high-cost regions at higher resolution. Across $24$ NLP datasets at Qwen3-4B, our label-free rule matches the per-dataset oracle on $17/24$ datasets ($70.8\%$), and improves the average task score by $+3.3\%$ with $-10.3\%$ average tokens relative to a pure-LLM.
  </details>

- **[GenOlympiad: Benchmarking Frontier Image Models at Their Capability Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26850)**  `arXiv:2610.26850`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xi Hu, Martin Bell, Liang Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image models have made tremendous progress and can produce high-quality images. However, a question which would be asked in any other discipline is where the frontier models fall short. We introduce GenOlympiad, a benchmark which provides the most challenging text prompts from nine capability axes for stress-testing frontier text-to-image models. The assessed capabilities include generating large quantities, performing deep reasoning, ensuring hard physical consistency, following complex rules and so on. The prompts are designed such that prompt alignment is visually verifiable (e.g., counting) instead of relying on subjectiveness (e.g., style). Based on this, we develop an automated evaluation framework which decomposes the prompt into easy and hard questions, and for most hard questions, draws help lines to ground evidence for accurate grading. We evaluate 20+ strong text-to-image models, including Nano Banana Pro, GPT-Image-2.5, Seedream-5.0 and so on. We report that even the best-performing T2I model (Nano Banana Pro) only achieves a strict score of 23.9 out of 100 and that coding models such as GPT-6 Astra have higher prompt alignment scores (but not as visually pleasing). We also derive insights in model evolution, alignment with Elo scores, and effectiveness of our grounding-based evaluation protocol. Code is available atthis https URLand the project page is atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Beyond Omni: A Unified MLLM for Embodied Sensory Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26751)**  `arXiv:2610.26751`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shiyun Xiong, Dongming Wu, Peiwen Sun, Bokang Yang, Jiaxu Wang, Xiaolou Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent embodied systems increasingly build on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for their pretrained understanding and reasoning capabilities, but their perception of the physical world remains largely limited to basic modalities such as vision and audio. However, broadening MLLMs to more diverse sensory modalities from the physical environment is challenging: their representations are often weakly connected to the MLLM and vary substantially in structure and statistics, while their interpretation still requires shared high-level understanding and reasoning. We therefore propose Unisense, a unified embodied MLLM framework that supports diverse physical sensory modalities within a shared multimodal architecture. Unisense follows a two-stage training process: (i) aligning sensory representations with language to establish a language-accessible interface; and (ii) tuning modality-specific experts within the shared LLM to provide specialized processing for different sensory modalities. To support broad sensory learning, we curate Unisense-1M, a large-scale embodied sensory training corpus comprising 1M samples across 8 sensory modalities. We further evaluate Unisense on 12 benchmarks spanning depth, point cloud, IMU, tactile, thermal, radar, motion, and smell understanding. Across these heterogeneous sensing tasks, Unisense achieves strong performance and outperforms both generalist and specialized models on most benchmarks, demonstrating the potential of pretrained MLLMs as unified models for embodied sensory understanding.
  </details>

- **[Long-form Video-to-Text Summarization using Recursive Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26431)**  `arXiv:2610.26431`  `cs.CV`  
  _Alessia Intini, Lorenzo Vaiani, Enrico Chen, Luca Cagliero_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While VideoLLMs have made significant progress in understanding short video benchmarks, they often struggle to summarize long-form ones. This is mainly due to performance degradation caused by long-context prompts, the inherent difficulty of preserving temporal continuity and a lack of semantically rich concepts in event descriptions. To tackle these issues, this paper introduces the first recursive Video Language Summarization Model (RVLSM) designed for video processing and tailored to long-form video-to-text summarization. RVLSM recursively decomposes videos into semantically coherent segments and delegates chunk-level reasoning to VideoLLM sub-models and external tools. It employs (1) Semantic Temporal Decomposition (STD), which preserves temporal coherence and continuity through a scene-aware chunking strategy that dynamically partitions videos according to semantic boundaries, and (2) Adaptive Multimodal Tool Calling (AMTC), which ensures semantic richness of the summary via a routing mechanism that selectively invokes auxiliary modules such as action recognition, optical character recognition, automatic speech recognition, and object detection. Experiments on the VideoXum and MixTyVSum benchmarks show that RVLSM consistently improves over zero-shot VideoLLM baselines across multiple model settings and performance metrics, and is competitive with a state-of-the-art fine-tuned model for video-to-text summarization. Importantly, the named entities in the RVLSM's summaries align more closely with those detected in the ground-truth summaries than those detected by baseline and state-of-the-art methods, confirming the saliency of the event descriptions it generates.
  </details>

- **[DCR-TSF: Dual-Level Compositional Reasoning with Triadic Spherical Fusion for Training-Free Zero-Shot Composed Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26078)**  `arXiv:2610.26078`  `cs.CV`  
  _Gwang-Ho Na, Ho-Joong Kim, Seong-Whan Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zero-shot composed image retrieval (ZS-CIR) aims to retrieve a target image that reflects an intended textual modification while maintaining semantic consistency with a reference image. Existing training-free ZS-CIR methods typically rely on fine-grained generated target descriptions, which often overemphasize reference-specific visual details and lead to appearance-biased retrieval. Moreover, since these descriptions are generated through multimodal large language models (MLLMs), the direct influence of the original image-text query can be weakened in the final retrieval representation. In this paper, we propose DCR-TSF, a training-free ZS-CIR framework that combines Dual-level Compositional Reasoning (DCR) and Triadic Spherical Fusion (TSF). DCR generates fine- and coarse-grained descriptions from the reference image and modification text, capturing complementary target-side semantics across different granularities. To preserve the original image-text query information, TSF constructs a composed-query anchor by spherically interpolating the reference image feature and modification text feature, and fuses it with the fine- and coarse-grained representations using a weighted Karcher mean on the unit hypersphere. The resulting final query representation balances detailed visual evidence, high-level semantic abstraction, and direct preservation of the original composed query. Extensive experiments on CIRCO, CIRR, and Fashion-IQ demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance among training-free methods.
  </details>

- **[OmniGround: Strengthening Omni-Modal Temporal Grounding via Attention-Consistency Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25929)**  `arXiv:2610.25929`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zixuan Chen, Depeng Wang, Hao Lin, Ke Xu, Ya Guo, Huijia Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models can jointly understand video, audio, and text, yet they remain weak at temporal grounding, a capability essential for real-world audio-visual applications. To understand this limitation, we look beyond final predictions and examine the model's internal temporal evidence. We identify a temporal generation gap: the internal attention of the model localizes the target moment more accurately than the timestamps it generates. To close this gap, we propose TACG (Temporal Attention-Consistency Grounding), a post-training method that treats internal attention as a reliable temporal pivot and aligns it with both the model prediction and the ground-truth annotation through attention-consistency training. We further build a 580K-sample training dataset by reconstructing temporal QA from dense audio-visual annotations and organizing general omni-modal instruction data. Using TACG and this training dataset, we train OmniGround, a general-purpose omni-modal model with stronger temporal grounding capability, without injecting any additional temporal cues. OmniGround reaches state-of-the-art omni-modal temporal grounding, including 37.5 mIoU on LongVALE and 35.7 mIoU on UnAV-100, while staying competitive on both general audio-visual understanding and vision-only grounding.
  </details>

- **[DiffGround: Semantic Difference-Guided Zero-Shot Video Question Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25570)**  `arXiv:2610.25570`  `cs.CV`  
  _Cheng-Han Lee, Amir Salimi, Hsiang-Wei Huang, Che-Chun Su, Min Sun, Cheng-Hao Kuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video Question Grounding (VideoQG) requires both accurate question answering and precise temporal localization. Existing supervised methods rely on costly temporal annotations, while existing zero-shot approaches are primarily designed for Video Question Answering (VQA) and are not optimized for temporal grounding. We propose DiffGround, an action-driven zero-shot framework built upon frozen Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Our framework first predicts an answer-relevant action cue that transforms the input question into an explicit semantic target for grounding. Action Cue converts a reasoningoriented question into an action-centric retrieval query. Guided by the action cue, a Vision-Language Model (VLM) efficiently selects informative keyframes for reasoning.
