Finally, we introduce Semantic Diff Refinement, a training-free temporal refinement method that exploits semantic differences between neighboring SigLIP2 embeddings to recover complete action segments from sparse predictions. Extensive experiments on NExT-GQA and STAR show that DiffGround consistently outperforms existing zero-shot and supervised methods, establishing a new state of the art for Video Question Grounding.
  </details>

- **[From Label Priors to Task Evidence: Efficient Long-Video Frame Selection via Bayesian GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25418)**  `arXiv:2610.25418`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuochen Chang, Bingjie Gao, Qingyang Liu, Qianli Ma, Yibo Miao, Haonan Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) face significant scalability challenges in long-video understanding due to quadratic attention complexity and context window constraints. Existing retrieval-based approaches typically rely on supervised learning from static keyframe annotations. However, these label priors often fail to align with the actual information required by downstream MLLMs for complex reasoning, creating a misalignment between the training target and inference needs. To bridge this gap, we propose a probabilistic framework that reformulates frame selection as latent-variable inference. We first initialize a lightweight policy using supervised learning to capture general visual saliency. Then, we treat a frozen MLLM as a task environment and use its answer likelihood as dense task evidence to refine the policy via Bayesian Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Within this formulation, the negative log-likelihood (NLL) over ground-truth answer tokens provides a principled dense reward, while a prior-matching KL term preserves semantic consistency with the elicited label prior. This process transitions the selection principle from static label priors to task evidence. Extensive experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench demonstrate that our selector achieves competitive accuracy with MLLM-based scorers while retaining the inference efficiency of lightweight methods. The selector is trained once with a frozen MLLM environment and transfers directly to multiple downstream MLLMs without retraining. Code is available at \url{this http URL}.
  </details>

- **[MotionShift: Environment-Compatible Motion Transfer via Planner-Evaluator Synergistic Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25290)**  `arXiv:2610.25290`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yanchen Liu, Junyao Gao, Yanan Sun, Wensheng Chen, Guangming Lu, Wenjie Pei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing motion transfer methods excel at exact, rigid motion replication from reference videos to target scenes, whereas they often fail to account for the compatibility between the transferred motion and the target scene environment in accordance with physical laws. In this work, we propose MotionShift, a novel motion transfer framework that achieves high-fidelity motion transfer while ensuring physical compatibility with the target scene. In particular, we design a planner--evaluator iterative motion adaptation mechanism, in which a Large Language Model (LLM) acts as the planner to generate motion trajectories by leveraging its strong semantic reasoning capabilities, while a Vision-Language Model (VLM) serves as the evaluator to rectify the motion based on its superior visual perception and scene understanding. Through this planner--evaluator synergistic reasoning process, the motion is adapted to the target scene in a training-free manner. The adapted motion is then used as a generative condition for an image-to-video (I2V) model to render videos in the target scene. Extensive experiments demonstrate that MotionShift achieves high-fidelity and physically compatible motion transfer across diverse and complex scenes. Moreover, we show that MotionShift can be readily integrated into existing trajectory-conditioned I2V models, endowing them with realistic motion transfer capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Visual Scaffolding: Toward Native Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25247)**  `arXiv:2610.25247`  `cs.CV`  
  _Run Shao, Zhaoyang Zhang, Ziyu Li, Linrui Xu, Ji Qi, Xiu Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The success of chain-of-thought (CoT) in complex reasoning can be attributed to a textual scaffolding effect: explicit intermediate results can be examined, reused, and composed to support subsequent reasoning, much as scaffolding supports a building under construction. However, mainstream multimodal LLMs lack an equivalent scaffold for visual distinctions that are difficult to express in concise verbal descriptions. We therefore ask: Can native visual representations become first-class citizens alongside language, providing a visual scaffold for reasoning? We define native multimodal reasoning as reasoning in which native visual representations and language jointly carry reusable intermediate states. As a concrete implementation, the visual scaffolding mechanism we proposed lets the MLLM autonomously initiate a retrieval action at any reasoning step. A lightweight retriever selects native visual features, which are then incorporated directly into the evolving CoT and remain available to subsequent steps. To evaluate this mechanism under controlled visual demands, we introduce NMR-Bench, comprising Burden Counting and Path Tracing. With 88M added parameters (approximately 2%) on Qwen3-VL-4B-Thinking, our model achieves 66.43% and 72.50% accuracy, respectively, surpassing the evaluated GPT-5.5 and Gemini 3.1 Pro baselines. Controlled interventions show that changing selected visual content affects task accuracy and can redirect subsequent visual selection even when intervening text is held fixed. These findings show that native visual states can function as intermediate reasoning material and point toward reusable scaffolds for additional modalities.
  </details>

- **[Goal Images Are Control Targets: Spatially-Grounded Goal-Image Synthesis for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24885)**  `arXiv:2610.24885`  `cs.CV`  
  _Riddhi Chatterjee, Ritish Shrirao, Kushagra Mehrotra, Viswanath Gopalakrishnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Goal-conditioned manipulation policies are steered by a goal image, a picture of the desired post-manipulation scene synthesized from the current observation and a language instruction. The prevailing assumption is that better image-editing quality yields better goals, so goal generators are built and compared using general image-editing metrics. We show this assumption is false. Across two independent goal-conditioned policies on CALVIN, the image editors that top aggregate editing-quality leaderboards (Gemini 3 Pro Image and GPT Image 1.5) produce worse control targets than our proposed far smaller finetuned open models, and the gap widens sharply for the multi-step (long-horizon) tasks robots actually face. A per-dimension analysis explains why. Downstream policy success is governed almost entirely by manipulation adherence (selecting the right object and the right final state; Spearman $\rho \approx 0.9$ with success), while photorealism and aesthetic fidelity, precisely where large proprietary editors excel, are uncorrelated with success ($\rho \approx 0$). This dissociation holds on both CALVIN and the real-world-aligned SIMPLER-Bridge benchmark. We then present SGE-Goal, a spatially-grounded decomposed editing framework that predicts initial and final 2D edit-region masks before goal-image synthesis. SGE-Goal turns open base models (FLUX.1 Kontext, FLUX.2 klein) into the strongest goal generators we evaluate on CALVIN, and second only to Gemini 3 Pro Image on SIMPLER-Bridge. Base models fine-tuned with SGE-Goal also hold up under chaining far better than the best proprietary editor, sustaining roughly seven times the success rate at the longest (five-step) chains. We release code, datasets, checkpoints, and a human-aligned diagnostic evaluator. Our results argue that goal generators must be selected by downstream utility, not by editing-quality leaderboards.
  </details>

- **[VTF900: A 900-Subject Visible-Thermal Face Recognition Dataset and Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24832)**  `arXiv:2610.24832`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jacob Abaare, Bianca Ianetta, Cedric Nimpa Fondje, Shuowen Hu, Benjamin S. Riggan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite advancements in visible-thermal face recognition, where thermal probe (query) face images are matched against visible enrollment templates, existing benchmarks are currently limited by small gallery sizes (N $\approx$ 100 subjects). This limitation inhibits the ability to accurately evaluate visible-thermal facial recognition at scale and to report significant recognition performance metrics, which have better operational relevance. Therefore, we introduce VTF900, a large-scale visible-thermal face recognition benchmark with 900 subjects and paired imagery that includes expression, pose, and occlusion variations. VTF900 is designed to support large-scale (i.e., large gallery) evaluations across verification (1:1 matching) and closed-set identification (1:N matching) for visible-thermal face recognition. Furthermore, VTF900 specifically addresses the challenging combination of cross-spectral and cross-dataset analysis. In this paper, after training models on the ARL-VTF dataset, we provide benchmark analyses across convolutional (baseline), vision transformers, and domain-adaptive neural network architectures to assess how these methods scale. At full gallery scale (N = 900), transformer models achieve strong discriminative performance under frontal conditions (e.g., ViT-B: 97.68% AUC, 51.10% TAR@1%FAR), while domain-adaptive methods such as DPIT-ResNet50 demonstrate greater robustness under off-pose conditions (94.68%AUC, 36.08% TAR@1%FAR). Our results show that high AUC at small scale does not translate to reliable performance at deployment scale, and that pose variation significantly amplifies performance degradation. VTF900 exposes these limitations, enabling more rigorous evaluation of cross-spectral face recognition systems.
  </details>

- **[Design Anything, Design at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24771)**  `arXiv:2610.24771`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xuchao Ren, Siyuan Feng, Jing Huang, Yanhua Yao, Jing Wang, Lingyu Alina Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As multimodal resources are transformed into media outputs, agentic AI is expected to stay aligned with design intent over long horizons. Designers follow briefs specifying goals, constraints, and deliverables, with later work building on earlier decisions. Recent work reuses design experience across tasks, yet alignment among artifacts within one project remains challenging. Agents therefore need to preserve and apply this intent across domains and as projects grow. We introduce Design Anything, a graph-based harness that preserves design intent outside the model and binds deliverables to the workflow steps that produce them through an intent-execution graph. A task contract retains constraints and decisions, selected designs become explicit inputs to later generations, and a deterministic controller uses bindings and execution records to check output types and counts, identify outstanding deliverables, and guide subsequent turns. We also introduce DesignAnything-Bench, with 228 tasks across 19 design domains, including 38 project families at scales of 6, 14, and 24 artifacts. With the LLM held fixed, Design Anything satisfies 85.0% and 92.5% of the brief's factual constraints with DeepSeek V4.1 Flash High and GPT-5.6 Sol High, respectively, exceeding the strongest same-model baselines by 15.4% and 14.4%. These gains reach 25.1% and 23.0% on 24-artifact tasks. Under each model, Design Anything leads or ties in constraint satisfaction in 18 of 19 domains. Professional designers also prefer its complete deliverable sets over those of same-model baselines in a blind evaluation.
  </details>

- **[VisConf: Quality-Aware Consensus for Best-of-N in Vision-Language Model Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24526)**  `arXiv:2610.24526`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muhammad Usman, Muhammad Qasim Ayub, Usman Ahad, Muhammad Tahir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Best-of-N methods improve text-only LLM reasoning by sampling multiple trajectories before selecting an answer. For Vision-Language Models (VLMs), answer frequency and model confidence alone can be insufficient, as sampled generations also vary in their engagement with the visual input. We find that distributional confidence and visual attention are complementary indicators of rollout quality. Each is limited on its own, while trajectories that show strong signals in both are more likely to be correct. Motivated by this, we introduce VisConf, a rollout-quality score that multiplicatively combines visual engagement with candidate-calibrated Self-Certainty, penalizing rollouts that are weak in either signal. We incorporate Rank-Weighted Consensus (RWC), a tuning-free aggregation rule that converts within-question VisConf ranks into answer-level vote weights. Across three VLMs on MathVista, MMMU-Pro, and MMStar, VisConf with RWC consistently outperforms strong BoN baselines. Our analysis shows particularly large gains when correct trajectories are present but underrepresented in the candidate pool.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchical and Query-Aware Visual Token Pruning for High-Resolution LVLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24102)**  `arXiv:2610.24102`  `cs.CV`  
  _Luther Wei, Ling Zou, Kang-Yang Huang, Yu-Syuan Xu, Wen-Huang Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-resolution Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) preserve critical, fine-grained visual details by encoding a global thumbnail alongside multiple local crops. However, the resulting thousands of vision tokens introduce severe computational bottlenecks during Large Language Model (LLM) inference. Existing token pruning methods rely primarily on query-agnostic visual saliency, while current query-aware approaches either operate at a coarse crop-level resolution or compute token relevance too late, after vision tokens have already entered the heavy LLM layers. To address this, we propose a training-free, hierarchical token pruning framework that utilizes query guidance prior to executing any LLM blocks. Our top-down hierarchy first operates at the instance-level, dynamically scaling the vision token retention ratio of each sample based on its visual complexity. Next, at the crop-level, we distribute this sample budget across local crops using sentence-level cross-modal similarity. Finally, at the token-level, we exploit projected vision tokens alongside textual query token embeddings in the shared space to measure fine-grained alignment, applying a text-debiasing normalization to produce high-fidelity query scores. These query-guided budgets and scores are fused with local and global visual saliency metrics to select the most critical vision tokens. Extensive experiments on LLaVA-NeXT-7B demonstrate that our plug-and-play framework retains 98.0% and 94.8% of unpruned performance at 25% and 10% vision token retention, respectively. Notably, at 25% retention, our method delivers a 2.0x inference throughput speedup and even surpasses the unpruned baseline on POPE by 0.7 points.
  </details>

- **[Single-Pass Decision Scores Enable Rapid Classification with Adjustable Thresholds in Medical Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23988)**  `arXiv:2610.23988`  `cs.CV`  
  _Patrick Wienholt, Frederik Hauke, Christiane Kuhl, Jakob Nikolas Kather, Sven Nebelung, Daniel Truhn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) answer binary clinical questions with a yes or no token, leaving the decision threshold implicit and fixed. We derive a continuous score from a single forward pass by renormalizing the probabilities of the yes and no tokens, allowing the threshold to be adapted to clinical needs without retraining. We evaluated this approach with MedGemma and Gemma-3 (4B and 27B) on six medical imaging datasets. The default threshold was frequently misaligned with the task: for identifying normal chest radiographs, MedGemma-27B labeled every image as abnormal, whereas a validation-selected threshold yielded a sensitivity of 0.945 and a specificity of 0.696. Normalization achieved the highest AUROC on four of six datasets (CheXpert: 0.819 vs. 0.777 for raw and 0.743 for text-elicited probabilities), and a single decision required 53-79 ms on one GPU. This simple post-hoc method turns medical MLLMs into fast classifiers with adjustable operating points. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[BlenderWorld: Benchmarking Visual Agents for 3D Creation from Real-World References](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23986)**  `arXiv:2610.23986`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinyu Fang, Yuchi Wang, Weihao Yu, Rongyao Fang, Zhaohai Li, Zhixun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled them to act as autonomous agents, creating and editing 3D models via code or direct software manipulation. In practical workflows, these models must infer object shapes, materials, spatial layouts, and lighting from diverse references while strictly following instructions to reconstruct, modify, or design 3D content. However, existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of such open-ended requests across real-world scenarios. To bridge this gap, we introduce BlenderWorld, the first comprehensive benchmark dedicated to evaluating frontier models on realistic 3D creation tasks. BlenderWorld comprises 223 tasks spanning architecture, products, natural environments, and scientific visualization, driven by text, image, video, and structured specification inputs. To rigorously assess these complex deliverables, we establish a unified evaluation protocol: we render the submitted Blender projects from multiple viewpoints and evaluate them against 2,651 task-specific binary rubrics alongside holistic visual quality scores. Benchmarking thirteen frontier models reveals that GPT-6-Astra achieves the highest overall fused score of 79.9, followed by Opus-5 (73.0) and GPT-5.6-Sol (67.7). Ultimately, our findings reveal a critical bottleneck: while current models can parse broad instructions, they still profoundly struggle with fine-grained 3D spatial coherence and accurate structural reproduction in Blender, leaving substantial headroom for future 3D-native agentics.
  </details>

- **[Grounding Confusable Movie Moments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23910)**  `arXiv:2610.23910`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dong-Hee Kim, Seonwoo Choi, Changbeen Kim, Juyeon Ko, Youngju Choi, Il Hyeon Mun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Characters, locations, and actions recur throughout a movie, so many of its moments differ only in fine-grained details. Grounding such a moment in a full-length movie requires resolving that detail rather than matching the attributes it shares with other moments, yet existing temporal movie grounding benchmarks rarely test this. We introduce CoM$^2$-Bench, a benchmark of confusable query pairs, where the two queries in each pair share most attributes but differ in a single distinguishing detail within a full movie, posing a challenge for recent temporal grounding approaches. To locate where grounding breaks down, we analyze a retrieve-then-localize pipeline, in which a retriever first selects a short candidate chunk and a localizer then performs temporal grounding within it. Separating the two stages lets us attribute each failure to either retrieval or localization. We find that retrieval is the major bottleneck: state-of-the-art retrievers often select a moment's confusable counterpart, because their choices follow the content the two moments share rather than the detail that tells them apart. To address these failures, we propose a simple, training-free remedy, Agentic Window Search (AWS): an MLLM agent $\textit{Decomposes}$ the query into attribute-level sub-queries, then $\textit{Inspects}$ each candidate window against them and $\textit{Expands}$ the window when evidence is cut off at its edge. AWS works with existing retrievers and localizers without additional training, raising mIoU from 18.5 to 26.8.
  </details>

- **[Xiaomi-Lens-0 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23279)**  `arXiv:2610.23279`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pei Fu, Yike Liu, Yue Sun, Hanmo Wang, Xiuwen Xi, Ruoceng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) answer questions about whole images well, yet they still struggle with fine-grained region understanding. A key reason is that text-only supervision never specifies which regions support an answer. To close this gap, we present Xiaomi-Lens-0, a 4B MLLM built on Qwen3.5 that predicts masks from its own visual representations. Specifically, a lightweight decoder combines vision-encoder patch features with the language model's mask-token states, termed visual latents, so that mask supervision reaches the shared backbone without an additional segmentation encoder. Because each latent decodes to a mask, the model can interleave reasoning with region-specific latents and thus think with visual latents, while grounded reasoning traces supervise the intermediate regions referenced during chain-of-thought. As a result, across all eight RefCOCO, RefCOCO+, and RefCOCOg splits, Xiaomi-Lens-0 gains 5.6 points on average over Qwen3.5 in referring comprehension and achieves the highest segmentation cIoU among compared models. Moreover, it surpasses Qwen3.5 across all perception benchmarks.Homepage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[LED-BENCH: A Benchmark for Large-format Engineering Drawing Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19571)**  `arXiv:2610.19571`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ke Li, Tianyi Wang, Enning You, Chaoqun Wan, Zhen Huang, Shuxian Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world engineering drawings are often large-format and information-dense. Answering practical engineering questions therefore requires both fine-grained understanding of engineering information and complex reasoning over objects, relations, and evidence distributed across different regions or views. Existing benchmarks cover a range of engineering-drawing understanding tasks involving local regions, individual views, specific elements, or more constrained visual settings. However, systematic evaluation of understanding over realistic, large-format engineering drawings remains limited. To address this gap, we introduce LED-BENCH, a multidisciplinary benchmark designed to evaluate fine-grained understanding and complex reasoning over large-format engineering drawings, spanning the mechanical, architectural, and transportation domains. Extensive evaluation of 11 representative multimodal large language models, including 8 proprietary models and 3 open-source models, reveals substantial remaining difficulty: GPT-5.6 Sol achieves 52.65% average accuracy, performance varies across engineering domains, and all evaluated models perform worse on questions requiring broader evidence integration. These results highlight fine-grained understanding, evidence integration, and complex reasoning over large-format engineering drawings as persistent challenges for current multimodal models.
  </details>

- **[Bifocal: Training-Free Mixed Granularity Visual Token Pruning for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18399)**  `arXiv:2610.18399`  `cs.CV`  
  _Peiran Yin, Hao Wu, Zihao Yang, Fengyu Zhou, Yijuan Liang, Haozhe Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual token pruning for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) appears highly effective, with existing methods retaining over 95% of unpruned performance on common VQA benchmarks using a quarter of the visual tokens or fewer. However, our extensive evaluation of ten methods across three MLLM families and five ability groups reveals a critical gap: strong general performance often comes at the cost of severe degradation on fine-grained tasks such as OCR and visual grounding. We trace these failures to two limitations of the prevailing paradigm of retaining the highest-ranked native patches. A single importance ranking overlooks the complementary evidence required by different tasks, while sparse patch selection disrupts both image content and spatial integrity. To address these limitations, we propose Bifocal, a training-free, two-stage pruning method built on a mixed-granularity framework. It allocates separate budgets to query-focused and image-intrinsic evidence, complemented by a pooled coarse representation of the entire image. Before the LLM, Bifocal matches question words to visual regions through a soft conjunction balanced against norm-debiased image saliency, while selecting image-intrinsic evidence to maximize representational coverage. Inside the LLM, it further prunes tokens using density-adjusted text-to-image attention. With roughly a quarter of the original visual tokens, Bifocal is the only evaluated method to retain over 90% of unpruned performance in every ability group across all three models, and ranks first on average in all nine model-budget settings. The code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Omni-RS: Unifying Multi-Task Understanding and Generation in Remote Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18323)**  `arXiv:2610.18323`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pan Yin, Shuai Yang, Yuanhao Liu, Datao Tang, Jialin Li, Hao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Practical remote sensing applications often require both understanding and generation capabilities for the same geographic scene. However, most existing methods address either understanding or generation in isolation, while existing unified remote sensing models' generative ability is limited to text-to-image. To bridge this gap, we propose Omni-RS, a unified framework supporting four remote sensing understanding tasks and five generation tasks. Specifically, we introduce the "Scene-Guided Two-Stage Training" strategy, which first adapts a multimodal large language model to remote sensing understanding and then uses its hidden states to guide a diffusion transformer for image generation. To enable joint learning across diverse generation tasks, we construct UniGen, a unified multi-task generation dataset, by organizing heterogeneous remote sensing data into a unified format. Furthermore, we introduce "Detailed Visual Conditioning", which uses visual conditions encoded by a VAE to guide the generation process, thereby supporting additional generation tasks that require reference images. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Omni-RS performs exceptionally well in understanding tasks and has achieved outstanding results in a variety of generative tasks.
  </details>

- **[DIGS: Distribution-Informed Gaussian Splatting for Training-Free Open-Vocabulary 3D Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16177)**  `arXiv:2610.16177`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhifan Zeng, Wenbo Xiao, Arcot Sowmya, Changming Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-vocabulary 3D Gaussian segmentation typically binds semantic features to the per-Gaussian state via per-scene gradient training (1-4 h) or the more recent closed-form distillation. Both commit each primitive to a single feature; therefore, boundary Gaussians spanning multiple 2D instances are forced into a hard decision that degrades retrieval accuracy and yields unstable mask boundaries at render time. We present DIGS, a training-free framework with two stages, class-agnostic mapping and multi-modal encoding, that decouples instance discovery from linguistic naming. At the Gaussian level, our distribution-informed representation maintains an explicit top-$M$ label distribution per primitive, updated by a tempered Dirichlet rule; preservation of multi-peaked label distributions at boundary primitives allows a late-argmax renderer to recover clean instance boundaries. A bidirectional state machine aggregates these distributions into a persistent class-agnostic instance ontology. The instance-level encoding stage then fuses the CLIP visual embedding of each instance's isolated 3D footprint with the CLIP-text embedding of an LLM-generated attribute description, disambiguating visually similar instances. On a single A100, DIGS completes mapping and instance encoding on LERF-OVS in 1.0-3.2 min per scene, depending on whether LLM fusion is enabled, and serves native 3D queries in 5.4 ms. It reaches 71.95% mean mIoU on LERF-OVS, outperforming both the training-free SFS and training-based LaGa baselines. DIGS also achieves the highest mean mIoU on LERF-Mask and 3D-OVS and surpasses Gaga and ObjectGS on Replica under their respective evaluation protocols. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Image Bitstream Fine-grained Understanding for Privacy-Friendly AIoT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08414)**  `arXiv:2610.08414`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhen Yu, Wenyang Liu, Kejun Wu, Chengwang Xiao, Renjie Qiao, Chengtao Cai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image Bitstream Fine-grained Understanding (IBFU) aims to directly perform fine-grained classification and semantic description generation from encoded image byte sequences. In contrast to conventional pixel-domain visual understanding, IBFU conducts semantic analysis without fully decoding images into the pixel domain. Since pixel-level visual content is not explicitly reconstructed during inference, this paradigm reduces visual exposure within the processing pipeline and suits privacy-friendly Artificial Intelligence of Things (AIoT) applications. In this paper, we propose Bitstream Fine-grained Generator (BFG), a novel foundation model tailored for IBFU. BFG consists of two main components: a Bitstream Semantic Encoder (BSeE) and a Fine-grained Semantic Generator (FSeG). BSeE directly models semantic representations from encoded image bitstreams without explicit pixel reconstruction, while FSeG transforms the extracted bitstream semantics into detailed natural-language descriptions through autoregressive generation. To train BFG and comprehensively evaluate IBFU in practical AIoT scenarios, where image bitstreams may suffer corruption during transmission and storage, we construct a large-scale Corrupted-bitstream Fine-grained Understanding dataset (CFU-D), containing both intact bitstreams and corrupted variants across multiple corruption types and severity levels. Experiments show that BFG maintains stable fine-grained caption generation under bitstream corruption. For example, the performance only has slight change from 0.6339 to 0.6077 in terms of average CIDEr score on Stanford Dogs Caption dataset, while vision-language models, such as Qwen-VL-Chat, BLIP-2, GLM, Gemini, and GPT suffer severe performance decrease. This paper provides a practical paradigm for privacy-friendly fine-grained understanding in AIoT.
  </details>

- **[Caption Bottleneck Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00578)**  `arXiv:2607.00578`  `cs.CV`  
  _Seref Baris Cagliyan, Umut Ozdemir, Merve Tapli, Emre Akbas_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) provide interpretability by routing predictions through a layer of human-understandable concepts. However, defining an optimal concept set for a specific dataset remains an open challenge. Existing approaches rely on expensive expert annotations or LLM-generated lists based solely on class names. Even "open-vocabulary" variants typically depend on static concept sets, which restrict discovery and introduce label bias. Furthermore, traditional CBMs often suffer from information leakage, where unmodeled visual features bypass the bottleneck and compromise the integrity of the explanations. To overcome these limitations, we propose Caption Bottleneck Models (CaBM), a framework that circumvents the need for predefined concept sets by replacing rigid concept layers with free-form natural language. By representing images via LMM-generated captions and training a classifier strictly on this text, CaBM ensures a leakage-free architecture by construction. Additionally, by analyzing the text classifier post-training, CaBM autonomously discovers high-quality, dataset-specific concepts. Our results across fine- and coarse-grained benchmarks demonstrate that CaBM achieves competitive accuracy while preserving interpretability without the constraints of external dictionaries or manual labeling.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Together: Multi-Robot Cooperative Egocentric Spatial Reasoning with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18431)**  `arXiv:2605.18431`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kunyu Peng, Zhikun Zhou, Kailun Yang, Di Wen, Ruiping Liu, Yufan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial progress in egocentric video understanding, but their ability to reason cooperatively from multiple embodied viewpoints remains largely unexplored. We study this problem through multi-robot cooperative dynamic spatial reasoning, where a model must answer spatial, temporal, visibility, and coordination questions by integrating synchronized egocentric videos from a team of moving robots. To support this setting, we introduce CoopSR, the first benchmark for this task, together with EgoTeam, a multi-robot egocentric QA dataset. EgoTeam contains 114,227 QA pairs spanning 19 question types, four difficulty tiers, and three team sizes in Habitat and iGibson, along with a real-world test set of around 2,326 QAs collected using two quadruped robots. We further propose SG-CoR (Spectral and Geometry-Aware Cooperative Reasoner), an MLLM framework for fine-grained cooperative spatial reasoning. SG-CoR combines dynamics-aware multi-robot frame sampling, spectral- and geometric-guided view fusion, and geometry- and motion-informed prompt distillation, enabling the model to benefit from privileged robot-pose supervision during training while requiring only egocentric videos at test time. Across 22 MLLM baselines, SG-CoR consistently improves cooperative reasoning, outperforming the strongest fine-tuned baseline by +3.87% on Habitat and +7.12% on iGibson. It also shows stronger generalization to unseen team sizes and real-world robot tests. Code can be found atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Test-Time Hinting for Black-Box Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16410)**  `arXiv:2605.16410`  `cs.CV`  
  _Kaihua Hou, Abhijith Varma Mudunuri, Jiaxing Qiu, Roxana Daneshjou, Thomas Hartvigsen, Ahmed Alaa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling (TTS) methods have proven highly effective for LLMs, yet their application to vision-language models (VLMs) remains relatively underexplored. Existing VLM TTS methods largely require open-weight model access or expensive repeated sampling, and are evaluated primarily on multimodal mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks rather than natural-image understanding. In this paper, we propose Test-Time Hinting, a method that improves VLM performance with a single call to the target VLM and requires only black-box API access, which makes it broadly applicable to frontier closed-weight models. Our method is motivated by the observation that VLM errors tend to cluster around recurring failure patterns that are shared across models and predictable from the input alone. We therefore train a lightweight hint generator model to predict, for a given test input, which "hint" should be prepended to the prompt, providing targeted contextual or procedural guidance that steers the VLM away from its characteristic failure modes without revealing the answer. We show that Test-Time Hinting significantly improves the accuracy of multiple closed-weight VLMs on natural-image VQA benchmarks and that these gains generalize to unseen benchmarks and VLMs without retraining the hint generator.
  </details>

- **[Training Long-Context Vision-Language Models Effectively with Generalization Beyond 128K Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13831)**  `arXiv:2605.13831`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaowei Wang, Lishu Luo, Haodong Duan, Weiwei Liu, Sijin Wu, Ji Luo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context modeling is becoming a core capability of modern large vision-language models (LVLMs), enabling sustained context management across long-document understanding, video analysis, and multi-turn tool use in long-horizon agents. Yet practical training recipes remain insufficiently explored, particularly for designing and balancing long-context data mixtures. In this work, we present a systematic study of long-context continued pre-training (LongPT) for LVLMs, extending a 7B model from 32K to 128K context with extensive ablations on long-document data. We first show that long-document VQA is substantially more effective than OCR transcription. Building on this observation, our ablations further yield three key findings: i) for sequence-length distribution, balanced data outperforms target-length-focused data (e.g., 128K), suggesting that long-context ability requires generalizable key-information retrieval across various lengths and positions; ii) retrieval remains the primary bottleneck, favoring retrieval-heavy mixtures with modest reasoning data for task diversity; iii) pure long-document VQA largely preserves short-context capabilities, suggesting that instruction-formatted long data reduces the need for short-data mixing. Based on these findings, we introduce MMProLong, obtained by long-context continued pre-training from Qwen2.5-VL-7B with only a 5B-token budget. MMProLong improves long-document VQA scores by 7.11% and maintains strong performance at 256K and 512K contexts beyond its 128K training window, without additional training. It further generalizes to webpage-based multimodal needle retrieval, long-context vision-text compression, and long-video understanding without task-specific supervision. Overall, our study establishes a practical LongPT recipe and an empirical foundation for advancing long-context vision-language models.
  </details>

- **[CoLVR: Enhancing Exploratory Latent Visual Reasoning via Contrastive Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08802)**  `arXiv:2605.08802`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ziyang Ding, Linjian Meng, Yiming Wu, Yuhan Li, Yuhao Liu, Zhen Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Due to the potential for exploratory reasoning of Latent Visual Reasoning, recent works tend to enable MLLMs (Multimodal Large Language Models) to perform visual reasoning by propagating continuous hidden states instead of decoding intermediate steps into discrete tokens. However, existing works typically rely on hard alignment objectives to force latent representations to match predefined visual features, thereby severely limiting the exploratory of latent reasoning process. To address this problem, we propose CoLVR (Contrastive Optimization for Latent Visual Reasoning). To obtain a more exploratory visual reasoning, CoLVR introduces a latent contrastive training framework. Firstly, CoLVR learns diverse and exploratory representations with a latent contrastive objective guided by angle-based perturbation, which expands the semantic latent space and avoids over-constrained embedding. Then, CoLVR employs a latent trajectory contrastive reward for RL (Reinforcement Learning) post-training to enable fine-grained optimization of latent visual reasoning process and thus fostering diverse reasoning behaviors. Experiments demonstrate that CoLVR significantly enhances the exploratory capability of latent representations, achieving average improvements of 5.83% on VSP and 8.00% on Jigsaw, while also outperforming existing latent models on out of domain benchmarks, with a 3.40% gain on MMStar. The data, codes, and models are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fundus-R1: Training a Fundus-Reading MLLM on Public Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08322)**  `arXiv:2604.08322`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchuan Deng, Qijie Wei, Kaiheng Qian, Jiazhen Liu, Bangxiang Lan, Qingwei Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fundus imaging such as CFP, OCT and UWF is crucial for the early detection of retinal anomalies and diseases. Fundus image understanding, due to its knowledge-intensive nature, poses a challenging vision-language task. An emerging approach to addressing the task is to post-train a generic multimodal large language model (MLLM), either by supervised finetuning (SFT) or by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), on a considerable amount of in-house samples paired with high-quality clinical reports. However, these valuable samples are not publicly accessible, which not only hinders reproducibility but also practically limits research to few players. To overcome the barrier, we make a novel attempt to train a reasoning-enhanced fundus-reading MLLM, which we term Fundus-R1, using exclusively public datasets, wherein over 94\% of the data are annotated with only image-level labels. Our technical contributions are two-fold. First, we propose a RAG-based method for composing image-specific, knowledge-aware reasoning traces. Such auto-generated traces link visual findings identified by a generic MLLM to the image labels in terms of ophthalmic knowledge. Second, we enhance RLVR with a process reward that encourages self-consistency of the generated reasoning trace in each rollout. Extensive experiments on three fundus-reading benchmarks, i.e., FunBench, Omni-Fundus and GMAI-Fundus, show that Fundus-R1 clearly outperforms multiple baselines, including its generic counterpart (Qwen2.5-VL) and a stronger edition post-trained without using the generated traces. This work paves the way for training powerful fundus-reading MLLMs with publicly available data.
  </details>

- **[On the Nature of Attention Sink that Shapes Decoding Strategy in Omni-LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14337)**  `arXiv:2603.14337`  `cs.CV`  
  _Suho Yoo, Youngjoon Jang, Joon Son Chung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The goal of this paper is to strengthen the reasoning of Omnimodal Large Language Models (Omni-LLMs) at inference time, without additional training. These models jointly process video, audio, and text, and given the large number of tokens they consume, how attention is routed across them is central to their behaviour. We focus specifically on attention sinks, tokens that absorb a disproportionate share of attention mass regardless of their semantic content, to understand how this routing unfolds. To this end, we conduct a systematic analysis of sink behaviour in Omni-LLMs. Our analysis yields two key findings: (i) high sink attention does not solely indicate head redundancy, suggesting that sink value representations play additional functional roles; (ii) the sink value vector acts as a shared bias added to every token's output, serving as a global signal that organises the representation as a whole. Building on this, we propose OutRo, which correspondingly aligns non-sink token representations with the sink in feature space, and relaxes the causal mask for sink tokens at an early layer to sharpen this bias before the rest of decoding proceeds. This design enhances the reasoning process without requiring additional forward passes or access to attention maps. Based on extensive experiments, OutRo consistently improves performance on seven video QA benchmarks and demonstrates strong generalisation, while incurring only a 1.1x decoding overhead.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Find Their Way in a City? Exploring Emergent Navigation from Web-Scale Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.15933)**  `arXiv:2512.15933`  `cs.CV`  
  _Dwip Dalal, Utkarsh Mishra, Narendra Ahuja, Nebojsa Jojic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Leveraging multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to develop embodied agents offers significant promise for addressing complex real-world tasks. However, current evaluation benchmarks remain predominantly language-centric or heavily reliant on simulated environments, rarely probing the nuanced, knowledge-intensive reasoning essential for practical, real-world scenarios. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce the task of Sparsely Grounded Visual Navigation, explicitly designed to evaluate the sequential decision-making abilities of MLLMs in challenging, knowledge-intensive real-world environment. We operationalize this task with CityNav, a comprehensive benchmark encompassing four diverse global cities, specifically constructed to assess raw MLLM-driven agents in city navigation. Agents are required to rely solely on visual inputs and internal multimodal reasoning to sequentially navigate 50+ decision points without additional environmental annotations or specialized architectural modifications. Crucially, agents must autonomously achieve localization through interpreting city-specific cues and recognizing landmarks, perform spatial reasoning, and strategically plan and execute routes to their destinations. Through extensive evaluations, we demonstrate that current state-of-the-art MLLMs, reasoning techniques (e.g., GEPA, chain-of-thought, reflection) and competitive baseline PReP significantly underperform in this challenging setting. To address this, we propose Verbalization of Path(VoP), which explicitly grounds the agent's internal reasoning by probing city-scale cognitive maps (key landmarks and directions toward the destination) from the MLLM, substantially enhancing navigation success. Project Webpage:this https URL
  </details>

- **[MLLM-Guided Autonomous Object Detection for Open-World Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.10844)**  `arXiv:2507.10844`  `cs.CV`  
  _Furkan Mumcu, Michael J. Jones, Anoop Cherian, Yasin Yilmaz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Object detection typically relies on predefined category vocabularies, while open-world and open-vocabulary approaches remain limited by generic unknown labels or externally specified detection concepts. We propose LAOD, an MLLM-Guided Autonomous Object Detection framework in which a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) generates an image-specific detection vocabulary that is grounded by an open-vocabulary detector. This decomposition separates semantic concept discovery from spatial localization and removes the need to manually enumerate target categories at inference time. We evaluate LAOD using Class-Agnostic Average Precision (CAAP), Semantic Naming Average Precision (SNAP), and Semantic Localization Average Precision (SemLoc-AP), which measure localization, semantic naming, and their correct association, respectively. Experiments on COCO-Val, LVIS-minival, and COCO-OOD across multiple MLLMs and detectors demonstrate strong performance across common, long-tailed, and out-of-distribution objects, while showing that grounding quality remains a major determinant of end-to-end performance. LAOD also substantially outperforms both direct MLLM-based object detection and dedicated models trained specifically for open-ended detection. Overall, the results support a modular formulation of autonomous object detection in which MLLMs determine what to detect and specialized open-vocabulary detectors determine where those concepts occur.
  </details>

- **[MITRA-lexicon: Lexica of attested translations from Sanskrit into English, Tibetan and Chinese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25934)**  `arXiv:2610.25934`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Sebastian Nehrdich, Kengo Harimoto, Sven Sellmer, Oliver Hellwig_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The standard Sanskrit lexica are monumental and indispensable, but they cite their attestations as abbreviated references to editions, without links to the digitized texts now widely available and without a translation of the passage, and they naturally do not reflect translations made after them. To address these gaps, we present MITRA-lexicon, a family of corpus-grounded lexica based on span annotations of translation equivalents with Sanskrit as the pivot: Sanskrit-English, derived from the Mitrasamgraha corpus, as well as Sanskrit-Tibetan and Sanskrit-Chinese, derived from the MITRA-parallel corpus of Buddhist literature. In total, the resource comprises 2.18 million headword entries in six directional lexica. Headwords are lemmas and compounds from silver annotations that follow the Digital Corpus of Sanskrit (DCS). Senses are ranked by frequency, and each of the 10.4 million attestations includes its sentence pair, with character offsets of the word and its equivalent. We propose a multi-step pipeline based on grammatical annotation models, fine-tuned large language models, and a teacher-student setup to produce the alignments. We evaluate the lexica against reference dictionaries and manually corrected gold alignments. We release the lexica and the fine-tuned span annotation model, together with a web interface for looking up headwords and their attested senses.
  </details>

- **[Cognitive Map in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25596)**  `arXiv:2610.25596`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Albert Qin, Jonathan Cohen, Gautam Reddy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  It has long been proposed that animals construct cognitive maps to support flexible behavior in novel environments. We tested whether large language models form similar representations using relational reasoning tasks and causal "mosaic experiments" that manipulate internal activations in open-weight language models. Models beyond the ten-billion-parameter scale successfully inferred unstated relationships between entities and generalized to synthetic entity names. Across model and task families, models generated abstract representations of relational structure and combined them additively with representations of entity identity, binding entities to roles within that structure. Models stored observed relationships as pairwise links that they composed retrospectively when queried, rather than prospectively positioning each entity in a global coordinate system. Binding was mediated by a sparse, distributed set of key--value pairs; by perturbing these pairs, we implanted "false memories" that altered model behavior in predictable ways. Overall, our findings reveal an internal organization analogous to a cognitive map that explains how language models reason with limited information.
  </details>

- **[LastWords: Boring Problems Revisited](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24894)**  `arXiv:2610.24894`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Huayang Li, Richard Sproat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In 2022, the second author published a Last Words piece in Computational Linguistics in which he pointed out that, despite the advances in AI up to that point, there were still some problems in natural language processing that on the one hand seemed trivial and perhaps boring, but on the other were hard to solve using purely neural methods. One such problem discussed in that context was text normalization, and a particular subproblem of that, namely number name reading. The piece came out just a few months before the first release of ChatGPT, and needless to say, a lot has happened since then. Indeed, as we document here, the problem (of number name reading) has practically been solved, at least by one of the latest frontier models. However, we believe that there are still open issues for such limited domain, constrained and limited resource problems, and that in some future machine learning approach, the massive resources of the latest frontier models would not be needed to solve them.
  </details>

- **[Who Gets Unmasked? Sampler-Induced Bias in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24795)**  `arXiv:2610.24795`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xujun Che, Farsheed Haque, Depeng Xu, Xi Niu, Haopeng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (dLLMs) generate by gradually unmasking a canvas, so their samplers decide not only which token to write but also when to commit it. We show that this second decision changes who appears in generated text, a sampler-induced bias on top of the model's own. Common commit rules, including LLaDA's default, commit the positions whose sampled tokens are most probable; this peeking favors majority options. To separate the sampler from the model, we read the model's belief about a fork, such as a protagonist's gender, in one forward pass, and show that this belief matches exact sampling from the model. Measured against it, peeking samplers on LLaDA and Dream amplify occupational gender stereotypes and collapse opinion distributions, and confidence-based ordering shifts gender-balanced occupations toward men, more so under faster parallel decoding. We propose Fork-First Sampling, which decides the fork before anything else is written: it follows the belief or any target distribution, improves fluency, and avoids the stories that remark on the protagonist's gender, which prompt-based fixes produce, at the cost of one forward pass. Fairness evaluations of dLLMs should report the commit rule.
  </details>

- **[Training with Ciphers to Enhance In-Context Terminology Learning For Translation from Rare Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24593)**  `arXiv:2610.24593`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Deevyanshu Malu, Deeptanshu Malu, Sunita Sarawagi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning LLMs with in-context examples (IC-Train) lets translation models adapt to new parallel data at deployment, but it is unclear whether they actually learn the translations of rare terms from those examples. We formalize this capability as in-context term learning (ICTL). We show that standard IC-Train develops this capability only weakly, and propose RareCipherFT, which strengthens it without any lexical resources. RareCipherFT pairs each training target with in-context examples that share a rare term with it. It then replaces that term with a character-shuffled cipher in both the context and the reference, so the term can only be recovered by copying from context. The ciphered fraction of training data is annealed to preserve translation quality. Across seven translation directions between English and Tamil, Assamese, Mizo, Nepali and Hausa, and four models from 1B to 7B parameters, RareCipherFT raises term recall over standard IC-Train by 5-15 absolute points without lowering COMET. Probes using synonyms, nonce words and controls for shared surface context show that these gains come from linking source terms to their in-context translations rather than from shortcuts.
  </details>

- **[BanglaShield: A Script-Robust Safety Classifier and Benchmark for Bangla Native, Romanized, and Code-Mixed Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24288)**  `arXiv:2610.24288`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Mohsinul Kabir, Md. Tariquzzaman, Mohammad Rashidujjaman Rifat, Tharindu Ranasinghe, Nishat Raihan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety classifiers for large language models (LLMs) are typically built and evaluated on standard-script text, yet Bangla, like many lower-resource languages, is routinely written in native script, in Latin-script romanization, and code-mixed with English, Hindi, or Urdu. We introduce BanglaShield, a resource and model for graded prompt-safety classification across these written forms. We consolidate 10,245 Bangla prompts from six sources under a four-level taxonomy (Safe, Offensive, Harmful, Severe) and render each prompt in three aligned forms that share labels and a group-aware split, enabling strictly paired comparisons. Benchmarking six contemporary LLMs, including a Bangla-specialized one, on 36,882 classification queries and 24,318 judged responses, we find that LLM classifiers label 47.0% of unsafe native-script prompts as Safe, rising to 63.4% under romanization, while response-level safety accuracy drops by 10.3 points. Three multilingual guard models also pass 30.3-57.3% of unsafe native-script prompts. Using a model's own classification as an input filter reduces harmful compliance by only about five points. In contrast, BanglaShield, a compact fine-tuned encoder, reaches 87.8 macro-F1 on native-script and 84.4 on romanized prompts, outperforming the best LLM on each form by 42.8-46.1 points with ~130x fewer parameters than Gemma 4 31B. We release the corpus, splits, judgments, and model.
  </details>

- **[Can This LLM Annotate My Data? Estimating Zero-Shot LLM Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24250)**  `arXiv:2610.24250`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Tong Lin, Grant Schoenebeck, Sabina Tomkins_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in annotation tasks with varying success. While several studies have demonstrated the opportunities and shortcomings of LLMs for annotation, a critical question remains unanswered: is it possible to predict how well they will perform on a new dataset? To answer this and related questions, we construct two task families which induce 461 text classification datasets. We find that it is possible to predict LLM performance - to a certain degree - and that contrary to other work which highlights the usefulness of LLM verbalizations of self-confidence, internal log-probabilities are the most predictive signal.
  </details>

- **[Preserving Reasoning Diversity in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards via Adaptive Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24236)**  `arXiv:2610.24236`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Wen-Zhi Li, Sainyam Galhotra, Claire Cardie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) improves the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but often reduces the diversity of their reasoning behaviors. We study this phenomenon from the perspective of finite-sample learning dynamics and show that limited on-policy rollouts can progressively suppress semantically distinct but equally rewarded reasoning modes. Once a mode becomes sufficiently under-sampled, standard on-policy RLVR rarely recovers it. Motivated by this observation, we propose TAR, a Targeted Adaptive Replay framework that preserves diverse correct reasoning behaviors already present in the base policy. TAR constructs a compact memory bank of diverse base-model solutions and selectively replays them through an adaptive mechanism that activates only when stored behaviors are being suppressed during RLVR. Across six mathematical reasoning benchmarks and two Qwen2.5 model scales, TAR consistently achieves a better balance between reasoning performance and diversity preservation than existing diversity-preserving post-training methods, while substantially reducing the forgetting of correct base-model solutions. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Do LMs Know What Variables Do? Interpreting Code Representations via Variable Roles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24118)**  `arXiv:2610.24118`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Célian Vasson, Daniil Ognev, Kentaro Inui, Benjamin Heinzerling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Variable roles describe recurring behaviors such as accumulation, traversal, and retaining the best candidate encountered. We use Sajaniemi's independently defined taxonomy to study which role distinctions can be recovered from large language models, how sparse autoencoders (SAEs) retain those distinctions, and whether associated directions support control of generation. We introduce static annotation rules for all eleven roles and a corpus of 126,926 natural-code files across five languages. Across five model-SAE systems, residual probes outperform individual SAE features and tested lexical baselines, with modest losses after neutral target renaming. Combining SAE features closes much of the single-feature gap, but probes on SAE reconstructions are less sensitive to role-changing edits reviewed by LLMs. High paired ranking accuracy coexists with frequent false positives at fixed thresholds. During generation, interventions often change role annotations at the cost of correctness; changes accompanied by passing tests show no clear aggregate advantage over random controls. The benchmark separates role-related recoverability, SAE fidelity, and correctness-preserving control through matched prediction, source edits, and generation interventions.
  </details>

- **[Lexical Reference Frames: Interpreting LM Residual Streams in Native Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22169)**  `arXiv:2610.22169`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jian Gu, Hongyu Zhang, Chunyang Chen, Aldeida Aleti_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The vocabulary of a language model is its native semantics, and the embedding and unembedding fix the meaning of a state at the two ends of the residual stream. Between them, existing methods reuse a fixed lens or treat states as a point cloud, and neither reads a state as the model does. We argue that every hidden state has a native reference frame, induced by the layers that remain and adaptive to the context. Lexical reference frames approximate it in a model-native and context-aware way, reading a state through the remaining layers with a given number of rounds of attention between positions and a typical attention output for the rest, fitting nothing to the model's output. No lens with fixed directions per layer can reach a mean cosine with the native frame's lexical readouts above the square root of their coherence, 0.30 and 0.35 at mid-depth of SmolLM3-3B and OLMo3-7B. LexFrame[1], of order 1, reaches 0.97 and 0.97, and its readings are on average within 0.16 and 0.15 nats of the model's output. Its coefficient differences attribute predictions more faithfully than attribution patching, and its lexical basis steers predictions while sparing other tokens. On average, its readings settle on the model's top token over the final 0.82 and 0.81 of the layers, those of lenses over at most 0.11. These frames thus make every hidden state readable, attributable and steerable.
  </details>

- **[Dissecting Training-Free Uncertainty Estimation in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22206)**  `arXiv:2609.22206`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Soroush Seifi, Vaggelis Dorovatas, Lin Li, Yarin Gal, Rahaf Aljundi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are increasingly deployed in safety-critical settings, yet estimating when their answers can be trusted remains underexplored. We systematically compare training-free verbalized, token-level and semantic uncertainty estimation methods on twelve MLLMs and five VQA benchmarks. We find that answer length decides which family wins: token-level entropy on short answers, verbalized methods on sentence-length responses, and semantic methods on long-form generation. Token-level entropy becomes far more informative when estimated at a higher temperature than the low ones used to generate answers, and asking a model to judge its own answer becomes more reliable with model size. Building on this, a length-aware recipe that additionally flags responses on which the model abstains outperforms every individual method on average at about a quarter of the cost of semantic methods, although no method attains the coverage required for safe deployment. These results provide practitioners with principled guidance for selecting estimators by answer length and model scale.
  </details>

- **[What Makes a Latent Thought Valid? Defining and Auditing Thought Representations in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.27378)**  `arXiv:2606.27378`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Fahd Seddik, Fatemeh Fard_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning improves the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs), yet it writes steps as text and follows one reasoning path at a time, which limits the capabilities of such models. Latent reasoning methods replace these steps with continuous thought representations and claim that a thought representation substitutes for the written reasoning, compresses trajectories, distinguishes semantic content, and achieves superposition of multiple exploration paths at once. These claims concern the content of the thought representation, whereas the evaluations of these methods rely on downstream task accuracy, token counts, and analyses specific to each method. We formalize these claims as a valid thought representation space and prove that a space is valid if and only if its thought representations attain four properties, namely causality, minimality, separability, and stability, each of which follows from one claim. Translating these properties into four measures and auditing three training-free and three trained methods shows gaps in these claims that accuracy may not necessarily reveal. Training-free methods fail all four properties and do not encode the reasoning they are meant to replace, whereas trained methods generally improve substitution. The four properties and measures therefore provide a theoretically grounded evaluation beyond downstream task accuracy and expose failure modes that may guide future development of latent reasoning methods.
  </details>

- **[REFLEX: Reflective Evolution from LLM Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.16496)**  `arXiv:2606.16496`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Pan Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal language models (MLLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for guiding evolutionary search toward interpretable programmatic policies. In existing program-evolution systems, however, reusable knowledge is usually carried by whole programs in the population, and it is difficult to trace how a visual observation led to a particular code change and its measured outcome. We present REFLEX, a train-free evolutionary framework that links these steps in one loop. A vision-enabled Critic turns task-specific behavioral evidence into a structured diagnosis; the diagnosis retrieves executable code snippets from a persistent Skill Memory; a text-only Actor writes the child program; and the child--parent fitness change updates the utility of each retrieved snippet. Every step is recorded in a single trace. Under matched backends and 100-call budgets over 10 paired seeds, REFLEX reaches the solve threshold in a median of 13, 20, and 24 LLM calls on Acrobot, Pendulum, and Lunar Lander, roughly half the calls required by official MLES and by a compute-matched Actor-only ablation. Frozen Skill Memory banks from Pendulum or Lunar Lander raise the final Acrobot score on all 10 paired seeds. On a 36-dimensional antenna-array design task with equal evaluation budgets and shared initialization, REFLEX reaches the harder $25.25$ score threshold on 9/10 seeds, compared with at most 3/10 for CMA-ES, GA, and PSO. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Language steering in latent space to mitigate unintended code-switching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.13849)**  `arXiv:2510.13849`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Andrey Goncharov, Nikolai Kondusov, Alexey Zaytsev_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) often exhibit hallucinations such as unintended code-switching, reducing reliability in downstream tasks. We propose latent-space language steering, a lightweight inference-time method that identifies language directions via Principal Component Analysis (PCA) on parallel translations and steers token embeddings along these axes to control language identity. Our approach mitigates code-switching (reduced next-token distributional divergence by up to 55\%, reduced Code-Switching Index by 63--99\%) while preserving broad semantic direction with negligible computational overhead and requires only minimal parallel data for calibration. Additionally, we evaluate language classification using a single principal component (96-100\% accuracy) and further analyze the layer-wise evolution of language representations.
  </details>

- **[Measurement Validity and Representation Dynamics of Confidence Signals in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25846)**  `arXiv:2610.25846`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Debojeet Ghosh, Shreyansh Kumar, Ayushman Garg_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Whether a language model "knows what it knows" matters for trustworthy deployment and for deciding when it should abstain. Prior work usually measures this through one channel: verbalized confidence, a linear probe on hidden states, or agreement among sampled answers (semantic entropy), and often treats these as interchangeable. We compare all three in a pre-registered audit of five Qwen2.5 models (0.5B-7B, including a base/instruct pair at 7B) on factual retrieval (PopQA, SimpleQA) and multi-step reasoning (GSM8K, MATH). First, probes trained on prefill states to predict whether sampled answers will disagree become informative earlier for retrieval (about 7% of layer depth on average) than for reasoning (about 28%). The pre-registered five-layer test supported this only marginally; a finer 21-layer follow-up, designed after those results, supports it more clearly. In most retrieval cells, however, a prompt-only TF-IDF classifier matches the probe, so the early signal may reflect surface features of the question. Second, verbalized confidence is unstable: slightly different prompt templates yield scores that barely correlate. Third, Expected Calibration Error is misleading: verbal confidence looks well calibrated because it clusters near the base rate while discriminating poorly, whereas sampling agreement has worse ECE but about twice the resolution. Of five pre-registered hypotheses, two were falsified, one was partly supported, one was not supported, and one could not be tested because the base model's verbal confidence was degenerate.
  </details>

- **[Calibration-Free Early Stopping for Chain-of-Thought Reasoning via E-processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25212)**  `arXiv:2610.25212`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Dong-Jae Lee, Gyojin Han, Junmo Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning large language models (LLMs) benefit from test-time scaling, motivating adaptive policies that spend tokens when they help and stop early otherwise. Existing rules halt when a confidence or stability signal crosses a threshold, but such thresholds rarely transfer across models and datasets, and validation-calibrated rules lose their guarantee under shifts in test-time horizon or signal dynamics. We reframe adaptive stopping as sequential evidence accumulation along the reasoning trajectory rather than threshold calibration on held-out data. Our framework runs two e-processes on a shared per-chunk signal: a commit stream tracks the signal's position relative to its slow trend, and an abstain stream tracks its signed velocity, making the two anti-correlated by construction. Under signal-level nulls on the per-chunk increments, Ville's inequality yields an anytime-valid signal-level false-alarm bound at user-specified significance levels, with no held-out calibration. Across various model--dataset combinations, the framework matches or exceeds calibration-based stopping on the token--accuracy Pareto front using zero calibration data, and the same significance level transfers unchanged across cells where calibration becomes infeasible.
  </details>

- **[TIRE: Top-Down Formula Instantiation with Reliable Execution for Physics Problem Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25134)**  `arXiv:2610.25134`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Wu, Zhaoxin Yu, Xinglin Xiao, Nan Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advancing physics reasoning in large language models requires scalable training data that connect physical laws, concrete problem scenarios, and verifiable numerical answers. Expert-designed problems offer top-down control over physical laws, conditions, and scenarios, but require costly human effort. LLM-based synthesis from texts scales efficiently, but model-generated answers and assessments leave supervision vulnerable to hallucinations. We introduce TIRE, a framework for Top-Down Formula Instantiation with Reliable Execution that brings knowledge-driven problem construction to large-scale data generation. Top-down instantiation uses reusable physical formulas and their applicability conditions to control the physical relations and numerical configurations of generated problems. Reliable execution establishes reference answers before problem generation, providing a common numerical foundation for problem construction and teacher distillation. Together, these mechanisms combine control over problem content with execution-grounded supervision for both supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning. Using TIRE, we construct TIRE-19k, spanning nine subfields of physics. Across 3 backbone LLMs and 4 benchmarks, TIRE-19k outperforms 5 representative datasets with equal amounts of SFT data, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.86 percentage points on average. Further GRPO raises Qwen3-8B's average accuracy by 1.52 percentage points over its TIRE-19k SFT checkpoint. These results demonstrate the value of generating physics training data from explicit physical knowledge with reliable execution.
  </details>

- **[Write Once, Check Many: Reusable Ledgers for Post-Hoc Policy Compliance Auditing of Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24483)**  `arXiv:2610.24483`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ashwin Nagarajan, Gursimran Panesar, Alicia Yang, Mona T. Diab, Virginia Smith, Aashiq Muhamed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auditing LLM agents against policies is critical to ensure safety but requires rereading long trajectories once per policy. Can we instead record each trajectory once and reuse that record for policies not known in advance? We propose write once, check many, a framework in which a writer model converts each agent trajectory into a policy-independent ledger of the agent's actions and outcomes. A reader then checks each new policy against the ledger alone, without access to the original trajectory. On 900 trajectories from SHADE-Arena, $\tau$-bench, and $\tau^2$-bench, we show that a 12B reader of narrative ledgers can either match or in fact achieve higher AUC than the same 12B model reading full trajectories. On SHADE-Arena policies that combine several facts, our approach outperforms a 31B model reading the trajectory (.777 vs. .713 AUC). When checking eight SHADE-Arena policies separately, ledgers use about $5\times$ fewer tokens, including the cost of writing them. A deeper analysis shows that rule-based extraction performs better on some single-action policies, and ledgers can miss evidence in long tool outputs and message contents. Our results suggest that reusable, policy-independent records can make retrospective agent auditing more efficient and effective, and highlight important considerations in ledger design.
  </details>

- **[What Matters for NVFP4 Training? A Comparative Study of Low-Precision Pretraining Recipes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24343)**  `arXiv:2610.24343`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Anjulie Agrusa, Andrei Panferov, Elizabeth Wei, Keith Wyss, Paul Gibbons, Erik Schultheis, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training large language models directly in 4-bit floating-point (FP) formats promises substantial improvements in throughput and energy efficiency. While some recipe design choices have been validated at scale, many promising approaches remain untested beyond small models and short token horizons, leaving open the question of which trends will hold. We present a systematic comparison of recent NVFP4 recipes at medium model scale, evaluating dense 8B and 30B-A3B MoE models trained for up to 1T tokens, with a focus on which ingredients are necessary for accuracy recovery. We propose a final recipe grounded in the principles behind established stable NVFP4 training at scale, incorporating state-of-the-art techniques such as unbiased gradient estimation with lower quantization error than stochastic rounding. To the best of our knowledge, this is the strongest FP4 training result demonstrated at this scale to date in loss gap to BF16. Through ablation studies, we find that: (i) each technique in the optimized recipe measurably improves loss trajectory, (ii) selective high-precision layers are necessary for recovering accuracy at scale, (iii) not all tensors in the backward pass benefit equally from de-biasing, leaving room to apply complementary error-reduction techniques to the remaining tensors.
  </details>

- **[Prefix-Tuning for Arbitrary Output Sequences on Pretrained Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24123)**  `arXiv:2610.24123`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Peter Cho-Ho Lam, Ziyi Wang, Zirui Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prefix-tuning is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method for large language models (LLMs) that steers model outputs by prepending task-specific prefix representations to the input sequence. Despite its practical success, the theoretical understanding of the expressiveness of prefixes remains incomplete. In particular, the following question remains open: Given a pretrained autoregressive Transformer, for any input-output sequence pair $(\mathtt{x},\mathtt{y})$, does there always exist a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ such that prepending $\mathcal{S}$ to $\mathtt{x}$ induces the model to generate the target output $\mathtt{y}$? In this work, we prove that under the assumption that $\mathtt{y}$ is in the range of the pretrained Transformer, such a prefix $\mathcal{S}$ exists and can be explicitly computed by solving multiple linear systems. We further show that this assumption is necessary, in the sense that such a prefix may not exist when the assumption fails, thereby providing a complete answer to the above question. Our analysis is constructive and the obtained theoretical results hold under fairly general assumptions on model architecture. Extensive experiments on GPT-2 and Qwen3-4B demonstrate that our algorithm produces prefixes with strong control over model outputs.
  </details>

- **[Composable Parametric Demographic Injection for Values Survey Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23867)**  `arXiv:2610.23867`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xinyuan Cao, Qingyi Pan, Bangde Du, Yujia Zhou, Hexi Wang, Weihang Su, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as simulated respondents in computational social science, yet accurately reproducing population-specific heterogeneous response distributions remains challenging. Existing methods such as demographic prompting are sensitive to prompt design and increase inference overhead, while parameter-based methods struggle to reuse demographic information efficiently, limiting generalization to unseen combinations. In this regard, we propose PARADE, a composable PARAmetric DEmographic injection framework that represents each demographic attribute value with a reusable and composable LoRA module. Specifically, PARADE separates task-common survey capability from demographic-specific value shifts via a shared Task LoRA and attribute-level Demographic LoRAs, with composition calibration to reduce parameter interference. At inference time, target population models can be constructed on demand by recombining the relevant modules, including demographic combinations not observed during training.
