Extensive experiments on the World Values Survey and SocioBench across three backbone models show that PARADE achieves strong population-level distribution alignment while maintaining competitive predictive performance, reducing KL divergence by up to 52.8% relative to the strongest baseline.
  </details>

- **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561)**  `arXiv:2610.21561`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Sergio Burdisso, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Dairazalia Sánchez-Cortés, Yanis Labrak, Jordi Luque, Milos Cernak, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. While factual hallucination has received extensive attention, a different failure mode has been overlooked: procedural hallucination, in which an agent produces individually fluent and factually plausible utterances while deviating from the intended conversational process. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. We release DialFork under open licenses atthis https URL, hoping the community will measure conversational process alongside content.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating the Robustness of Proof Autoformalization in Lean 4](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14867)**  `arXiv:2606.14867`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengtao Gui, Sheng Yang, Zhouxing Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Proof autoformalization aims to translate a mathematical informal proof in natural language into a formal proof in a formal language such as Lean 4. Existing LLM-based models for proof autoformalization are typically evaluated only on well-formed informal proofs from curated datasets. We argue that a robust proof autoformalizer must remain faithful even for informal proofs that diverge from these idealized ones, and we present the first study on the robustness of proof autoformalization models. We formulate two categories of perturbations: a global perturbation paraphrases the informal theorem-proof pair in a different style, under which the formalization should remain consistent; a local perturbation alters a value, symbol, or proof step, possibly in a counterfactual way, and a robust formalization should faithfully reflect the perturbation rather than reverting to the original or inferring a different one on its own. We build a benchmark with both perturbations on miniF2F and MATH-500, and automatically measure how stable a proof autoformalization's correctness is under global perturbations and how faithfully its output reflects local perturbations. We evaluate seven recent models: their correctness varies under global perturbations, and they mostly fail to remain faithful under local perturbations.
  </details>

- **[Bringing Up a Bilingual BabyLM: Investigating Multilingual Language Acquisition Using Small-Scale Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.29552)**  `arXiv:2603.29552`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Linda Zeng, Steven Y. Feng, Michael C. Frank_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingualism is incredibly common around the world, leading to many important theoretical and practical questions, including whether multilingual acquisition leads to delays in learning and whether there are better and worse ways to structure multilingual input. Many correlational studies address these questions, but it is surprisingly difficult to get definitive answers because children cannot be randomly assigned to be multilingual and data are typically not matched between languages. We create matched mono- and bilingual datasets using synthetic data and machine translation to simulate a variety of controlled exposure conditions, pairing English with Spanish, Chinese, and Dutch input. We train GPT-2 models on these datasets and evaluate their performance on perplexity, grammaticality, and semantic knowledge. Across model scales, measures, and languages, bilingual models perform similarly to monolingual models in the first language, but show strong performance in the second as well. Control experiments show that our findings hold on real-world language data and even on shuffled second-language input. These results suggest that there are no strong differences between different bilingual exposure regimes, and bilingual input poses no in-principle challenges for agnostic statistical learners.
  </details>

- **[EngTrace: A Symbolic Benchmark for Verifiable Process Evaluation of Engineering Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.01650)**  `arXiv:2511.01650`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ayesha Gull, Muhammad Usman Safder, Rania Elbadry, Fan Zhang, Veselin Stoyanov, Preslav Nakov, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Most engineering problems involve complex multi-step derivations through checkable intermediate quantities. Grading only the final answer misjudges LLMs, crediting correct answers reached through flawed steps and penalizing wrong answers built on mostly sound ones. Yet, existing benchmarks such as SuperGPQA and EEE-Bench score only final answers, and step-level ones such as TPS-CalcBench rely on LLM judges or human graders, or cover a single domain. We therefore introduce EngTrace, a symbolic benchmark for verifiable process evaluation of engineering reasoning. Its 150 expert-certified templates, spanning five branches, 15 domains, and 42 areas, generate each problem with a gold reasoning trace, so that every intermediate quantity is known exactly. We design and validate an evaluator that checks final answers, coverage of the gold trace's intermediate quantities (milestones), and displayed arithmetic, mostly without an LLM judge. Across 11 LLMs, final answers barely separate the strongest models, while their derivations differ. Wrong answers still state about half of the gold milestones, and domain experts trace most wrong answers to calculation slips, with conceptual errors rarer on easy problems than on harder ones. Overall, as LLMs near the final-answer ceiling, checking derivations, as EngTrace does, reveals differences that final answers miss.
  </details>

- **[RefCraft: Benchmarking Visual-Reference-Conditioned Agentic Generation through Web Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26826)**  `arXiv:2610.26826`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchi Wang, Rongyao Fang, Weihao Yu, Xilin Wei, Tiancheng Gu, Dingkang Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Beyond basic tool use and coding skills, effectively incorporating visual references into agentic generation is equally essential for applying AI agents to real-world productivity tasks. We study this crucial capability through webpage development, a practical and readily evaluable setting in which existing benchmarks remain largely idealized: they typically focus on reconstructing visual targets or generating webpages from textual instructions alone, overlooking the more common workflow of developing from visual examples. Specifically, we introduce RefCraft, a benchmark comprising 300 realistic multimodal webpage-development tasks that combine natural-language instructions with screenshots from one to five reference webpages. Agents must selectively interpret and utilize these references while rendering, visually inspecting, and iteratively refining their outputs. We evaluate the resulting open-ended artifacts using independently verifiable keypoints and complementary programmatic and VLM judges. Across nine frontier (M)LLMs, the strongest system achieves an overall score of only 52.12 out of 100, revealing that visual-reference-conditioned agentic generation remains a substantial challenge for current models in practical webpage-development settings.
  </details>

- **[LatComm: Reusable Context Compression for Latent Communication between Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26840)**  `arXiv:2610.26840`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Huawei Lin, Jinting Liu, Tan Yu, Weijie Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Systems that combine several large language models (LLMs) often use one context for many questions. When the context is passed between models as text, the receiving model processes all of its tokens again for every question. Avoiding this cost requires a context representation that is compact, reusable across questions, and readable by a different LLM. Existing compression and latent communication methods each provide only some of these properties. We introduce LatComm, a Latent Communication interface that provides all three by separating encoding from reading: the context is encoded once without the question, and the receiver selects what to read for each question. A frozen context encoder LLM compresses the context into a fixed-size packet and sends it to a different frozen receiver LLM, which reads the packet through cross-attention, with gates computed from its prompt. On 12 multiple-choice question-answering datasets, LatComm improves average accuracy over single-vector baselines by up to 10.2 points. On QuALITY, it reduces the receiver's input by 57x and answers the questions of an article 3x faster.
  </details>

- **[TokenGate: Adapting LLMs for Fast and Memory-Efficient Prefill with Learned Token Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26754)**  `arXiv:2610.26754`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Liana Patel, Rishabh Ranjan, Ion Stoica, Matei Zaharia, Carlos Guestrin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expert human readers process large inputs (e.g., code repositories and long documents) by skimming to closely read only what matters. On the other hand, language models process all input tokens through each model layer, resulting in significant processing costs for long-context inputs. We introduce TokenGate, a small, drop-in component that extends existing trained models to enable end-to-end learnable token pruning over long-context inputs for fast and memory-efficient prefill. To enable end-to-end learning despite the non-differentiable top-K operator for token pruning, we introduce token-gated attention, a mechanism inspired by Mixture-of-Experts that re-weights attention logits using token importance scores. Across diverse tasks, TokenGate achieves $1.4$-$11.3\times$ faster time-to-first token (TTFT) and $1.5$-$3.4\times$ lower peak memory at no accuracy degradation for prefill in transformers. Compared to the best-performing prior methods for token-pruning, TokenGate gives up to $6.4\times$ faster prefill and $2\times$ lower peak memory. TokenGate natively extends both standard transformers (e.g., Qwen3) and hybrid-attention models (e.g., Qwen3.5) with simple lightweight adaptation comparable to standard parameter-efficient fine-tuning. We make our code available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Honeyed Words, Hidden Daggers: Benchmarking and Training LLMs to See Through Mock Politeness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26059)**  `arXiv:2610.26059`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Caixin Kang, Sitong Gong, Tianyu Yan, Kaipeng Zhang, Yoichi Sato, Yifei Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Chinese idiom "kou mi fu jian" ("honeyed words, hidden daggers") names speech that is polite in form but hostile in function. We present ZhenhuanBench, a 1,592-item benchmark built from expert commentary on Empresses in the Palace, covering speaker intent, emotion-language mismatch, and per-listener uptake. Text-only evaluation covers 34 LLMs. GPT-6 Astra leads the leaderboard at 73.48% overall, while its listener-reaction score is 58.37%. The annotations also supply supervised traces and string-verifiable training rewards. With 338 gold traces, an 8B model outperforms teacher distillation on identical items. Subsequent RL saturates, and chance correction reduces the estimated recoverable performance gap by a factor of 7.5. Our CPR-GRPO loop (Compress-Probe-Replenish) uses this estimate to weight gold supervision, reaching 67.0 on public multiple-choice tasks and 74.5 under the training-matched hidden protocol. Without target-task training, CPR outperforms Base, SFT, and GRPO across six Chinese and English pragmatic and sarcasm evaluations. On three external datasets, training with CPR yields higher mean Macro-F1 than SFT and GRPO.
  </details>

- **[Accelerated Drift of Scientific Prose in the LLM Era: Evidence from an Interrupted Time-Series Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26008)**  `arXiv:2610.26008`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hanna Hubarava, Benjamin V. Ineichen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every scientific text is embedded in the writing norms of its time: it reflects earlier publications while also shaping the texts that follow. The growing use of LLM-based writing tools introduces a new factor in this evolving system, but the linguistic changes coinciding with their emergence remain insufficiently understood. We investigate whether the trajectories of scientific-preprint writing changed in rate or direction after conversational LLMs became publicly accessible, using the release of ChatGPT in November 2022 as the observational breakpoint. We compare pre- and post-2022 trajectories across vocabulary, punctuation, readability, and syntax in abstracts from arXiv's artificial intelligence, statistics, and quantitative biology categories and from medRxiv. Using interrupted time-series regression, we find substantial post-2022 changes across multiple feature families and research domains, including increases in average word length, reduced formula-based readability, and declines in maximum dependency-tree depth and sentence-level depth variation. Individual features follow different dynamics: some lexical markers accelerate persistently, while others rise and then recede. Our findings show that isolated marker frequencies capture only part of the shift: changes in the rate, direction, and structure of linguistic trajectories provide complementary evidence of broader post-2022 change in scientific-preprint writing. More broadly, these findings motivate monitoring scientific writing as a multidimensional, evolving system rather than through a fixed set of LLM-associated markers.
  </details>

- **[AdaExploreBench: Benchmarking LLM Agents for Proactive Exploration and Test-time Adaptation in Evolving Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25950)**  `arXiv:2610.25950`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Wang, Zishen Lai, Jiayu Liu, Cheng Qian, Jeonghwan Kim, Heng Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.
  </details>

- **[No Matter Who You Are: Addressivity and Sycophancy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25728)**  `arXiv:2610.25728`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Seungjong Sun, Hyunmin Jun, Minsik Yoon, Eungu Lee, Choongwon Kang, Young Seok Woo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sycophancy, where an LLM becomes overly agreeable, deferential, or flattering toward the user, has been identified as a major failure mode. While post-training has been implicated as a cause, how it internally gives rise to sycophantic behavior remains insufficiently understood. Post-training transforms the model's output from text completion into utterances directed toward an addressee, thereby giving the output \textit{addressivity}. We hypothesize that the accompanying pressure to follow the addressee's instructions and align with their preferences links this addressivity to sycophancy. To test this, we use Sparse Autoencoders to extract addressivity directions from pre-trained and post-trained models and examine their relationship with sycophancy. Steering models with addressivity directions can amplify or suppress sycophancy, and this effect is observed only in post-trained models. Furthermore, comparing the internal mechanisms of both model types confirms that the association between addressivity and sycophancy is strengthened through post-training. Finally, through a controlled post-training experiment, we find that models are sycophantic not toward a specific addressee but toward any recognized addressee, suggesting that the presence of an addressee, rather than the addressee's identity, is what elicits sycophancy.
  </details>

- **[MapSteal: Map-based Autonomous Knowledge Stealing Attack Against Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25626)**  `arXiv:2610.25626`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengao Li, Chuxu Zhang, Yushun Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge acquisition from black-box large language models (LLMs) offers a practical approach to adapting smaller student models. However, many existing methods rely on predefined queries or student feedback to guide acquisition, leaving autonomous expansion of knowledge targets an important challenge. We propose \textbf{MapSteal}, a student-independent framework for structured recursive knowledge acquisition. Starting from a small set of initial roots, MapSteal represents acquisition targets using structured states and recursively expands them through teacher-proposed directions. Question realization, proposal admission, and deduplication constrain the acquisition process, while root-aware scheduling organizes exploration under a fixed QA budget. The resulting question--answer pairs are used to adapt student models without requiring student feedback during acquisition. Experiments with Qwen3.5 students at 2B, 4B, and 9B scales show competitive downstream performance across six medical and cybersecurity benchmarks. On the 4B medical evaluation, Full MapSteal improves average accuracy by 1.17 percentage points over a simplified recursive acquisition variant. Cross-domain evaluations show that gains depend on the domain and evaluation panel. These results demonstrate the feasibility of structured, student-independent recursive acquisition while highlighting the distinction between generating acquisition targets and obtaining useful training supervision. Our code is available at \href{this https URL} {this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[DiaLong: Benchmarking Reminiscence in Long-Context Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25463)**  `arXiv:2610.25463`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Owen Bianchi, Samer Aslan, Ryunosuke Saito_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How well can transformer-based Large Language Models (LLMs) retain and recall memories in long contexts of dialogue? LLMs are known to forget long-range factual information in their context, yet the field lacks a comprehensive benchmark to quantitatively assess these shortcomings. We present DiaLong, a long-context dialogue dataset and accompanying memory task that standardizes evaluation in this space. It consists of 100 unique conversations created by humans and then augmented with LLMs, extending beyond lengths found in existing datasets. Our selected memory task is fact recall: classify 40 candidate facts about a dialogue as true or false. This task directly probes the model's ability to actively retain and retrieve information, serving as a critical measure of memory within extended conversational contexts. In testing both the latest LLMs and proposed memory enhancements on DiaLong, we demonstrate that more work needs to be done to enable LLMs with better memory performance. By enhancing memory capabilities, LLMs can improve in maintaining coherence, relevance, and human-like interaction over extended conversation. Improving these capabilities will help LLMs fulfill diverse user needs and expectations in various conversational scenarios.
  </details>

- **[Aligning Cultural Behaviors in Arabic Language Models via Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25362)**  `arXiv:2610.25362`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junhong Liang, Daniil Orel, Saeed Almheiri, Bilal Elbouardi, Younes Samih, Abed Alhakim Freihat, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Arabic LLMs have improved rapidly, but aligning them with country-specific cultural behavioral preferences remains underexplored. Previous work has focused primarily on instruction-tuning for factual knowledge, overlooking nuanced cultural behaviors. We propose ArabPref, which to our knowledge is the first large-scale pan-Arab preference data set for the alignment of cultural behavior conditioned on the country. Our contribution includes two resources in English and Modern Standard Arabic: a culturally grounded preference dataset for training and evaluation, and a multiple-choice benchmark designed to assess culturally aligned behavior across nations. All test data are validated by native speakers to ensure authenticity. To optimize cultural alignment, we experiment with post-training methods across multilingual and Arabic-centric language models, evaluating performance on both generation tasks and our cultural multiple-choice benchmark. By releasing an evaluation benchmark, we provide a foundation for systematically studying and improving culturally aligned behavior in language models.
  </details>

- **[Reranking Answers of Large Language Models with Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25185)**  `arXiv:2610.25185`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mikhail Salnikov, Hai Le, Olga Tsymboi, Ivan Lazichny, Dmitrii Iarosh, Egor Cheremiskin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answering natural language questions over knowledge graph data is challenging due to the vast number of facts, which can be difficult to process and navigate. One potential solution for this issue is to use mined subgraphs related to the query, although this process still requires extracting these subgraphs. This research presents a solution for extracting subgraphs related to entity candidates from a question-and-answer set, which can be obtained by inferring a large language model by calculating the shortest paths between entities. The proposed approaches detail various features that can be extracted from the subgraphs and reranking models to select the most probable answers from a list of candidates. Experiments were conducted on Wikidata to evaluate the effectiveness of the proposed approaches. This involved enumerating all the main feature types that can be extracted from mined subgraphs and a detailed analysis of the proposed features and reranking method combinations. In addition, a public web application that provides a useful web tool for studying the graph space between question and answer entities has been developed to work with subgraphs. This includes visualization of the extracted subgraph and automatic generation of natural language text to describe it.
  </details>

- **[One Head Away from Language Confusion: Single Attention Heads Across Model Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25026)**  `arXiv:2610.25026`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiwon Jeong, Anjelo Jann Laroza, Chaewoon Yoon, Arjun Pillai, Pranav Kokati, Christian Hoang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models sometimes reply in an unexpected language, a failure known as language confusion. We ablate individual attention heads in 12 model checkpoints spanning seven model series, using FLORES-200 continuations in five languages and Language Confusion Benchmark (LCB) chat replies. In ten models, zeroing one head causes substantially more departures from the expected language than zeroing any other head in its layer; its LCB pass-rate drop persists, though reduced, under mean ablation. Seven heads move 8.0 to 51.5 percent of all FLORES continuations out of the prompt's language, versus at most 1.4 percent for other same-layer heads; the other three mainly affect explicitly requested languages. These seven heads matter less in base models given plain text. In two models, replacing the values a head reads at the requested-language name with another language's mean makes replies fail and shifts some into that language, unlike a random change of equal size. Replacing a head's output with another language's mean redirects 84 to 99 percent of scored non-English monolingual LCB replies into that language in five models. Yet the requested language's mean improves crosslingual replies beyond every control head only in the two Gemma models, whose baselines are low. Code and results are available in the public repository atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Can't Jev Decide? Probing the Limits of One-Pass Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24929)**  `arXiv:2610.24929`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Wang Yang, Xinpeng Li, Yanyan Zhang, Chuang Ma, Shouren Wang, Jianzhe Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Jev and its open replicas choose among the options a caller supplies with a single forward pass, fast enough to decide inside an agent loop. We ask what such a model cannot decide, and find a simple limit: Jev cannot make a decision that an LLM makes only by writing out intermediate steps. On a benchmark that varies one factor at a time, Jev finds and selects records without error up to 20k tokens and 64 options, but once the answer depends on two or more updates of one user, accuracy falls, to 53-80% at sixteen updates, because similar records interfere. Given a stale summary of the state, Jev follows it. On real decision items graded by how many thinking tokens a reference LLM needs, Jev answers 97.5% of the items that one forward pass of that LLM solves, but only 74% of the others. Jev also does not know its limit: its confidence flags the errors it makes itself but not those it inherits from a stale input, and neither an explicit nor a trained hand-off option catches the latter. The open models we evaluate share these limits. Checking the state such models rely on requires a model that reasons.
  </details>

- **[How Well Can LLMs Verbalize Mathematical Formulas? A Multi-Domain Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24917)**  `arXiv:2610.24917`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Peter Volbach, Tim Schlippe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown strong capabilities across natural language tasks. But their ability to generate accurate written verbalizations of mathematical formulas has not been systematically evaluated. Such verbalization is important for text-to-speech systems supporting audio-based learning and visually impaired users. We evaluated five state-of-the-art LLMs across six scientific domains and constructed Read-Aloud Math, a balanced multi-domain corpus with LaTeX formulas, manually validated simple and extended transcriptions, multiple variants, textual context, domain labels, and formula complexity levels. Using multi-reference BLEU, GPT-5.2 achieved the highest score for simple transcriptions (75.9% without context, 64.9% with context), although without context it did not significantly outperform Grok 4.1 Fast or Claude Sonnet 4.5. Claude Sonnet 4.5 achieved the highest score for extended transcriptions without context (36.5%), and Grok 4.1 Fast with context (48.8%). For extended transcriptions, Statistics and Mathematics scored highest across the two context settings, while Physics scored lowest. Formula length was significantly negatively associated with BLEU for simple transcriptions without context, but not with context, whereas BLEU increased significantly with formula length for extended transcriptions in both settings. Grouping symbols were most frequently involved in transcription differences, while variable names, capitalization, and Greek letters were associated with lower BLEU scores. Overall, LLM-based formula verbalization is promising but remains sensitive to transcription type, textual context, and formula characteristics.
  </details>

- **[DiffuPlan: Editable Planning State for Diffusion Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24717)**  `arXiv:2610.24717`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Qisheng Zhou, Zhen Xiong, Qiaoyu Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (dLLMs) are emerging as an alternative to autoregressive (AR) language models, yet dLLM agents largely inherit an interaction protocol designed for AR generation: once intermediate state is written, later observations can only be incorporated by appending more text. This is a poor match to a defining capability of dLLMs -- reconstructing earlier text conditioned on context that appears both before and after it. We ask whether this capability can support a different agent-state paradigm in which intermediate planning state is directly revisable as evidence arrives. We introduce DiffuPlan, a diffusion search agent that maintains a single editable planning state and reconstructs only the affected steps when later evidence resolves an entity or changes the search route. To support variable-length revisions, we introduce Expansion-Partition Marginalization (EPM), which marginalizes over equivalent expansion paths. We further adapt diffusion trajectory RL to multi-turn search. Across four multi-hop QA benchmarks, DiffuPlan achieves 50.24 average F1, outperforming strong AR search agents. More importantly, under identical data and training, editable planning state improves the diffusion agent by 2.44 F1 versus only 0.47 for its AR source model. These results show that dLLMs need not imitate append-only AR agents: revising committed state is a distinct agentic advantage.
  </details>

- **[Which Long Documents Matter? A Controlled Study of Data Selection for Context Extension](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24469)**  `arXiv:2610.24469`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junlong Jia, Jiang Zhou, Xing Wu, Chaochen Gao Tinghao Yu, Feng Zhang, Lilin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural long documents are widely used for context extension, but which selection pipelines are effective remains unclear. We conduct a controlled comparison on Qwen2.5-3B at 128K under matched 6B-token continued-training budgets. Random sampling of natural long documents (random-long) outperforms repacking at longer evaluation lengths. Within the same candidate pool, selecting documents with high LongPPL key-token density yields further gains, unlike the evaluated generic-quality and LLM-judge selectors. We introduce truncLongPPL to reduce scoring cost by computing key-token density over a truncated prefix. At 128K, scoring the first half of the reference span yields downstream performance close to full-span scoring with a measured $2.28\times$ end-to-end scoring speedup. Across three training seeds, truncLongPPL achieves the highest mean scores among evaluated pipelines, exceeding random-long by 4.54 points on validation HELMET and 3.28 on held-out RULER, with gains most consistent in retrieval. At a 256K target context, it gains 2.82 average RULER points over random-long on Qwen2.5-3B. In short/long mixed training across Qwen2.5-3B, Qwen3-8B, and OLMo~3-7B, it gains 1.47--7.31 average RULER points over random-long mixing, with seven-task short-context averages within 0.23 points of the corresponding baselines. After supervised fine-tuning (SFT), Qwen3-8B and OLMo~3-7B models trained with selected long documents outperform their random-long counterparts by 1.13--1.67 points on LongBench v2 and 0.62--1.44 points on MRCR.
  </details>

- **[OR-License: Evaluating LLM Agents as Real-World Operations Research Practitioners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24087)**  `arXiv:2610.24087`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Peisong Wang, Feixue Liu, Yize Meng, Bowen Liu, Ruoyu Chen, Gaoyang Dai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can large language model (LLM) agents deliver expert-level operations research (OR) solutions directly from original client materials, without prior expert reformulation? Existing OR benchmarks typically begin with self-contained descriptions or predefined optimization tasks, underrepresenting the translation of original business materials into professionally validated solutions. We introduce OR-License, an end-to-end benchmark of 31 instances across 10 industrial scenarios from paid OR engagements totaling USD 3.68 million. It preserves original requirements, input data, deliverable formats, and information boundaries after de-identification. To study information acquisition, we introduce ORSim, a controlled client simulator that discloses business facts without supplying OR expertise. Hidden evaluation checks feasibility and compares objective quality against original expert solutions accepted by clients before benchmark construction. Deployment-Adjusted Quality (DAQ) averages expert-relative quality across instances, assigning zero to infeasible deliveries. Across 11 frontier LLMs using coding-agent harnesses, several exceed the expert reference in conditional objective quality, but none matches its DAQ; the strongest primary Claude Code configuration achieves 88.39% strict feasibility and a DAQ of 0.87 against the expert reference of 1. Client information acquisition extends beyond clarification into solution development and validation, with some later stages showing higher confirmed issue-resolution proportions. Trajectory analysis identifies formulation and implementation failures, while task-equalized research-chain completion rates range from 24% to 45% across models on evaluable tasks. These findings motivate sustained information-gap resolution, faithful problem formulation, and evidence-linked self-improvement for reliable long-horizon agents.
  </details>

- **[From Solvers to Research: Large Language Model-Driven Formal Mathematics at the Research Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07779)**  `arXiv:2607.07779`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Eric Jiang, Xiao Liang, Yingjia Wan, Mengting Li, Haikang Deng, Alexander Taylor, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent developments in AI for Mathematics (AI4Math), especially Large Language Model (LLM)-driven theorem provers, has achieved remarkable success in formal proof generation for well-defined mathematical problems through Interactive Theorem Proving (ITP) languages. However, current systems remain fundamentally limited in tackling frontier research mathematics, such as discovering new theorems or resolving open conjectures, which are often open-ended, under-specified, and involve multiple layers of abstraction. We argue that the next leap in AI4Math systems requires a decisive shift from predefined problem-solvers to research agents that can address frontier mathematical challenges with rigorous formal mathematical reasoning. In this position paper, we provide a systematic review of the field, covering datasets, auto-formalization, and proof synthesis. More importantly, we identify core limitations of existing systems in serving as mathematical research agents, examining issues across datasets, relational structure, mathematical exploration, tool ecosystem, and human-AI collaboration, outlining a strategic road-map for the future of AI4Math.
  </details>

- **[Is LLM's Moral Decision Compositional?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11232)**  `arXiv:2606.11232`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Weijia Zhang, Ruiqi Chen, Yunze Xiao, Weihao Xuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Moral evaluations of language models typically infer a model's values from its choices between single acts, yet in everyday life a person is judged on their history of good and bad acts. Such evaluations cannot capture how a model combines one person's acts. We test whether a model's moral judgment is compositional, that is, whether its judgment of a person follows from its judgments of each act. We introduce Moral Composition Arena, which asks which of two people is morally better to give Elo ratings to 229 human-validated acts across five Moral Foundations Theory foundations (act Elo) and to 160 controlled composite profiles, each a person described by two acts (composite Elo). Across ten frontier models and 29,134 choices, summed act Elo explains 73% of the variance in composite Elo, but the composition rule is not additive. With nearly equal mean summed act Elo, one extreme virtue with a minor wrong outranks two minor virtues in every model (mean +35.0 Elo), an extremity premium that survives a nonlinear baseline and human relabeling of act intensities. Single-act evaluations thus predict most of how these models judge people described by two acts, but miss a systematic premium on extreme acts.
  </details>

- **[Probing Persona-Dependent Preferences in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13339)**  `arXiv:2605.13339`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Oscar Gilg, Pierre Beckmann, Daniel Paleka, Patrick Butlin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can be said to have preferences: they reliably pick certain tasks and outputs over others, and preferences shaped by post-training and prompting appear to influence much of their behaviour. But models can also adopt different personas which have radically different preferences. How is this implemented internally? Does each persona use its own preference representations, or are some representations shared? We train linear probes on residual-stream activations of Gemma-3-27B and Qwen-3.5-122B to predict revealed pairwise task choices, and identify a genuine preference vector: it tracks the model's preferences as they shift across a range of prompts and situations, and on Gemma-3-27B steering along it causally controls pairwise choice. Some preference information transfers across the prompted personas we test: a probe trained on the helpful assistant predicts and steers the choices of qualitatively different personas, including an evil persona whose preferences anti-correlate with the Assistant's.
  </details>

- **[RuPLaR : Efficient Latent Compression of LLM Reasoning Chains with Rule-Based Priors From Multi-Step to One-Step](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09346)**  `arXiv:2605.09346`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaocheng Luo, Kang Wang, Zaifu Zhan, Yuechi Zhou, Xiangyu Duan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Chain-of-Thought (CoT) paradigm, while enhancing the interpretability of Large Language Models (LLMs), is constrained by the inefficiencies and expressive limits of natural language. Latent Chain-of-Thought (latent CoT) reasoning, which operates in a continuous latent space, offers a promising alternative but faces challenges from structural complexities in existing multi-step or multi-model paradigms, such as error propagation and coordination overhead. In this paper, we introduce One-Model One-Step, a novel compression framework for Latent Reasoning with Rule-Based Priors(RuPLaR) to address this challenge. Our method trains an LLM to autonomously generate latent reasoning tokens in a single training stage, guided by rule-based prior probability distributions, thereby eliminating cascaded processes and inter-model dependencies. To ensure reasoning quality, we design a joint training objective that enforces answer consistency via cross-entropy, aligns soft token distributions with rule-based priors via KL divergence (the Soft Thinking constraint), and adds a problem-thought semantic alignment constraint in the representation space. Extensive experiments show that our compression framework not only improves accuracy by 11.03% over existing latent CoT methods on GSM8k-Aug but also achieves this with minimal token usage, underscoring its effectiveness and extensibility. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Latent Abstraction for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17866)**  `arXiv:2604.17866`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ha Lan N.T, Minh-Anh Nguyen, Dung D. Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become a standard approach for enhancing large language models (LLMs) with external knowledge, mitigating hallucinations, and improving factuality. However, existing systems rely on generating natural language queries at each hop and maintaining a strict architectural separation between retriever and generator, preventing them from leveraging the full representational capacity of the LLM. We propose \textbf{LAnR} (Latent Abstraction for RAG), a unified framework in which a single LLM jointly performs encoding, retrieval, and generation entirely within its own latent space. Rather than generating textual queries, LAnR produces dense retrieval vectors from the hidden states of a designated \texttt{[PRED]} token and uses them to match against encoded document representations from the same model. Furthermore, LAnR adaptively decides when sufficient evidence has been retrieved using a lightweight MLP control head over those same hidden states, eliminating both the separate retriever and explicit token-level stopping reasoning. This design is motivated by our empirical observation that answer token entropy reliably signals retrieval sufficiency. Extensive experiments on six QA benchmarks spanning single-hop and multi-hop settings demonstrate that LAnR outperforms existing RAG methods, while achieving improved inference efficiency through reduced number of retrieval calls and tighter model integration.
  </details>

- **[SHINE: A Scalable In-Context Hypernetwork for Mapping Context to LoRA in a Single Pass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06358)**  `arXiv:2602.06358`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yewei Liu, Xiyuan Wang, Yansheng Mao, Yoav Gelberg, Haggai Maron, Muhan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose SHINE (Scalable Hyper In-context NEtwork), a scalable hypernetwork that can map diverse meaningful contexts into high-quality LoRA adapters for large language models (LLMs). By reusing the frozen LLM's own parameters in an in-context hypernetwork design and introducing architectural innovations, SHINE overcomes key limitations of prior hypernetworks and achieves strong expressive power with a relatively small number of parameters. We introduce a pretraining and instruction fine-tuning pipeline, and train our hypernetwork to generate high quality LoRA adapters from diverse meaningful contexts in a single forward pass. It updates LLM parameters without any fine-tuning, and immediately enables complex question answering tasks related to the context without directly accessing the context, effectively transforming in-context knowledge to in-parameter knowledge in one pass. Our work achieves outstanding results on various tasks, greatly saves time, computation and memory costs compared to SFT-based LLM adaptation, and shows great potential for scaling. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[ASTOR: Benchmarking and Improving Autoformulation for Soft-Constraint Optimization with a Formal Grammar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26819)**  `arXiv:2610.26819`  `cs.CL`  
  _Amer Goel, Yeganeh Alimohammadi, Andrew Daw_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world decision problems combine two qualitatively different kinds of constraints: requirements that must be satisfied, called hard constraints, and preferences that can be violated, called soft constraints. Because these problems are often posed to decision-makers in natural language, large language models offer a natural tool for assisting with their solution. This has given rise to autoformulation, which focuses on using LLMs to translate natural-language descriptions of decision problems into executable mathematical optimization models. However, this research often neglects soft constraints like those proposed by consumers, managers, and planners, despite the fact that many practical problems require modeling them explicitly. We introduce ASTOR, an inference-time framework that maps a natural-language decision problem to a grammar-guided abstract syntax tree (AST) before deterministically compiling the resulting representation into executable optimization code. We also introduce SoftOR, a unified benchmark of natural language problems which focuses on soft constraint formulations. On SoftOR, ASTOR achieves the best aggregate accuracy among comparable single-request and multi-agent LLM methods while remaining competitive on standard autoformulation benchmarks that include only hard constraints. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Safety Mid-Training: Internalizing LLM Safety as a Foundational Capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26809)**  `arXiv:2610.26809`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhengyue Zhao, Liwei Jiang, Yejin Choi, Chaowei Xiao1_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety alignment for large language models (LLMs) is primarily performed during post-training, while recent work has also begun incorporating safety data into pre-training. Yet safety can degrade when later training no longer includes safety data, and gains in safety often come at the cost of general capability. This tension suggests that safety and general reasoning should not be treated as separate training goals. We instead view safety as an LLM capability that can be learned through a reasoning structure shared with general tasks. Both require the model to understand intent or task goals, apply relevant knowledge, and reason about potential consequences. We introduce safety mid-training, a stage between pre-training and post-training that jointly develops safety and general reasoning through this shared structure. Given safety and utility prompts, the base model generates structured documents and reasoning traces that are then used for mid-training. Safety mid-training reduces average attack success rate (ASR) by 72% to 81% relative to Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B base models, while also improving general capability. The benefits persist through subsequent post-training. After general-only SFT, mid-trained models largely retain their safety, achieving lower ASR than the baselines without sacrificing general capability. They also retain safety under general-only GRPO without safety rewards, while subsequent RL reaches a better safety-utility frontier when initialized from the mid-trained models. Together, these results suggest that learning safety and general reasoning within a shared mid-training stage can reduce the safety-utility trade-off and make safety more robust to subsequent training.
  </details>

- **[AfriGuard: Safety and Robust Refusal For African Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26474)**  `arXiv:2610.26474`  `cs.CL`  
  _Israel Abebe Azime, Jesujoba O. Alabi, Iffat Maab, Alexander D. Cai, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Atnafu Lambebo Tonja, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) designed to interact with users are typically trained to respond appropriately to harmful or toxic content. However, ensuring appropriate responses becomes more challenging in diverse linguistic and cultural contexts, where safety risks may be expressed or interpreted differently. Building safe AI systems therefore requires detecting safety risks, understanding their nature, and responding appropriately, while accounting for linguistic and cultural variations that add complexity to each stage. In this work, we introduce AfriGuard, a culturally grounded multilingual dataset for studying harmful intent, safety-category understanding, and refusal behavior across 10 African languages and English. Our findings reveal that guardrail models underperform both open- and closed-source LLMs on safety-intent and safety-category classification, while models show limited understanding of appropriate safety behavior, scoring below 60% F1. We further find that models often fail to respond appropriately to unsafe prompts not because they explicitly refuse, but because they produce linguistically inadequate responses that result in incidental non-assistance rather than proper refusals. Finally, using AfriGuard, we develop efficient guardrail models that achieve performance competitive with models up to 30x larger, introduce refusal detectors that improve average performance by 43.9% over existing detectors, and demonstrate that supervised fine-tuning can substantially improve overall safety performance.
  </details>

- **[Balance-Aware Pivot Selection for Parallel Diffusion LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26040)**  `arXiv:2610.26040`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yejin Lee, Hyundong Jin, Yo-Sub Han_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models enable parallel token generation, offering a promising alternative to sequential autoregressive decoding. Existing acceleration methods use predictive confidence to enable parallel token commitment while preserving generation quality. However, confidence-based selection prioritizes reliable token predictions without explicitly considering how token positions shape the remaining decoding schedule. Moreover, such ordering does not always reflect inter-token dependencies, suggesting that confidence need not be the sole criterion for token selection. Motivated by this limitation, we propose Balance-Aware Pivot Decoding (BPD), which favors central pivots within contiguous masked intervals and decodes the selected positions in parallel. Balanced pivots yield logarithmic decoding depth in the interval length. Our practical method accounts for model uncertainty by combining confidence-based prefilling with confidence-thresholded center selection. We evaluate the resulting quality-efficiency tradeoff on LLaDA and Dream across five mathematical reasoning, code generation, and instruction-following benchmarks. Our implementation is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Scaling Synthetic Pre-training Data and Evaluation for Indic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26006)**  `arXiv:2610.26006`  `cs.CL`  
  _Abhinav Khattar, Aishwarya Chhabra, Ameya Velingker, Anusha Kamath, Brandon Norick, Kanishk Singla, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building capable language models for India requires scalable multilingual data and robust evaluation. We introduce Nemotron-Pretraining-Indic-v1, a synthetic pretraining corpus of approximately 2.83 trillion tokens covering all 22 scheduled Indian languages in native script and twelve in romanized form. It is the largest openly released Indic pretraining corpus. Using our GPU-accelerated pipeline, we translate curated English documents, assess translation quality, and repair low-quality outputs while preserving technical content and document structure and maintaining source traceability. Through continued-pretraining experiments across twelve languages, we show that combining our synthetic corpus with natural Indic text improves knowledge and reasoning over a public-corpus baseline. Additional experiments show how data volume, script coverage, and Indic training share affect English performance. We also introduce the Indic-LLM-Eval-Suite, a multilingual benchmark suite for evaluating Indic language models across chat, instruction following, reasoning, knowledge, code, and translation. The suite combines existing Indic benchmarks with task-aware translations of English benchmarks, using automated quality assessment, targeted correction, and native-speaker review in a human-in-the-loop pipeline. We use the suite to comprehensively benchmark contemporary open-weight Indic and multilingual models across capabilities and languages, revealing substantial variation across tasks and languages. Together, these resources provide a scalable foundation for developing and evaluating more capable Indic language models.
  </details>

- **[Speech LLMs That Breathe: Modeling Non-Verbal Vocalizations in Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25942)**  `arXiv:2610.25942`  `cs.CL`  
  _Heyang Liu, Lixin Zhang, Jiayi Huang, Ziyang Cheng, Yuhao Wang, Ronghua Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The user experience of speech interaction models has improved, benefiting from the modeling of paralinguistic attributes and advances in speech reconstruction paradigms. However, human communication follows extensive norms regarding non-verbal vocalizations (NVVs) spanning respiratory, affective, and discourse. The omission of these events imposes inherent limitations on the naturalness of current approaches. In this work, we cover 13 major types of NVVs and construct a natural dialogue dataset that conforms to human conversational norms through an automated synthesis pipeline. We enhance the ability of speech interaction models to generate contextually appropriate NVV events through multi-stage fine-tuning and NVV-aware GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization). Based on distinct modeling paradigms, we substantially improve the expressiveness of VocalNet and VITA-Qinyu in empathy interactions and role-play dialogues, and further discuss the architectural necessity for achieving high-quality natural speech generation.
  </details>

- **[FisherEdit: Fisher Information Guided Knowledge Editing for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25914)**  `arXiv:2610.25914`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yizhou Yang, Jiahong Fu, Zhiwu Wang, Kailong Fan, Haokun Lin, Yichen Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sequential knowledge editing in Large Language Models (LLMs) often leads to a degradation of general capabilities due to cumulative parameter interference. In this paper, we revisit prominent projection-based methods like AlphaEdit and reveal a surprising mismatch: their robustness is primarily driven by update regularization rather than the intended null-space projection. Based on this insight, we propose FisherEdit, a theoretically grounded method built on Fisher geometry, which replaces coarse activation-space projections with importance-aware regularization in parameter space. It penalizes changes to sensitive parameters more strongly while retaining flexibility for new facts, and admits an efficient row-wise closed-form solution. Experiments on Llama-3, Qwen2.5, and Olmo-3 across multiple benchmarks demonstrate that FisherEdit achieves state-of-the-art performance while better preserving model stability and downstream task accuracy.
  </details>

- **[CALM: Consistency-Aware Logit Masking in Decoding Space for Mitigating Overthinking of Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25908)**  `arXiv:2610.25908`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shengjing Liu, Feiyang Li, Yuxuan Yang, Yile Wang, Hui Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models (LRMs) have achieved strong performance on challenging mathematical and scientific tasks through long chain-of-thought reasoning, but they often suffer from overthinking with unnecessary reasoning steps. Existing truncation-based methods typically detect consecutive trial-answer agreement and force early exit, yet such local stopping signals do not necessarily indicate final-answer stability and can disrupt useful subsequent reflection or verification, potentially leading to performance degradation. We propose consistency-aware logit masking (CALM), a training-free decoding intervention for mitigating overthinking in LRMs. CALM first monitors trial answers at step boundaries and then applies logit masking to action transition point (ATP) tokens such as "Wait", "Alternatively", and "Hmm", suppressing redundant transitional reasoning while allowing the model to continue its thought process more naturally. Experiments across four advanced LRMs and five benchmarks show that CALM reduces token consumption by 18.6%-25.0% compared with full CoT reasoning while maintaining performance, and can even improve accuracy by 0.07%-1.48% by reducing redundant reasoning. LLM-as-a-Judge results also demonstrate that CALM provides more coherent and concise results.
  </details>

- **[CoMem: Learning to Anticipate Failures from Retrieved Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25790)**  `arXiv:2610.25790`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yun Shen, Ming Shen, Tianjun Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reflection lets LLM agents improve from their own attempts, but the memory it builds is local to a single task, so an agent reflecting on its own tends to revisit the same explanation for failure. Cross-task reflective memory addresses this in two forms: retrieved memory keeps past experience explicit and looks up related cases, which is specific but bounded by the bank, and parametric memory internalizes experience into parameters, which generalizes but is grounded in nothing beyond the task description. When both are used, existing agents combine them additively, feeding their outputs to the solver side by side, so retrieved experience never changes what the learned memory produces. We introduce CoMem (Compounding Memory), which makes retrieved experience an input to the learned memory itself, at training and at inference. The agent builds a bank of its own successful and failed reflections, learns from retrieved cases to write failure hypotheses, candidate explanations of how an attempt is likely to go wrong, and at test time reasons from newly retrieved cases to guide each attempt. Varying the retrieved cases gives the memory different perspectives, yielding reflective diversity that is not merely sampled but also grounded in retrieved experience. Across two backbones and five benchmarks in math, code, and multi-hop QA, CoMem achieves strong overall performance. Additional evaluations show that it generalizes to out-of-distribution tasks and enables smaller models to provide effective guidance to stronger solvers.
  </details>

- **[Truncated Reasoning Under a Budget: Evaluating System-1 Models as Decision-makers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25768)**  `arXiv:2610.25768`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhongyin Wang, Zhaoqian Xue, Junting Mu, Haozheng Liu, Ye Yuan, Haolun Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often generate more reasoning tokens than needed. Limiting the reasoning budget reduces cost, but forcing an answer from an incomplete trace can hurt accuracy. System-1 decision models select among candidate options without producing a reasoning response. We evaluate Jev as a decision reader of truncated reasoning across seven LLM reasoners and five multiple-choice benchmarks (35 pairs). Jev achieves higher mean accuracy than forced answering at tested budgets from 500 to 16,000 tokens, including the estimated costs of reasoning and answering. At a 1,500-token budget, it improves accuracy by 4.1 pp overall and 8.6 pp on truncated questions, with significant gains in 17 pairs under Holm-corrected bootstrap tests. Offline replay estimates 34.5% of the full-run cost, with a 1.13 pp accuracy loss. A policy selected on training folds saves 71.3% of the estimated full-run cost on held-out folds, with a 0.58 pp accuracy loss. We also find that Jev shows strong question-only accuracy, exceeding 500-token forced answering on average (68.0% vs. 65.5%), although this advantage varies by benchmark. These findings suggest that System-1 decision models can complement LLM reasoning and reduce the need for costly reasoning completion.
  </details>

- **[Does Unlearning Survive Collaboration? Robust Unlearning for Shared-Model LLM Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25731)**  `arXiv:2610.25731`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hengsong Liu, Liangyu Teng, Jingnan Cai, Yuntian Shi, Jingyu Zhang, Yang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM unlearning is essential for removing sensitive or unwanted knowledge while preserving useful capabilities. As LLM teams are increasingly used to solve complex problems, forgetting must remain effective during collaboration. Despite advances in forgetting effectiveness, efficiency, and robustness to relearning, team deployment remains underexplored. We find that collaboration weakens forgetting: final-answer evaluations miss recoverable evidence in team messages, including complementary clues supporting joint recovery. We establish theoretically and confirm empirically that controlling final answers or individual messages does not ensure low recovery from their combination. To address this challenge, we propose Aggregation-Conditioned Unlearning (ACU). ACU trains a shared model using reconstruction feedback from individual and pooled messages, prioritizes views with higher recovery risk, and uses replacement-based credit assignment while protecting direct and collaborative utility. TOFU evaluates trajectory-level recovery, while WMDP-bio tests native forgetting and retained utility. On TOFU, ACU lowers full-trajectory recovery from 41.8% to 19.3% in ROUGE-L recall and factual recovery from 34.2% to 18.7% versus output-supervised training, with comparable retained-task performance. These results demonstrate that ACU curbs information leakage in LLM teams and generalizes across team sizes and readers.
  </details>

- **[Inside the Scorer: Mechanistic Evidence on Rubric Control in LLM-Based Automated Essay Scoring from 25 Base Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25724)**  `arXiv:2610.25724`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chungche Huang, Po-Ya Angela Wang, Shu-Kai Hsieh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated essay scoring (AES) using large language models (LLMs) offers promising use cases for language assessment, yet high-stakes testing requires scores that are both controllable and verifiable, which current LLM-based scorers lack. Given that, we investigate whether LLM-based scorers form distinct internal representations for individual scoring rubrics and whether these representations can be independently controlled. Probing and steering 25 open-weight base LLMs (0.13B-10.31B parameters; 14 families) on human-scored BESTEP English writing tasks, we find that holistic scores are decodable from model activations for the longer tasks (Parts 2 and 3) and only weakly for the shortest (Part 1). Furthermore, scoring rubrics have partly separable directions in representation space for the longer tasks, though not for short responses (Part 1). However, causal steering reveals a critical limitation: applying a matching intervention (e.g., steering "grammar" on a grammar-degraded response) yields only a negligible advantage (about 2% of the effect) over a mismatched intervention (e.g., steering "vocabulary"), and none that is detectable in any of the 10 testable comparisons across reliably measured rubrics. Rather than controlling distinct evaluative dimensions, the rubric directions share a dominant lever whose rubric-specific parts are not selective, which may mirror the halo structure of human ratings. Our contributions are two-fold. First, we show that analytic scoring rubrics designed for human raters do not behave as independent controls in base LLMs, even among the seven models whose scores agree best with human raters ($r \geq 0.6$). Second, we establish a methodology that shows that score agreement and decodability do not imply rubric-level control in LLM-based assessment.
  </details>

- **[Assertion and Belief: Moore's Paradox in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25586)**  `arXiv:2610.25586`  `cs.CL`  
  _Anshun Asher Zheng, Weili Cao, Fangcong Yin, David Beaver, Junyi Jessy Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Moore's paradox \citep{moore1942} concerns sentences such as ``It is raining, but I don't believe that it is,'' which may well be true yet cannot be sensibly asserted. The absurdity lies not in the content but in the act, since to assert is to vouch for one's own belief. This makes the paradox a test of whether language models take the speech act into account when computing meaning. We present the first test of Moore's paradox in LLMs, evaluating 12 models on a curated set of Moorean sentences in varied contexts. We find that models largely recognize the infelicity of Moorean sentences, and that the judgement is not tied to a memorized form and tracks the speech act. Using the Rational Speech Act framework (RSA; \citealt{goodmanfrank2016}), we then test whether these judgements arise from a listener reasoning about a speaker. We find the models' own listeners do not reason about their own speakers, and their reasoning traces suggest that they instead recall knowledge about the paradox and apply rules therein. The models thus appear to have learned the paradox as a fact about sentences, not as a requirement on speakers.
  </details>

- **[Representations in Motion: Layer-wise Rotation and Trajectory Divergence in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25574)**  `arXiv:2610.25574`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhuonan Yang, Yik Siu Chan, Wannan Yang, Ellie Pavlick_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work suggests that activation dynamics--how representations change during inference--might be more revealing about language model behavior than static readouts from individual layers. In this work, we investigate the layer-wise evolution of representations of factuality, using true and false statements when the model is prompted to either lie or to be honest. We report a geometric phenomenon in which the factuality directions extracted from the lying and honest conditions appear to rotate away from each other across layers. We find that such layer-wise rotation also occurs in a task that requires the model to take different visual perspectives in the 3D world, suggesting that the rotation phenomenon might be indicative of manipulating conceptual spaces in general, rather than unique to factuality. We then investigate the "real time" transformation of conceptual representations on a trial-to-trial basis. We show that it is possible to make strong predictions about the model's target task and behavior from trajectories alone. In addition, diverging points in single-trial trajectories correspond to the onset of rotation in aggregated-trial analysis. Finally, we show that both rotation and divergent trajectories occur in more naturalistic settings in which models are not overtly prompted to "lie" but rather report false statements in the course of completing a different task.
  </details>

- **[Where Are Language-Specific Features Represented in Multilingual LLMs? Everywhere](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25505)**  `arXiv:2610.25505`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mikaela Martins, Jonathan Dunn_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Previous work has argued that multilingual LLMs encode language-specific features most strongly in their initial and final layers, implying that intermediate layers support language-agnostic representations. This paper reconsiders this finding by performing four different experiments: three that undertake a fine-grained analysis of representations of Portuguese and Spanish, two closely related languages, and one large-scale language identification experiment that covers over 200 languages. The results of these experiments challenge the strict separation between language-specific and language-agnostic layers in multilingual LLMs, using Llama 3.2 1B as a test case. By analyzing and manipulating contextual sentence embeddings, we demonstrate that language identity remains strongly recoverable throughout the model rather than being restricted to initial and final layers. Moreover, we find that the model tends to classify ambiguous strings as closer to Spanish than to Portuguese, revealing a skew in model behavior that differs from human judgments.
  </details>

- **[Mixture-of-Memories: Low-Overhead Language Modeling with Modular Knowledge Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25425)**  `arXiv:2610.25425`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yifan Lu, Rubin Wei, Ziyun Zou, Junhong Liang, Haolin Yang, Xinlin Zhuang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models store substantial factual knowledge in their parameters, yet this knowledge is difficult to control, update, and reliably elicit at inference time. Existing memory-augmented approaches typically rely on a single auxiliary mechanism, such as retrieval, parameter updates, or neural memory, and therefore apply the same form of correction to tokens with very different knowledge demands. We propose Mixture-of-Memories (MoM), a memory-augmented language modeling framework that separates routed neural memory from writable factual memory. MoM combines a frozen base LM with two neural memory experts: a latent memory that provides low-latency hidden-state corrections and a lexical memory that provides a token-space knowledge pathway. A lightweight Budget Router sets per-token mixture weights from uncertainty and disagreement features, replacing the fixed interpolation of prior memory modules. For post-hoc updates, an external explicit memory stays outside the router, because a router trained before facts are written learns to down-weight it; new facts are appended without retraining any component and used only when an answer-level gate accepts the retrieved fact. Experiments across language modeling, factual QA, update analysis, and efficiency show that MoM improves both predictive accuracy and updateability while retaining low inference overhead.
  </details>

- **[Towards Figurative Language Understanding in Moroccan Darija: An Evaluation of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25406)**  `arXiv:2610.25406`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jihad R'baiti, Youssef Hmamouche, Emmanuele Chersoni, Chu-Ren Huang, Amal El Fallah Seghrouchni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Figurative language remains a major challenge for Machine Translation (MT) systems, as the intended meaning of such expressions often goes beyond literal translation. Although recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) improved the translation of Arabic dialects, benchmark resources for evaluating figurative language translation in Moroccan Darija remain scarce. To this end, we introduce the Moroccan Darija Figurative Language Corpus (MDFC), a benchmark dataset for MT and multiple-choice question (MCQ) selection covering idioms, metaphors, and proverbs. Using MDFC, we evaluate 19 open- and closed-weight language models and MT systems at both the expression and contextual levels under zero-shot and six-shot settings. We also conduct layer-wise similarity-based probing to determine how model representations distinguish figurative from literal meanings. Our findings reveal that closed-weight models achieve the strongest translation performance, while the benefits of contextual information vary across models and tasks. Differences between translation performance and MCQ accuracy highlight the distinction between generating an appropriate figurative translation and recognizing the intended meaning among candidate translations.
  </details>

- **[From Unverifiable Domain Text to Cross-Lingual RLVR via Code-Switched Task Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25399)**  `arXiv:2610.25399`  `cs.CL`  
  _Cheonbok Park, Jihwan Kim, Shinyoung Joo, Joosung Lee, Hwiyeol Jo, Jeonghoon Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) can improve reasoning in large language models, but specialized non-English domains expose a data mismatch. Raw English domain text is often available, while target-language RLVR tasks with verifiable rewards are scarce. We introduce code-switched task synthesis for cross-lingual RLVR, a data-centric approach that derives target-language and code-switched RLVR task variants from otherwise unverifiable English domain text. Using fill-in-the-middle multiple-choice QA as a seed task format, we synthesize automatically verifiable tasks from domain passages and reshape them into cross-lingual variants. These variants preserve the same evidence-answer relation but change how English and target-language evidence are presented during RLVR. On Korean medical benchmarks and cybersecurity benchmarks across four target languages, code-switched training outperforms matched monolingual and bilingual-mixture baselines on average target-language performance while maintaining comparable English performance. Additional analyses provide evidence of better target-language input understanding and stronger cross-lingual alignment in both model outputs and internal representations. These results suggest that code-switched training helps target-language inputs access domain reasoning learned more strongly in English.
  </details>

- **[SPARC: Spoken Profile Authorship Recognition Corpus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25265)**  `arXiv:2610.25265`  `cs.CL`  
  _Efthymios Tsaprazlis, Bowen Yi, Tiantian Feng, Junghwan Kim, David Jurgens, Shrikanth Narayanan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speech anonymization has focused on the acoustic signal, leaving open how much speaker information the transcribed words themselves retain. In this work, we study pairwise speaker and demographic-attribute verification from transcribed speech. We introduce SPARC (Spoken Profile Authorship Recognition Corpus), a benchmark built from two podcast corpora comprising over 12,500 speakers, 1.5M quality-filtered utterances, and speaker-level inferred or source-provided labels for sex, age, and accent, and evaluate five attribution models and three LLMs on identity- and attribute-verification trials with episode- and metadata-based topic proxies. LLM adversaries distinguish same-speaker pairs from text alone. Attribute-label agreement is harder to verify, but LLMs perform sex- and accent-label verification above chance. Finally, releasing only short probe texts (50 words) degrades the strongest adversaries the most, offering a partial reduction in observed linkage.
  </details>

- **[Hidden Cartographer: Hidden State Guided Data Selection for Dual Budgeted Instruction Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25215)**  `arXiv:2610.25215`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zixuan Qin, Qi Qin, Erbo Li, Ting Wei, Yifan Sun, Zhaoxin Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Given the high cost of expert annotation and limited fine-tuning resources, effective data selection from large unlabeled pools under strict budgets has become a crucial step in adapting LLMs to specialized domains. Existing label-dependent methods risk distorted selection when using pseudo-labels, while traditional active learning methods do not explicitly characterize instruction-tuning dynamics. To address these limitations, we propose Hidden Cartographer (HC), a two-stage framework that uses hidden states to guide annotation and exact training dynamics to select the final training subset. The first stage is Progressive Candidate Expansion, which utilizes the proxy to iteratively select top unlabeled candidates for expert annotation, merging them to recompute training dynamics and update the proxy until the annotation budget is exhausted; the second stage is Exact Cartography Pruning, which ranks these annotated candidates by their exact dynamics scores to isolate a compact subset under the final training budget. Experiments across the medical, legal, and financial domains demonstrate that HC achieves the highest average accuracy among all budgeted baselines, requiring annotation for only 20--30% of the original pool and fine-tuning on just 10--15%.
  </details>

- **[Acharya 2.0: An AI Tutor for Self-Learning Subjects in Hindi](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25192)**  `arXiv:2610.25192`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shantipriya Parida, Debasish Dhal, Sonal Khosla, Sambit Sekhar, Muhammed Abdur Rahmaan Kamaldeen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text processing is crucial for low-resource languages as it enables the development of tools and technologies that bridge the digital divide and provide equitable access to information and services. By empowering low-resource languages with robust text processing, we can preserve linguistic diversity, enhance education, improve communi- cation, and foster inclusivity in a rapidly digitizing world. Due to the lack of sufficient annotated datasets and lin- guistic resources, it is challenging to develop accurate and reliable language technologies for these low-resource languages. Large Language Models (LLMs) have attracted sig- nificant attention with their performance and the value they produce. However, with the ever-evolving language, adapting LLMs for a specific task is time-consuming and resource-intensive. Thus, Retrieval-Augmented Genera- tion (RAG) has overcome this limitation by augmenting LLMs with external knowledge, thereby improving their efficiency and extensive task-specific adaptation. Most applications of the RAG technique have been limited to English and has seen limited application in In- dic languages. This paper introduces the RAG-powered AI-Tutor application, Acharya 2.0, designed to produce academic assistance to Hindi-speaking students. The client-server-based web application integrates many fea- tures to support students in accessing and interacting with educational content.
  </details>

- **[Do LLMs Reproduce the Age Gap in Loneliness? Evaluating Survey Simulation against European Survey Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25158)**  `arXiv:2610.25158`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuliya Li, Achim Rettinger, Dominik Beyer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are often used to simulate survey respondents, but matching survey averages does not establish accurate demographic differences. We test whether LLM outputs reproduce within-country age gaps in loneliness using the 2022 EU Loneliness Survey, comparing adults aged 18-34 and 65 or older in France, Italy, Poland, and Sweden. We evaluate simulation with and without age information and direct distribution estimation against a probability-based sample, with a non-probability sensitivity reference. Both human samples show higher mean loneliness among younger adults. Under age-specified simulation with standard response-option order, Mistral Small 4 and GPT-5.6 Terra reverse this ordering under all three prompt framings in three and two countries, respectively, mainly by overestimating older-adult means. DeepSeek V4 Flash preserves the ordering everywhere but underestimates both group means, with errors largely cancelling in France and Italy.
