Against the probability-based reference, direct estimation has lower absolute age-gap error than simulation for Mistral and Terra in every country. For DeepSeek, simulation has lower gap error but direct estimation has lower group-mean error; these comparisons depend on low-validity older-adult simulation cells. Evaluating LLM outputs as proxies for age groups within countries therefore requires checking both group means and their contrast against human survey data.
  </details>

- **[Morpheus: a fully local, defense-in-depth de-identification engine for Greek clinical text - a benchmark evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25155)**  `arXiv:2610.25155`  `cs.CL`  
  _Filippos-Paschalis Rorris, Nikoleta Stanitsa, Tatiana Rorri, Foteini Rorri, Constantine N. Antonopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background and Objective: Clinical free text cannot leave the hospital without de-identification, yet no de-identification system or benchmark exists for Greek clinical text. We evaluated Morpheus, a fully local de-identification pipeline for Greek clinical documents, on 500 synthetic discharge summaries. Methods: The notes (300 cardiac surgery, 100 cardiology, 100 nephrology) were generated by a frontier large language model (Gemini 3.1 Pro) constrained to authentic Greek hospital document layouts. Notes were pre-annotated at generation; two clinicians then independently reviewed and corrected every note, blinded to each other, with disagreements arbitrated. Primary outcomes were recall over must-redact identifiers and over-redaction of clinical content, measured over must-survive probes. Secondary outcomes: residual leaks flagged by an independent egress-review layer, processing time and reproducibility. Results: Inter-annotator agreement was F1 = 0.998. The de-identification rate was 96.59% (7,666/7,937 identifier instances; 95% CI 96.16-96.96): 98.49% for direct and 93.42% for quasi-identifiers. No patient's own complete identifier survived in any note; of 271 residual instances, 199 (73.4%) concerned institutional identity, chiefly the hospital letterhead. The over-redaction rate was 2.82% (190/6,747 probes; 95% CI 2.45-3.24). The egress guard flagged 2 of 271 residuals, both complete telephone numbers. Two independent runs gave identical verdicts. Conclusions: A fully local, deterministic pipeline removed 98.5% of direct patient identifiers from Greek clinical text while altering under 3% of protected clinical content; most residuals concerned the institution, not the patient. To our knowledge this is the first benchmark evaluation of clinical-text de-identification for Greek and the first quantified evaluation of an independent egress-guard layer.
  </details>

- **[MemBubble: Benchmarking How Agent Memory Shapes LLM Response Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25127)**  `arXiv:2610.25127`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiajun Tan, Fei Sun, Xinyu Ma, Shixuan Zhang, Du Su, Qi Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory serves as the core component of LLM-based agents, enabling personalized responses via extracting and using user preferences from past interactions. However, its use may reduce response diversity, thereby narrowing the range of information available to users. Existing evaluations rely largely on synthetic interactions and cover few memory systems, leaving the effects of memory on response diversity insufficiently evaluated in real-world scenarios. We introduce MemBubble, a benchmark of 1000 instances built on preferences extracted from real user-assistant conversations. To measure the extent to which memory concentrates response distributions, we construct test queries in scenarios distinct from user preferences while preserving topical relevance through shared preference categories. Across multiple LLMs and memory mechanisms, entropy decreases by 34.7% on average compared with directly providing the full interaction history, highlighting the important role of memory processing in diversity loss. We also explore simple user-side interventions and changes in information availability but find that these do not effectively close the diversity gap. Our findings highlight the need to consider preserving diversity and user access to information alongside improvements in personalization when designing and using agent memory systems.
  </details>

- **[Memories That Outlive Their Reasons: Dependency-Tethered Memory for Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25123)**  `arXiv:2610.25123`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqi Wang, Jinqian Ju, Jiaqi Zhang, Zhangkai Wu, Yiqun Duan, Zequn Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents now act on the Web, publishing packages, models and datasets through registry APIs, and remembering past verifications spares them the work. But a verdict rests on premises. When a signing key is revoked, everything built on it stops passing, yet the advisory names only the key, and the old verdicts of its dependents may never share a retrieval window with it. This non-locality of invalidation turns memory into a liability: in long simulated chats, an agent reading a memory of atomic facts publishes 62% of the objects whose failure lies upstream, each backed by a record that was true when written. Yet derivation is local even when invalidation is not. Tether records, at write time, the exact versions each verdict derives from, and at read time withdraws any verdict whose support has failed anywhere in the store; the agent reads the same fields as before. On Fallout, a generator of versioned worlds with hypothesis-blind simulated histories shared by every memory, Tether publishes 0.3% of upstream-failing objects across three domains when relations are stated with their verdicts, with 37% fewer verification calls than checking everything. A-Mem and Graphiti re-verify most candidates (0.83-0.86 checks per candidate), which limits their savings, yet publish 9% of upstream-failing objects; Mem0 relies on memory more and publishes 42%. Flushing every verdict on any failure is as safe as Tether but costs 32% more checks. The gain over fact memory holds with three open-weight backbones and against a whole-store window. These gains depend on capturing dependencies: when relations arrive in sessions apart from their verdicts, Tether's downstream unsafe publication rate rises to 9.4-22.1%, and a weak writer that misses edges loses safety in the same way.
  </details>

- **[Deep Fusion Memory: A Chunk-Level Parametric Memory for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25100)**  `arXiv:2610.25100`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rubin Wei, Siyuan Sun, Hao Sun, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Shi, Jiarui Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models need effective ways to acquire and use external knowledge beyond what they retain from pretraining. Continued pretraining risks catastrophic forgetting, while retrieval at inference time can add substantial overhead. External parametric memory avoids online retrieval, but constructing its training data requires extensive offline search, and output-level interpolation provides only shallow fusion. We introduce Deep Fusion Memory (DFM), a parametric memory architecture that reduces this construction workload and incorporates learned memory into intermediate hidden states. A causal memory predictor learns to generate memory from the preceding context, using retrieved chunk embeddings from the corpus as supervision. The predicted memory is shared across tokens within each chunk and fused into their hidden states through gated cross-attention. Memory prediction and fusion are trained end to end with the backbone frozen, and inference requires no explicit retrieval. Compared with existing parametric memory approaches, which often construct token-level memory targets, DFM requires $32\times$ fewer retrieval queries and $2048\times$ fewer retrieved items to construct its training data. Across six QA tasks, DFM performs better over the strongest baseline by 12.71% on Mistral-7B-v0.3 and 11.19% on Qwen3-8B-Base. Beyond QA, domain adaptation yields average gains of 6.51 and 4.38 points over Qwen3-4B-Base and Qwen3-8B-Base, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Factual Accuracy: A Benchmark for Evaluating Perspective Alignment in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25087)**  `arXiv:2610.25087`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sunghoon Kim, Sangyeop Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models often generate factually correct responses that fail to maintain user-requested perspectives, blending multiple perspectives and forcing users to filter through unwanted information---a perspective control failure that factual accuracy metrics cannot detect. We introduce PerspectiveBench, a benchmark quantifying perspective alignment by constructing scenarios where multiple valid reasoning paths coexist, and propose the Perspective Alignment Score (PAS) to measure adherence to specified perspectives. Experiments reveal that state-of-the-art models successfully include target content but fail to exclude non-requested perspectives. Providing documents through RAG improves factual grounding but does not resolve perspective drift, as models prioritize comprehensive coverage over strict user constraints. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Measurement Perspective on Benchmarking LLM-Generated Text Detectors using Item Response Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24972)**  `arXiv:2610.24972`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yanbin Zhao, Zhipeng Chen, Guangsheng Bao, Guandong Zhang, Yan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarking large language model (LLM)-generated text detectors commonly relies on aggregate detector-level metrics such as accuracy, F1 score, and AUC. Although useful for summarizing overall performance, these metrics provide limited insight into how benchmark composition shapes detector comparisons or which texts distinguish detectors with different response profiles. We propose a measurement-based benchmarking framework for jointly characterizing benchmark heterogeneity and detector behavior using multidimensional item response theory (IRT). By treating benchmark texts as measurement items and detectors as respondents, the framework estimates text-level discrimination and difficulty-location parameters alongside multidimensional detector abilities. We apply the framework to a benchmark of 32K human-written and machine-generated texts across four domains, evaluated by 14 detectors. The estimated IRT item-parameter space reveals distinct but overlapping human-machine distributions, with substantial variation across domains and text lengths. The human-machine label shows the strongest multivariate association with this space, while domain and text length further contribute to benchmark heterogeneity. Detector ability profiles reveal multidimensional and domain-dependent response patterns that are not fully reflected in aggregate performance rankings. As a practical application, clustering texts in the estimated IRT space yields a compact subset of 2,088 texts (6.5\% of the full 32K benchmark) that largely preserves the comparative detector-performance patterns observed on the full benchmark. Overall, the proposed framework provides a principled and interpretable methodology for benchmarking heterogeneous detection technologies while jointly characterizing the measurement properties of the texts used for their evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Rubrics Carry the Standard: Shapley Attribution for Checklist-Based LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24971)**  `arXiv:2610.24971`  `cs.CL`  
  _Saeid Asgari, Rui Ying, Sara Malvar, Rakshanda Agarwal, Bruce Sun, Claire Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Checklist rubrics applied by large language model (LLM) judges have become a standard instrument for evaluating open-ended outputs and for rewarding them during training. Two assumptions surround this practice: that leniency or strictness is a property of the judge model, and that a rubric can be improved by asking a model to review it. We test both in a controlled grading task with planted flaws and on two human-labeled datasets, SummEdits and RAGTruth. Holding the judge and the outputs fixed, rubric content moves the pass rate of correct outputs from 2% to 100%, whereas changing the judge model or the rendering of the same checks moves it by a few points. Model self-review then drifts the rubric toward the reviser's own standard: it adds requirements the task never stated and deletes checks that human labels had taught, with three reviser models and on all three datasets. To measure and control this, we show that under all-must-pass aggregation the Shapley value of each check has an exact closed form, which yields per-check catch credit, false-fail blame and unique catches in linear time. Attribution shows that self-review deletes the checks that do unique work and keeps the checks that cause false fails. A change-control gate that combines these quantities with a provenance freeze on label-derived checks holds nearly every drifting revision chain at its starting quality, with identical decisions under three judge models.
  </details>

- **[DeGMatch: Target-Aware Reasoning Data Selection via Deconfounded Gradient Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24963)**  `arXiv:2610.24963`  `cs.CL`  
  _Changho Lee, Janghoon Han, Rajdeep Mukherjee, Yeda Song, Lening Nick Cui, Xinya Du, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning post-training on long trajectories has substantially improved the reasoning capabilities of large language models, but scaling such training to large datasets is increasingly expensive. We study target-aware reasoning data selection, where a small reference set specifies a target capability and guides the selection of training trajectories for specialization. We find that existing target-aware methods can exhibit limited target discrimination on long reasoning trajectories, while dominant population-level structure in candidate gradients can obscure finer target-dependent variation during gradient matching. To address these limitations, we introduce Deconfounded Gradient Matching (DeGMatch), which first constructs a target-relevance gate and then reranks candidates using model-specific gradients. DeGMatch centers gradients using the candidate-population mean and removes leading principal directions before target matching, reducing the influence of dominant population-level structure and improving target discrimination. Across mathematics, science, and code, DeGMatch outperforms strong heuristic, target-aware, and reasoning-specific data selection baselines, while remaining effective across selection budgets, model families, and scales. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MisleadQA: Diagnosing LLM Failures under Misleading Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24851)**  `arXiv:2610.24851`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hui Wang, Bohao Chu, Qianli Wang, Shuning Zhang, Norbert Fuhr_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are widely used to answer questions from retrieved or user-provided context. However, provided context can be factually true yet out-of-scope, describing a different entity, time, or location from the one the question targets, and still lead models to a wrong answer. Prior work has mainly studied factually false or irrelevant context. It remains unclear whether such out-of-scope context misleads models as much as false context, and whether adding gold context prevents models from being misled by either. We introduce **MisleadQA**, a diagnostic dataset of multiple-choice questions with an abstention option. Each question pairs gold context (G) with true but out-of-scope context (S) and false but in-scope context (F) supporting the same wrong answer. We evaluate six open-weight LLMs. On questions they answer correctly without context, providing S alone leads them to switch to this wrong answer in 29.3% of cases, and providing F alone does so in 75.4%. Adding G nearly removes the effect of S, but F still misleads models in 21.0% of cases, especially when F comes first. A context-faithful instruction reduces these errors mainly through abstention and lowers accuracy even with G. These findings suggest that evaluations should check whether context applies to the question, not only whether it is true, and should report correct, misled, and abstention rates together. Data and code are available at [this https URL](this https URL).
  </details>

- **[Ask, Don't Guess: Targeted Clarification for Speech Recognition Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24809)**  `arXiv:2610.24809`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sankalp Parashar, Preethi Jyothi, Sunita Sarawagi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speech recognition in real time in far-field conversational settings still incurs significant errors. Identifying potential error spans during streaming ASR permits a recourse mechanism where the speaker can be asked to repeat the affected content under cleaner acoustic conditions. We propose \sysname, a new framework for online error localization, that identifies spans likely to benefit from this intervention. Our supervision uses clean audio to estimate how the recognizer's output might change under improved acoustic conditions. We probabilistically align noisy and clean decodes using a pair-HMM to obtain soft supervision. These soft targets train a counterfactual gain scorer by combining a suite of acoustic and language features to produce token-level scores, that are further grouped into spans for targeted recourse. An LLM uses the ASR transcript of the repetition to revise only the selected span. At a precision of $70\%$, \sysname achieves the highest recall by identifying up to 52\% more erroneous tokens compared to the strongest baseline. Targeted repetition yields relative WER reductions of up to 24\% under a budget that accounts for both interruption frequency and the lengths of re-uttered spans.
  </details>

- **[BanglaBBQ: A Bias Benchmark for Question Answering in the Bangladeshi Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24764)**  `arXiv:2610.24764`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sumit Anthony Gomes, Yasin Rahman, Taeeba Tasnia, Faiza Nooren Suhita_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces BanglaBBQ, a question-answering bias benchmark designed to capture stereotypes grounded in the Bangladeshi context, in both Bangla and English. Following KoBBQ and PakBBQ, BanglaBBQ adapts the original Bias Benchmark for Question Answering (BBQ) by classifying its templates into three groups: directly translated (DT), modified to align target groups or contexts with Bangladesh (TM), and removed when deemed unsuitable for the Bangladeshi context (SR). This process retains 140 templates across six categories, comprising 76 DT and 64 TM templates. Five newly authored (NA) categories, based on stereotypes common in Bangladeshi society, are then added: marital status, age and political perception, regional origin, educational background, and mental-health status. Native Bangladeshi annotators judged all 466 newly authored templates, and 372 were retained because a strict majority of English annotators identified the intended target and rated the attitude as common enough. Each newly authored template is linked to a documented source and a verbatim quote from it. Each evaluated template also has a counter-stereotypical version of its disambiguated context. The dataset was evaluated using two open-weight models (Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B) and two closed models (GPT-4.1-mini and Gemini 2.5 Flash). All four lean towards the stereotyped person when the context is ambiguous, in both languages, and the effect of language differs by model, so English results do not predict behaviour in Bangla. On the adapted BBQ templates, both Qwen3 models are also less accurate when the context contradicts a stereotype than when it confirms it. On the newly authored templates, the closed models follow counter-stereotypical evidence almost perfectly, and all four models are more biased on the templates that annotators validated than on those they rejected.
  </details>

- **[PV-Decoding: Progressive Verification Decoding with Decode Gating for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24722)**  `arXiv:2610.24722`  `cs.CL`  
  _Renxi Liu, Yiming Li, Shengyin Sun, Chen Chen, Xian Wang, Shuai Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Discrete diffusion language models (dLLMs) offer an attractive alternative to autoregressive generation by updating multiple tokens in parallel, but their inference efficiency is still limited by the cost of safely committing tentative predictions. In particular, speculative decoding for dLLMs is challenging because speculative tokens within the same diffusion block may depend on one another, making aggressive acceptance prone to error propagation and strict verification computationally expensive. We present PV-Decoding, a confidence-driven speculative decoding framework with progressive verification for block diffusion inference. PV-Decoding partitions masked positions into directly decoded tokens, speculative tokens, and unresolved tokens using two confidence thresholds, and then verifies speculative tokens with progressively stronger policies, including single-batch, dual-batch, and multi-batch verification. This design exposes a practical accuracy--efficiency trade-off: weaker verification minimizes overhead, whereas stronger verification improves acceptance precision and robustness. Experiments on three diffusion language models across GSM8K, MBPP, and HumanEval show that PV-Decoding reduces sequential model forward passes (MFPs) by 8%--37% relative to confidence-based decoding, while keeping the largest observed accuracy drop to 1.3%. On Dream-7B-Instruct/GSM8K, PV-Decoding (1-batch) and PV-Decoding (2-batch) improve throughput by 7.0%--13.6% under the same hardware budget, whereas PV-Decoding (multi-batch) is slower under the current runtime. These results suggest that confidence-guided speculation with structured verification is a practical path toward efficient and reliable dLLM inference.
  </details>

- **[Safety Kernel: Mechanistic Alignment for Cross-Lingual Defense in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24614)**  `arXiv:2610.24614`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chengyu Xie, Fei Shen, Shuyi Miao, Yue Feng, Jian Yu, Pengyang Shao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The safety capabilities of large language models (LLMs) are highly uneven across different languages, often allowing cross-lingual attacks to bypass safety guardrails. Existing alignment methods predominantly rely on external alignment signals and provide limited insight into underlying neuron-level mechanisms, making it difficult to provide a unified internal target for cross-lingual safety interventions. To address this, we analyze cross-lingual safety behavior at the neuron level and identify a compact set of safety-related neurons shared across languages, termed the safety kernel (SK). Specifically, we first utilize multilingual parallel safe and unsafe prompts to compute safety contrast with activation responsiveness (SCAR) scores, identifying language-wise safety-neuron candidates from internal activation patterns. We then intersect the resulting language-wise safety-neuron sets to obtain the SK, and validate its functional relevance to cross-lingual safety behavior through activation suppression and activation-state transfer interventions. Finally, building on the SK coordinates, we introduce SK-Masked LoRA, which constrains LoRA adaptation to the feed-forward network (FFN) output dimensions corresponding to the SK, thereby focusing safety-related changes on the cross-lingually shared neuron coordinates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SK-Masked LoRA substantially reduces attack success rates across multiple languages, including languages excluded from SK discovery, while intervening on only a small fraction of internal neuron coordinates and producing generally limited changes in the model's general capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Ben-Misandry: A Bengali Dataset and Late-Fusion Transformer Framework for Detecting Anti-Male Gender Hate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24474)**  `arXiv:2610.24474`  `cs.CL`  
  _Md Mehedi Hasan Nipu, Chinmoy Mitra, Tarannum Ahmed Nowshin, Israt Moyeen Noumi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gender based hate speech research in Bengali has focused almost exclusively on misogyny, leaving anti male hate, or misandry, entirely unstudied for this language and creating a systematic blind spot in moderation systems trained to catch attacks on women but not on men. We introduce Ben-Misandry, the first Bengali misandry detection dataset: 5,000 comments across six taxonomy classes, including a novel category for mocking male emotional vulnerability with no direct analogue in existing misogyny taxonomies. The corpus is taxonomy-controlled synthetic data generated with GPT-4.1, spot-checked by four human reviewers on a 500-comment sample who confirmed the large majority of assigned labels; a full inter-annotator- agreement study is left to future work. We benchmark it with a classical pipeline (seven classifiers over four text representations) and a transformer pipeline (BanglaBERT, XLM-RoBERTa, and two fusion strategies). The best classical model, a linear SVM over character-level TF-IDF, reaches 91.8% accuracy; a late fusion ensemble averaging BanglaBERT and XLM-RoBERTa softmax outputs reaches 96.6% accuracy and macro ROC-AUC above 99%, nominally ahead of every single model and early fusion, though we have not run significance testing to confirm these margins are meaningful. We relate this to classifier combination theory, under which averaging encoders with decorrelated errors is expected to reduce variance, and corroborate the decision boundary with Shapley explanations that recover the same class- discriminative vocabulary found in our exploratory analysis, while noting this cannot rule out the model partly keying on synthetic-generation artifacts. Our results indicate misandry detection in Bengali is feasible and measurable on this synthetic corpus, offered as an initial public resource pending validation on real-world data.
  </details>

- **[Ask Less, Ask Better: Allocating User Attention in Proactive LLM Agents with Interruption Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24411)**  `arXiv:2610.24411`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xuanming Zhang, Amir H. Rezaeian, Miguel Ballesteros, Weiyi Sun, Werner Geyer, Lydia B. Chilton, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can ask clarifying questions to complete tasks, but answering each question demands users' time and effort. Existing evaluations typically measure this burden by counting questions, treating necessary and avoidable questions equally. We propose an evaluation protocol that distinguishes how often agents ask from how well they ask. An interruption budget limits the number of questions, while a user-attention cost charges extra for questions that are hard to read or not worth asking, as rated by a human-validated LLM judge. Beyond question counts, this cost captures perceived effort: on conversation pairs with the same number of questions, human annotators agreed with the cost on which conversation required more effort 75% of the time. Across eight LLMs and four customer-service domains, limiting agents to one to three questions reduces user-attention cost by 36-73% while keeping 86-94% of the task reward they earn with unlimited questions. At the same budget, reviewing each question against the memory of users' earlier answers cuts attention cost by another 13% with little reward loss and yields more valuable questions. Models also differ sharply in how well they ask: at the same user-attention cost, the most efficient models gain more than twice as much reward from their questions as the others. These results show that asking less and asking better are distinct goals, and that evaluations of proactive agents should measure both.
  </details>

- **[FlashMap: Flow Map Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24365)**  `arXiv:2610.24365`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeff Shen, Zhejian Zhou, Ryan Lee, Jonathan May, Shirley Ho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates large language model (LLM) inference by using a lightweight drafter to propose a block of tokens that the target model verifies in one parallel pass. Autoregressive drafters generate this block one token at a time, whereas block diffusion drafters such as DFlash generate it in one pass, making drafting faster. However, these diffusion drafters predict each token independently, ignoring dependencies between tokens in the block. We introduce FlashMap, a continuous flow drafter that conditions a flow map language model on the target model's hidden states and generates the whole block in one forward pass while capturing inter-token dependencies. On Qwen3-8B, FlashMap is faster than a matched DFlash baseline on every benchmark, with 7% higher average speedup and up to 6.1x lossless acceleration.
  </details>

- **[Best of Both Worlds: Fine-Tuned Small Models Complement LLMs for Cost and Performance Benefits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24260)**  `arXiv:2610.24260`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shivam Mathur, Sri Harish Popuri, Priya Krishnan Sundararajan, Kevin Horecka, Michael Mugo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  BERT-based models require thousands of labeled samples to train. Larger models like GPT-X require only 1-5 examples per class. However, performance, cost and time tradeoffs of such models remain under-explored. In this work, we classify survey text data into 16 classes. We focus on experimental approaches and results on performance, cost, and time taken for this problem. We compare these parameters of a trained RoBERTa-based model, and an (off-the-shelf) GPT-3.5 model for text classification. Baseline experiments show that the time taken by GPT-3.5 for generating predictions on 10,000 records is 6 hours, whereas a RoBERTa-based model takes only 12 mins on a T4 GPU. To identify the best prompting approach for GPT-3.5, we explore batch prompting. We experiment with (1) batch-size, and (2) few-shot sampling. We find the best batch size is 20 with human few-shot curation. Experiments show the F1 score of a trained RoBERTa-based model (96%) is much higher than a GPT-3.5 model (83%). In case of GPT-3.5, batch prompting is beneficial in lowering inference costs by ~70%, without hindering task performance. We find the best approach is an ensemble of a RoBERTa-based and GPT-3.5 model (F1: 97.5%) incurring only 4.4% of inference cost compared to a standalone GPT-3.5.
  </details>

- **[SLA-LENS: From Classroom Transcripts to CSLA-Informed and Evidence-Linked L2 Interaction Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24259)**  `arXiv:2610.24259`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nemika Tyagi, Olga Kellert, Phanos Kareen, Nelvin Licona-Guevara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What happens after a learner makes an error, and how does the instructor's response shape the next turn? Investigating these exchanges across classrooms requires context-sensitive annotation that is difficult to scale manually. We introduce SLA-LENS, an end-to-end LLM-assisted framework that connects classroom transcripts to structured pedagogical annotations and evidence-linked descriptive analytics. Grounded in interactionist Second Language Acquisition (SLA) theory, its annotation schema captures second language learner errors, pedagogical functions, and uptake or repair while accounting for speaker roles, conversational context, and hierarchical labels. We apply the framework to 40 authentic online L2 Spanish classroom transcripts comprising over 21,000 sentence-level instances. We evaluate proprietary and open-weight LLMs, including task-specific QLoRA adaptation, using expert-reviewed gold annotations, held-out silver data, and metrics sensitive to different types of annotation error. A deterministic reporting layer generates reproducible visualizations of participation, feedback, learner errors, uptake, and temporal interaction patterns, linked to supporting transcript evidence. By making these patterns accessible and inspectable, SLA-LENS supports systematic classroom discourse research, pedagogical reflection, and curriculum design.
  </details>

- **[Language Models are Lazy Poets: Creative Mode Collapse at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24223)**  `arXiv:2610.24223`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jacob A. Matthews, Sil Hamilton_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While aligned large language models (LLMs) can successfully complete many creative tasks, their outputs tend to be highly self-similar. Though solutions to this problem of "mode collapse" have been proposed, comprehensive large-scale surveys of the phenomenon in specific textual genres are still lacking. We address this gap through an extensive treatment of LLM mode collapse in poetry. Across 15 poetic forms, 248 subject matter tags, and 16 open-weight models, we generate over 1.1 million poems. To our knowledge, this constitutes the largest corpus of LLM-generated poetry to date. At million-poem scale, we find that LLM poetic vocabularies and rhyming abilities are substantial, but severely underutilized. In other words, LLMs are "lazy" poets: despite possessing large poetic inventories, LLMs over-rely on a small number of words, opening phrases, and rhymes. Further, we find that lexical homogenization differs across models and poetic forms, noting observed rhyming vocabulary is correlated with model size.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning with Context Management and External Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24187)**  `arXiv:2610.24187`  `cs.CL`  
  _Man-Ling Sung, William Hartmann, Jiangying Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning models maintain information about the status of their reasoning process through in-context memory. Complex problems require larger context windows and memory requirements. In this scenario, context management without caching risks discarding useful information for reasoning. We propose RCMEM, an approach that extends context management with an external memory to maintain additional information without burdening the reasoning model with additional context. RCMEM significantly reduces computation time on problems that require repeated computation of intermediate steps, while achieving better performance on complex problems. We demonstrate our approach on a few mathematics problems using model sizes ranging from 0.4 to 25 million parameters, and GPT-2.
  </details>

- **[Multi-author style detection with stylistic embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24184)**  `arXiv:2610.24184`  `cs.CL`  
  _Man-Ling Sung, Chak Fai Li, Damianos Karakos, Fred Bernardin, Brian Ulicny_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in multi-author style change detection. Using off-the-shelf LLMs or supervised fine-tuning solely within the target domain fails to yield effective features; for this reason, we propose cross-domain transfer learning with LLM-based embeddings fine-tuned for authorship verification. We fine-tune Mistral-7B embedding model and compare it with DeBERTa, used in the state-of-the-art (SOTA) systems in PAN-25 multi-author writing style analysis challenge. Results show that DeBERTa and Mistral excel in different user-generated text scenarios. We exploit this diversity by combining both embeddings, leading to more effective style change detection.
  </details>

- **[Translating in style: An open-weight alternative to GPT-4 for culturally aligned machine translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23868)**  `arXiv:2610.23868`  `cs.CL`  
  _Thomas Rietman, Hadi Mohammadi, Robert A. Bagheri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine translation systems often preserve meaning but systematically flatten linguistic style, a failure that is most acute when translating into non-Western languages. The RASTA framework of Havaldar et al. (2025a) addresses this through retrieval-augmented stylistic alignment, but relies on GPT-4, a closed, proprietary model. We investigate whether an open-weight translation specialist, Hunyuan-MT-7B, can serve as an open-weight alternative. We evaluate three variants of the model, the base model, a LoRA supervised fine-tune, and a GRPO-steered policy. We do this across three style dimensions (formality, politeness, and intimacy) and twelve directed language pairs, measuring style alignment with the correlation-based metric $\mathcal{A}$. We find that fine-tuning does not reliably improve alignment: the three variants are statistically indistinguishable in aggregate. The open backbone does not match RASTA-GPT-4 overall, but the shortfall is concentrated in formality and largely closes for intimacy and Chinese directions, where the open model matches or surpasses GPT-4 on individual pairs. An accessible RASTA pipeline built on an open model is thus already viable for parts of the problem.
  </details>

- **[ReaDy: Recurrent States as Dynamic Context for Speculative Drafting in Hybrid Linear Attention Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20503)**  `arXiv:2610.20503`  `cs.CL`  
  _Bohan Sun, Zunhai Su, Xialie Zhuang, Jianjin Li, Ngai Wong, Chuan-Wei Kuo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates autoregressive generation by drafting multiple tokens per step, yet feature-based drafters rely on target hidden features whose contextual emphasis may not align with the needs of subsequent draft positions. Hybrid linear attention (HLA) models maintain compact recurrent states that accumulate historical associations, offering an efficient source of complementary context for speculative drafting. We introduce ReaDy, the first speculative decoding method, to the best of our knowledge, to leverage HLA recurrent states for position-specific context retrieval during drafting. ReaDy equips each draft position with a learned query to retrieve supplementary context directly from the target's committed recurrent states. A lightweight state reader integrates this context into draft representations through residual updates, while low-rank state reads and fused operators keep additional computation and I/O overhead low. Building on DSpark's parallel backbone and sequential prediction head, ReaDy employs a lightweight two-stage adaptation strategy, with reader capacity, state sources, and residual placement examined through ablation studies. Across seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, and dialogue, ReaDy achieves an average $3.16\times$ decoding speedup over autoregressive decoding with Qwen3.8-27B on a single NVIDIA B200 GPU. It outperforms DSpark on every benchmark, improving throughput by 7.97% and acceptance length by 8.50% on average. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RouteWeaver: Weaving Mode Selection and Execution into Unified LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19705)**  `arXiv:2610.19705`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaohan Wang, Haozhen Zhang, Qingyuan Liu, Tao Feng, Wenya Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM routing dynamically selects and coordinates heterogeneous language models across diverse tasks. However, existing approaches are largely built around a predefined execution paradigm, such as single-round, multi-round, or agentic routing, which fixes how model calls are organized and limits adaptation to different query needs. We propose RouteWeaver, a unified routing framework that integrates these routing modes within a single LLM policy, jointly learning which mode to use and how to select models within that mode. To jointly optimize mode selection and within-mode execution, we develop COMET-GRPO (COordinated Mode-Execution Training), which decouples the trajectory-level advantage into separate signals for mode selection and within-mode execution, enabling more targeted credit assignment to each decision level. We further introduce progressive exploration, which gradually shifts training from externally assigned modes to autonomous selection, so that the router first learns how to execute each mode before deciding when to use it. We evaluate RouteWeaver across mathematics, coding, knowledge, logical reasoning, and factual recall. RouteWeaver achieves an average accuracy of 61.4%, an 11.03% relative improvement over the strongest baseline, and outperforms all compared baselines in each of the five domains. Ablations show that both COMET-GRPO and progressive exploration are important for adaptive routing; without progressive exploration, the routing policy collapses entirely to single-round execution.
  </details>

- **[Are We Really Measuring Forgetting? Pitfalls in Evaluating Machine Unlearning for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19613)**  `arXiv:2610.19613`  `cs.CL`  
  _Oscar Chew, Po-Yi Lu, Yi-Jie Cheng, Kunal Jain, Hsuan-Tien Lin, Zhengzhong Tu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As machine unlearning for multimodal large language models (MLLMs) rapidly advances, rigorous evaluation of its effectiveness becomes increasingly important. However, existing benchmarks largely inherit evaluation protocols from text-only unlearning and overlook the unique challenges of unlearning in multimodal settings. In this work, we identify three major pitfalls that may lead to misleading conclusions: the lack of heldout evaluation to distinguish genuine concept-wise forgetting from instance-wise overfitting, the reliance on synthetic, fine-tuned knowledge rather than real-world knowledge acquired during pre-training, and unfair method comparisons caused by applying the same training settings to different unlearning methods. To address these challenges, we introduce GEMMU (Generalizable Evaluation for Multimodal Machine Unlearning), a benchmark built on real-world knowledge and a principled evaluation protocol. Re-evaluating representative unlearning methods under GEMMU reveals substantial generalization gaps and challenges conclusions drawn from previous benchmarks. Our findings highlight the importance of rigorous evaluation for advancing multimodal machine unlearning and provide guidelines for future benchmark design.
  </details>

- **[DUALVIEW: Leveraging Broad and Output-Specific Evidence for LLM-based Multi-Output Text Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19206)**  `arXiv:2610.19206`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yiheng Su, Junyi Jessy Li, Matthew Lease_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While work on LLM-based regression has largely focused on scalar prediction, multi-output text regression remains less explored. We present DualView, a parameter-efficient approach to leverage LLMs for multi-output text regression. DualView first extracts broad and output-specific representations from a frozen LLM, then uses a dual-branch architecture to produce the full score vector. Across five heterogeneous regression tasks and eight LLMs (up to 122B), DualView outperforms seven supervised regression baselines while requiring 82--1842 times fewer fitted parameters than the three strongest baselines. These improvements extend across individual output dimensions and persist even with limited training data. Notably, DualView using a smaller open-weight LLM can outperform unsupervised prediction with Gemini 3.1 Pro. Controlled ablations show that broad and output-specific evidence provide complementary predictive benefits, while lightweight shared predictors achieve strong performance with fewer trainable parameters.
  </details>

- **[HERO: Harnessing Evidence for Test-time Refinement and Prompt Optimization in Program Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19184)**  `arXiv:2610.19184`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyu Lu, Timothy Ossowski, Tristan Naumann, Hoifung Poon, Peniel Argaw_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness optimization improves LLMs by refining reusable prompts and scaffolding, yet requires further adaptation for program generation tasks such as text-to-SQL and text-to-Python. Existing methods often rely on LLM reflectors to propose harness updates, which can fix observed failures but also disrupt previously correct behavior. Text-to-code tasks offer a natural way to mitigate this issue: we can execute generated outputs, allowing us to evaluate and verify the effects of proposed updates directly. Building on this signal, we introduce HERO Harnessing Evidence for Refinement and Optimization), which combines offline and test-time harness optimization. At the first stage, HERO learns reusable prompt updates from execution-verified successes and failures and validates candidate revisions before adoption. At test time, we treat the generated code as an instance-specific harness and refine it using execution feedback. Across four SQL, Cypher, and Python benchmarks, HERO achieves the best offline performance, improving pass@1 by 7.6 points over the initial prompt, while test-time refinement adds another 2.0 points and improves execution efficiency.
  </details>

- **[UZHCL_for_the_win at Touche: Encoder Fine-Tuning, Synthetic Augmentation, and an Underpowered Encoder-LLM Comparison](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17077)**  `arXiv:2610.17077`  `cs.CL`  
  _Stylianos Psychias_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Online discussions on platforms like Reddit are a productive testbed for fallacy detection: short texts, broad topical range, and the full inventory of informal fallacies. The Touche 2026 lab at CLEF formalises this as a shared task on a Reddit-derived corpus that combines fallacy detection with argument-scheme classification. This paper describes our participation in all three subtasks (ST1 binary fallacy detection, ST2 eight-way fallacy classification, ST3 four-way argument-scheme classification) and both evaluation tracks. Our submitted systems are fine-tuned RoBERTa-large encoders with task-specific heads, augmented with a small batch of LLM-generated synthetic contrastive pairs; for ST2 and ST3 on the enhanced track we additionally added confidence-filtered pseudo-labelled examples from the unlabelled pool. A wider backbone comparison spanning additional encoders (DeBERTa-v3, ModernBERT) and an LLM track (four Qwen variants from 3B to 32B, zero-shot through CoT-LoRA) showed no validated benefit over RoBERTa-large at this corpus scale, with 5-fold variance wide enough that we call the comparison underpowered rather than settled. We also characterise how much of the enhanced track's lift reflects organiser-rewritten inputs carrying label information rather than genuine argument clarification. On the official Touche 2026 test set our systems ranked first on both base-track classification cells (ST2 and ST3) and second on both binary-detection (ST1) tracks, with the two enhanced-track classification cells topped by cascaded and multitask systems. We close with the rare-class limitation on ST3 and the most useful follow-ups. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Where Is the "Self" of a Large Language Model? -- From Self-Index to Self-Reference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16717)**  `arXiv:2610.16717`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuchen Li, Xintong Li, Zongqi Fan, Ken-tye Yong, Nguyen H. Tran_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We separate two functional levels of self familiar from biology: a self-index, which separates one's own from external material and shapes processing, as corollary discharge does in nearly all animals, and self-reference, the higher ability to recognize what is one's own, as in mirror self-recognition. We probe both by placing the same text in an Assistant, User, or System message rendered by the model's own chat template. In Qwen3.8-27B we report three levels of evidence. Persistence: as predicted in advance, the representation of the text separates the model's own message from both external sources at all 64 layers and for every text. Coupling: asked who wrote the preceding message, the model claims its own messages (0.77) and assigns User and System messages alike to the user, and how strongly it claims each message is predicted by the size of its self-index (Spearman up to 0.68). Intervention: exchanging the internal states that follow a message reverses the self-attribution between layers 15 and 31; removing the self-index direction from them at layer 39 lowers the claim more than a random direction; and in arithmetic the model adopts a wrong step more often when it wrote the step itself (0.92 versus 0.75). Across eight open-weight models from four families, the levels are nested and do not follow model size: three models show neither, three a self-index without self-reference, Qwen3.8-Flash-Next recognizes its own messages only after reasoning (0.66, none of the external ones), and only Qwen3.8-27B shows both without thinking; no model shows self-reference without a self-index, and reasoning brings it out only where one exists. We propose that self-reference is a higher-level ability built on a self-index, a content-independent displacement superimposed on the whole representation of the model's own messages, and that neither level is a by-product of problem-solving ability.
  </details>

- **[LoGRA: Scaling LLM Reinforcement Learning with Low-Rank Gradient Sketches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06647)**  `arXiv:2610.06647`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shaokun Zhang, Yifan Zhang, Jian Hu, Yueying Li, Hao Zhang, Binfeng Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning has greatly advanced the capabilities of large language models, but its memory demands remain a barrier to broader adoption. We introduce LoGRA, an approach to RL post-training that reduces memory by retaining useful learning signals in low-rank gradient sketches. These compact representations support both model updates and efficient policy synchronization. To prevent overly large updates from disrupting learning, we complement gradient compression with predicted-KL step control, which estimates policy changes before applying each update and adjusts its magnitude accordingly. With all techniques combined, LoGRA reduces average training memory usage by up to 45.7% across reasoning tasks without compromising performance. It also enables stable training of a 27B-parameter model for over 1,100 steps on a single eight-GPU node, where dense Adam runs out of memory, making previously memory-infeasible RL training practical. Code is available in the \href{this https URL}{Molt library}.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Biomedical Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02949)**  `arXiv:2610.02949`  `cs.CL`  
  _Songtao Li, Yijia Zhang, Jianyuan Yuan, Shidi Zhang, Fengyu Zhang, Hongfei Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction tuning has become a common paradigm for applying large language models (LLMs) to biomedical named entity recognition (BioNER). However, existing instruction-tuning approaches still face two key challenges. First, conventional natural-language instructions typically serialize BioNER annotations as flat textual outputs, providing limited structural constraints for typed entity extraction. Second, high-quality biomedical annotations are limited, and learning from a single serialized output form may restrict structural diversity and reduce model robustness. Although external biomedical knowledge can be introduced to alleviate data scarcity, it often requires costly resource construction. To address these challenges, we propose MITE, a Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble method for BioNER. MITE reformulates BioNER as a structure-to-structure generation task by representing both instructions and entity outputs in code-formatted representations. Specifically, each training instance is transformed into multiple programming-language formats, including Python, C++, and Java, while preserving the same underlying entity semantics. These language-specific representations provide structurally diverse supervision without requiring external biomedical knowledge or additional annotations. During inference, MITE aggregates predictions from different code formats through an entity-level voting strategy, reducing language-specific prediction variance and improving robustness. Experiments on three widely used BioNER datasets demonstrate that MITE achieves competitive performance against representative BERT-based and LLM-based baselines, attaining the best results on two benchmarks. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of multi-language representations.
  </details>

- **[Debias It Yourself: Teaching LLMs Cognitive Bias Mitigation Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.40124)**  `arXiv:2609.40124`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chahat Raj, Sina Mansouri, Aylin Caliskan, Antonios Anastasopoulos, Ziwei Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bias has long been studied in social psychology and cognitive science, where decades of research have produced a body of validated interventions that reduce stereotypical thinking and prejudiced responses in humans. We propose Debias It Yourself (DIY), a cognitively grounded framework that translates five such interventions into debiasing procedures for large language models and delivers them through three established paradigms: Show (in-context examples), Train (instruction tuning), and Revise (guided self-revision). Across three models, five bias benchmarks, eleven debiasing baselines, and three reasoning benchmarks, Train+Revise and Revise alone attain the top two average ranks, lead the bias-reasoning tradeoff (mean bias as low as 2% at 90% reasoning accuracy), and reduce bias on unseen dimensions by up to 14.8%. Our code and data are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[ATTUNER: Recomputation-Free Agentic Context KV Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36722)**  `arXiv:2609.36722`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinghao Chen, Junnan Dong, Cai Ke, Chak Tou Leong, Haocheng Sun, Keyu Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents repeatedly load reusable content, such as skills, documents, and memory entries, into the current context. Re-encoding this content for every request wastes computation. Position-independent caching (PIC) alleviates this by encoding each artifact independently and reusing its key-value (KV) states at arbitrary positions, but it incurs a quality loss relative to full-context prefill. Existing methods repair this loss by restoring global position IDs or recomputing selected tokens. In this work, we isolate the source of the loss, finding that the positional mismatch has minor effect, and independently cached artifacts retain faithful representations: reading a provided artifact stays largely accurate, and performance degrades only when the model must select among multiple artifacts. Moreover, replacing PIC's attention scores with full-prefill scores recovers performance with the cached KV unchanged, localizing the failure to the attention rather than KV recomputation. Motivated by this, we propose \textsc{Attuner}, a query-side adaptation method that learns to read a frozen artifact cache. \textsc{Attuner} inserts low-rank adapters into the query projections and is trained by distilling full-prefill distribution into the student. It trains fewer than 0.05\% of the model parameters and, at inference, requires neither cache recomputation nor a full-context reference. On Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B across seven benchmarks covering skills, documents, memory, and code, \textsc{Attuner} substantially outperforms prior PIC baselines in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings, matches full-context prefill quality while providing up to $3.73\times$ speedup.
  </details>

- **[Move by Move: Measuring and Steering How LLMs Conduct Psychotherapy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21325)**  `arXiv:2608.21325`  `cs.CL`  
  _Afonso Baldo, Hugo Pitorro, Areti Vassilopoulos, Anabela C. Areias, Maya D'Eon, Fabíola Costa, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As the demand for mental health services outpaces clinical workforce capacity, users are increasingly turning to large language models (LLMs) for accessible and private support. Although recent evaluations suggest LLM care can reduce clinical symptoms, how models actually conduct therapeutic interactions compared to trained human clinicians remains largely unknown. Drawing on established clinical psychology frameworks that conceptualize therapy as a sequential decision-making process, we can systematically analyze the conversational interventions used in AI-driven sessions. To achieve this, we introduce an original, clinically validated ontology of ten distinct therapeutic moves to descriptively categorize strategies LLMs favor or neglect. Applying this ontology at scale reveals that models drastically over-use probing, disregard common CBT-favored techniques and are strongly context-anchored. Moreover, we propose exposing these categories as callable tools, bringing models significantly closer to the human clinical baseline.
  </details>

- **[Hear2Act: Benchmarking When Prosody Should Change What an Assistant Does](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19515)**  `arXiv:2608.19515`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinyi Liu, Hooshang Nayyeri, Dilek Hakkani-Tur, Emine Yilmaz, Joo-Kyung Kim, Yifei Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prosodic cues can carry task-relevant information that words omit, but existing benchmarks rarely test whether it changes downstream decisions. We introduce Hear2Act, a 480-scenario protocol with unstated user needs and verifiable task success. Across matched scenarios, we keep the task and user needs fixed, varying whether an unmet need is stated in words or absent from the transcript while the user's reaction is conveyed through prosody, yielding Explicit lexical and Prosody-mediated conditions. We vary assistant access to transcript, audio, and an indication of whether the recommendation is resolved, and evaluate two audio-capable LLMs and five text LLMs on behavior and task outcomes. Our results show an asymmetry: prosodic information is valuable when the need is absent from the transcript, but adds little when that need is stated in words. Yet neither audio-capable LLM benefits much from raw audio: adding audio to the transcript changes task success only from 14.3% to 15.1%. When models infer from audio whether the recommendation remains unresolved and use that inference with the transcript, success rises to 39.6%, close to 40.5% with ground-truth state. This reveals a perception-action gap: raw audio barely helps, while making the information explicit in text enables much better decisions.
  </details>

- **[Security and Privacy Taxonomy Generation from Mobile App Reviews](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09049)**  `arXiv:2608.09049`  `cs.CL`  
  _Moghis Fereidouni, Krishna Upadhyay, Umar Farooq, A.B. Siddique_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mobile app reviews are a rich, continuously renewing source of how users experience privacy and security, yet existing taxonomies of these concerns are hand-crafted and cannot keep pace with the evolving nature of the data. Automating taxonomy construction is the natural response, but scalability is the core challenge: current LLM- and clustering-based methods are developed for scientific corpora of a few thousand documents and do not extend to app review collections numbering in the hundreds of thousands. We address this gap in two ways. First, we filter app reviews for privacy- and security-related content, yielding a comprehensive corpus of over 600K reviews. Second, we introduce TaxoScale, a pipeline that handles taxonomy construction at this scale by extending an expert-defined taxonomy via Recursive Hierarchical Clustering and LLM-based node naming. TaxoScale outperforms strong automatic-taxonomy baselines on path, level, coverage, and novelty metrics, and discovers novel branches absent from prior taxonomies. We publicly release our code and data to support reproducibility and future research.
  </details>

- **[Trust Before Fusion: QIMG-7 and Source-Aware Resolution for Polluted Multimodal RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.10798)**  `arXiv:2607.10798`  `cs.CL`  
  _Saadeldine Eletter, Owais Aijaz, Preslav Nakov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is often evaluated with clean evidence, yet real retrieval can return topically relevant but unreliable content: false text and misleading images from corrupted metadata, entity swaps, typographic overlays, semantic edits, adversarial patches, blends, or style transfer. We introduce QIMG-7, a controlled benchmark for multimodal retrieval pollution in multi-sentence factual QA, spanning four datasets, seven image-attack families, and 16 paired clean/polluted regimes, for 1,760 evaluation rows per method. Across four generator/gate stacks, naive multimodal fusion is brittle: in the main gpt-4o-mini stack, Full-MM support drops from 0.908 with clean text to 0.490 with polluted text, often making Parametric fallback safer than retrieval. We propose source-aware trust resolution (SATR), a training-free approach that compares Parametric, Text-only, and Full-MM candidate answers and selects among candidate answers or falls back based on source reliability. The Field-Selector variant achieves the best balanced score, 0.816, improving over Full-MM by 11.7 points and over the Cascaded Router by 2.7 points. Ablations show that, in this text-first setting, explicit text-reliability modeling is the dominant driver of these gains. Overall, in text-first factual QA with multimodal retrieval conflict, our results support selective trust rather than unconditional fusion. Artifacts are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Mechanism-Aligned Observability for Training Instability of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28116)**  `arXiv:2606.28116`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruixuan Huang, Yipei Wang, Wenyi Fang, Hantao Huang, Ansheng You, Shuai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier large language model training consumes massive accelerator fleets over long wall-clock times, making stability failures extremely costly. However, global symptoms such as loss divergences often emerge only after the underlying fault has already affected the training dynamics, further increasing the sunk compute. In which internal representation can a training fault become persistently and interpretably observable before it appears in global training metrics? We propose a mechanism-aligned principle for training observability: identify the internal state into which a fault is persistently written, derive the structure induced by its mechanism, and construct monitors in representations aligned with the computation of the affected module. For FlashAttention, we show that biased backward errors accumulate into a persistent low-dimensional structure in weight updates, producing spectral concentration. For MoE routers, we characterize routing dynamics through per-token routing behavior rather than parameter similarity, which may change without altering the routing function. Experiments show that the two monitors respond selectively to changes in their corresponding modules, before degradation becomes visible in global training metrics.
  </details>

- **[AGORA: An Archive-Grounded Benchmark for Agentic Workplace Document Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24526)**  `arXiv:2606.24526`  `cs.CL`  
  _Honglin Guo, Qi Zhang, Yu Zhang, Weijie Li, Rui Zheng, Zhikai Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly deployed as agents that reason over documents rather than answer from parametric knowledge. We study archive-grounded reasoning: locating sparse evidence across a large, messy collection of workplace files, reconciling inconsistent terminology, units, and time conventions, and computing an answer. Existing benchmarks address only parts of this setting and none jointly stresses archive-groundedness, agentic exploration, and cross-domain coverage. We introduce Agora, a benchmark pairing 362 questions with eight domain collections of 9,664 authentic documents and 372M tokens, far exceeding any model's context window, so agents must explore deliberately rather than scan exhaustively. Agora is built by an agentic pipeline combining cross-document task synthesis, leakage-preventing obfuscation, and difficulty filtering. Evaluating eight models, we find the task far from solved: even the strongest reaches only 59.4% accuracy, with notable variation across domains.
  </details>

- **[EvoBrowseComp: Benchmarking Search Agents on Evolving Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.13120)**  `arXiv:2606.13120`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yunhan Wang, Jiaan Wang, Lianzhe Huang, Xianfeng Zeng, Fandong Meng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Search Agents -- large language models augmented with search tools -- have intensified the need for future-proof evaluation benchmarks. Existing benchmarks such as BrowseComp rely on static knowledge, making them vulnerable to test-set contamination and parametric memorization. Consequently, models can achieve high scores through fact recall rather than genuine retrieval, obscuring true browsing competence via reasoning shortcuts.In this paper, we introduce EvoBrowseComp, an evolving benchmark of 400 English and 400 Chinese contamination-free complex questions synthesized via live-web traversal. To collect these questions, we design a three-agent collaborative framework: (1) a QA synthesis agent that retrieves fresh knowledge from the live web to synthesize QA pairs; (2) an information filtering agent that filters retrieved knowledge in terms of credibility and popularity to block parametric shortcuts; and (3) a high-level guidance agent that formalizes questions into reasoning graphs to reduce logical redundancy and shortcuts in synthesized QA pairs. Because the framework supports fully automated synthesis, EvoBrowseComp can be regularly updated to prevent data contamination and maintain temporal freshness. Extensive experiments confirm its great difficulty, requiring broad horizontal search. It establishes a scalable paradigm for auto-updatable, high-difficulty benchmarking that keeps pace with both evolving world knowledge and advancing agent capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Not What, But How: A Framework for Auditing LLM Responses across Positioning, Generalization, Anthropomorphism, and Maxims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02493)**  `arXiv:2606.02493`  `cs.CL`  
  _Siddhesh Milind Pawar, Sarah Masud, Haneul Yoo, Alice Oh, Isabelle Augenstein_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are being increasingly used to answer subjective, information-seeking questions, where users are sensitive to how responses are communicated, not just whether the answers are correct. Existing LLM evaluations for subjective cultural queries largely focus on factual correctness, ignoring how the response is framed. To this end, we introduce FRANZ, an automated FRAmework for respoNse characteriZation to conduct communicative audit of LLM responses along four dimensions: cultural positioning, use of generalizing language, anthropomorphic cues, and adherence to conversational maxims. To enable this evaluation, we contribute SQUARE - a corpus of 376k subjective questions sourced from 57 subreddits, and mapped to 7 countries and 19 question categories. We demonstrate FRANZ's applicability by scoring responses from three open-weight LLMs. We observe that LLMs show statistically significant differences in the frequency with which they employ each response characteristic. Unlike single-dimensional audits, FRANZ reveals that insider positioning and anthropomorphism are positively coupled, with the degree of coupling varying by country, providing a diagnostic lens for identifying framing divergences.
  </details>

- **[Not All Flips Are Conformity: Decomposing Stance Convergence in Multi-Agent LLM Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00820)**  `arXiv:2606.00820`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiqi Hao, Zengqing Wu, Yu-Xuan Qiu, Chuan Xiao, Ruiqi Xu, Shuyuan Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent debate (MAD) among large language models (LLMs) is often judged by answer changes and convergence, yet these outcomes alone cannot establish that peers improved the answer. We study this gap with matched self-reflection, stance-only, and full-reasoning conditions. With five GPT-4o agents on MMLU-Pro disagreement questions, 37% of agent-question pairs change even without peer information. Among pairs stable under this control, 29.3% adopt a peer answer after seeing peer stances. These adoptions include corrections, new errors, and changes between wrong answers. The outcomes vary with correct-answer availability. When a correct peer answer is available, correction exceeds harmful adoption after accounting for peer support in the primary setting. Full reasoning improves individual accuracy but provides no additional majority-vote gain over self-reflection while using 57% more tokens. Responses to constructed peer materials vary across GPT-4o, DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and Qwen3-Plus. An answer-agnostic intervention suppresses both harmful and beneficial peer adoption without a detectable accuracy gain. Together, the results show that convergence is an ambiguous signal of value and that debate must be judged by what peer information contributes to the final group answer.
  </details>

- **[ClinSeekAgent: Automating Multimodal Evidence Seeking for Agentic Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20176)**  `arXiv:2605.20176`  `cs.CL`  
  _Juncheng Wu, Letian Zhang, Yuhan Wang, Haoqin Tu, Hardy Chen, Zijun Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems have shown promise for clinical decision support, but existing works largely assume that evidence has already been curated and handed to the model. Real-world clinical workflows instead require agents to actively seek, iteratively plan, and synthesize multimodal evidence from heterogeneous sources. In this paper, we introduce ClinSeekAgent, an automated agentic framework for dynamic multimodal evidence seeking that shifts the paradigm from passive evidence consumption to active evidence acquisition. Given a clinical task and access to raw patient data, ClinSeekAgent queries longitudinal EHR records and invokes imaging analysis tools when relevant; refines its hypotheses as new information emerges; and integrates the collected evidence into grounded clinical decisions. We validate ClinSeekAgent in two roles: as an inference-time agent for frontier LLMs and as a training pipeline for open-source models. For inference-time validation, we construct ClinSeek-Bench, which covers 13 text-only EHR task categories and 6 multimodal EHR--chest X-ray task categories, and pairs each question with both a Curated Input of pre-selected evidence and the raw patient data for automated evidence seeking. Without any benchmark-specific curation, ClinSeekAgent significantly outperforms curated input for both Claude Opus 5 and GPT-5.6 Sol, improving overall F1 from 66.5 to 68.8 and from 60.0 to 62.5 on multimodal tasks. The ablations show that the performance gain come from multi-round evidence seeking rather than from access to more of the patient record. For training-time validation, we fine-tune Qwen3.5-35B-A3B on ClinSeekAgent trajectories generated by GPT-5.6 Sol. The resulting ClinSeek-35B-A3B reaches 35.6 average F1 on AgentEHR-Bench, outperforming all evaluated open-source models, including ones more than ten times larger, as well as Claude Opus 5.
  </details>

- **[CompactAttention: Accelerating Chunked Prefill with Block-Union KV Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16839)**  `arXiv:2605.16839`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiwon Song, Dongwon Jo, Beomseok Kang, Jae-Joon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chunked prefill has become a widely adopted serving strategy for long-context large language models, but efficient attention computation in this regime remains challenging. Existing sparse attention methods are primarily designed for one-shot prefill and do not translate efficiently to chunked prefill: block-sparse kernels lose efficiency when the query length is limited by the chunk size, while fine-grained pattern search becomes costly when repeated over the accumulated KV cache at every chunk. QUOKA, a recent method that directly targets chunked prefill, avoids sparse-kernel overhead but relies on query-subsampled, token-level KV selection, which can miss query-specific KV entries and introduce explicit KV-copy overhead. To address these limitations, we propose CompactAttention, a chunked-prefill attention mechanism based on Block-Union KV Selection. CompactAttention treats 2D block-sparse masks as KV-selection signals rather than direct sparse-kernel execution plans, and converts them into GQA-aware per-group KV block tables through Q-block union and intra-group union. This construction produces block tables that preserve all KV blocks selected by the input masks under paged execution constraints, enabling selected KV blocks to be accessed in place without explicit KV compaction. On LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct, CompactAttention maintains accuracy close to dense attention on the RULER benchmark while delivering up to 2.79$\times$ attention speedup over dense attention and 1.25$\times$ over FlashPrefill at 128K context length under chunked prefill.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Perspective Evidence Synthesis and Reasoning for Unsupervised Multimodal Entity Linking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.20283)**  `arXiv:2604.20283`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mo Zhou, Jianwei Wang, Kai Wang, Helen Paik, Ying Zhang, Wenjie Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Entity Linking (MEL) is a fundamental task in data management that maps ambiguous mentions with diverse modalities to the multimodal entities in a knowledge base. However, most existing MEL approaches primarily focus on optimizing instance-centric features and evidence, leaving broader forms of evidence and their intricate interdependencies insufficiently explored. Motivated by the observation that human expert decision-making process relies on multi-perspective judgment, in this work, we propose MSR-MEL, a Multi-perspective Evidence Synthesis and Reasoning framework with Large Language Models (LLMs) for unsupervised MEL. Specifically, we adopt a two-stage framework: (1) Offline Multi-Perspective Evidence Synthesis constructs a comprehensive set of evidence. This includes instance-centric evidence capturing the instance-centric multimodal information of mentions and entities, group-level evidence that aggregates neighborhood information, lexical evidence based on string overlap ratio, and statistical evidence based on simple summary statistics. A core contribution of our framework is the synthesis of group-level evidence, which effectively aggregates vital neighborhood information by graph. We first construct LLM-enhanced contextualized graphs. Subsequently, different modalities are jointly aligned through an asymmetric teacher-student graph neural network. (2) Online Multi-Perspective Evidence Reasoning leverages the power of LLM as a reasoning module to analyze the correlation and semantics of the multi-perspective evidence to induce an effective ranking strategy for accurate entity linking without supervision. Extensive experiments on widely used MEL benchmarks demonstrate that MSR-MEL consistently outperforms state-of-the-art unsupervised methods. The source code of this paper was available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Language Models as Higher-Order Planning Formalizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23844)**  `arXiv:2603.23844`  `cs.CL`  
  _Owen Jiang, Cassie Huang, Ashish Sabharwal, Li Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work provides overwhelming evidence that LLMs, even those trained to scale their reasoning trace, quickly deteriorate at planning as problems become more complex. LLM-as-Formalizers aim to address this by employing LLMs as a bridge to translate natural language descriptions into structured planning representations such as PDDL, which are then fed to a programmatic solver. We observe that its success may be overstated because planning problem descriptions in standard benchmarks often have a one-to-one mapping to PDDL, which departs from real use cases. To address this, we introduce the notion of unraveling problems where a natural yet succinct description translates into a very large PDDL representation. Using unraveling variants of four standard planning domains, we demonstrate that LLM Formalizers also do not always scale. We tackle this challenge by introducing a new paradigm, LLM-as-Higher-Order-Formalizer, where the LLM generates a high-level program that captures the recurrent logic within the description and in turn generates the larger PDDL representation. This decouples token output from the combinatorial explosion of the underlying formalization and search space, leading to improved performance for complex problems.
  </details>

- **[DebugLM: Learning Traceable Training Data Provenance for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17884)**  `arXiv:2603.17884`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenjie Jacky Mo, Qin Liu, Xiaofei Wen, Wenxuan Zhou, Zhe Zhao, Muhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are trained through multi-stage pipelines over heterogeneous data sources, yet developers lack a principled way to pinpoint the specific data responsible for an observed behavior. This lack of observability reduces debugging to reactive patching and makes failures prone to recur under distribution shift or subsequent model updates. To address this limitation, we propose DebugLM, a framework that equips LLMs with built-in data provenance, enabling them to explicitly trace the origins of their behaviors to specific training data sources. Specifically, the model learns to associate its responses with unique provenance tags that indicate the responsible dataset, empowering developers to precisely identify where undesirable behaviors are learned. Building on this capability, DebugLM further supports targeted test-time remediation, enabling developers to selectively trigger targeted refusal for specified data sources without retraining or modifying model parameters. Experiments demonstrate that DebugLM provides accurate behavior tracing in multi-stage training pipelines and effective test-time remediation while preserving the general utility of the model.
  </details>

- **[Overton Pluralistic Reinforcement Learning for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20759)**  `arXiv:2602.20759`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yu Fu, Seongho Son, Ilija Bogunovic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing alignment paradigms remain limited in capturing the pluralistic nature of human values. Overton Pluralism (OP) addresses this gap by generating responses with diverse perspectives from a single query. This paper introduces OP-GRPO (Overton Pluralistic Group Relative Policy Optimization), a reinforcement learning framework for implicit Overton Pluralism that enables a single LLM to produce pluralistic responses without explicit prompting or modular orchestration.
