Our workflow consists of two main steps: 1) Similarity estimator training, which fine-tunes a Sentence Transformer for OP tasks to provide a more accurate coverage evaluation of the given responses; and 2) OP-GRPO training, which incorporates this similarity estimator into a carefully designed dual-reward system to ensure both broad coverage of genuine human perspectives and the uniqueness of each perspective, thereby promoting diversity. Empirical results demonstrate that OP-GRPO achieves a "Small models, Big perspective coverage" effect: our trained Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct surpasses the GPT-OSS (20B) baseline with a 37.4% relative accuracy gain on the Natural Language Inference (NLI) benchmark. It also outperforms a modular-architecture baseline with a 19.1% relative improvement. Evaluations with GPT-5.4 as the LLM judge and OP-Benchmark further confirm the robustness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[PaperAudit-Bench: Benchmarking Error Detection in Research Papers for Critical Automated Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.19916)**  `arXiv:2601.19916`  `cs.CL`  
  _Songjun Tu, Yiwen Ma, Jiahao Lin, Qichao Zhang, Xiangyuan Lan, Junfeng.Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate fluent peer reviews but often miss sparse, subtle, and document-level scientific errors. We introduce PaperAudit-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating paper-level error detection and error-aware automated review. It comprises (1) PaperAudit-Dataset, 220 high-quality AI/ML papers containing more than 2,200 controlled synthetic errors across eight categories, and (2) PaperAudit-Review, an evidence-aware framework that links structured error detection to review generation. Experiments across models, error categories, paper sections, and analysis depths show that sparse long-context auditing remains challenging for current LLMs. Conditioning review generation on explicit, manuscript-grounded error evidence produces more conservative and discriminative assessments that align more closely with human judgments than representative automated-review baselines. The dataset also supports post-training compact detectors through SFT and RL; Qwen3-14B achieves 55.8% F1 at Detection@1 under a fixed held-out protocol. A PubPeer transfer study and deployment in ScienceOne, a scientific foundation-model platform led by the Chinese Academy of Sciences, further illustrate practical applicability.Code and Dataset Page:this https URLScienceOne Manuscript Review:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Identifying and Mitigating Bottlenecks in Role-Playing Agents: A Systematic Study of Disentangling Character Profile Axes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.04716)**  `arXiv:2601.04716`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yonghyun Jun, Junhyuk Choi, Jeonghyun Park, Jihyeong Park, Liu Nicole Geumheon, Hwanhee Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Large Language Model (LLM) role-playing agents have advanced rapidly, it remains unclear which profile elements genuinely drive role-playing quality. To bridge this gap, we introduce a systematic diagnostic framework that disentangles the impact of character profiles along three axes: Familiarity (Known vs. Unknown), Structure (Structured vs. Unstructured), and Disposition (Moral vs. Immoral). Utilizing a unified hierarchical schema (5 dimensions, 28 fields), we construct a controlled dataset of 211 personas and evaluate five LLMs on both single- and multi-turn interactions. Our results reveal a striking asymmetry: \textbf{Familiarity} and \textbf{Structure} show negligible impact, while \textbf{Disposition} produces large, consistent performance degradation for immoral characters across all conditions. Further analyses suggest that the Moral--Immoral gap is amplified by post-SFT alignment, and that this degradation varies substantially across profile attributes. To mitigate this bottleneck, we propose Field-Aware Contrastive Decoding (FACD), a training-free strategy that amplifies suppressed disposition-sensitive signals, significantly closing the performance gap without sacrificing moral-character performance.
  </details>

- **[REAP: Enhancing RAG with Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning for Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09966)**  `arXiv:2511.09966`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yijie Zhu, Haojie Zhou, Wanting Hong, Tailin Liu, Ning Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has been extensively employed to mitigate hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). However, existing methods for multi-hop reasoning tasks often lack global planning, increasing the risk of falling into local reasoning impasses. Insufficient exploitation of retrieved content and the neglect of latent clues fail to ensure the accuracy of reasoning outcomes. To overcome these limitations, we propose Recursive Evaluation and Adaptive Planning (REAP), whose core idea is to explicitly maintain structured sub-tasks and facts related to the current task through the Sub-task Planner (SP) and Fact Extractor (FE) modules. SP maintains a global perspective, guiding the overall reasoning direction and evaluating the task state based on the outcomes of FE, enabling dynamic optimization of the task-solving trajectory. FE performs fine-grained analysis over retrieved content to extract reliable answers and clues. These two modules incrementally enrich a logically coherent representation of global knowledge, enhancing the reliability and the traceability of the reasoning process. Furthermore, we propose a unified task paradigm design that enables effective multi-task fine-tuning, significantly enhancing SP's performance on complex, data-scarce tasks. We conduct extensive experiments on multiple public multi-hop datasets, and the results demonstrate that our method significantly outperforms existing RAG methods in both in-domain and out-of-domain settings, validating its effectiveness in complex multi-hop reasoning tasks.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning for Hierarchical Text Classification: The Case of Patents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07167)**  `arXiv:2510.07167`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lekang Jiang, Wenjun Sun, Stephan Goetz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical text classification (HTC) assigns documents to multiple levels of a pre-defined taxonomy. Automated patent subject classification represents one of the hardest HTC scenarios because of professional difficulties and extensive labels. Prior approaches only output a flat label set, which offers little insight into the reason behind predictions. Therefore, we propose Reasoning for Hierarchical Classification (RHC), a novel framework that reformulates HTC as a step-by-step reasoning task to sequentially deduce hierarchical labels. RHC trains large language models (LLMs) in two stages: a cold-start stage that aligns outputs with chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning format and a reinforcement learning (RL) stage to enhance multi-step reasoning ability. RHC demonstrates four advantages in our experiments. (1) Effectiveness: RHC surpasses previous baselines and outperforms the supervised fine-tuning counterparts by approximately 3% in accuracy and macro F1. (2) Explainability: RHC produces natural-language justifications before prediction to facilitate human inspection. (3) Scalability: RHC scales favorably with model size with larger gains compared to standard fine-tuning. (4) Applicability: Beyond patents, we further demonstrate that RHC achieves state-of-the-art performance on other widely used HTC benchmarks, which highlights its broad applicability.
  </details>

- **[Enriching Patent Claim Generation with European Patent Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.12568)**  `arXiv:2505.12568`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lekang Jiang, Chengzu Li, Stephan Goetz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Drafting patent claims is time-intensive, costly, and requires professional skill. Therefore, researchers have investigated large language models (LLMs) to assist inventors in writing claims. However, existing work has largely relied on datasets from the United States Patent and Trademark Office (USPTO). To enlarge research scope regarding various jurisdictions, drafting conventions, and legal standards, we introduce EPD, a European patent dataset. EPD presents rich textual data and structured metadata to support multiple patent-related tasks, including claim generation. This dataset enriches the field in three critical aspects: (1) Jurisdictional diversity: Patents from different offices vary in legal and drafting conventions. EPD fills a critical gap by providing a benchmark of European patents to enable more comprehensive evaluation. (2) Quality improvement: EPD offers high-quality granted patents with finalized and legally approved texts, whereas others consist of patent applications that are unexamined or provisional. Experiments show that LLMs fine-tuned on EPD significantly outperform those trained on previous datasets and even GPT-4o in claim quality and cross-domain generalization. (3) Real-world simulation: We propose a difficult subset of EPD to better reflect real-world challenges. Results reveal that all tested LLMs perform substantially worse on challenging samples, which highlights the need for future research.
  </details>

- **[Autonomous intelligence for mining productivity: a multi-agent AI approach to operational value creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26369)**  `arXiv:2610.26369`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _N Guerin, A Dehoux, M Nakhla_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Integrated Remote Operation Centres (IROCs) are emerging as the architectural frontier of Industry 4.0 for capital-intensive sectors such as mining, oil and gas, and energy. Yet two intertwined gaps constrain their evolution towards full autonomy. First, no integrated agentic architecture has been proposed for mining IROCs that orchestrates Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition systems, digital twins, predictive analytics and human-machine interfaces under safety-critical constraints. Second, no evaluation framework jointly addresses the real-time, Health-Safety-Environment, regulatory-auditability and cost-per-decision requirements that govern industrial agentic deployments. This paper makes four contributions. It synthesises industry needs and quantified gains documented in analogous industries that motivate an agentic approach to mining IROCs. It introduces EI2ROC, a three-tier multi-agent architecture combining a Large Language Model orchestrator with specialised monitoring and production-capacityprediction agents communicating through a Model Context Protocol-style tool interface. It provides a critical review of agentic evaluation along six orthogonal dimensions and identifies the industrial blind spot of current benchmarks. Finally, it proposes a multi-dimensional benchmarking framework for industrial agentic AI comprising scenario library, harness, metrics and humancalibration protocol. The paper states explicitly which components are implemented in the current prototype, which belong to the proposed reference design and which remain future work, and reports preliminary cost and latency observations from an operating site. Together, EI2ROC and the benchmarking framework constitute a methodological blueprint transferable across CAPEX intensive industries.
  </details>

- **[Explore or Confirm? LLM Agents Misallocate Measurements Under Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25111)**  `arXiv:2610.25111`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Abdul Karim, Matt Keon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As model reasoning gets cheaper, the physical experiment becomes the slow and costly step in discovery. Each experiment returns a noisy reading: a high value can mean a good candidate or a lucky draw. With a fixed budget, an AI agent must keep choosing between exploring a new candidate and confirming a promising one by re-testing it. We measure that choice in Wellocate, a simulated assay campaign with known true values. It is built on a generated landscape and on two real protein datasets, GB1 and PhoQ, scrambled so no model can answer from memory. We test five language models as minimal agents, with no tools and no memory beyond the history in each prompt. They misallocate in both directions. Three over-confirm, spending most of the budget re-testing. On the generated landscape one under-confirms, trusting single readings, and there every model ends with a worse final pick than a basic scripted strategy. Each failure has its own cause. The over-confirmers run short of new candidates worth trying: a list of untried candidates cuts their re-testing and, on real protein data, makes two of them pick much better. A running summary of their readings or a description of the landscape changes it by at most five percentage points. For the under-confirmer, re-testing its top candidates in a final round, replayed on its own campaigns, recovers only about a quarter of what it loses by picking the wrong candidate. This suggests confirmation has to run through the whole campaign. We release Wellocate as a benchmark and reinforcement-learning environment with frozen splits and scripted baselines. With it, anyone can measure how an agent splits its budget before trusting it with real experiments, and train agents to split it better. Code, data and the environment are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[IF-MAP: Influence-Function-Inspired Agent Prioritization in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24383)**  `arXiv:2610.24383`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jie Mao, Pingchen Lu, Zikun Qu, Jiahang Sun, Xiangyi Wang, Zhiwei Shang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving every agent in an LLM-based multi-agent system can be prohibitively costly, making it important to identify which agent should be prioritized to achieve the greatest system-level gain with limited resources. Failure attribution can inform this decision, but an agent's responsibility for failures does not directly reveal the system-level gains achievable by improving it. We therefore introduce IF-MAP, an influence-function-inspired method for identifying agents with high potential for system-level improvement. For each agent, IF-MAP measures end-to-end loss changes under controlled semantic prompt perturbations, fits a regularized local response model in a low-dimensional prompt-embedding subspace, and uses the estimated response norm as a priority score. This black-box procedure requires no access to model parameters or gradients and produces agent priorities that can guide subsequent improvement effort. We evaluate IF-MAP across six multi-agent workflows spanning code generation, question answering, and mathematical reasoning, using the same priorities for prompt optimization and model upgrades. Optimizing the selected agents' prompts achieves a macro-average gain of 3.81 percentage points, compared with 1.85 for uniform agent selection. Across five target models, upgrading the selected agents yields macro-average gains of 7.62-16.42 percentage points, compared with 3.81-8.62 for uniform agent selection. Despite its probing overhead, IF-MAP reduces Qwen3-8B and DeepSeek-V3.2 token use by 74.2% and 35.2%, respectively, relative to optimizing every agent, and reduces target-model tokens by 63.6-64.9% relative to upgrading every agent. These results support the use of IF-MAP to identify promising agents and direct improvement effort toward greater end-to-end performance gains.
  </details>

- **[Cascade Failure Attribution for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.29646)**  `arXiv:2608.29646`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jiayi Zhang, Zexin Wang, Degang Sun, Changhua Pei, Fei Sun, Gaogang Xie, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based agents have shown strong potential in solving complex tasks through multi-step reasoning, yet they remain vulnerable to execution failures. Accurate failure attribution is therefore critical for improving agent reliability. Existing topology- and spectrum-based methods exploit trajectory structures but often overlook fine-grained semantics, while LLM-based attribution methods capture semantic cues but suffer from long-context degradation over lengthy trajectories. To address these challenges, we propose DuoTrace, a plug-and-play detection filter for LLM-based failure attribution. DuoTrace follows a detect-before-attribute paradigm: it first detects anomalous executions and then supplies focused trajectory evidence to downstream LLM-based attribution methods. For effective VAE-based anomaly detection on agent trajectories, DuoTrace integrates dual-view semantic-structural node representations, a Tree-LSTM-based trajectory encoder, and data augmentation through outcome-verified LLM replay and root-to-node path extraction to handle heterogeneous nodes, hierarchical execution structures, and limited normal execution data. Experiments with six LLM-based attribution baselines show that DuoTrace improves agent-level and step-level attribution accuracy by 11.2% and 7.7%,respectively.
  </details>

- **[Scientific discovery as meta-optimization: a combinatorial optimization case study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26728)**  `arXiv:2606.26728`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Yuan-Hang Zhang, Chesson Sipling, Massimiliano Di Ventra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific discovery is fundamentally an optimization problem, defined by a vast ``state space'' of theories and experiments, and an evaluation criterion based on quality, novelty, and validity. Large language models (LLMs) have enabled automated exploration of this state space, but we argue that simultaneous modification of the evaluation criteria is equally important. Here, we propose formalizing research as meta-optimization, where the optimization objective itself is also being optimized. Our key contribution is ``consensus objective aggregation,'' where a portfolio of LLM-generated objective functions is combined via correlation-weighted voting, allowing the evaluation criterion to evolve alongside the solutions. We apply this framework to algorithm discovery for planted 3-SAT problems based on digital memcomputing machines. The leading solver from the consensus search reduces median integration steps by more than $38\times$ relative to a tuned baseline at 2,560 variables. Objective ablations show that a well-chosen fixed expert objective can also discover strong solvers, while consensus supplies a stable ranking that mitigates reward hacking behaviors. By bringing objective engineering into the discovery loop, this framework offers a practical route toward more adaptable automated research.
  </details>

- **[VeriCUA: Learning Proactive Verification for Reliable GUI Agent Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26469)**  `arXiv:2610.26469`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhizhou Wang, Yaxi Lu, Zhong Zhang, Zikun Hu, Xin Cong, Yankai Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for computer-use agents increasingly relies on proactive reward agents that interact with the post-execution GUI environment to verify what recorded screenshots cannot reveal. The reliability of such verification hinges on two coupled decisions: what to check and when to stop. Existing methods leave these decisions to prompted foundation models. However, neither scaling up the model nor providing few-shot examples reliably improves verification: prompted Judges ranging from 4B to GPT-5.4 all plateau at 68%-70% macro-average agreement. This is because the value of a check depends on the environment rather than the task alone, and becomes apparent only through execution. Learning from execution is itself constrained, as the Actor's terminal state cannot be faithfully replicated during training, allowing only one verification attempt per state. We introduce VeriCUA, a proactive reward agent that learns its verification strategy from these single attempts. A trainable Judge decides what to check and when to stop, while a frozen Probe executes the checks in the live environment. Online reinforcement learning optimizes the Judge for both verdict correctness and efficient Probe use, with returns normalized across terminal states. Trained only on OSWorld and evaluated without further training on MobileWorld and WebArena-Verified, VeriCUA achieves the highest reward agreement across all three benchmarks and lies on the agreement-cost Pareto frontier among proactive verifiers. It surpasses few-shot GPT-5.4 by 5.36 points with 47.6%-59.8% fewer Probe tokens, and even a 1B VeriCUA exceeds every prompted Judge. As the reward for Actor RL, it raises task success by 3.21 points overall and 5.40 points on held-out multi-application tasks. These results identify the verification strategy, not verifier scale, as the key to reliable GUI rewards.
  </details>

- **[Productive State-Space Exploration for Multi-turn RL in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25957)**  `arXiv:2610.25957`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammed Haroon Dupty, Yong Liang Goh, Song Guo, Wee Sun Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning (RL) for Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multi-turn, long-horizon environments remains challenging due to inefficient action selection and repetitive behavioral patterns. In particular, agents often become trapped in high-confidence yet unproductive behaviors, repeatedly executing invalid actions or entering action loops that hinder task completion. We observe that these frequently violate environment preconditions, revealing a mismatch between the agent's representation of the internal state and the dynamics of the underlying environment. In addition, repetitive high-confidence actions lead to cyclical behaviors that provide little to no progress towards the goal. We argue that addressing these challenges requires more productive task-aligned exploration to improve the agent's understanding of environment dynamics. Motivated by intrinsic curiosity-driven exploration, we propose a conditional state-space exploration framework that balances task completion with intrinsic information-seeking behavior. Specifically, we introduce an intrinsic reward that penalizes actions whose observation duplicates prior state memory. This provides the benefit of reducing redundant behaviors and encouraging state-space coverage without relying on a general information-theoretic measure. Strong experimental results demonstrate that our approach substantially improves policy learning in multi-turn environments, mitigating invalid actions and repetitive loops.
  </details>

- **[OrderGPT: Generation Order as a Learnable Modeling Choice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25949)**  `arXiv:2610.25949`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Chenhe Yang, Yuhuan Lyu, Tianyang Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative modeling of discrete data has two popular paradigms: autoregressive models such as GPT, which factorize a sequence in a left-to-right (L2R) order, and masked diffusion models, which are essentially any-order autoregressive modeling. Rather than competing methods, these are the two extreme points of a single axis -- the generation order. For structured discrete domains without a natural traversal, both extremes may be suboptimal, yet the intermediate choice -- a single well-adapted order -- remains largely unexplored. We introduce OrderGPT, which builds on Any-Order GPT to learn a task-level generation order without canonical-order supervision. Our key observation is that predicting variables from randomly sampled reveal prefixes produces recurring attention patterns in the backbone that provide a model-dependent signal of predictive dependencies. We aggregate this signal into a reciprocal dependency graph and use a spectral teacher to derive continuous generation priorities. A lightweight OrderMLP integrated into the GPT backbone learns to predict these priorities from attention, incorporating order learning into the architecture within a single training run. Training gradually shifts toward the learned order, which is then frozen for subsequent autoregressive training and generation. The same mechanism adapts across modalities: adaptively it recovers near-L2R dependency structure in language, learns anatomically organized factorizations on human pose, and discovers a factorization on tabular data that places statistically and semantically related fields nearby, while improving downstream modeling metrics across all domains. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25858)**  `arXiv:2610.25858`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyin Chen, Yizheng Huang, Yinglun Xu, Ziyun Zhao, Emmett McQuinn, Julie Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents are often deployed in environments where new knowledge, tools, and workflows keep arriving. Such an agent is therefore expected to adapt continually, learning new tasks as they arrive without forgetting earlier ones. This resembles the continual learning problem, but at a pace that conventional gradient updates cannot sustain. Nonparametric self-evolving agents are a natural alternative: they adapt by rewriting their own harness, such as prompts, memory, and skills, from task feedback while the model weights stay frozen. We study whether such agents are effective continual learners across four drifting task streams that shift in topic, workflow, or domain (built from BIRD, BFCL, and ScienceWorld). Evaluating three representative methods spanning experience retrieval and self-modification (ReasoningBank, GEPA, and Meta-Harness), we find that although self-evolution improves aggregate performance on most streams, higher plasticity consistently comes at the cost of stability. Specifically, self-modifying methods apply global edits to a shared prompt or workspace, interfering with unseen tasks and overwriting earlier knowledge as the stream shifts. Conversely, similarity-based memory retrieval localizes updates across distant domains, but still retrieves foreign memories on unseen tasks and suffers from retrieval collision when similar queries require conflicting workflows. These findings show that current self-evolving agents remain incomplete continual learners.
  </details>

- **[Spherical Fixed-Point Reasoners: Adaptive Loop Halting on the Hypersphere](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25646)**  `arXiv:2610.25646`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Arber Zela, Artavazd Maranjyan, Pol Puigdemont, David Berghaus, Valentyn Boreiko, Volkan Cevher_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped (weight-tied) transformers enable adaptive computation by allocating more iterations to harder inputs. Fixed-Point Reasoners (FPRM) achieve this with a damped fixed-point solver, but operate in an unbounded Euclidean residual space with coupled rescaling and relative-norm halting. We introduce Spherical Fixed-Point Reasoners (SFPRM), which perform the same computation on the unit hypersphere of normalized transformers (nGPT). Input injection, damping, and halting become geodesic operations, yielding boundedness by construction and eliminating coefficient coupling. We prove fixed-point existence, local convergence under an explicit step-size condition, and global convergence under strong injection. On Sudoku-Extreme, Maze-Hard, and Mini-ARC, SFPRM achieves 96.6%, 87.9%, and 59.1% test accuracy, respectively, outperforming FPRM and other baselines while allocating test-time compute more effectively. Its converged geometry is also interpretable: token-input angles capture puzzle structure and predict correctness.
  </details>

- **[Means-Ends Analysis Helps LLMs Plan in Continuous-State Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24928)**  `arXiv:2610.24928`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jiayi Li, Carl Kingsford_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM planning research has traditionally focused on discrete-state domains, where actions form a finite, enumerable set. Existing LLM planning frameworks largely assume actions are enumerable, modeling planning as a search process over candidate actions. However, in continuous-state domains, where actions form a continuum and cannot be enumerated, this assumption breaks down. We find a remedy in Newell and Simon's Means-Ends Analysis (MEA), a general problem-solving technique rooted in heuristics from human psychology. We develop Medaka, an MEA-inspired structured prompting framework that encourages recursive goal decomposition, algebraic derivation, and per-step state tracking. To evaluate native LLM planning abilities in continuous-state domains, we develop Liquidworld, a systematic testbed on liquid mixing. On Liquidworld, Medaka can improve performance across model families, suggesting that classical means-ends analysis elicits LLM-native reasoning abilities that are helpful for continuous-state planning.
  </details>

- **[Good Judgment, Bad Advice: Decision-Model Controllers Raise Agent Cost Without Improving Success](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24649)**  `arXiv:2610.24649`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Naman Vats_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent scaffolds increasingly delegate small decisions (whether to stop, verify, or change approach) to cheaper models. We ask whether a typed decision model, Jev 1.13, consulted only at deterministic decision points, makes a Claude Sonnet 5.5 terminal agent cheaper without making it worse. In a pre-specified study on Terminal-Bench 2.1, we hold one agent loop fixed and vary only the controller: none, deterministic rules, a small LLM (Claude Haiku 5.5) answering the same questions, Jev, and a placebo that samples Jev's development-phase choice frequencies at the same moments. On 21 held-out tasks (315 trials), Jev raised cost per attempt by 19% (geometric-mean ratio 1.19, 95% CI 1.02-1.36) at an identical pass rate (73.0% vs. 73.0%). Jev was cheaper than the small-LLM controller (ratio 0.81) but 22% more expensive than the placebo, and no pass-rate difference between arms was detectable. Offline, Jev ranked finish requests better than the alternatives (AUROC 0.78 vs. 0.62 for rules and 0.60 for the small LLM) but was poorly calibrated: its mean probability that a run was complete was 0.50 when 87% of those runs passed, so it asked the agent to verify work that was already correct. No controller ever chose to abandon a run. Informative decision signals did not become useful interventions; we discuss what that would require.
  </details>

- **[Cortex: A Fixed-Point Theory of Governed Coding Agents - Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24255)**  `arXiv:2610.24255`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Marius-Constantin Dinu, Florian Zeba_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A coding agent combines a large language model (LLM) with a harness that plans, edits, and executes code. We study Cortex, a supervisory layer for pre-execution governance, requirement tracking, and iterative validation and repair. We formalize requirement closure on a finite lattice. For a fixed requirement set and a sound, monotone validation rule with persistent evidence, exhaustive iteration reaches the least fixed point with no more strict increases than there are requirements. Fair consequence scheduling reaches the same closure. A separate stochastic model bounds expected completion time under a uniform positive-progress assumption. These results depend on their stated assumptions; artifact repair, fallible validation, and finite execution budgets do not satisfy them automatically. We define Gauntlet's trajectory-similarity and build-gated scores, attack-success estimators, Wilson intervals, and case-cluster bootstrap summaries. Effective-feedback accounting counts newly certified requirements, rather than estimating their information value or utility. The five evaluation families support raw-versus-governed comparisons. We report retained benchmark observations, including rescoring of existing artifacts; constructed outcomes are excluded. Unequal observation sets and incomplete provenance prevent causal claims about governance or measured long-horizon scaling.
  </details>

- **[Unified Multiparadigm Fusion Intelligence for Rare Disease Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24024)**  `arXiv:2610.24024`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jian Yang, Peiling Liu, Songda Li, Yu Cao, Yuqing Feng, Lan Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rare diseases (RDs) affect an estimated 300 million people globally, leaving patients to navigate a protracted diagnostic odyssey constrained by clinical scarcity and fragmented medical knowledge. Artificial intelligence (AI) has progressively advanced RD diagnostics, from symbolic methods and deep learning to large language model (LLM)-based systems. However, each paradigm carries structural limitations: symbolic methods are bounded by knowledge completeness, neural models sacrifice interpretability, and LLM-based systems are vulnerable to evaluation biases like data leakage. In this study, we introduce FusionRare, a unified multiparadigm fusion framework integrating knowledge-driven symbolic reasoning, deep-learning pattern recognition, case-based patient retrieval, and LLM-based heuristic inference via statistical meta-aggregation and knowledge-enhanced agentic reflection. Evaluated on 2,528 strictly isolated clinical cases, FusionRare achieves state-of-the-art RD prioritization, attaining a macro-averaged Recall @1 of 51% for phenotype-only diagnosis and 79% for phenotype-genotype joint analysis. These results represent decisive advantages over the advanced agentic system DeepRare, frontier LLMs including GPT 5.5, and specialized tools like PhenoBrain and LIRICAL under stringent anti-leakage protocols. A randomized, crossover clinician-AI validation demonstrated that FusionRare elevates the true diagnostic rank in 45% of decisions, improves physician confidence in 62%, and reduces time to diagnosis by 21%. These findings indicate that reconciling these orthogonal AI paradigms significantly enhances diagnostic capabilities, bridging the gap between computational benchmarks and actionable clinical reliability. More broadly, this work provides a replicable framework for deploying trustworthy diagnostic AI across complex, high-stakes, and underserved medical domains.
  </details>

- **[A Controlled LLM Workflow for Evidence-Grounded Diagnosis of Risk Score Differences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23962)**  `arXiv:2610.23962`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hanqing Dou, Zijia Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a controlled Large Language Model (LLM) workflow for explaining risk score differences using similar historical transactions. The workflow combines reference retrieval and contrastive SHapley Additive exPlanations (SHAP) evidence with reference-based LLM reasoning and deterministic validation. Experiments on a test dataset show that the workflow produces more consistent, evidence-grounded and traceable diagnoses than the same LLM without structured workflow guidance.
  </details>

- **[How Do Agentic LLMs Decide to Call Tools? A Tool-Call Vector Shaped by Suppression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09624)**  `arXiv:2610.09624`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xijie Gong, Tingxu Han, Jiahao Zhang, Wei Song, Ziqi Ding, Hanqi Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool calling, invoking external tools on demand, is central to agentic LLMs, yet the mechanism that decides whether a model calls a tool or responds directly remains poorly understood. Agentic prompts are long and heavily scaffolded, combining role instructions, tool schemas, format templates, and the user's request across hundreds of tokens, creating a noisy, highly entangled context in which no single controllable variable for mechanistic analysis is obvious. To obtain such a variable, we propose a method that converts complex agentic prompts into minimal contrastive pairs in which a single request verb determines the tool-call decision: replacing an execution-verb (e.g., \textit{write}) with an analysis-verb (e.g., \textit{discuss}) reliably flips the decision, suggesting it is mediated by a compact internal state. We construct 500 such paired prompts across Python, Java, and C++ (300 for mechanistic analysis, 200 held out for evaluation). We trace the decision to a vector, $\mu_\Delta$, that is both causally necessary and sufficient and generalizes beyond the discovery prompts to native multi-turn $\tau^2$-Bench trajectories and verb-free requests. Behavioral ablations show that the scaffold establishes a tool-call prior; Transcoder decomposition then reveals that analysis verbs suppress this prior through features signaling that tool use is unnecessary, whereas execution verbs largely leave it intact. Downstream scaffold-reading attention heads and MLP features read out the resulting state, and the same mechanism recurs across seven models from the Qwen, Mistral, and Granite families. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Harnessing Textual Refusal Directions for Multimodal Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31876)**  `arXiv:2606.31876`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Moreno D'Incà, Nicu Sebe, Massimiliano Mancini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) depends on unsafe multimodal data for post-training alignment or activation steering. However, such data is hard to collect, must be gathered anew for each modality, and its collection and distribution raise ethical and legal concerns. In this work, we relax this constraint and investigate whether textual refusal directions, extracted directly from the LLM backbone, generalize across modalities (i.e., image, video). Preliminary findings confirm this ability, though effectiveness is conditioned by layer selection, steering strength, and cross-modal alignment, with the latter causing safe multimodal inputs to be spuriously steered toward refusal. Building on this, we introduce Modality-Agnostic Refusal Steering (MARS), a lightweight training-free approach that injects multimodal safety without the need for multimodal safety data. MARS corrects modality misalignment via activation centering, adaptively scales steering strength within a geometrically defined trust region, and selects the optimal intervention layer, operating at the first generated token. Evaluated on five SOTA MLLMs across safety, utility, and video jailbreak, MARS achieves consistent safety gains while preserving utility up to over-refusal. MARS reveals that safety-relevant structure is shared across modalities and that textual refusal directions are a powerful and underexplored foundation for multimodal alignment.
  </details>

- **[Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27044)**  `arXiv:2608.27044`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Wei-Yao Wang, Kazuya Tateishi, Shuyang Cui, Christian Simon, Takashi Shibuya, Shusuke Takahashi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal representation learning has been shifting from traditional two-tower architectures to large language model (LLM)-based embedders due to their strong instruction-following capabilities. Despite this progress, existing approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities, which also remain the dominant modalities for user-conditioned interactions in current embedders. In this paper, we propose the first Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder (OmniUE), which not only learns a unified embedding space across text, video, and audio by leveraging intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens, but also supports omni-interactive querying, enabling users to provide inputs in the form of text, visual regions of interest, and audio spans. Within OmniUE, visual and audio segmenters process diverse user interactions and integrate them with an omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. To evaluate OmniUE's omni-interactive capabilities, we introduce OmniCHOIR, benchmarking models for omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval based on the given text, video, and audio as well as unimodal or multimodal interaction prompts. OmniUE consistently surpasses state-of-the-art baselines across diverse modalities, with average improvements of 10.4% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.0% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.1% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 21.9% on our omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark. We believe that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[MOIRA: Policy Memory and Belief Revision for Language-Model Agents in Non-Stationary Pair Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01954)**  `arXiv:2605.01954`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Polydoros Giannouris, Yuechen Jiang, Lingfei Qian, Yuyan Wang, Xueqing Peng, Jimin Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many sequential decision-making problems exhibit hierarchical structure, where high-level semantic choices constrain downstream actions and feedback is delayed and ambiguous. Learning in such settings is challenging due to credit assignment: performance degradation may arise from flawed abstractions, suboptimal execution, or their interaction. We study this challenge through pair trading, a domain that naturally combines long-horizon semantic reasoning for asset pair selection with short-horizon execution under partial observability. We formulate pair trading as a hierarchical reinforcement learning problem and propose a language-driven optimization framework in which both high-level and low-level policies are parameterized by large language models (LLMs) and optimized exclusively through prompt updates. Our approach leverages pretrained LLMs as hierarchical policies and uses trajectory- and episode-level textual feedback to adapt abstractions and execution without gradient-based fine-tuning. By explicitly separating abstraction selection from execution, the framework reduces non-stationarity across hierarchical levels and enables targeted adaptation under delayed feedback. Experiments on real-world market data show consistent improvements over traditional and LLM-based baselines, demonstrating the effectiveness of language-driven hierarchical reinforcement learning.
  </details>

- **[Agent World Model in the Wild: Computer-Use Agent Environment Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26828)**  `arXiv:2610.26828`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhaoyang Wang, Qianhui Wu, Haorui Wang, Simon Yu, Baolin Peng, Canwen Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent learning is shifting from learning from human data toward learning through interaction with the world. However, scaling such learning directly in real-world computer systems is challenging and can introduce substantial security risks. We therefore propose Agent World Model in the Wild (WildAWM), a scalable pipeline for synthesizing controllable environments and tasks for training general computer-use agents (CUAs). Whereas existing approaches typically specialize in a single agent interaction interface, we construct executable environments that jointly support graphical user interfaces, model context protocol services, and command-line interfaces, allowing agents to flexibly combine visual interaction, tool use, and code execution. WildAWM is entirely powered by open-source models, demonstrating that high-quality computer-use environments can be generated without proprietary frontier models. Beyond environments, we address three recurring challenges in task synthesis: ensuring solvability, maintaining sufficient difficulty, and producing reliable verifiers, by introducing execution-based quality gates that combine heterogeneous agent rollouts, cross-judge consistency checks, and counterfactual probes of judgment robustness. The resulting pipeline synthesizes 341 long-horizon environment-task pairs that can require over 100 agent steps to solve and remain challenging even for frontier models, with GPT-6-Astra solving only 60% of them. We train two Qwen models of different sizes exclusively in these synthetic worlds. Both models achieve substantial gains across 8 out-of-distribution benchmarks spanning web, claw, and computer-use agents, demonstrating that learning in WildAWM's synthesized worlds generalizes broadly to unseen computer-use tasks. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RouterWorld: Scaling Conversational AI with Latency-Aware LLM Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26511)**  `arXiv:2610.26511`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinyang Li, Denis Emelin, Simone Tedeschi, Weiwei Cheng, Cristina Morariu, Haytham Assem, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational AI systems built on Large Language Models (LLMs) require routing mechanisms that balance answer quality and response latency at scale. However, existing LLM routing benchmarks assume full observability of all candidate model outcomes, under-emphasize latency as a first-class metric, and fragment evaluation across heterogeneous protocols misaligned with real-world settings. We introduce RouterWorld, a comprehensive benchmark for LLM routing in conversational GenAI. RouterWorld unifies 371k single-turn responses and 76k multi-turn agentic trajectories, adopts natural user interactions with a standardized LLM-as-a-judge protocol, and decouples efficiency from transient pricing via hardware-grounded latency annotations. Crucially, RouterWorld formalizes routing under partial observability through two setups: Cold-Start, which restricts learning to sparse, static logs, and Voyager, which models continuous data streams where routing decisions shape future training distributions. Evaluating 13 routing baselines, we find that most routers over-emphasize accuracy while ignoring latency under partial observability. Code and benchmark data are publicly available.
  </details>

- **[Narrow Decision Models: A System-1 Architecture for Calibrated, Typed Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25553)**  `arXiv:2610.25553`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Carlos André Antunes, Aldry Albuquerque, Gabriel de Melo Schmith, Natã Batista, Pedro Oziel Escobar da Silva, Stefano Benko Rossi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  System One models, introduced with TypeSafe's Jev, answer typed questions about a state with calibrated probabilities instead of generated text: choosing among options described in natural language (Choice), placing the state on an ordered scale (Score), or judging a yes/no statement (Noul). Jev is general-purpose: one set of weights, served as an API and adapted to a domain only through the request. We study the other end of the design space. A Narrow Decision Model (NDM) offers the same interface but is trained for the decisions of one organization, is small enough to run on its own infrastructure, and has an open architecture whose properties can be stated and tested. The state can be any text or structured record about which decisions are made; our running case is the routing of court communications. The architecture serializes a request as a prefix tree (the state, then each question, then each option) and runs a bidirectional encoder under a block attention mask with logical positions, so that each answer is, by construction, invariant to the order of its options and independent of the other questions asked. Routing through an organization chart is a composition of Choice decisions with an explicit "none of these" option. We describe training with proper scoring rules through a curriculum, calibration and abstention, decisions composed by rubrics, and a protocol that accepts or rejects the mechanism before held-out data is opened. A minimal validation on public and fictitious data shows that the invariances hold to rounding in a trained model, that a small instance routes on organization charts it has never seen well above lexical retrieval, and that, at equal compute, the block architecture reaches a lower loss than a pairwise cross-encoder. A first instance trained on real legal documents agrees with the LLM classifier in production on 91% of held-out documents.
  </details>

- **[Outcome-Anchored Credit Reallocation for Agentic Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25384)**  `arXiv:2610.25384`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Mingyuan Li, Yucheng Li, Xiaxia Wang, Gong Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) requires large language models (LLMs) to solve complex questions through multi-turn interactions with a knowledge graph (KG). Outcome-supervised reinforcement learning derives verifiable outcome rewards from final answers but uniformly assigns each rollout's outcome-level advantage to all policy-generated tokens, creating a trajectory-to-operation mismatch since KG operations within a rollout can have varying effects on the search process and final answer. To address this, we propose Outcome-Anchored Credit Reallocation (OACR), which employs a training-only KG-state outcome model to estimate outcome-reward changes between adjacent KG search states and converts them into operation credit, from which token-wise weights are derived. These multiplicative weights reallocate token-level update magnitudes while preserving the sign of the outcome-level advantage at every generated token. Across three benchmarks, OACR achieves stronger overall performance than existing KGQA methods while using fewer inference-time LLM calls and tokens.
  </details>

- **[Dialogue Generation for Conversational Recommendation Using User Search Interactions and Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25182)**  `arXiv:2610.25182`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Minsung Hwang, Donggyu Yoon, Gowoon Hwang, Kwanghee Jang, Joyce Jiyoung Whang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large-scale conversational recommendation datasets typically require manual dialogue construction or annotation. We propose SearCl, which automatically generates conversational recommendation dialogues grounded in user search and click behaviors. SearCl segments user interactions into query sessions and derives each recommendation target from content selected after a search query. A configurable probabilistic state machine controls the dialogue flow, while two LLM agents acting as the seeker and recommender generate utterances according to turn-level constraints. To support recommendation, the recommender is provided with knowledge graphs constructed from metadata about the content items used in each dialogue, capturing their attributes and relationships. Using SearCl, we generate 3,538 dialogues from content search and click interaction data, covering 1,731 unique target items. Under an LLM-as-a-judge protocol, the resulting dialogues achieve higher quality scores than dialogues from existing benchmarks. These results demonstrate that SearCl enables controllable and knowledge-grounded dataset construction without human annotations.
  </details>

- **[Revealing what benchmark scores hide: Jointly measuring the breadth and reliability of LLM capabilities using accessibility profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24957)**  `arXiv:2610.24957`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Kazem Faghih, Yize Cheng, Shoumik Saha, Mobina Pournemat, Armin Gerami, Soheil Feizi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language model benchmarks assign each question a single outcome from one fixed wording, even though the same underlying request can be expressed in many equivalent ways. We argue that the quantity of interest should therefore not be correctness on one wording, but the accessibility of an inquiry, which we define as the probability that a model succeeds across meaning-preserving formulations. The distribution of accessibility across inquiries reveals a structure that accuracy and existing best-, worst-, or average-case prompt scores collapse: how broadly a model's capabilities extend across inquiries and how dependably those capabilities can be elicited. Two models can have the same average performance while one succeeds across more inquiries unreliably and the other succeeds across fewer inquiries but more consistently, leading to opposite preferences depending on whether the task allows multiple retries until one success or when repeated consistent success is required. Across six benchmarks and more than twenty models, this tradeoff is common: for about half of the model pairs with nearly equal mean accessibility, which model looks better depends on whether one success across five formulations is enough or all five must succeed, quantities we call Access@5 and Reliable@5, respectively. For some pairs the two measures diverge significantly, by up to 10 points in opposite directions. Accessibility also reveals that scaling, reasoning, and prompt optimization can redistribute capability across inquiries in ways aggregate accuracy obscures, sometimes broadening the set of inquiries a model can succeed on while making success on them less repeatable. Accessibility turns formulation sensitivity from a nuisance into a measurable property of capability, distinguishing what a model can sometimes succeed on from what users can actually depend on.
  </details>

- **[WIDE: Boosting Adaptive LLM Inference via Token-level Dynamic Width Pruning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.28418)**  `arXiv:2607.28418`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Haozhe Hu, Hao Wu, Peiran Yin, Chao Han, Yunpu Ma, Xiaoyu Shen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pruning is a promising approach for improving the efficiency of large language models (LLMs). Static structured pruning is hardware-friendly, but its input-agnostic computation allocation often degrades model quality at high sparsity. Dynamic pruning adapts computation to individual tokens, but coarse-grained layer-level routing limits allocation flexibility, and finer-grained decisions introduce irregular execution that can offset computational savings. To address these challenges, we present WIDE, the first end-to-end differentiable framework that combines token-wise structured dynamic width pruning with GPU kernel co-design for both prefill and decoding. WIDE uses differentiable routers to select GQA-aligned query-head groups and GPU-tile-aligned FFN-channel groups for each token. To execute these decisions efficiently, it reorders routing masks to cluster active tokens within each group, enabling hardware-agnostic block-level skipping and architecture-aware skipping of inactive loads and tensor-core operations. Experiments on the Llama3 and Qwen3 families demonstrate improved quality retention over representative static and dynamic pruning methods. At 50\% target routing sparsity, WIDE improves Llama3.1-8B average zero-shot accuracy by up to 20.26 points over the strongest dynamic-depth baseline under calibration-only settings, while retaining 97.6\%/88.2\% of dense Qwen3-14B likelihood/generation performance after LoRA tuning. Separate latency benchmarks demonstrate end-to-end speedups of up to $1.68\times$ for prefill and $1.55\times$ for decoding over the dense baseline. These results demonstrate that pruning--kernel co-design pushes the frontier of token-wise dynamic structured pruning, combining improved quality retention with practical acceleration across both prefill and decoding. Our code is avaliable at \url{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[From Outcomes to Components: A Counterfactual Evaluation Framework for LLM-Proposed Repairs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26650)**  `arXiv:2610.26650`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hengyu Ye, Songhua Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An LLM-generated repair action specifies which records to change and by how much. A failed action may still contain a useful target set or numerical update, depending on how the two are paired. We evaluate this problem in traffic networks and databases, where aggregate observations obscure the records that need correction. We examine the pairings by executing each combination from the same initial state, using a reference action that restores the affected records. In 76 of 114 traffic actions, the gain from correcting both parts exceeds the sum of the gains from correcting each part alone. Six of these actions become worse after either correction alone, but improve when both parts are corrected. In database repair, replacing only the proposed update with the reference update worsens 17 of 108 model actions, all of which select unaffected records. We also test whether parts of two proposals can be reused. On held-out observations, selecting among the proposals and their recombined target-update pairs recovers 61.6% of the reference action's loss reduction, compared with 58.0% from selecting between the proposals alone.
  </details>

- **[VibeTraining-Bench: Data-Trainer-Harness Co-Design for Iterative System Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26342)**  `arXiv:2610.26342`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ruotian Peng, Yuanyi Ren, Shuaiqi Liu, Xin Guo, Fang Zhu, Yangyi Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive self-improvement (RSI) requires agents to diagnose system bottlenecks and coordinate improvements across training and inference. We refer to the agents' capability to jointly optimize training data, the trainer, and the inference harness as cross-component co-design. We introduce VibeTraining-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating this capability across tasks spanning terminal operation, software engineering, multi-turn tool use, and mathematical reasoning. Within each task, research agents start from an identical initial agent system under a fixed resource budget. Guided by validation feedback, they iteratively refine data, trainers, and inference harnesses, then select and submit a final agent system for evaluation on the test set. To prevent reward hacking, we enforce three safeguards: test data isolation, separate research and grading containers, and network restrictions for research agents. Our evaluation results show that frontier research agents substantially improve target agent systems. For example, on SWE-bench Verified, GPT-6-Astra raises the target agent system's performance from 32.9% to 53.7%, comparable to the human-developed instruct model's 55.4%. However, there is still room to improve their ability to co-design and iteratively refine data, trainer, and harness to achieve cumulative performance gains. We hope that VibeTraining-Bench will serve as a unified platform for advancing RSI, deepening our understanding of cross-component optimization for agent systems. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Standing on the Shoulders of Giants: Enhancing LLM Agents for Automated Fact-Checking with Human Expert Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25777)**  `arXiv:2610.25777`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinwen Chen, Haorui He, Yupeng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated fact-checking (AFC) systems powered by large language model (LLM) agents still face challenges in evidence retrieval and claim verification. Existing approaches improve their capabilities through computationally costly post-training or human-in-the-loop feedback that is difficult to scale. To transfer professional fact-checking expertise to LLM agents efficiently, we distill publicly available guidelines published by Agence France-Presse (AFP) Fact Check into a reusable, modular Professional Fact-Checking Skill (PFC-Skill). We then present SkillCheck, the first AFC system that gives LLM agents dynamic access to PFC-Skill to guide evidence retrieval and claim verification at inference time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SkillCheck improves upon skill-free DEFAME baselines across multiple LLM backbones, with gains of 2.2--4.8 pp. in Accuracy and 1.5--6.3 pp. in Macro-F1 on the AVeriTeC benchmark. Notably, SkillCheck with GPT-4o-Mini exceeds DEFAME with GPT-4o by 3.4 pp. in Accuracy while cutting inference cost by roughly 61%. The performance gains also extend to open-web retrieval. Ablations show that incorporating the skill during evidence retrieval provides most of the gains, while incorporation into both stages yields the best overall performance. Case studies show that PFC-Skill enables more targeted search and closer scrutiny of evidence. To conclude, our work shows that LLM agents can "stand on the shoulders of giants" by drawing on the best practices of professional fact-checkers, substantially enhancing AFC performance without extra training or continuous human supervision.
  </details>

- **[Whose Credential Is It? How Owner Attribution in Agent MemoryShapes Cross-User Account Misuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25707)**  `arXiv:2610.25707`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hau-Sing So, Daixian Liu, Carl Hou, Yuhaohang He, Mengzhu Wang, Hai-Tao Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Shared long-term memory lets an LLM agent serving one user inadvertently retrieve another user's credentials, but it is unclear when such exposure leads to actual harm. We introduce a three-stage audit that separates three events: a foreign credential reaches the prompt (exposure), the agent acts with it (behavior), and the other user's account changes (state). In AppWorld, a store that mixes 763 past logins from all users retrieves another user's credential for 84.7 percent of required apps, yet agents rarely act on it. Misuse appears in a stress test where memory lacks the user's own credential and presents a single foreign one as the user's record: seven agents then use it in 80 to 94 percent of runs and change the victim's account in up to 43 percent, sometimes passing the official check. Naming the owner before or inside each credential line prevents most of this: with all else fixed, misuse falls from 92 to 1 of 305 paired runs, also with name-free usernames, though owner-bound notes cost 3 to 5 successful tasks per model on clean memory. A wrongly written owner largely restores misuse; trailing owner tags and added safety instructions help only some models. Agent memory should consistently record whose credential it stores, and evaluations should measure behavior, not only exposure.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Tool Traces in Agentic LLMs: Source Localization and Counterfactual Dependence Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25540)**  `arXiv:2610.25540`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Junhao Liu, Tianxiao Li, Yongxian Wu, Ziheng Duan, Ren Li, Jing Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic LLMs increasingly support scientific prediction by grounding decisions in external evidence. In biology, they promise accurate, interpretable predictions for important problems by integrating complex experimental and computational evidence. However, serialized tool results can contain hidden label shortcuts, such as dataset identifiers or template artifacts, that create apparent success without biological reasoning. To address this risk, we introduce a two-stage audit. Stage 1, Per-Tool TF-IDF Decomposition (PT2D), localizes label-predictive patterns to individual tool results while avoiding signal dilution in the concatenated tool trace. Stage 2, Token-Class Redaction, counterfactually tests whether the frozen LLM depends on the localized shortcut. We apply this audit to TCR-pMHC binding, where tool augmentation substantially outperforms a no-evidence baseline. At face value, this suggests that the LLM has learned to use scientific evidence. The audit overturns this interpretation: PT2D localizes the strongest signal to identifiers in one tool result, and redacting them reduces performance to chance. These findings show how hidden label shortcuts can masquerade as scientific reasoning or discovery, motivating source-level audits before benchmark gains are trusted.
  </details>

- **[System X: An LLM Decision Model That Replies from Its Own KV Cache](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25456)**  `arXiv:2610.25456`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Syed Usama Khalid Bukhari, Syed Safwan Khalid, Muhammad Aamir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models return probabilities over supplied options, but a deployed agent may also need to reply in words. The present study introduces System X, a large language model (LLM) decision model that adds a text-reply option to the LLM-as-Jev readout. When this option wins, or confidence is low, the same model continues from the decision's key-value (KV) cache, avoiding a repeated prompt prefill. Qwen3-0.6B has been fine-tuned with LoRA on Reason-9k and Bitext reply rows. JevBench accuracy shows no statistically significant change (55.4% to 54.5%, p=0.87), while expected calibration error falls from 0.292 to 0.169. Held-out Bitext routing accuracy reaches 98.3%. Cache reuse saves 15-37% of latency on an Apple M4 Pro, with token-identical replies in the FP32 demonstration cases. Scoring options on copies of the prompt cache matches full-sequence scoring to within 1.4e-6 in probability. Adding dialogue, industry and complaint data (System X+) improves support-task results but lowers IFEval accuracy from 55.5% to 45.0%. System X v4 uses Qwen3-1.7B, rebalanced data and teacher-rewritten reply targets. Against its base LLM, JevBench accuracy rises from 55.0% to 61.0% (p=0.04), with no statistically significant IFEval change (p=0.71). A matched latency test on unadapted Qwen3-1.7B saves 17-39%. One LLM judge prefers v4 replies to an unadapted 1.7B second call (31 to 11 pairs, p=0.003); the other's preference is not significant. These reply comparisons also differ in prompts and generation limits. State conflicts and unsupported reply details remain failure modes. Further studies with several seeds, human raters and real support data are recommended.
  </details>

- **[A Cross-Domain Evaluation of Prompting Techniques for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24913)**  `arXiv:2610.24913`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Kevin Krazius, Tim Schlippe, Paul Libbrecht_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prompt engineering has become a central mechanism for guiding the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs) across a wide range of reasoning and language understanding tasks [1], [2]. However, existing studies frequently evaluate prompting techniques using different LLMs, datasets, or task settings, making direct comparisons across methods and domains difficult [3]. Consequently, in this paper we present a controlled cross-domain evaluation of Zero-Shot prompting, Few-Shot prompting, Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), and automatic prompt optimization based on a PE2-inspired optimization pipeline [4]-[6]. All experiments were conducted with the same LLM and evaluated on six benchmark datasets covering arithmetic reasoning, logical reasoning, commonsense reasoning, and factual verification. The results show that no prompting strategy consistently outperforms the others across all domains, and pairwise statistical comparisons of the highest-performing configurations revealed no significant differences. RAG achieves the highest overall average accuracy of 78.33% when 300 examples are available for retrieval, while Few-Shot Prompting achieves comparatively high accuracies across several structured reasoning tasks. Zero-Shot Prompting remains competitive on tasks with self-contained inputs, whereas the PE2-inspired optimization approach produces stable but not systematically superior results compared to the other evaluated prompting techniques. Furthermore, increasing the number of task-specific examples does not consistently improve performance across prompting techniques, indicating strong domain and task dependency. These findings suggest that prompting techniques should be selected adaptively based on task characteristics rather than treated as universally applicable optimization strategies.
  </details>

- **[HybridResearcher: Bridging the Structured-Unstructured Boundary for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24899)**  `arXiv:2610.24899`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Soyoung Yoon, Peiran Xu, Han Wang, Ruofan Wu, Xiaodong Yu, Feng Yan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While LLM agents are increasingly capable of gathering and synthesizing evidence from a single homogeneous source, such as structured tables or unstructured data, real-world analytical questions frequently span heterogeneous sources. For example, answering one enterprise question may require both a result computed over an internal database and evidence found on the web. Recent evaluations on benchmarks like HybridDeepResearch show that even frontier agents struggle when evidence must cross this boundary. While these benchmarks have exposed the limitation, methodologies to explicitly teach cross-source reasoning remain absent. To address this, we introduce HybridResearcher, a 4B agent trained to bridge evidence across the structured and unstructured boundary. To build it, we synthesize cross-source bridge trajectories by starting with validated single-source tasks-such as existing text-to-SQL or web search queries-and fabricating the missing source around a bridge entity drawn from the seed's own output. We retain a sample only when strict construction gates confirm that neither side alone suffices, ensuring the data requires true cross-source interdependence. Finally, we train on this curated dataset using supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning. Evaluated on the existing HybridDeepResearch benchmark, HybridResearcher improves over our base model, Qwen3.5-4B, by 30.0 points pass@8 and surpasses the far larger Qwen3.5-397B-A17B by 8.3 points, while also improving over its base model on established, held-out single-source benchmarks (BrowseComp and BrowseComp-Plus), demonstrating that this complex cross-source reasoning can be taught directly to small models without triggering catastrophic forgetting.
  </details>

- **[Medical Fine-Tuning Reshapes, Not Reduces, Patient-Facing AI Risk: A Rubric-Based, LLM-Judge Evaluation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24807)**  `arXiv:2610.24807`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Savan Doshi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patient-facing medical question answering with large language models (LLMs) is moving from research demonstrations to production deployments, yet evaluation remains anchored on factual correctness. Factuality is necessary but not sufficient: a response can be factually grounded and still issue a directive that, if acted on, would harm the patient. We introduce a risk-sensitive evaluation framework that scores responses by the presence and severity of six categories of risk-bearing language (treatment directives, contraindications, dosage expressions, urgency-triage cues, high-alert medication mentions, and overconfident assertions), anchored on published clinical harm taxonomies. Severity is classified by a three-judge LLM ensemble. We apply this framework to six 7-9B instruction-tuned models, three general (Mistral-7B, Phi-3.5-mini, Llama-3.1-8B) and three medically adapted (BioMistral-7B, OpenBioLLM-Llama3-8B, Med42-Llama3-8B), on 200 real patient questions from the K-QA benchmark. Two findings emerge. First, ensemble risk-bearing language is statistically independent of factual hallucination rate (pooled Spearman $\rho = -0.065$, $n = 300$, 95% CI $[-0.18, +0.05]$; distance correlation $= 0.10$, independence test $p = 0.24$). Second, medical fine-tuning does not reduce aggregate risk but reshapes it: continued-pretraining adaptations (BioMistral, OpenBioLLM) consistently suppress high-alert medication mentions while amplifying overconfident assertions, whereas clinical instruction-tuning (Med42) suppresses dosing only, without amplifying overconfidence. Per-category Krippendorff $\alpha$ is fair to good for the four linguistically anchored categories (0.53-0.71) and low for the two clinical-judgment-dependent categories (0.19, 0.31), indicating where automatic safety evaluation is and is not yet reliable. We release the rubric, judge prompts, and evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[Axiomatic World Modeling for Physics Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24519)**  `arXiv:2610.24519`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xinye Yang, Zhenyang Liu, Yuxuan Wang, Yuanyuan Lei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent research-level physics-reasoning benchmarks for LLMs reveal a failure mode that lies not in knowledge or execution, but in modeling the world being reasoned about. Models often silently alter given quantities, fabricate laws, or inject unstated assumptions to force a problem into familiar patterns, and then reason inside this corrupted world. We define this premise-level divergence as modeling drift: the gap between a model's internal axiomatic world model (AWM) and the true physical world specified in the problem. Although RLVR and PRM have yielded substantial gains in mathematics and code, their reward shapes do not directly supervise world modeling in axiomatic domains such as physics. We propose Reinforcement Learning with Axiomatic World Modeling (RLAWM), which optimizes the policy by penalizing divergence between its inferred axiomatic world model and the true physics world. RLAWM probes physics reasoning at two hierarchical levels (modeling and solving), each scored by a physically grounded reward. The modeling reward targets abstract knowledge formulation, evaluating whether the model captures the true axiomatic world before attempting calculation. The solving reward targets instantiated knowledge reasoning, checking whether the model's concrete derivation remains consistent with the axiomatic world. This two-level structure separates what world the model believes it is reasoning in from how it reasons within that world. The framework operates exclusively during training, with no extra cost during inference. RLAWM improves performance by up to +12.2 points on PhysReason and +5.2 on PHYSICS over the baseline. It also generalizes zero-shot across difficulty, source, and domain, with a 2.45x gain and +10 points over the strongest baseline on olympiad-level physics and math.
  </details>

- **[The Judge Can Debias Itself: Awareness and Self-Debiasing for Reliable LLM-as-a-Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24320)**  `arXiv:2610.24320`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Youbing Yin, Jingyu Wu, Anoop Kumar, Scott Novotney, Daben Liu, Erin Babinsky_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-Judge systems are increasingly employed for automated evaluation, yet remain susceptible to systematic biases that undermine their reliability. We demonstrate that the judge can effectively debias itself without additional training. We propose two lightweight strategies: bias-aware prompting, which explicitly alerts the judge to potential target biases prior to evaluation, and self-debiasing, in which the judge critiques the initial judgment for bias influence. To broaden the evaluation scope, we introduce three novel benchmarks--authority bias (fabricated citations), confidence bias (assertive phrasing), and formatting bias (structured presentation)--and evaluate across seven bias types in total. Across three frontier models, self-debiasing improves performance in 20 out of 21 model-bias combinations with gains up to 15 percentage points, while bias-aware prompting also yields consistent improvements. The recent fine-tuned external critic, however, does not generalize as reliably to the newly-introduced bias types (degradation observed in 5 out of 9 cases). Our findings demonstrate that prompting a judge to reason about bias, either preemptively or retrospectively, is a robust and generalizable approach for enhancing evaluation integrity.
  </details>

- **[GardenBench: A lightweight, daily evaluation of LLM capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23866)**  `arXiv:2610.23866`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Benedict Cummins-Mburu, Mariana Garcia Mejia, Oscar Heath, Sabrina Kreyzerman, Ellie Murray, Arusan Surendiran, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce GardenBench, a benchmark with 11 test suites that evaluate the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) across a range of tasks. By systematically prompting LLMs with various lightweight queries, we document day-to-day changes in model performance, including run costs and times, that other benchmarks miss. This allows us to compare the accuracy and features of responses across model providers and versions. We account for daily variations in model accuracy, capturing both model progress and degradation, for instance, when a model is changed post-deployment or when a new model is released. We base our evaluation on general-use tasks and make our results publicly accessible via a live dashboard, diversifying existing approaches to LLM testing. Our main contribution is an innovative, lightweight, and inexpensive benchmark, designed to be consumer-facing and for everyday tasks.
  </details>

- **[Planning Commitment of LLM under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21108)**  `arXiv:2610.21108`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Cassie Huang, Zhanwei Cao, Yeawon Lee, Nathalia Gomez, Zhuoyi Yang, Ian G. Harris, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based planning agents commit to plans of different lengths before interacting with a partially observable environment. Some aggressively commit to a long plan based on assumptions about unobserved states, while others conservatively plan only over what they have observed. Both behaviors emerge implicitly in pre-trained models, whether the LLM outputs a plan directly or generates a PDDL formulation for a classical solver. Aggressive commitment risks costly failures, while conservative commitment risks excessive LLM calls. This choice pits safety against efficiency, a trade-off that has been neither characterized systematically nor made controllable. We investigate aggressive and conservative LLM planners and formalizers on two partially observable domains, where priors are provided as either explicit statistics or common-sense knowledge. Across three LLMs, both strategies reach similar accuracy. Conservative methods achieve fewer failed attempts (safety) at the cost of more API calls (efficiency). Aggressive methods fail more often but replan efficiently only when priors are informative. Interestingly, analysis shows that LLMs treat the provided priors nearly as facts, which risks failure when priors are unreliable. We also explore hybrid strategies with tunable hyperparameters that adjust the balance between safety and efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Read the Room Before Your Model Generates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18820)**  `arXiv:2610.18820`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Muyao Kong, Zhihao Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) generally rely on sampling temperature to regulate the amount of randomness in their outputs. Mainstream methods for selecting temperature typically involve fixed, heuristic global settings and context or token-level calibration, rather than a geometrically interpretable representation of the communicative scenario. In this work, we introduce a simple and interpretable temperature selection framework, the Scenario-Actor, which adapts decoding to the inferred situational characteristics. Scenario-Actor extracts scenario-relevant structures from Transformer hidden states and determines the temperature from scenario embedding, a structured geometric representation of the communicative setting.
