Empirical results show that on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Scenario-Actor achieves strong goal-completion performance in interactive social scenarios, significant gains on open-ended instruction following, and competitive performance on multi-turn conversation compared to multiple baselines.
  </details>

- **[Break It Down, Pass It On: Cross-Task Skill Transfer in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.20274)**  `arXiv:2608.20274`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yiyang Feng, Biddut Sarker Bijoy, Niranjan Balasubramanian, Jiawei Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can induce skills from completed tasks and reuse them later to grow more capable with experience. In practice, induced skills may transfer unreliably and can even harm the agent that retrieves them. When agent-induced skills transfer reliably across tasks remains an open question. We conduct a comprehensive and controlled study of how the way skills are induced shapes their transfer across tasks. Specifically, we compare task-level with subtask-level skill induction and text with code skill formats, the two axes along which existing methods differ. Task-level skills mostly reduce the agent's performance below its no-memory baseline while subtask-level skills raise it above on average, and text skills transfer better than code skills. To further understand our findings, we examine two complementary properties of the induced skills: specificity, which measures how closely a skill matches real tasks, and abstractness, which measures how evenly its relevance spreads across tasks. Neither property alone predicts task success, but their combined effect does, which we propose as a skill utility score. The score correlates consistently with task success when skills are transferred, and subtask-level and text skills score higher. Computing skill utility only needs the skills and task descriptions, so our score serves as a lightweight diagnostic of a skill memory for practitioners.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Objective Structured Pruning of LLMs for Latency and Model Size Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22583)**  `arXiv:2607.22583`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Muhammad Junaid Ali, Smail Niar, El-Ghazali Talbi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved widespread adoption because of their strong reasoning and query-response capabilities. However, deploying them in embedded and edge computing environments remains challenging because of strict latency, memory, and energy constraints. Their large parameter counts and computational demands hinder efficient execution on resource-constrained platforms. Although model pruning has emerged as a viable solution for reducing scale while preserving performance, jointly optimizing layers, attention heads, and Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) dimensions remains highly complex. Exhaustively exploring this combined design space is computationally expensive and often leads to local optima or unstable configurations. To address these limitations, we propose a hardware-aware, multi-objective structured pruning framework. The proposed two-stage method explicitly targets latency and model size for efficient deployment on edge devices. In the coarse-grained stage, multi-objective depth pruning removes entire attention and MLP blocks to reduce computational load and memory usage. In the subsequent fine-grained stage, Parallel Bayesian Optimization (PBO) searches for the optimal layer-wise pruning ratios for pruning under latency constraints, while importance-based strategies rank the specific components to be pruned within each layer's allocated budget. Experimental results show that our approach reduces model complexity with minimal impact on commonsense reasoning tasks and zero-shot performance. Our method achieves a favorable trade-off among accuracy, latency, and model size, making it suitable for edge deployment. Across multiple LLMs at 37.5% and 50% pruning ratios, the proposed approach achieves better performance on commonsense reasoning tasks than existing methods while significantly reducing inference cost.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Cooperative Simulators: Generating Realistic User Personas for Robust Evaluation of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12894)**  `arXiv:2605.12894`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Harshita Chopra, Kshitish Ghate, Aylin Caliskan, Tadayoshi Kohno, Chirag Shah, Natasha Jaques_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in settings where they interact with diverse users, including those who are unclear, impatient, or reluctant to share information. However, collecting real interaction data at scale remains expensive. The field has turned to LLM-based \emph{user simulators} as stand-ins, but these simulators inherit the behavior of their underlying models: cooperative and homogeneous. As a result, agents that appear strong in simulation often fail in real human interactions. To narrow this gap, we introduce Persona Policies (PPol), a plug-and-play control layer that induces realistic behavioral variation in user simulators while preserving original task goals. Rather than hand-crafting personas, we employ an evolutionary coding agent to discover persona generation programs optimized for human-likeness and behavioral coverage over real user conversations. The evolved program generates diverse, human-like personas for any task in the domain. Across 4 benchmarks--including $\tau^2$-bench Retail and Airline, ColBench, and WildChat--evolved PPol yield 28-72% absolute gains in fitness score over the baseline simulator. In blinded evaluations, annotators judged PPol users as 'human' 80.4% of the time, nearly 2x more than the baseline simulators. Training agents with PPol also improves real-world performance: our user study with live human-agent interactions showed that fine-tuning with our method boosted task success by +23% over default baselines. PPol thus offers a novel approach to strengthen simulator-based evaluation and training without changing underlying tasks.
  </details>

- **[Same Simulation, Different Evidence: Where Retrieved Tool Output Costs LLM Agents Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26836)**  `arXiv:2610.26836`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ke Zhang, Sahchit Chundur, Jerry Peprah Owusu, Mohammad Javad Abdolhosseini Qomi, Maziar Raissi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents that compute the right answer can still answer wrongly. Agent harnesses increasingly keep long tool outputs out of the context window, saving them to files that the agent searches or reads on demand. We ask where this costs accuracy with a real scientific tool: PHREEQC, a widely used geochemistry simulator from the USGS, whose reports reach 846.8k characters and whose determinism lets every printed value be traced. Agents use it to answer 158 execution-verified multiple-choice items. When agents read the simulator's report through a section index rather than in context, GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2 lose 11.3 and 7.9 points of accuracy, while Claude Opus 4.6 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 show no detectable cost. Re-running all 11,007 logged simulator calls shows that both GPT models compute the keyed value about as often as before; what changes is the evidence they answer from. Under retrieval they often skip the report, answer from their own higher-precision output files, and misround to a neighboring option, a failure we call evidence substitution. With the simulation held fixed, retrieval still costs GPT-5.2 9.4 points. For GPT-5.4, more frequent correct simulations mask an evidence-use loss of about ten points, leaving a net change indistinguishable from zero. Naming the authoritative output raises accuracy by 6.3-7.9 points over plain retrieval for the three GPT models; for GPT-5.1 and GPT-5.2, inlining outputs that fit works as well, while asking for correct rounding does not. Context-management choices in agent harnesses should be judged by where accuracy is lost, not only by final accuracy.
  </details>

- **[LG-Bench: A Graph-Structured Evaluation Benchmark for Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26787)**  `arXiv:2610.26787`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lu Sun, Xiangyi Zhang, Xiangyang Zhu, Kaiyuan Ji, Zijian Chen, Qiang Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional evaluation benchmarks reduce inherently interconnected scientific knowledge in life sciences into flat lists of questions, disregarding the underlying topological structure of the knowledge. We introduce, the first graph-structured benchmark for life sciences, featuring over 10,000 high-quality multiple-choice questions across medicine, biology, and chemistry. Our approach constructs a weighted evaluation graph using bidirectional matching and semantic similarity algorithms, where nodes represent questions and edge weights capture their semantic relationships. Leveraging this graph topology, we design two novel evaluation metrics. The Global Coherence Score (GCS) measures a model's consistency within semantically related neighborhoods, while Knowledge Balance Score (KBS) analyzes how model errors are distributed across the graph to reveal conceptual blind spots. LG-Bench facilitates fine-grained comparison of LLMs by surfacing differences in conceptual coherence and patterns of knowledge organization across models. Our framework shifts the evaluation paradigm from flat accuracy metrics to structure-aware analysis, offering a new lens for diagnosing and improving LLM performance in the life sciences domain.
  </details>

- **[AllPath: Layered Verification for a Deployed, Self-Revising LLM Trading Agent under Human-Gated and Autonomous Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26662)**  `arXiv:2610.26662`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tian Zhang, Meng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM trading agents are evaluated almost exclusively in backtests and autonomous benchmark harnesses. Deployment, where actions are irreversible, raises a different question: who verifies the agent's actions, and which failures can each verifier structurally catch? We report on AllPath, a personal LLM trading agent deployed against a live paper-brokerage account. The system watches positions through a deterministic rules engine, and each night it reviews its own day and proposes revisions to its own trading strategies. Verification is split across three layers. Code verifies what must not depend on judgment: deterministic gates enforce risk limits and block any strategy revision that fails an integrity check. The agent verifies itself within fixed bounds: its nightly review has an iteration cap and no tools that can act. A human verifies judgment, through approval screens that show the current state of each proposal. We also compared two verification policies for the agent's self-revisions on the live system: two and a half weeks with every self-revision human-gated, then five weeks with reflection revisions auto-applied behind the same code gates, while the operator's chat channel stayed human-gated throughout. Each layer caught a different kind of failure. The code gates rejected no proposal; their role was keeping revisions consistent. The human rejected two proposals that passed every code check, both over a disagreement about the market, and later removed 11 of the 19 rules the agent had added on its own, at a median lag of eight days. The most instructive production incidents, such as strategies silently left unmonitored, passed every gate; the operator found them by hand, and the fix each time was an interface change that makes the condition visible. We contribute the layered architecture, the two-regime account, and a production incident taxonomy, none observable in a backtest.
  </details>

- **[BOSS: Token-Budgeted Online Sampling and Selection for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26383)**  `arXiv:2610.26383`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yupeng Chang, Yahan Li, Chenlu Guo, Diana Inkpen, Yuan Wu, Yi Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling can improve LLM reasoning, but existing approaches typically adapt generation or apply answer selection after a predetermined generation schedule. We study within-instance allocation: under a hard per-instance prompt-plus-completion token cap, when should inference stop, generate another candidate, or spend residual tokens adjudicating candidates already observed? BOSS is a training-free policy for this three-way decision, with worst-case budget checks before every generation and judge call. Its key contribution is online generation-adjudication allocation under one shared token ledger. Across two Llama-3.1 models and four reasoning benchmarks, BOSS attains the highest accuracy in 22 of 24 model-task-budget cells under the common hard cap. At Llama-3.1-8B and B=4k, it reaches 60.5 macro-average accuracy versus 58.6 for Adaptive-Consistency, 59.1 for TALE-EP, and 58.9 for fixed-schedule League. On identical Stage-I candidate pools, randomized A/B judging with repeated voting improves plurality from 58.4 to 60.3, with the full BOSS selector reaching 60.5. Approximately matched-cost controls further characterize how the benefit of adjudication changes with available inference budget. Together, these results establish generation-adjudication allocation as a distinct design axis for reasoning under strict per-instance caps.
  </details>

- **[GAIDA: Generative AI for Human-in-the-Loop Document Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26362)**  `arXiv:2610.26362`  `cs.AI`  
  _Islam Mesabah, Ali Elhosary, Priyabanta Sandulu, Darko Obradovic, Sebastian J. Vollmer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Document annotation is a fundamental yet labor-intensive step in building document information extraction systems. While Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and Large Language Models (LLMs) can automate parts of this process, we argue that annotation systems should be judged not by how completely they remove human effort, but by how effectively they redirect it toward review and correction. We propose a modular human-in-the-loop architecture that integrates OCR-based extraction, LLM-based semantic pre-annotation, and human validation in a single editable workflow, and present \textbf{GAIDA}, a research prototype serving as its reference implementation. GAIDA supports interchangeable OCR engines, LLM backends, and prompt configurations, and enables stage-specific measurement of annotation effort. In an initial single-annotator, stage-specific study on the FUNSD scanned-form and WildReceipt retail-receipt benchmarks, OCR assistance reduced region-creation and transcription time by up to \textbf{80.9\%}, and reviewing LLM pre-annotations reduced semantic-labeling time by up to \textbf{73.7\%}, despite imperfect pre-annotations of 78.86\% and 86.98\% Micro F1 on FUNSD and WildReceipt, respectively. Finally, we reuse the same implementation, adapting only OCR and prompt configuration, to build and openly release \textbf{AraReceipt}, a manually annotated dataset of 100 Arabic receipt images labeled with 25 key-information classes. The results indicate that imperfect automated outputs provide practical value when presented as editable suggestions under human control.
  </details>

- **[StrucChunk: Structure-Aware Retrieval for Legal Document RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26313)**  `arXiv:2610.26313`  `cs.AI`  
  _Surya Deep Singh, Ashutosh Tripathi, Gaurav Deep Singh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can ground LLM outputs in domain-specific knowledge, but legal documents are hard: standard chunking fragments hierarchical argument structure, and generic embeddings struggle with both precise citation matching and semantic understanding. We propose StrucChunk (Structure Aware Chunking) with hybrid dense-sparse retrieval for hierarchically structured legal documents. It treats documents as directed acyclic graphs of provisions, preserves clause boundaries, resolves cross-references before embedding, and combines BM25 with BGE-M3 via Reciprocal Rank Fusion. We evaluate on five corpora, three legal traditions and two languages. StrucChunk reaches 100% boundary precision (vs. 7.7% for recursive splitting on CrPC), preserves 97.6% of documented cross-references (vs. 0%), and matches section coverage with 40.3% fewer chunks on GDPR. On LegalBench-RAG (6,858 query-passage pairs), it achieves Char-Recall@8 of 48.18% vs. 23.59% for the best baseline (+104%), surpassing the published state of the art by +83%. On 501 queries each for CrPC and CPC (Indian law), Recall@5 improves from 0.557 to 0.804 (+44.4%) and from 0.158 to 0.259 (+64.6%); chunking accounts for 88% and 73% of the gain. On 501 GDPR queries, Recall@5 is 0.868 with R@1 improving +59%. On NitiBench-CCL (3,730 Thai financial law queries), HR@5 is 0.856 vs. 0.594 for naive chunking (+44.1%), showing that section-boundary alignment is the dominant factor across languages, and cross-reference augmentation adds +2.3% on cross-reference queries. We release the implementation, 5,256 evaluation queries across five legal corpora, annotation methodology and chunking quality metrics. Code:this http URL
  </details>

- **[LLMs Prompted for Legal Context Refuse More: Overrefusal from Small On-Premises LLMs in Criminal Legal Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26211)**  `arXiv:2610.26211`  `cs.AI`  
  _Anastasiia Kucherenko, Francois Brouchoud, Dimitri Percia David, Andrei Kucharavy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Confidentiality requirements increasingly push legal professionals toward small, on-device LLMs that keep case data in-house, used for everyday tasks such as summarization, translation, and reformulation. Following standard prompting guidance, a user naturally states their role (``I am a lawyer...''). We show that this innocuous habit has an unexpected cost: such authority framing can make the model selectively refuse legitimate requests, introducing bias through uneven access to assistance. We evaluate four modern small open-weight LLMs on benign legal-domain prompts to measure this over-refusal.Surprisingly, we find that authority-style prefixes (''I am legal counsel working for a national supreme court'', ''I'm a defense lawyer'') systematically increase refusal rates by 2--20x over the no-prefix baseline, while a known role-play jailbreak prefix shows mixed effects, sharply increasing refusals in some models and barely shifting them in others. The finding suggests that small on-prem deployable LLMs are unstable under contextual framings that a real institutional user might naturally introduce, and further investigation is essential to minimize opportunities for bias.
  </details>

- **[MoveBench: A Rubric-Based Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models in Sports and Health Coaching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26185)**  `arXiv:2610.26185`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitong Li, Jiang Bian, Jingling Wang, Pan Ding, Chengying Gu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite rapid advances in large language models (LLMs), their deployment in the sports and health domain remains high-risk due to the absence of domain-specific evaluation benchmarks. Existing general-purpose benchmarks (e.g., MMLU and HELM) primarily assess broad knowledge and reasoning capabilities, but fail to capture critical domain requirements such as exercise safety, physiological appropriateness, personalization, and actionable coaching quality. This gap is particularly concerning in fitness and health applications, where incorrect or unsafe recommendations can lead to physical harm. To address this limitation, we introduce MoveBench, the first comprehensive benchmarking framework specifically designed for evaluating LLMs in sports and health coaching. The proposed benchmark integrates heterogeneous task formats, including multiple-choice questions (MCQs) and open-ended question answering (OpenQA), grounded in real-world user scenarios and paired with structured rubrics emphasizing safety compliance, scientific validity, and training logic. Through large-scale evaluation of 15 diverse LLMs on 809 questions with 5,012 rubric-level criteria, our results reveal substantial performance variability, particularly in safety awareness, structured program design, and personalization capability, highlighting a critical gap between knowing and doing. Our work establishes a foundational evaluation standard for sports and health LLM applications and provides a reproducible framework for advancing safe, reliable, and context-aware AI-driven coaching systems. Open resources are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reliable Before Profitable: QuantHarness for Governing Financial Strategy Development with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26101)**  `arXiv:2610.26101`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiasheng Mao, Jiacheng Liu, Hengwei Ye, Zheng Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can turn natural-language financial research requests into executable and backtested strategies, but a successful run may still implement the wrong rule, use information unavailable at decision time, or obscure how repairs and candidate selection changed the strategy. Because these failures arise throughout development, final code and performance metrics alone are insufficient to establish either fidelity to the request or the validity of the development process. We therefore address the reliability of financial strategy development at the trajectory level: the system must control how intermediate outputs become downstream state and preserve the evidence needed to justify the terminal outcome. We introduce QuantHarness, a two-layer agent harness in which a finance-specific Workflow structures specification, data grounding, construction, testing, and review, while Harness Core enforces stage-specific information, action, and commitment boundaries. We also introduce FinRelBench, a benchmark that evaluates strategy-development reliability through execution success, requirement satisfaction, and runtime evidence. QuantHarness achieves the highest Task Success at 90.72\%, compared with 74.23\%--84.54\% for the comparison systems. Controlled stress tests show that its boundaries contain targeted violations, while structured run records improve evidence retrieval and support decision replay under the evaluated protocol.
  </details>

- **[FinSafeGuard: A Quality-Driven Synthetic Dataset for Financial AI Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26066)**  `arXiv:2610.26066`  `cs.AI`  
  _Joseph Thomas Thacil, Reetu Raj Harsh, Krati Saxena, Bhaskarjit Sarmah, Stefano Pasquali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating high-quality synthetic datasets at scale remains a fundamental challenge for training specialized AI systems in data-scarce domains. We present a comprehensive methodology that produces 14.3 million quality-filtered synthetic training examples from a structured taxonomy of 632,000 scenario specifications. Our four-stage pipeline ad- dresses critical challenges in synthetic data quality, diversity, and realism through: (1) systematic pre-generation research establishing optimal generation parameters via diminishing returns analysis, (2) conditional auto evolution mechanisms that remediate low-diversity batches during generation, (3) a two-layer semantic deduplication architecture combining lexical and embedding-based methods, and (4) LLM-as-Judge quality evaluation filtering samples based on behavioral correctness and tuple fidelity. Empirical analysis across three domains (Banking, Insurance, Financial Services) demonstrates domain-specific deduplication patterns ranging from 9.9% to 40.6%, with output generation modes exhibiting highest redundancy. Our batch size optimization reveals mode-specific diversity saturation thresholds, enabling principled allocation strategies. The complete pipeline reduces 26.4M raw samples to 14.3M high-confidence examples through merge (20.9% reduction), semantic deduplication (18.7% reduction), and quality-based filtering (15.9% reduction), providing a reproducible framework for domain-specific synthetic data generation with rigorous quality control.
  </details>

- **[TimesJev: Thinking Fast on Time Series with System 1 Sufficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26002)**  `arXiv:2610.26002`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chenghao Liu, Wei Fan, Jindong Han, Dongjie Wang, Aniket Mahanti, Pengyang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown strong potential for time series reasoning, while models that reason over time series are usually with long chains of thought. However, this process often consumes thousands of tokens, directly leading to substantial latency during inference. In contrast, System 1 models such as Jev make decisions instantly and can naturally be an alternative, but raw time series are not compatible with the embedding space of these models, thus hindering the reasoning performance, especially when input series contain thousands of numeric points. In this paper, we make a very early attempt to propose a time series System 1 model, namely TimesJev. Specifically, we first formalize System 1 sufficiency in time series reasoning, which motivates a compressed representation for time series with key frequency patterns preserved. To construct this representation, we derive a dimension reduction objective that combines (i) a sufficiency divergence that measures the core information lost during compression, and (ii) a realizability divergence that quantifies the incompatibility of the System 1 model to the optimal prediction given this representation. We optimize this objective by learning time series compression with a gating mechanism for sufficiency, and then aligning the selected representation with the embedding space for realizability. Finally, experiments on 13 datasets demonstrate that TimesJev outperforms reasoning models by over 62% with 62.9% less inference time than their fine-tuned versions on average.
  </details>

- **[Procedural Memory for Language Agents: A Neurosymbolic Perspective on Explicit Goal Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25996)**  `arXiv:2610.25996`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jonathan Leung, Zhiqi Shen, Deheng Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models remain unreliable on long-horizon tasks requiring procedural reasoning. We argue that a key cause is the absence of explicit procedural memory: accessible, modifiable knowledge of how tasks are accomplished, distinct from semantic and episodic memory. Goal-Oriented Graphs offer such a representation, encoding goals and prerequisite dependencies as inspectable symbolic structure. We examine their construction, the challenges they pose, and principles for integrating them with neural reasoning.
  </details>

- **[GraphEvoResearcher: Graph-Aware Training-Free Self-Reflective Reasoning for Cost-Bounded Multi-Hop Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25978)**  `arXiv:2610.25978`  `cs.AI`  
  _Suxing Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-hop question answering (QA) requires models to chain intermediate conclusions while remaining cost-aware. Existing training-free self-reflective protocols (generate -> critique -> revise) improve accuracy but operate on a flat token stream: revisions cannot identify which prior step is unreliable, and early stopping cannot exploit structural convergence. We introduce GraphEvoResearcher, a training-free protocol that lifts EvoResearcher's self-reflective meta-reward onto an explicit reasoning graph - nodes are intermediate conclusions/evidence and edges encode support, contradict, and depends_on. A graph-aware meta-reward combines correctness, efficiency, reflection, diversity, and four structural signals (connectivity, acyclicity, branch coverage, path convergence) to decide whether to revise, backtrack, or stop. A graph-convergence early-stop terminates when evidence branches converge on a single conclusion. On a real LLM (DeepSeek), over GSM8K, MATH, and BBH (n=50 each), GraphEvoResearcher reaches 98%, 92%, and 82% respectively, the highest of all baselines, with the largest gains on the hardest set (MATH: +24 pp over single-shot, +12 pp over flat reflection), while keeping construction cost bounded at ~2 steps / 4.5-5 nodes. Our results indicate the structural signal of a reasoning graph is most valuable exactly where single-step reasoning is unreliable.
  </details>

- **[CXBench: A Benchmark for Process-Compliant LLM Agents in Regulated Customer-Experience Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25954)**  `arXiv:2610.25954`  `cs.AI`  
  _Boxi Yu, Yuzhong Zhang, Zhenfeng Su, Linfu Xi, Yinghan Yao, Shitao Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using language agents increasingly perform regulated customer-service workflows, where a correct final state is insufficient: agents must also provide disclosures, obtain consent, and follow required procedures. Existing tool-agent benchmarks largely score final business states, accepting trajectories that reach correct outcomes through non-compliant processes.We introduce cxbench, a benchmark that evaluates outcome and process compliance separately across six regulated domains. Each task combines a customer request, a tool-based environment, and explicit process requirements. Rule cards link cited regulatory obligations to observable trace evidence. A hybrid grader combines deterministic checks for ordering and authority with an LLM judge for semantic requirements. We define a false-pass as an outcome-pass run that fails process checks.Across seven models and 120 tasks, 55.5% of the 840 retained executions are false-passes, representing 80.1% of outcome-passing runs; only 13.8% pass both checks. Process-inclusive evaluation changes the observed model ordering and exposes differences hidden by outcome-only scoring. cxbench makes these failures inspectable through trace-linked criteria.
  </details>

- **[TESS: LLM-guided Translation, Exploration, Simulation, and Search for Embodied Task Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25930)**  `arXiv:2610.25930`  `cs.AI`  
  _Peiran Xu, Yixiang Yang, Jinghan Li, Fei Li, Cao Sheng, JiaZhong Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work we focus on the problem of embodied task planning, which requires an agent to accomplish a complex task by separating it into atomic actions. Recent studies have developed LLM-based methods to address this problem. However, the executability and correctness of the generated plans remain suboptimal in complex environments and tasks. On the other hand, classical search algorithms have the potential to solve the planning problems perfectly, yet they require fully observable environments, known state transitions and goal state, which are often unattainable in general task domains. Therefore, we propose to implement an LLM-guided search algorithm that makes the search process possible and efficient by incorporating LLMs' commonsense knowledge. Our method leverages LLMs to (1) translate natural language instructions into formatted goal conditions, (2) facilitate efficient exploration in partially observable environments, (3) implement a rule-based world simulator, and (4) prune the space of candidate actions to improve search efficiency. Experimental results on VirtualHome and ALFWorld demonstrate its superior performance.
  </details>

- **[Benefit-Targeted Routing and Bounded Revision for LLM-Based Evidence Screening in Manufacturing Supplier Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25867)**  `arXiv:2610.25867`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijiashun Qi, Hanzhe Guo, Yuxuan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An evidence-review system must allocate additional model judgments and control their use. We study these software decisions on 6,164 frozen manufacturing source-task pairs. Benefit-targeted routing learns recovered agreement minus newly introduced disagreement. On the original 300-case test, 60 consultations yield 202/231 agreements with two human references versus 189/218 for a logistic-risk router. An additional 300 cases were independently annotated twice using equal frozen evidence. Without retraining the historical policies, the corresponding counts are 164/190 versus 163/193; the differences do not resolve an advantage. Strict scalar-Ridge controls share preprocessing, regularization and fitting, and remain inconclusive. Quotation and requested-state replacement gates retain rejected cases and budget costs; their joint gate rejects one original and two additional consultations without changing primary outcomes. Evidence ablations, fusion and bounded updates supply further controls. A completed 200-pair identity audit yields 97.45 percent weighted merge precision after eight author adjudications. This audits a provisional map, while prospective company-disjoint performance remains untested.
  </details>

- **[A Cue's Hidden Price: Auditing Seller Influence on AI Shoppers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25843)**  `arXiv:2610.25843`  `cs.AI`  
  _Felix Buecheler, Gerardo Berbeglia, Mingyu Guo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly asked to shop on a user's behalf, which lets seller-controlled content such as badges, endorsements, and framing steer what they recommend. That poses the question at the core of generative engine optimization (GEO): not whether seller content shifts an agent's choice, but how much. The usual gauge of that influence, how often a cue wins the choice, has a blind spot: once a cue is strong enough to be picked almost every time, the choice rate can no longer show how much stronger one cue is than another. We introduce price-equivalent (PE) auditing, which removes this ceiling by pricing the cue in money. In a counterbalanced, position-controlled forced choice, we raise the cued product's price until it is chosen only half the time, and report that premium as the cue's price-equivalent: a formally defined, policy-conditioned parity threshold that makes very different cues comparable. Priced this way, cues that look equally strong at equal price differ by a wide margin, so choice-rate audits substantially under-resolve seller influence. We run the audit over 113,472 trials, seven models across families, and two product categories. To our knowledge, we also run the first price-equivalent audit of a decision-native model, Jev, which outputs a choice probability directly instead of generating text; strikingly, its profile is unlike any text model we tested, resisting most cues yet remaining susceptible to the strongest social-proof claims. We release the stimuli, logs, and code as a benchmark that reports each model's cue-price profile rather than a single score, giving developers and auditors a common, reproducible way to measure how content moves AI shopping agents.
  </details>

- **[When to Stop Asking: Factor Coverage Bias and LLM Determinability Judgments with Underspecified Information in Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25819)**  `arXiv:2610.25819`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yusuke Watanabe, Yohei Kobashi, Taisei Tosaki, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yasushi Okuno, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. This calls for judging determinability (whether the available information is sufficient for a decision) by considering whether missing information could change the conclusion, not simply the amount of available decision-relevant information. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. Our work provides an evaluation framework for LLM trustworthiness under underspecification.
  </details>

- **[Can LLM User-Proxies Stand In for Humans in Reliance Measurement? Replicating Rel-A.I. with Simulated Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25788)**  `arXiv:2610.25788`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuai Wang, Rui Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can language-model user proxies reproduce human reliance on an AI answer across contexts? Published simulated-user studies do not establish validity for this partial-answer decision, and Rel-A.I.'s released human record omits trial-level choices. We audit its 90 printed questions and 15 domain-by-marker reliance rates using sampled decisions and counterbalanced A/B probabilities from open models through 72B parameters. A matched crossover varies the printed agent specialty within each question, separating question content, label averages, and their interaction. Mistral Small's strong-marker math-minus-law label marginal is -0.4 percentage points, yet matching the agent label to the question domain raises reliance by 13.3 points on average across the five domains. The best A/B proxy has 22.5-point absolute-rate error and 8.3-point domain-effect error against the human table. A two-sample independent-binomial human replication has 3.5-point expected domain-effect error under the stated working model. The interaction is model dependent, and inference layout changes identical-prompt probabilities. This single-study audit does not establish proxy validity for human effect sizes or general directional screening.
  </details>

- **[TimelineAgent: An LLM-based Framework for Query-Centric Event Timeline Extraction Across Multiple Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25733)**  `arXiv:2610.25733`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qingkai Min, Yunxin Bao, Yifeng Lu, Zhenyu Feng, Yulong Chen, Andreas Vlachos, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-centric cross-document event timeline extraction aims to construct event timelines from multiple documents under diverse query focuses. The task is very challenging due to the heterogeneous event modeling and reasoning requirements arising from both the intrinsic complexity of cross-document timeline construction and the diversity of query focuses. In this paper, we propose TimelineAgent, an LLM-based multi-agent framework featuring a multi-stage reasoning workflow, where heterogeneous event features are organized into reusable skills and orchestrated through a goal-oriented protocol. To facilitate evaluation, we construct a benchmark covering diverse query focuses. Experimental results show that TimelineAgent consistently outperforms existing methods, offering a practical framework for complex real-world timeline construction.
  </details>

- **[PFedQFT: Personalized Federated Quantum-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25712)**  `arXiv:2610.25712`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qihan Huang, Lingxia Zhang, Guye Zhang, Zizhu Wang, Xiaowei Mao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical data remain fragmented across institutional silos because of privacy, governance, and ownership constraints. Federated learning enables medical institutions to adapt large language models (LLMs) collaboratively without transferring raw records. However, independently parameterized low-rank adapters incur repeated parameter and communication costs across Transformer projections, while uniform aggregation may limit adaptation to heterogeneous institutional data. We propose PFedQFT, a quantum-inspired framework for personalized federated fine-tuning with a compact shared adapter. Specifically, PFedQFT constructs reusable orthonormal adaptation bases from compact skew-symmetric generators using a truncated matrix-exponential approximation followed by QR orthonormalization. Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. Each client further maintains a lightweight private residual operator, whose directions are learned globally and whose coefficients remain local. Experiments on seven public medical tasks and multiple LLM backbones show substantial reductions in trainable parameters and per-round adapter upload volume, with competitive predictive performance on most tasks.
  </details>

- **[From Black Box to Controller: Inference-Time Steering of LLM via Feature Amplification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25700)**  `arXiv:2610.25700`  `cs.AI`  
  _Keqi Chen, Zekai Sun, Yuhua Wen, Tianyi Tian, Xinyang Huang, Huijun Lian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inference-time steering is crucial for deploying Large Language Models (LLMs) in real-world applications, where they must flexibly adjust behavior preferences. For instance, a model should dynamically adjust its caution level based on the risk of the scenario. This requirement introduces a challenge: modulating the intensity of LLM preferences. Prompt engineering can require task-specific prompt design, while fine-tuning requires additional optimization and data. These requirements can make repeated behavioral adjustments costly. To address this, we introduce NeuroSteer, a plug-and-play framework that enables flexible, quantitative adjustment of LLM preferences at inference time. NeuroSteer employs a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) as its controller. Comparing neuron activation frequencies between positive and negative examples isolates "feature neurons" that correspond to target preferences such as politeness and positive sentiment. During inference, the activations of selected SAE features are amplified in the latent representations to steer the output distribution. Users can adjust the strength of this steering on a per-scenario or per-user basis, granting the LLM fine-grained, quantifiable trait modulation. Our experiments show that NeuroSteer can alter sentiment, toxicity, stance, and politeness at inference time. It achieves higher target-direction steering scores than the compared baselines in several settings, with task- and strength-dependent trade-offs in generation quality.
  </details>

- **[CVaR-RMD: CVaR Selection Pressure for Robust Metaheuristic Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25697)**  `arXiv:2610.25697`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ruodai Cui, Li Niu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have recently been used to discover heuristics through iterative program search, offering a promising approach to automated algorithm design. However, candidates selected by average performance may degrade across instance families, random seeds, and problem scales because mean-based objectives can obscure rare but severe failures. We propose CVaR-RMD, a risk-sensitive evolutionary framework that uses an LLM to mutate executable heuristic programs and ranks candidates by empirical conditional value-at-risk over heterogeneous evaluation conditions. Across three combinatorial optimization domains, CVaR-based selection improves upper-tail performance and reduces failure rates while maintaining competitive mean performance. These gains persist on larger unseen instances and independently curated within-domain benchmarks
  </details>

- **[When Safety Fails Collectively: Robust Alignment Against Harmful Fine-Tuning via Joint Weight Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25683)**  `arXiv:2610.25683`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyuan Wang, Yuezhi Zhou, Zhu Zhang, Zhixing Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harmful fine-tuning can rapidly erode the safety alignment of large language models, creating substantial risks when aligned models are adapted to downstream tasks. Existing alignment-stage defenses use harmful examples and simulated harmful perturbations during safety alignment to prepare models for later harmful fine-tuning, yet the collective effects of harmful weight perturbations remain underexplored. We identify a collective failure pattern: disjoint parts of a harmful perturbation have limited effects in isolation, but their joint application increases the model's harmfulness beyond the sum of their individual effects. Guided by this observation, we propose Joint Weight Perturbations (JWP), an alignment-stage defense that masks different parts of a harmful perturbation estimated from the current model and trains the model to respond safely in the resulting states. This exposes the model to different combinations of harmful perturbations and reduces its sensitivity to their accumulation during subsequent harmful fine-tuning. Experiments across downstream tasks and model families show that JWP offers stronger resistance to harmful fine-tuning than prior defenses while largely preserving task performance.
  </details>

- **[Neither Over- nor Under-Constrained: Evaluating LLM Personalization under a Constraint Qualification Audit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25676)**  `arXiv:2610.25676`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuran Li, Guanqin Zhang, Imran Razzak, Hakim Hacid, Eleanna Kafeza, Hao Xue, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurate evaluation of LLM personalization is hindered by current evaluation paradigms that over- or under-constrain the response space, yielding frequent false rejections and false acceptances. To reduce these false rates, we introduce ConQual-Audit, an audit-based framework that evaluates LLM personalization through a three-stage process: (1) extracting semantic constraints from user profiles and task-grounded reference; (2) qualifying constraints by pruning unnecessary constraints and recovering missing constraints; and (3) verifying candidate responses against the audited constraints via natural language inference. Across PrefEval and LaMP, ConQual-Audit outperforms nine baselines, attaining top AUCs (0.88 and 0.90) and the lowest false rate (0.21) on the leanest constraint sets at an amortizable audit cost. Crucially, this constraint audit is portable: integrating it into ExPerT and PREF helps revise smaller, higher-quality constraint sets to reduce their evaluation false rates.
  </details>

- **[LIRQuant: An Efficient Locally Isolated Rotation Quantization Method for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25675)**  `arXiv:2610.25675`  `cs.AI`  
  _Desheng Xu, Hanglin Li, Tianchen Song, Zhiyong Zhao, Kun Zhan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) enables efficient deployment of Large Language Models (LLMs), yet activation outliers remain a key challenge for low-bit quantization. Existing global transformation methods overlook heterogeneous channel sensitivity to quantization noise, leading to suboptimal accuracy-efficiency trade-offs. To address this, we propose \textbf{Locally Isolated Rotation Quantization (LIRQuant)}, a sensitivity-aware framework that selectively applies transformations. LIRQuant identifies sensitive channels using a noise-weighted sensitivity metric, applying expressive learnable rotations only to these channels and lightweight fixed rotations to the remaining ones. This decoupled design reduces optimization complexity without sacrificing outlier smoothing capability. Extensive experiments across the LLaMA-2/3, Mistral, Qwen3, and Mixtral families demonstrate that LIRQuant achieves state-of-the-art accuracy under the challenging W4A4KV4 setting. Meanwhile, LIRQuant calibrates LLaMA-3-8B in only 25 minutes with 20.3 GB peak GPU memory, achieving the fastest calibration and the lowest memory footprint among recent learnable-rotation methods, and its fused inference kernels deliver up to $2.3\times$ prefill speedup and $3.5\times$ memory saving over FP16.
  </details>

- **[Think, Compress, Answer: Reinforcement Learning for Multimodal Grounding via Reasoning Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25640)**  `arXiv:2610.25640`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinbae Seo, Hyeongjun Kwon, Jiyoung Lee, Hyunsung Jang, Namkoo Ha, Jungin Park, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Extended reasoning has substantially improved multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on tasks requiring compositional and relational inference. However, multimodal grounding imposes a distinct requirement: reasoning must ultimately converge on a target-specific compact representation that supports precise localization. As reasoning trajectories grow longer, they often expand into broad scene narratives and peripheral relational details, which can dilute grounded perceptual evidence and lead to ambiguous or incorrect predictions. Motivated by the information bottleneck principle, we propose Think-Compress-Answer (TCA), a reinforcement learning framework that decomposes multimodal grounding into three phases: free-form reasoning, target-aware context compression, and answer generation conditioned on the compressed context. TCA is optimized through a multi-stage Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with progressive rewards that establish the structured output protocol, minimize reasoning-specific information while preserving target-relevant content, and directly improve localization accuracy. This staged design enables MLLMs to retain the benefits of extended reasoning while distilling it into the minimal context required for grounding. Experiments across six multimodal grounding benchmarks show that TCA attains substantial zero-shot performance gains, demonstrating the effectiveness of reasoning compression.
  </details>

- **[Learning Which Device to Call: Online Tool Grounding for LLM Agents in IoT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25563)**  `arXiv:2610.25563`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yichen Liu, Xiaochong Jiang, Haoran Yu, Lifei Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Internet of Things (IoT) gives large language model (LLM) agents access to physical observations, but one requested capability is usually offered by several devices, and the agent's plan does not say which one to call. Existing work retrieves relevant tools and checks plan feasibility, without learning which eligible device returns a useful observation. Unlike previous works, we study online tool grounding: binding an already specified tool call to one physical endpoint under service cost and device participation constraints. We propose a framework that learns which physical device should execute each agent-issued call. It formulates selection as a contextual bandit problem and combines adaptive context partitions with hierarchical endpoint trees, so that execution feedback is reused across related requests and devices. Selection balances task success against service cost while respecting device capability, authorization, battery reserves, and availability. Gaussian reward aggregation provides an optional mechanism for protecting individual feedback values. Theoretical results establish a finite-horizon regret bound and differential privacy for released reward statistics under fixed, reward-independent assignments. In a simulated loading-dock workload, the proposed router achieves higher step success and better cost-adjusted service, at lower simulated device energy and latency, than nearest-device, advertised-quality, UCB1, and LinUCB selection.
  </details>

- **[Symbolic Constraint Agents: Mining Formal Tool-Call Constraints to Prevent Agent Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25552)**  `arXiv:2610.25552`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinu Lee, Shraddha Barke_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic constraints (programmatic, deterministic checks for tool calls) enable deterministic verification of LLM agents' tool calls, but existing approaches primarily enforce predefined safety policies rather than tool dependencies in complex tool environments. We explore whether symbolic constraints can be mined from environments and observations beyond predefined policies, and whether they can improve end-to-end task performance. We present Symbolic Constraint Agents (SCA), a framework that mines symbolic constraints from API documentation, refines them against execution traces, and checks the agent's tool calls with an SMT solver at runtime. A violation triggers a natural-language warning or, when the valid replacement is uniquely determined, an automatic repair. On AppWorld, refinement raises error-detection F1 from 37.0 to 58.3 on GPT-5.5 traces and from 39.3 to 61.9 on Qwen3.5-27B traces. At runtime, repair raises GPT-5.4-mini's task success by 5.4 points under a 40-call budget and 9.8 points when environment errors are hidden, while removing 59--87\% of failed calls across models, and transfers to $\tau^2$-bench with gains of up to 5.6 points. These findings show that automatically acquired symbolic constraints can improve tool-calling reliability and task completion, particularly when failures are costly or difficult to diagnose.
  </details>

- **[Editing as Language: Teaching LLM Agents a Grammar for Native Context Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25550)**  `arXiv:2610.25550`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yunlong Deng, Yan Li, Xing Hu, Yida Zhu, Sean Du, Weiyang Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A synopsis in novels folds a story into a few lines; a function in code gathers many steps with a single execution. Context editing is native to language itself. In this paper, we propose the Native Context Editing (NCE) framework, which gives language model agents a grammar for natively editing their own context. By encoding context-editing operations in a grammar of dedicated special tokens, we teach the agents to invoke edits through the language itself. We train agents on iteratively constructed trajectory segments, with each teacher edit conditioned on the evolving context. This establishes context editing as an atomic and inherent capability of LLM agents, enabling them to dynamically modify their context as they generate responses over long-horizon reasoning and interaction. Compared with context-editing methods that learn to generate tool-use code within specific external harness environments, our approach integrates context editing as an internal mechanism of the model's native generation process, enabling better generalization across diverse real-world workflows. On four long-horizon agentic benchmarks (Terminal-Bench 2.0, SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Multilingual, and SWE-bench Pro), NCE improves the average task success rate over the base model by 5.6 percentage points for LongCat-Flash-Lite-Sparse and by 4.5 for Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, while reducing average token usage by 36% and 15%, respectively.
  </details>

- **[StopLoss: Teaching Agents to Predict Failure and Stop Early](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25525)**  `arXiv:2610.25525`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiarong Liang, Xueguang Ma, Dongfu Jiang, Yuxuan Zhang, Ping Nie, Wenhu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents keep working on runs that will fail verification, and asking a frontier model to watch for this is expensive in its own right. We introduce StopLoss, a small external monitor that learns failure recognition from completed trajectories and their verifier outcomes. The name is inspired by the stop-loss order in finance: StopLoss ends a run early when the run is likely to fail, which saves resources. Final outcomes label earlier local windows, so a fine-tuned language model can score the most recent tool steps in a single forward pass, and a threshold calibrated on successful runs turns that score into an abort decision without changing the executor. Trained on GPT-family SWE-rebench trajectories and evaluated on SWE-bench Verified and a SWE-bench Pro subset with GPT and Claude executors, StopLoss is compared against six general-purpose judges reading identical windows. Outcome supervision lifts failure prediction from near chance to a useful signal at the first check, at one to two orders of magnitude lower cost per check than any tested judge. This translates into savings: relative to completing every task, StopLoss lowers cost per retained solve by 4.5\% on Verified while preserving 99\% of successful runs, and by up to 31\% on the costlier Pro subset, reaching 48\% with a Qwen3-8B backbone, and in all four settings its cost per retained solve is below that of every judge policy. Outcome-trained small monitors can make failure recognition cheap enough for early stopping to pay for itself.
  </details>

- **[When Empathy Misses the Goal: A Benchmark for Goal Displacement in LLM Advice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25522)**  `arXiv:2610.25522`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dean Ariel, Guy Laban_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational large language models can respond with warmth and reassurance while failing to advance the user's stated goal. We conceptualize this failure as $\textbf{goal displacement}$, the substitution of persona-maintenance content for goal-relevant substance. To measure it, we introduce $\textbf{GDB}$ (Goal Displacement Benchmark), comprising 230 scenarios across nine domains and $4{,}134$ human-scored responses from 18 models. A $2 \times 2$ taxonomy crosses hard constraints with emotional distress, distinguishing failures to confront infeasible goals or combine empathy with practical guidance from reverse displacement, where unsolicited problem-solving replaces requested emotional support. A deletion-test rubric assesses whether substantive help remains after purely supportive sentences are removed. Independent raters achieve weighted $\kappa = 0.82$ against author scores for displacement severity. Trap displacement ranges from $0.0\%$ to $37.7\%$ and is strongly negatively correlated with Chatbot Arena Elo ($\rho = -0.706$, $n = 15$). Reverse displacement reaches $41.9\%$, including substantial rates in models with negligible trap displacement. A factorial probe supports a distinction from social sycophancy. Human-scored responses in the high-complexity, low-pressure condition exhibit $35.0\%$ sycophancy but $7.5\%$ displacement, with little association between the constructs. Matched OLMo-2 checkpoints show that mixed empathy-with-constraints displacement remains at $52.3\%$ after supervised fine-tuning and falls to $13.8\%$ after direct preference optimization. GDB provides a reproducible diagnostic of whether responses advance the operationalized user goal, supporting model comparison, post-training evaluation, and regression testing. We release the dataset, annotations, and rubric under CC-BY-4.0 and the evaluation code under MIT.
  </details>

- **[Formal Raising: Embedding Verifiable Ethical Properties into the Development of AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25501)**  `arXiv:2610.25501`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rolf Drechsler, Christina Plump_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Alan Turing proposed constructing a ``child machine'' and educating it rather than directly programming an adult-like intelligent machine. Recent work on model raising revives this developmental view for Large Language Models by arguing that moral values should accompany capability acquisition from the beginning rather than be added only through post-hoc alignment. This paper proposes Formal Raising (FR): selected moral values are represented as explicit, machine-checkable properties that participate in development, evaluation, and action selection. We present a C-based reasoning framework in which candidate actions are evaluated against prioritized ethical properties. Conflicts are resolved w.r.t. a given order, strict rules are non-defeasible, and underdetermination is reported explicitly. Exceptions are not encoded as special cases but emerge from the comparison of alternatives. We provide examples that cover conflicts involving life, truthfulness, harm, privacy, autonomy, and strict rules. We outline a property layer inspired by a Property Specification Language (PSL) for state and temporal requirements and discuss its integration into a raising process.
  </details>

- **[Stepwise Reasoning Verification: Don't Generate, Classify!](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25441)**  `arXiv:2610.25441`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yifei Shi, Michalis Korakakis, Rami Aly, Eric Chamoun, Lukasz Szpruch, Andreas Vlachos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifying the reasoning of large language models is increasingly important as they are applied to complex mathematical and scientific problems. Existing work focuses on large decoder models and reports mixed results for generative and discriminative verifiers, leaving unclear which paradigm is more effective and whether large models are necessary. In this work, we cast stepwise verification as a natural language inference task: the problem and partial solution prefix form the premise, while the next reasoning step serves as the hypothesis to be classified as entailed or non-entailed. This formulation enables error localisation at individual reasoning steps and direct comparison of encoder classification, decoder classification, and decoder verdict generation across model scales and annotation budgets. Across five reasoning datasets, a 0.15B encoder classifier outperforms most generative verifiers with up to 4B parameters. On identical decoder backbones, classification outperforms generation from 0.5B to 4B parameters and matches it on the strongest 7B backbone---reaching comparable performance with roughly one tenth as many annotated problems. Our findings establish classification as a parameter- and data-efficient approach to stepwise verification and show that strong verification does not require large generative decoders.
  </details>

- **[When Form Changes but Logic Doesn't: Building Logic-invariant LLMs through Logical Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25402)**  `arXiv:2610.25402`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuyuan Liu, Xinshuai Dong, Elynn Chen, Yujun Yan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance across diverse reasoning tasks, yet it remains unclear whether this performance reflects an understanding of underlying logical structure or a reliance on shallow semantic cues. A key indicator of such understanding is whether models treat logically equivalent inputs consistently, producing the same predictions despite differences in expression. Through systematic evaluation on curated data, we show that even strong LLMs often fail this test: they do not consistently preserve predictions across logically equivalent inputs and generalize poorly to unseen logical forms. To address this limitation, we propose LoGIcal STructure-guided reasoning (LoGIST), a framework that guides LLM reasoning with explicit logical structure. LoGIST maps each input instance to a decision diagram, a directed acyclic graph that captures the underlying logical structure, and encodes this graph into a logical embedding that conditions the LLM's reasoning process. Across multiple training settings, LoGIST reduces inconsistency under logical equivalence and improves generalization to unseen logical forms, suggesting that logical invariance is a useful target for building more robust and generalizable logical reasoning in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[BrandEval: A Two-Track Multi-Agent Benchmark for Risk-Sensitive LLM Crisis Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25208)**  `arXiv:2610.25208`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Gan, Weidong Tang, Hanbin Sun, Yi Luo, Xintian Li, Jiayong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating LLMs for crisis communication requires more than judging isolated responses, because public feedback and competitor actions can reshape trust over time. Existing benchmarks remain largely static and single-turn, offering limited evidence about long-horizon risk-sensitive communication. We introduce \textbf{BrandEval}, a two-track benchmark for product-crisis communication, focusing on public brand responses to product-related safety, quality, service, and trust incidents. BrandEval-Static evaluates stakeholder-aware strategic analysis, while BrandEval-Dynamic stress-tests response policies in \textbf{BrandPolis}, a controlled multi-agent sandbox with consumer feedback, competitor responses, and trust-risk trajectories. We also construct \textbf{BrandSRD}, a Chinese crisis-response dataset, and instantiate a reference Strategic Agent using \textbf{Strategic Rationale (SR)}. Across proprietary and open-source models, we find that static strategic quality is informative but incomplete: it aligns with some end-state outcomes but does not reliably predict volatility and delayed recovery; dynamic stress tests further expose tail-risk failures under manipulative or weakly grounded policies. The code and dataset will be available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[One Skill Takes Over: Clashing Agent Skills and a One-File Fix](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25064)**  `arXiv:2610.25064`  `cs.AI`  
  _Parsa Razmara, Seyedarmin Azizi, Ardalan Aryashad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent Skills are instruction files that tell a coding agent how to do one job. Users install skills from many authors, so two skills for the same job can be active at the same time. We ask what a model does when both skill files are in its context: does it combine their compatible rules, or follow one skill and drop the other? We build SkillClash, 16 pairs of same-job skills in which code checks every rule and a reference output shows the best result that can be reached. On seven models from five families, from 4B open models to Qwen3-235B and GPT-4.1, a skill's rules are followed 93 to 99% of the time when it is loaded alone and 61 to 62% when a same-job skill is loaded next to it. Most outputs show takeover: every rule that only one skill asks for is followed, and every rule that only the other skill asks for is dropped. The worked examples inside the skills drive this. When each skill file instead ends with one example that follows both skills, takeover falls to 1 to 15% on the six models we tested. Telling the model which skill has priority does not bring the dropped skill back, and telling it to apply both skills brings the dropped skill back only on the two strong models and leaves the interacting rules broken. One merged skill file written by the strongest model we used, Claude Opus 5.5, lets all seven models follow 96 to 99% of the achievable rules. Most of its gain over a plain list of both skills' rules comes from its one worked example, and such an example helps only when it is correct. Code and data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SteadyThought: Mitigating LLM Under-Thinking via Thought-Level Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25045)**  `arXiv:2610.25045`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhenyue Peng, Lin Yang, Jie Wang, Xiaoqi Ni, Hanzhu Chen, Xize Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flexible switching between reasoning trajectories (i.e., thought switching) has significantly enhanced the reasoning capabilities of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs). However, existing models often switch excessively yet fail to sustain promising reasoning thoughts, a phenomenon termed "under-thinking". While recent efforts suppress switching to mitigate this, such over-correction may discard valuable trajectories. To address this challenge, we propose Steady Thought (ST), a novel thought-level preference optimization framework. ST formalizes under-thinking as a preference issue at switching points, forcing single-path continuations from identified points to yield high-quality reasoning. Then, ST performs thought-level preference optimization by treating the newly generated response as preferred and the original one as dis-preferred. Experiments across multiple models and datasets show that ST effectively reduces token consumption, achieving reductions of up to 63.8%, while maintaining strong reasoning accuracy. Further analysis suggests that ST helps models acquire a generalizable ability to reduce redundant switching across domains and languages. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CorrectRTL: Boost RTL Generation Quality Through Automated Self-Correction with Instruction Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24977)**  `arXiv:2610.24977`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jing Wang, Shang Liu, Hangan Zhou, Wenji Fang, Yuchao Wu, Yugao Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong promise in specification-to-RTL generation. Among the explorations, the self-correction paradigm has significantly advanced LLM-based RTL generation. However, two challenges limit further progress. Existing benchmarks are saturated by advanced LLMs, leaving little room to evaluate the agentic self-correction framework. The monolithic self-correction paradigm rewrites the entire design at once, where LLMs are susceptible to error confusion and corruption of originally correct code. To address the benchmark challenge, we contribute ScaleBench, a more challenging benchmark of 200 complex RTL designs. To address the challenge in methodology, we propose CorrectRTL, an agentic framework that realizes a new decompose-then-correct paradigm, in which the LLM agent explicitly decouples scaffold construction from detailed implementation. CorrectRTL contributes three core techniques: (1)~\emph{Structure-oriented decomposition}, (2)~\emph{Snippet-based RAG}, and (3)~\emph{SVA-based self-correction}. On ScaleBench, CorrectRTL improves Pass@1 from 20.5% to 49.0% (+28.5%). On VerilogEval, CorrectRTL achieves up to 96.1% Pass@1, demonstrating consistent improvements across both simple and complex designs.
  </details>

- **[MemPrice: Online Memory Allocation via Adaptive Pricing for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24955)**  `arXiv:2610.24955`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiming Zong, Shiyuan Piao, Jiashuo Jiang, Fugee Tsung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents face a fundamental allocation problem as interaction histories grow: which information to retain, how much context to devote to it, and at what granularity. Existing memory mechanisms largely focus on relevance, retrieval, or compression, leaving this allocation trade-off implicit. We introduce MemPrice, which treats active context as a shared resource and dynamically allocates it across memories and representation levels. MemPrice turns accumulated memory pressure into an adaptive price on context, encouraging detail only when its estimated retention value justifies the additional footprint. This pressure persists across memory refreshes, allowing the contents of memory to evolve while preserving long-run control over context usage. We show that irreversible compression can force linear worst-case regret against hindsight following its own memory trajectory, and introduce a same-state benchmark to evaluate allocation on MemPrice's realized memory states. MemPrice enforces the hard context budget at every round and regulates long-run occupancy through adaptive pricing, without prior knowledge of the interaction horizon. Under priced-frontier regularity, its average allocation-regret bound against this benchmark vanishes when accumulated estimation error is sublinear. We evaluate the framework under matched memory budgets on LongMemEval, LoCoMo, AppWorld, and WebShop across three foundation models, spanning both query-blind memory construction and goal-directed agent context management.
  </details>

- **[IndustrialCAD-1400: Benchmarking Detailed Industrial Assemblies from Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24789)**  `arXiv:2610.24789`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hebao Zhu, Kang An, Zhuoke Liao, Faqiang Qian, Ziliang Wang, Mingang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial assemblies require more than a recognizable shape: functional parts, interfaces, dimensions and service features must satisfy a detailed request together. We introduce IndustrialCAD-1400, a text-to-CAD benchmark for this industrial setting. Its 1,400 requests combine 400 equipment categories across 40 domains with 250 four-case design families. Family requests specify a median of 17 named component or configuration roles and six explicit numeric targets; controlled variants test whether dimensions change while shared structure is retained. Task construction draws on documented manufacturer information, owner specifications and engineering guidance. Models generate geometry programs from requests, and a common Blender environment executes them to produce scenes, optional measurements, and standardized views for evaluation. A request assessment of front, isometric, and top views measures visible component, arrangement, and interface fulfillment without requiring a single reference geometry. Five stratified panels preserve complete families while covering the benchmark exactly once, enabling balanced comparisons and separating industrial breadth from controlled numerical responsiveness. In conclusion, IndustrialCAD-1400 extends text-to-CAD from recognizable part geometry to industrial assembly synthesis under joint component, interface, spatial, and numerical specifications.
  </details>

- **[ReTRACE: Feedback-Guided LLM Agent in a Chemistry Harness for Retrosynthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24744)**  `arXiv:2610.24744`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinxiang Xie, Yijian Zhang, Qingyuan Yi, Jianyi Wang, Hao Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer-aided retrosynthesis has advanced through single-step prediction, tree search and route scoring, but complex targets still require judgement over the whole route, where stereochemistry, functional-group compatibility, cost and greenness interact. Here we present \textbf{\mbox{ReTRACE}} (\textbf{RE}trosynthesis with \textbf{T}ool-orchestrated \textbf{R}easoning \textbf{A}nd \textbf{C}ontext-aware \textbf{E}vidence), an agent that places a large language model (LLM) in a chemistry harness that integrates molecular tools, complementary single-step prediction engines, a purchasable starting-material library, a route memory and a multi-criterion route evaluator. Routes are judged by a new metric that combines programmatic checks with adversarial LLM review, and its verdicts are compared with a synthetic chemist's. The agent learns continually from evaluator feedback during search, diagnosing weak routes and backtracking to revise them. With Claude Opus 4.8, at least one of the top 10 routes proposed by ReTRACE passed the audit for 87.4\%, 95.0\% and 86.7\% of targets on the three benchmarks. Two prospective wet-lab syntheses show that ReTRACE route proposals can be converted into executable procedures after expert revision.
  </details>

- **[Can LLM Agents Resolve Service Failures? Simulating Multi-Turn Complaint Handling under Information Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24733)**  `arXiv:2610.24733`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wailing Ng, Mengze Hong, Chen Jason Zhang, Shiqi Fan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are widely adopted in service chatbots for rule-guided support and information access, yet emotionally demanding complaint handling still relies heavily on human agents. We introduce ServiceBench, to our knowledge the first benchmark dedicated to complaint handling, with 2,500 scenarios spanning 184 complaint categories and five service industries. We propose Asymmetric Progressive Complaint Simulation (APCS) to simulate realistic complaint handling, together with a justice-based evaluation framework. Our experiments reveal that even capable LLM agents struggle to handle complaints effectively. They lack the ability to proactively gather information and strategically guide conversations toward resolution, often resorting to premature compensation. We further show that targeted post-training with SFT and DPO substantially improves these capabilities, enabling a smaller model to match or surpass much larger models on several outcomes.
  </details>

- **[Where Natural Language Ends in Agents: Executable Skill Composition with MetaSkill](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24723)**  `arXiv:2610.24723`  `cs.AI`  
  _Meng Shu, Wei He, Hang Zhou, Runke Liu, Kai Han, Yunhe Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent Skills express open-ended task knowledge in natural language, but composing them requires persistent execution order and upstream-output handoffs. We propose selective formalization at Skill boundaries: coordination relations with stable references and machine-checkable constraints become executable, while substantive task judgments remain within Skills. We introduce MetaSkill, a hybrid representation that preserves complete Skill packages and local instructions while making invocation dependencies and predecessor-text bindings executable through a directed acyclic graph (DAG) runtime. This interface supports explicit coordination without prescribing how each Skill performs its local task. Across 20 multi-Skill artifact tasks and two LLMs, we compare natural-language procedures, prompt-visible graphs, and MetaSkill derived from a common frozen plan per task, using the same designated Skill packages. Additional comparisons evaluate AgentSkillOS with the same available Skill packages as MetaSkill, as well as agent systems using their configured tools. Blinded artifact judgments yield favorable preference point estimates across all reported comparisons, with 95% task-bootstrap intervals excluding parity against Prompt Graph under DeepSeek-V4-Flash and OpenClaw under GLM-5.2. In audited GLM-5.2 execution traces, all planned nodes complete and all declared precedence constraints are satisfied. Together, these findings support MetaSkill as a practical interface for composing open-ended Skills, combining local linguistic flexibility with executable dependencies, explicit predecessor-text bindings, and auditable coordination. Code is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Selm: Self-Evolving Library Management for Multi-Skill Agent Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24630)**  `arXiv:2610.24630`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mengxi Hu, Yuxuan Hu, Jinbo Su, Ke Wang, Honghao Shi, Wenli Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use skills as a lightweight way to acquire task-specific knowledge. Recent work enables skills to evolve from task trajectories. However, these methods often lack systematic management of evolved skills, making it difficult for agents to reuse and accumulate skills across domains, thus limiting performance on subsequentthis http URLreliably accumulate, reuse, and maintain useful skills across tasks, we present SELM (Self-Evolving Library Management), a framework that formulates skill evolution as library-level management. SELM jointly manages skill retrieval, updating, and lifecycle by maintaining skill confidence and utility signals. It introduces utility-aware retrieval to select useful skills, a Map-Reduce framework to generate targeted updates from task-specific experience, and lifecycle controls to retain or remove skills based on their effectiveness.
